![обложка|156](<The Māyāpur-Vṛndāvana Festivals with Śrīla Prabhupāda (1972–77)/обложка.jpeg>)

This book shows how the festivals grew, how Prabhupada developed them from scratch in the pioneering days, carefully nurturing them into glorious events. Remembering our experiences with Prabhupada at the festivals and recalling his thoughts, deeds, and instructions during the festival days will help us keep the International Society for Krishna Consciousness Founder-acarya, Srila Prabhupada in its center.

# The Māyāpur-Vṛndāvana Festivals with Śrīla Prabhupāda (1972–77)

## Dedication

We are pleased to dedicate this book to the hero and heart of the Māyāpur-Vṛndāvana Festivals, Śrīla Prabhupāda and all those sincere followers by whose enduring efforts — past, present and future, these festivals will continue to expand our devotion.

## About this Edition

We did it! Again!

*Festivals* was my very first full-length book compiled to commemorate the Thirtieth Anniversary of the first Māyāpur festival in 1972.

With the advent of the Golden Jubilee Anniversary of ISKCON in 2016 coinciding with a demand for a sold out first edition of *Festivals* , we have pleasure is presenting this second edition now entitled *The Māyāpur-Vrindāvana Festivals: With Śrīla Prabhupāda (1972-77)*

This updated edition sports a new cover, simply, but attractively designed pages, as well as a 16-page gallery taking you on a visual experience.

In addition to the valuable contribution of the devotees in completing this initial project in 2001, for this edition I would like to thank Puṇḍarīka Vidyānidhī Dāsa for coordinating and overseeing the publication, Radha Prema Dāsi, for pagination of the index and Dīnānukaṁpā Dāsi for proofreading.

*Lokanāth Swami*

## Foreword

In our modern world, festivals are loved by one and all, so much so that they constitute an integral part of peoples’ lives. There are film festivals, shopping festivals, music festivals—you name it, they celebrate it. However, the spiritual world takes festivals to a whole new dimension. There, every step is a dance, every word a song and every day a festival. Śrīla Prabhupāda brought the spiritual world to even the remote parts of our planet. Festivals are an inseparable part of our lives and they inspire us to progress rapidly and enthusiastically in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. The Vaiṣṇava calendar is filled with a plethora of festivals. While local temples celebrated most festivals, Śrīla Prabhupāda wanted two festivals where all of his followers from across the world could gather together in the spiritual embassies on earth—Māyāpur and Vṛndāvana.

From the time Śrīla Prabhupāda began to establish his projects in Māyāpur and Vṛndāvana, he instructed us to have an annual Gaura Pūrṇimā festival. The first Gaura Pūrṇimā festival in Māyāpur was in 1972. I was fortunate to help organize this festival and subsequent ones. In a couple of years, this festival was followed by a similar one in Vṛndāvana. During these festivals, devotees had the opportunity to be absorbed in the five primary items of Kṛṣṇa consciousness as taught by Lord Caitanya to Sanātana Gosvāmi: “One should associate with devotees, chant the Lord’s holy name, hear *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*, reside at Mathurā and worship the Deity with faith and veneration” (*Caitanya-caritāmṛta, Madhya* . [22.128](https://vedarama.com/en/books/cc-madhya/22.128). ).

Śrīla Prabhupāda’s followers spend a large part of the year giving out Kṛṣṇa consciousness to the unfortunate, conditioned souls in every nook and corner of the planet. They often do this in unfavorable circumstances or in challenging environments. Kali‘s presence is strong everywhere. Therefore, Śrīla Prabhupāda wanted to create an environment where these selfless preachers and devotees could get together and associate with each other to rejuvenate and strengthen their Kṛṣṇa consciousness, like an oasis for their spiritual lives. In fact, he once indicated that to visit Māyāpur and Vṛndāvana every year is as important as honoring Kṛṣṇa-*prasāda*. To regularly visit the holy places of Navadvīpa and Vraja in the association of devotees in good standing is not merely desirable; it is essential for one to progress in Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Thakura also instructed that Lord Caitanya’s devotees visit Śrī Māyāpur at least once a year just as during the Lord’s manifest pastimes. His devotees would visit Him personally, every year. He also noted that Gauḍīya Vaiṣṇavas have a natural attraction for Śrī Vṛndāvana.

Lokanāth Swami has comprehensively described the history of the annual festivals in Māyāpur and Vṛndāvana in this book. Since the early days Mahārāja has played a very active role in these festivals. Thus, his unique vantage point makes him well-equipped to write this book. Mahārāja has received much mercy from Śrīla Prabhupāda. He has dedicated his life to carrying out His Divine Grace’s instructions. He leads *padayātrās* worldwide along with his GBC Ministry’s responsibilities and his duties as a Guru. Lokanāth Mahārāja is co-chairman of the Māyāpur festival and annually arranges the Navadvīpa-maṇḍala *parikramā*. Every year he also takes devotees out on Vraja-maṇḍala *parikramā*.

We hope that all devotees will imbibe Śrīla Prabhupāda’s teachings seriously and turn every day of their lives into a festival. I am sure that by reading this book, you will be inspired to participate in the annual Māyāpur-Vṛndāvana festival! See you soon!

*Jayapatākā Swami*

## Preface

While compiling a history of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s activities in India I gathered more material than would fit into a single volume, so I decided to publish first this separate book about the Māyāpur and Vṛndāvana festivals. Jananivāsa Prabhu, the head *pūjārī* of Śrī Śrī Rādhā-Mādhava and a permanent resident of Māyāpur, said something that inspired me. To my surprise, he said, “Most of the time that Śrīla Prabhupāda stayed in Māyāpur was during the festivals.” The actual time Prabhupāda spent at festivals in Māyāpur and Vṛndāvana added up to about six months, and the more I thought about it, the more I realized the importance of the festivals and why they deserved a book of their own.

I’ve taken part in hundreds of festivals worldwide in my forty-four years as a disciple of Śrīla Prabhupāda, but the mother of them all is the annual Māyāpur-Vṛndāvana festival. If you go to this festival, you experience the International Society for Krishna consciousness (ISKCON)—you see devotees from many nations. The Hare Kṛṣṇa movement would not be the same without it. It’s been a part of most devotees’ lives at least occasionally, if not annually. Devotees pray to go. The holy *dhāmas* Māyāpur and Vṛndāvana are to the devotees what Mecca is to the Muslims and Jerusalem to the Jews. Every devotee attending the festival can gain a deeper understanding of Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

Śrīla Prabhupāda’s idea was to introduce us to the *dhāmas* at the auspicious festival time of Gaura Pūrṇimā. Prabhupāda was training us to grasp the truth of the *dhāmas,* and the annual festivals were the closest we could get. Our visiting the *dhāmas* during the festivals is like an apprenticeship. We practice serving there, and that gives us a glimpse of going back to Godhead. Śrīla Prabhupāda revealed the *dhāmas* and linked us to them. He went around the world, stole conditioned souls from the clutches of *māyā,* and released them in the *dhāmas.* On behalf of previous *ācāryas,* Śrīla Prabhupāda gave us the opportunity to make a pilgrimage right to the lotus feet of Śrī Kṛṣṇa Caitanya and Śrī Śrī Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa.

This book highlights, among other things, how the first Māyāpur-Vṛndāvana festivals molded Śrīla Prabhupāda’s followers during their spiritual childhood. My friend from the United Kingdom Ajāmila Prabhu was a *brahmacari* in Māyāpur in the mid-1970’s, doing deity worship, studying Prabhupāda’s books, and helping look after cows. He said, “Although conditions at the festival were very austere, the many hundreds of devotees were undeterred. Our all-out enthusiasm in the *kīrtanas* and festivities produced inescapable feelings of bliss that inscribed in us life-altering memories.” Ajāmila’s life was certainly altered. He later published three volumes of selected letters of Prabhupāda; he began to fulfill Prabhupāda’s desire that the *Bhāgavatam* be presented for children by coming out with a beautiful *Illustrated Bhāgavatam;* he saw his teenage son through the Māyāpur *gurukula* and into the university; and he is continuing his publishing ventures. That’s almost a lifetime of activity, initially inspired by the Māyāpur festival.

The festivals during Śrīla Prabhupāda’s days had a special flavor. At those festivals the devotees saw Prabhupāda daily for almost a month, whereas most devotees normally did not see him for a whole year. Devotees aspired for these opportunities to stay at the same temple with Prabhupāda and sometimes in the same building, and they testified to his sweet nature in Māyāpur and Vṛndāvana.

**Mahānidhi Swami:** Naturally devotees hoped to be with Prabhupāda in India. Where I was serving—America—we heard that Prabhupāda was easily approachable in India. In America we observed formalities with Prabhupāda, so I looked forward to my first festival, eager to see Prabhupāda in a new mood—the relaxed, informal mood that we’d heard about from godbrothers.

**Gaṅgāmayī Dāsi:** At these festivals Prabhupāda gave us a lot of nectar. In London he emphasized: “Chant sixteen rounds; follow the principles.” But in Māyāpur and Vṛndāvana he gave us Kṛṣṇa consciousness without telling us that so much. It seemed we had gotten past that stage.

Cooperation reached all-time highs during the festival days. The devotees’ enthusiasm—their natural love and selfless dedication to Prabhupāda—shone through. Willingness to sacrifice and perform austerities became second nature. Because devotees need these qualities to thrive and survive, I hope that readers imbibe them by taking this tour of the past.

I also hope that this book helps to further increase the loving memories my godbrothers and godsisters have of Śrīla Prabhupāda. When Prabhupāda was with us, work became play. We overcame differences and difficulties, and we still need that spirit. These days we visit the same Māyāpur, the same Vṛndāvana—we see the same deities at the same time of year in the same climate—but many of us feel something is missing. One missing factor is Śrīla Prabhupāda in person. I pray that *The Māyāpur-Vṛndāvana Festivals* fills this vacuum to some extent. If it does so, I will consider my efforts worthwhile. This book shows how the festivals grew, how Prabhupāda developed them from scratch in the pioneering days, carefully nurturing them into glorious events. Remembering our experiences with Prabhupāda at the festivals and recalling his thoughts, deeds, and instructions during the festival days will help us keep Śrīla Prabhupāda in the center of ISKCON.

By reading, even people who couldn’t be there will now be able to see Prabhupāda’s vision unfold, just as it did before our amazed eyes. If some devotees nowadays cannot attend the festivals, they can relax in their homes or temples and relish these memories of Prabhupāda at the festivals and receive the benefit of attending. Besides being a historical record, this book will help pilgrims prepare mentally, culturally, and spiritually.

Themes recur each year: Śrīla Prabhupāda’s arrival, progress on buildings, devotees’ accommodations, *parikramās* to holy sites, classes and *darśanas*, morning walks, and health problems. Each year’s festival offers new flavors and highlights, and each chapter makes you feel as if you are present at the festival, dancing in the *kīrtana* or hearing Śrīla Prabhupāda speak.

To compile the text, I watched videos, read Prabhupāda’s talks, letters, and conversations, and interviewed devotees who attended the festivals. About twenty-five years have passed since the festivals, so our memories have faded and personal perceptions may have replaced some facts. If there are discrepancies over details, please forgive that. Although I did not interview everybody, I did my best to present things as they were. If devotees had kept diaries, much more could be published, but I have dedicated this much. I used the interview material in three ways: first, setting the scenes with general information; second, quoting devotees’ remembrances; and third, letting devotees describe special incidents between themselves and Prabhupāda, which I call *“uvācas”* — e.g*.,* “Mālatī *uvāca.”* I included only samples from Śrīla Prabhupāda’s talks and conversations, and I sometimes put them into a narrative format for easy reading, but I did not intend to minimize Prabhupāda’s words in any way. Readers are invited to study the complete material in the Vedabase or books compiled by the Bhaktivedānta Book Trust (BBT) Archives.

Māyāpur is important to Vaiṣṇavas because they become qualified to enter Vṛndāvana by the mercy of Māyāpur, just as they obtain Kṛṣṇa by worshiping Gaurānga. Śrīla Prabhupāda first got the Māyāpur festival going in 1972, and then he added the Vṛndāvana festival in 1974, a year before he opened the Krishna -Balaram temple. Since then, the Māyāpur-Vṛndāvana festivals have been held annually. Hare Kṛṣṇa.

*Lokanāth Swami*  
*Māyāpur-Vṛndāvana Festival*  
*2001, Vṛndāvana*

## Prologue

Śrīla Prabhupāda left India in August 1965 with nothing but three trunks of his published books. He returned in August 1970 with forty temples and a few thousand disciples under his guidance and a $60,000 printing contract with a major publisher in Japan. Two American disciples, Acyutānanda Dāsa and Jayapatākā Dāsa, were already in India, establishing a footing for ISKCON, and Prabhupāda came to boost their efforts. To show Indians that Western people were adopting the principles of Kṛṣṇa consciousness, he brought along a large group of his young disciples, knowing this would increase the Indian’s faith in their own spiritual culture. Prabhupāda arrived with ten devotees, and he’d asked his leaders in the West for twenty more within a month. Prabhupāda knew it would be difficult persuading Indians to reactivate their Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Although under the reigns of Mahārāja Yudhiṣṭhira and other *rājarṣis,* India was Kṛṣṇa conscious, recently India had been under foreign rule—the Moguls and then the British. The intelligentsia—and to a lesser degree the Indian people in general—had lost respect for their own culture.

**Dhanañjaya Dāsa:** For centuries Westerners had gone to India and taught that economic development is the path to success. But Śrīla Prabhupāda’s disciples were teaching Indians that materialism isn’t the goal of human life and that industrialization won’t solve their problems. Only Westerners can teach this to Indians because Westerners know the emptiness and frustration of materialism. The modern generation of Indians hasn’t been through this yet, so they’re easily led to believe that modernization will make them happy.

Prabhupāda’s disciples were on the streets daily, performing *harināma* and distributing books, and they got a good response. They were wearing *dhotīs* and saris, playing *kartālas* and *mṛdaṅgas,* chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa with heart and soul, quoting Sanskrit verses from the *Bhagavad-gītā* affirming Lord Kṛṣṇa to be the Supreme Personality of Godhead. For Indians this was sensational, and hundreds gathered to watch. Prabhupāda knew the great appeal his disciples would have. Everyone would want to see them. Thus he affectionately called them his dancing white elephants. This was not a material tactic but a spiritual strength. Prabhupāda stressed to them that their pure behavior would attract the Indians.

Prabhupāda’s idea of *saṅkīrtana,* however, went far beyond a novel *harināma* party. He’d returned to India determined to firmly establish ISKCON’s activities, just as he was doing worldwide. He felt confident that Lord Kṛṣṇa and Lord Caitanya would help him achieve his purpose on a grand scale. Prabhupāda was no longer a lone *sannyāsī* selling his *Back to Godhead* magazine. He had succeeded beyond his dreams in the West, and he’d returned for a victory tour. The local population took notice of their hometown hero, acknowledging that he was doing the impossible. Prabhupāda wasn’t aspiring to any Western role model. Rather, Western devotees were aspiring to be like him.

So began the World *Saṅkīrtana* Party. After returning, Prabhupāda traveled across the Indian subcontinent for eight months, holding *paṇḍāl* programs and home programs. All across India, people talked about the Hare Kṛṣṇa sensation. He accepted as many invitations as possible—more than possible, it seemed to his disciples. Some days Prabhupāda attended five programs, and his disciples often lagged behind. But Prabhupāda felt at home. India had been his preaching field for thirty years. He found the climate, the food, the culture, and the atmosphere all agreeable.

In early 1971 the World *Saṅkīrtana* Party held a *paṇḍāl* program in Bombay (now Mumbai). This was my first contact with the Hare Kṛṣṇa movement. I began seeing pictures of Śrīla Prabhupāda all over Bombay. His photograph was superimposed over a picture of a globe, showing that Prabhupāda had covered the world. This portrait could be seen all over the city on posters, buses, billboards, and banners hanging across the streets.

The publicity, which emphasized American and European *sādhus,* created excitement in Bombay. “American” and *“sādhu”* were two words no Indian expected to see together. Indians in Bombay took it for granted that *sādhus* came from Haridwar, Vāraṇasī, Pandharpur, or other holy places, not America or Europe. So I was curious, and something inside me pulled me to the *paṇḍāl.*

I attended the event from the first night to the last. A hot-air balloon, bearing the *mahā-mantra* in huge letters, announced the festival for miles around. A book table was set up at the entrance and another table for *Back to Godhead* magazines on the opposite side. Printed in Japan to a high standard of excellence, the literature drew lots of attention. At the *Back to Godhead* table, devotees served *halavā prasāda* in leaf-cups. There were always hundreds and hundreds of people forming long queues, waiting for the Lord’s mercy.

Prabhupāda appealed to his audiences in a simple, straightforward manner:

This Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement is being spread all over the world on the scientific basis of Vedic culture. We have almost fifty branches. Now we have come to establish some branches in India. We invite everyone. There is no discrimination. Anyone can join us, and joining is very simple. Please come and try to understand the philosophy. Then you will see how effective it is. You will feel transcendental bliss and become spiritually advanced. Please join this movement, and you will be happy.

Another curious onlooker at the Bombay *paṇḍāl* program was Rādhānātha Swami. He said:

I was out walking one day and saw a huge sign about a Hare Kṛṣṇa festival at Cross Maidan. So I walked there. It was the first day of the program. The public was overwhelmed, seeing white people in saris and robes, dancing and playing *mṛdaṅgas* and the harmonium. I sat kind of close to Prabhupāda and observed the devotees and audience, and I could see how moved and amazed the audience was that these foreign devotees were surrendering their lives to Prabhupāda. The Indians were impressed that Prabhupāda had transformed the devotees’ lives.

The *paṇḍāl* program was held in late March and early April of 1971. In early March Prabhupāda had completed his negotiations for land in Māyāpur. He’d sent Tamal Krishna and Bali Mardana to buy a plot of land less than a mile from Caitanya Mahāprabhu’s birthsite. The plot measured nine *bighās,* or five-and-a-half acres. The owners, two Muslim brothers, settled for a payment of 14,500 rupees. After the purchase, Prabhāsa Dāsa, a Bengali disciple, constructed a hut, lived on the property, and worshiped Śrīla Prabhupāda’s picture. Tatpar Dāsa, another Bengali disciple, joined him in late 1971. Prabhupāda could not send Western devotees to live there because of a war between Pakistan and Bangladesh. The Indian government restricted Westerners from staying overnight in border regions, which included the Nadia district, where Māyāpur is located.

**Tatpar Swami:** In January 1972 about fifteen dacoits on a looting spree attacked our property. At that time Prabhāsa was in Calcutta, and I was alone. I was asleep, and their yelling woke me up. They had guns, so I could not stop them. They took everything: the stove, utensils, even my blanket. Later I somehow got a message to Calcutta: “We cannot stay alone because thieves are coming. We are feeling lonely and unprotected.” The reply came from Prabhupāda: “Do not fear. I am sending five people.”

Prabhupāda wanted special permits for his Western disciples to enter Māyāpur, not only because they could join his Indian disciples and help secure things, but also because he was planning a Gaura Pūrṇimā celebration for 1972. On January 21, Prabhupāda wrote to Jayapatākā Swami, the president of ISKCON Calcutta:

I especially want to stay in India for this Māyāpur celebration... Nobody will check us if we simply go there as ordinary pilgrims. Try for permission for all our men. One form has been sent to all our men outside of Calcutta to apply for the permission. I understand that all the Calcutta men are already applying directly from there.... Such a festival should be held very grandly. Gurudāsa Dāsa has gone to Delhi, so he can take further permission from there. There is a good chance the ban may be lifted by February 15, so as soon as five men can go, they should go there immediately to make preparations for a grand festival. I do not think that there will be difficulty if we just set up our camp there for *kīrtana* continuously. Anyone will see we are only serious devotees of Lord Caitanya and not Pakistani spies. So I think you should plan that we shall definitely be coming to Māyāpur and holding a grand festival there on Lord Caitanya’s appearance day.

Prabhupāda sent Jayapatākā Swami, Jananivāsa, and a few others to Māyāpur, along with the medium-size Rādhā-Mādhava deities he had first worshiped in Allahabad during the January 1971 Ardha-kumbha-melā *(ardha* means “half,” or halfway between the main Kumbha-melās, which are held every twelve years). Prabhupāda had continued worshiping these deities in Gorakhpur, and that is where he first taught the devotees the song *“Jaya* Rādhā-Mādhava,” and they were named Rādhā-Mādhava.

Prabhupāda wanted Indira Gandhi, India’s prime minister, to attend the Gaura Pūrṇimā festival and told Gurudāsa Dāsa that if he got her to come, then Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura would take him back to Vaikuṇṭha. Gurudāsa Dāsa invited her, but she declined, not wishing to appear sectarian. Prabhupāda then tried to invite the Governor of Bengal. He emphasized to Tamal Krishna that a big man should be present to make it “successful and auspicious”:

... if it is not possible to get Indira Gandhi or the Governor or the American ambassador for the festival at Māyāpur, then what is the use? In that case, the program should be cancelled until some later date, My going there depends on whether such a meeting can be arranged; otherwise, there is no need in my going. Unless some big man lays the cornerstone, our whole attempt will be frustrated. Actually the function in Māyāpur will be held for this purpose.

The “big man” that Prabhupāda wanted never materialized. But Prabhupāda was unstoppable in his vision to establish the Māyāpur project and went ahead with the first Gaura Pūrṇimā festival anyway.

## Chapter 1. The First Māyāpur Festival

In February 1972 Śrīla Prabhupāda was touring India. In a letter dated February 18, to Kṣīrodakśayī Dāsa, the president of ISKCON Vṛndāvana, he estimated how many people would attend the Māyāpur festival: “You may come to Māyāpur, and I have also invited Rāmānanda and all my disciples in India, and I think that we shall number at least fifty, because we are being joined by my godbrother Purī Mahārāja and some of his close assistants.” His last stop before Māyāpur was Visakha-patnam, where he stayed five days and lectured in Hindi at Śrī Śrīmad B. V. Purī Mahārāja’s Caitanya Maṭha. Prabhupāda arrived in Māyāpur in late February.

Prabhupāda had had a hand in purchasing the land in Māyāpur, but this was his first visit to the ISKCON property. Jayapatākā Swami and Pradyumna Dāsa, Prabhupāda’s assistant in translating the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam,* arrived with him. Before the visiting devotees began to arrive, only a few devotees were staying there.

Outside the property they had erected a roadside sign that read “Welcome Śrīla Prabhupāda!” “Prabhupāda” was written in big letters. Seeing it, Prabhupāda sarcastically said, “My godbrothers will like it.” They used the same title for Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura, so he anticipated their disapproval.

A makeshift bridge across a ditch led onto the ISKCON compound. The neighboring properties had no buildings or huts, but nearby there were a few Gauḍīya Maṭha temples with high, colorful spires. ISKCON’s property was completely surrounded by flat fields of rice, and devotees could see the horizon in all directions. The atmosphere was uncommonly serene and beautiful.

About seventy devotees attended the first Māyāpur festival, which lasted five days. The camp and tents were set up close to the road, visible to thousands of people from Navadvīpa walking to Yoga-pīṭha, the birthplace of Caitanya Mahāprabhu. These pilgrims were amazed to see foreign devotees. Perhaps they began to understand that this was the practical fulfillment of Lord Caitanya’s prophecy that Kṛṣṇa’s names would be heard and chanted around the world. Thus ISKCON’s first Gaura Pūrṇimā festival, though small, was greatly significant.

Prabhupāda’s younger sister Bhavatāriṇī, whom the devotees affectionately called Pisimā (meaning “Auntie,” or “the father’s sister”), also came to Māyāpur. During their childhood she had assisted Prabhupāda in his devotional service. Together they would see Rādhā-Govinda in the temple, and they held Lord Jagannātha’s Ratha-yātrā with their friends. She was also Śrīla Prabhupāda’s godsister, having received first initiation from Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī.

## The Bhajan-Kuṭīr and Basic Accommodations

Prabhupāda lived in a thatched hut with a dirt floor—a *bhajan-kuṭīr,* or saintly person’s dwelling. A replica of it stands adjacent to the current main entrance to the Māyāpur temple compound. Inside the hut, a curtain divided the room. One side was Prabhupāda’s quarters; the other side was for the deities and Prabhupāda’s servant and secretary. Prabhupāda had a cot, a desk, a fan, and some mats on the floor for guests. The deities stood on a tiled platform, simply decorated, and Prabhupāda would take their *darśana* many times during the day. Sometime later, an extension to the hut was built for the deities. A small veranda ran around the front of the *bhajan-kuṭīr,* and a garden had been made at the back, where Prabhupāda would sit for his massage. Water for cooking and bathing was drawn from a hand pump out back.

**Tamal Krishna Goswami:** The hut was home for Śrīla Prabhupāda, even though he had traveled widely and lived in skyscrapers and palaces. Prabhupāda said it was the best home. When the devotees apologized for offering him such a humble residence, he said he liked the natural simplicity. “Even if you built me a beautiful palace,” he kindly replied, “I would still prefer to live here.”

At night Prabhupāda used a kerosene lamp. Because it was one of the few lights for miles around, it attracted bugs and mosquitoes. Devotees repelled the insects by burning *gobar—*dried cow–dung patties. The smoke made the devotees’ eyes water, but Prabhupāda was undisturbed. He said, “My Guru Mahārāja used to take *capātīs* cooked on *gobar.* It gives a nice flavor.”

**Tatpar Swami:** Prabhupāda acted like an ordinary person in India. When he came to Māyāpur, he was laughing and talking like a friend. He lived almost like the villagers. He’d talk with the Muslim farmers, and they sometimes gave him date-palm juice. Prabhupāda liked it. I used to bring him a pot of Gaṅgā water every day, and he would drink a little and then pour a little into everyone’s hand.

Devotees learned from Prabhupāda’s example how to live on the land without ordinary amenities, but Prabhupāda wanted guesthouses built in the future so that his disciples and guests would have proper places to stay. For the first couple of years, however, the accommodations in Māyāpur were meager. Next to Prabhupāda’s hut stood two spacious white tents, one for the men and one for the women. The women’s tent stood between the hut and the men’s tent. Many of the devotees present at the festival had traveled around India with Prabhupāda for the past year and a half, sometimes living in cold tents or guesthouses with bare rooms. They were getting used to basic living conditions.

**Viśākhā Dāsi:** There was a fluorescent light inside our tent, with no on-and-off switch; it was always on. The entrance to our tent was only six feet from the door to Prabhupāda’s *bhajan-kuṭīr*—close enough that in the early morning we could hear him translating the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam.*

A small tent behind the *bhajan-kuṭīr* served as the kitchen. Jananivāsa cooked, and the devotees took *prasāda* behind the *kuṭīr.* Sometimes Prabhupāda walked outside to observe them eating. He saw his disciples as pioneers undergoing great austerities to help him.

Cooking for Prabhupāda, Mālatī Dāsi went through one ordeal after another.

**Mālatī** ***uvāca*****:** I had to go to the treasurer for money to buy the vegetables. Usually a treasurer is trained to refuse requests for money. This one was really tough, so my days started with a big argument. He would not give me a *paisa* more than what the locals paid, and that made the rest of my morning intense. I had to get a ricksha to the dock to get a boat to Navadvīpa, and this entailed more arguments about money. Then there were arguments with the vegetable sellers. The whole morning I was arguing over money. Finally, I would return, take care of my husband and daughter, and then start cooking.

Getting water for cooking meant going to the pump. Every day the area around the pump was muddier and muddier. For stoves I had two metal buckets, and we used coal for fuel. It was difficult to get everything together on those bucket-stoves. And just when I needed to get the *capātīs* going—the most intense time of cooking—Prabhupāda’s servant might come along and take away both buckets so that he could heat Prabhupāda’s bath water.

When you cooked for Prabhupāda, everything had to be precise. You couldn’t give the excuse “I’ll be ready in a few minutes, Śrīla Prabhupāda.” The concept that *prasāda* would be late just did not exist. He would take his massage, his bath, then sit to chant Gāyatrī, and then his plate had to be there.

It was really a nonstop meditation. I would be thinking about what to buy and how to prepare it from the time I woke up till the time I went to sleep. I really thought about how I could please Śrīla Prabhupāda. But every day he would correct something I’d done wrong. One day he called me in and said, “Not enough salt.” I was trying to be conscientious, so I added salt. The next day he told me there was too much salt. I remember thinking, *Maybe I shouldn’t do this service. I don’t seem to be able to do anything right.* Not only that, but by the time I finished my service, all the *prasāda* would be gone, and I didn’t even get any of his remnants.

Just when I decided to tell Prabhupāda that I’d get someone else to cook, I heard that his godbrothers were coming for lunch. Prabhupāda started telling me what to prepare. So I couldn’t tell him then. I thought I would tell him the next day.

The next day, when I started serving *prasāda*, I saw a room full of elderly *sannyāsīs*. Prabhupāda was sitting at the end of the room. The room wasn’t long, but it seemed long because I had to walk in. Feeling completely intimidated by all of them, I just went down on my knees and covered my head with my sari. I was holding a plate, and I crawled the short distance to Prabhupāda on my knees and put the plate down in front of him.

Then Prabhupāda said, “She cooks for me, and I criticize her.” He paused, then continued, “But she would slit her throat for me, and I would do the same for her.”

I was completely stunned and realized that I had been seeing my situation in a mundane way, misunderstanding our relationship as guru and disciple. Dealing with money, taking care of my husband and daughter, and trying to cook had become too intense. But the next day I couldn’t wait to begin cooking, and all the arguing I had to do became enlivening.

One evening I served fried eggplants. Prabhupāda liked them and called them all-attractive. Then, referring to my three-year-old, he asked, “Where is your all-attractive daughter?”

“She is in all-attractive māyā,” I said.

Actually, she’s all-attractive,” Prabhupāda said, chuckling. “Everyone loves her.”

Sarasvatī was the most fortunate girl, the only person allowed into Prabhupāda’s quarters unannounced.

## Kīrtanas and Parikramās

Villagers and pilgrims, who had never seen white *sādhus,* viewed them with curiosity. As people walked past the property, the devotees gave them *prasāda.* When Prabhupāda arrived, he’d immediately arranged a twenty-four-hour *kīrtana,* with small groups chanting in two-hour shifts. He also encouraged a group of devotees to go on *parikramā* in the mornings, walking to nearby places of Lord Caitanya’s pastimes. After Caitanya Mahāprabhu’s disappearance, the Ganges flooded Māyāpur for a few hundred years. There are controversies about the location of His birthsite and other pastime places. Śrīla Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura labored to ascertain these places, and he wrote *Navadvīpa Dhāma Māhātmya* to describe Lord Nityānanda’s Navadvīpa-maṇḍala *parikramā* with Śrīla Jīva Gosvāmī—the first of its kind. The Ṭhākura said the whole world would be liberated by performing Navadvīpa-maṇḍala *parikramā,* and his son Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Mahārāja organized this *parikramā* eight times and celebrated Gaura Pūrṇimā festivals in Māyāpur with thousands of disciples. Śrīla Prabhupāda naturally wanted to perpetuate the Gaura Pūrṇimā festival in Māyāpur. Prabhupāda was doing as all the Gauḍīya Maṭhas did, but with a difference: His *parikramā* party consisted of the worldwide followers of Caitanya Mahāprabhu. Prabhupāda had fulfilled the dream of Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura, who predicted:

Lord Caitanya says in the *Caitanya-bhāgavata,* “In every town, country, and village, My name will be sung.” Very soon the unparalleled path of *harināma-saṅkīrtana* will be propagated all over the world. ... Oh, for that day when the fortunate English, French, Russian, German, and American people take up banners, *mṛdaṅgas* and *kartālas* and raise *kīrtana* through their streets and towns! When will that day come when the fair-skinned men chant *“Jaya* Śacīnandana! *Jaya* Śacīnandana!” and join with the Bengali devotees?

The *parikramās* were held on a small scale because very few devotees knew the places to visit. Acyutānanda Swami, who’d already been in India for five years, was the most knowledgeable. He was also an expert *kīrtana* leader, Bengali style. So he led the *parikramās.* One day the devotees visited the birthsite of Lord Caitanya, another day the house of Chand Kazi, and still another day the house of Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura. The devotees also took bath in the Gaṅgā every day. *Parikramā* was kept simple.

**Yamunā Dāsi:** We didn’t go to Māyāpur in the mood of exploring the *dhāma* but with the idea of laying a cornerstone for a building and experiencing the *dhāma* with our spiritual master. We had no independent idea of anything. Anything Śrīla Prabhupāda did and said was the main feature of the festival. Everything revolved around him.

## Perfect Questions, Perfect Answers

Bob Cohen joined the Peace Corps in 1971 and went to India as a science teacher. There he inquired about the Hare Kṛṣṇa movement. In February 1972 he met devotees, who invited him to the Māyāpur festival. For several days in a row, starting on February 27, Śrīla Prabhupāda invited Bob into his *kuṭīr* for talks. Their discussions were recorded and later published as a small book: *Perfect Questions, Perfect Answers.*

In this book Bob writes: “Before I met Śrīla Prabhupāda and his disciples, spiritual life was always obscure and nebulous. But the discussions with Śrīla Prabhupāda were realistic, clear, and exciting. Even though I had little ability to express myself, Śrīla Prabhupāda understood my every inquiry and answered perfectly.”

These meetings were a turning point for Bob. He became serious about Kṛṣṇa consciousness, and later he and his wife became Brahma-tīrtha Dāsa and Bhakti-devī Dāsi.

## Gaura Pūrṇimā

On the morning of February 29, in response to Prabhupāda’s invitations to take part in a cornerstone-laying ceremony, ten of his *sannyāsī* godbrothers arrived: Śrī Śrīmad Śrīdhar Mahārāja, Śrī Śrīmad B. V. Purī Mahārāja, and others —along with some of their disciples.

With plans in mind for a guesthouse and temple on the limited property that he had, Prabhupāda was prepared to lay a cornerstone. His special guests, his disciples, and hundreds of visitors took part in the ceremony. For such a ceremony, the Vedic scriptures prescribe placing five types each of various items into a pit in the earth. The items are flowers, grains, leaves, metals, nectars, fruits, jewels, and colors. The *Śrī Īśopaniṣad* mantras were chanted as an auspicious invocation. B. V. Purī Mahārāja descended the ladder into the pit and put these articles on an altar made of bricks. Next Śrīla Prabhupāda went down the ladder, carrying a box containing a gold, ruby-eyed *mūrti* of Ananta Śeṣa. Earlier that morning Prabhupāda had shown the *mūrti* to a few disciples.

**Yamunā Dāsi:** He put the beautiful deity on his finger and held it up for us to see. The bottom—the tail end —was curled, and the top—the hood—went up. It was solid gold, decorated with jewels. Śrīla Prabhupāda said, “The universe rests on the head of Ananta Śeṣa, and now He will hold up our temple.”

After Prabhupāda placed Ananta Śeṣa on the altar, he and Purī Mahārāja climbed up the ladder. At Prabhupāda’s request, everyone then threw in offerings of flowers and money, followed by handfuls of earth. (The site of this ceremony turned out not to be the actual site of ISKCON’s first building; more land was soon acquired, even further from the road, and it was there the first building was constructed.)

**Mālatī Dāsi:** After laying the cornerstone Prabhupāda asked me to speak in front of the *sannyāsīs.* I was a bit shocked because I knew only enough to know that I didn’t know much. I just spoke about Śrīla Prabhupāda, because I knew him. At first, I doubted whether what I said was enough of a śāstric presentation. Then I realized it was only proper that I glorify Prabhupāda’s achievements—to give his godbrothers a sense of why we were there.

Prabhupāda was cordial and friendly, gratified that his godbrothers were with him to dedicate the world headquarters of the International Society for Krishna consciousness. Some of them spoke in Bengali, expressing appreciations of Prabhupāda’s work in the West. A photograph was taken of Prabhupāda and his godbrothers, who were also gurus and heads of institutions. Prabhupāda later asked that the photo be made into a painting ten feet wide, and he eventually had it hung in his room when the guesthouse was built.

In the afternoon, Prabhupāda initiated six Bengali devotees and gave second initiation to Jananivāsa and to Yaduvara and his wife, Viśākhā, a team who would in the future start ISKCON Cinema. He was a moviemaker, and she a photographer and writer.

From his window, Prabhupāda was pleased to watch *prasāda* distribution go on. Hundreds of villagers squatted in long rows, eating *kicharī* from round leaf-plates. Prabhupāda told his disciples, “Always distribute *prasāda.* Continue this forever”—a theme repeated in his purports.

*Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* ([5.19.24](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-5/19.24), purport): The Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement has established its center in Māyāpur, the birthsite of Lord Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu, to give men the great opportunity to go there and perform a constant festival of *saṅkīrtana-yajña*. . . and to distribute *prasāda* to millions of hungry people hankering for spiritual emancipation.

## Prabhupāda Pacifies His Godbrothers

Soon after Gaura Pūrṇimā, several of Prabhupāda’s godbrothers returned. Just as Prabhupāda had predicted, they objected to the wording of the sign.

**Jananivāsa Dāsa:** Five or six *sannyāsīs* entered Śrīla Prabhupāda’s *bhajan-kuṭīr.* They demanded of Prabhupāda: “Why are you using Prabhupāda’s name? This is our Guru Mahārāja’s name. You have no right to use it.” Prabhupāda replied simply and honestly: “What can I do? My disciples wanted to call me Prabhupāda. It is not wrong for disciples to do that.” A discussion followed in the presence of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s disciples, who became disturbed seeing Prabhupāda verbally attacked. They had never seen anyone speak to Prabhupāda like that. Prabhupāda sent them out, but everyone continued listening from outside, looking through the cracks of the bamboo walls.

Eventually Prabhupāda called for his secretary and had him bring in Prabhupāda’s stationery. “See! I am not using Prabhupāda,” he said, showing them his letterhead, which read *Tridaṇḍi Gosvāmī A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami.* After that his godbrothers were somewhat pacified. Actually, Prabhupāda had not taken the name himself. Prabhupāda’s disciples had given him the name in America, when he had advised them that the spiritual master is usually addressed by names like Gurudeva or Prabhupāda.

**Tatpar Swami:** When the Gauḍīya *sannyāsīs* came back, they also questioned Prabhupāda: “Why are you giving *sannyāsa* and brahminical initiation to Westerners?” Prabhupāda asked them to take *prasāda,* but they refused. “At least take some water,” Prabhupāda said, but they refused even water. After they left, Prabhupāda told us that they were envious and were simply coming to quarrel.

After the festival, Tatpar, Guru-kṛpā, and a few others stayed behind to maintain the land and the deities. Jananivāsa had been staying near the deities, watching the *pūjā* and thinking, “What a wonderful way to become Kṛṣṇa conscious—directly serving the Lord!” Because the former *pūjārī* was leaving, Jananivāsa took over his service. However, being the only *pūjārī,* he had to do everything.

After the festival hardly anyone visited. Only Prabhupāda’s *kuṭīr* was visible, and it wasn’t apparent that deities were there. But a few people passed the land on their way to work, and one day Jananivāsa saw people approach the *kuṭīr* for *darśana.* He ran to the *kuṭīr* and hoped to give the visitors a *Back to Godhead* magazine.

As he reached the door, Prabhupāda came out, saying, “Someone is coming. Somebody should be here to receive them. Why is no one here? Give them *caraṇāmṛta.”* Prabhupāda then said that Jananivāsa needed some help because he could not do everything by himself.

In early March Prabhupāda left Māyāpur for Vṛndāvana. There, on March 13, he held the cornerstone-laying ceremony for the Krishna-Balaram temple. Then he visited Bombay for the second *paṇḍāl* program there.

## Discarding College Paraphernalia

Around the time Śrīla Prabhupāda left Māyāpur, I was wrestling with my conscience. In March 1972 I couldn’t bear my college exams. Ever since I’d met the Hare Kṛṣṇa devotees I was constantly thinking about them, and wanted to join them, but family obligations stopped me. So I had to endure the boredom of exams.

One day as I carried my books, I longed to be far away from everyone and everything I knew. I took a long walk that lasted hours and hours. I contemplated how I’d gotten into this unwanted situation, when all I wished to do was chant and dance with devotees. I did not want to sit for exams. I was doing it to satisfy my family. The further I walked, the clearer it became that I could no longer pretend. Although I’d been the top student in my primary and secondary schools, once I entered college something changed. I was putting on a good show, but my heart wasn’t in it. Spiritual influences were acting on me. My desire to serve others was rapidly growing. This thought had inspired me since childhood.

I felt disgusted carrying all sorts of books and notebooks and pens and papers. As I walked along, I took the items one by one and threw them away with all my strength— as far as I could. I threw away everything: a chemistry book, assignment papers, everything—until I was left with only empty hands and empty pockets. A great sense of relief and victory washed over me. Why hadn’t I done this earlier? I’d been waiting years for this: to be free from all shackles—free like a bird to soar to new heights. Now I’d finally found the strength. It was a long, satisfying walk; I headed home, excited.

My only goal now was to find the Hare Kṛṣṇa devotees. I had not seen them since April of the year before. I wondered whether I’d ever see them again. How could I find them? To my surprise and relief, two weeks later I saw an advertisement in a newspaper for a Hare Kṛṣṇa festival at ISKCON’s new property in Juhu, Bombay. It was scheduled for March 17. At that festival, the second *paṇdāl* program in Bombay, I saw Śrīla Prabhupāda again. After the festival I joined the devotees for good.

## Chapter 2. Kārttika in Vṛndāvana, 1972

Prabhupāda had left India on March 31 for six months, to preach in Australia, America and Europe. In August he wrote to Tejiyas Dāsa and Gurudāsa Dāsa: “My plan is to come to Vṛndāvana for Kārttika, and I want to stay in the Rādhā-Dāmodara temple until nearly the end of November. I will lecture daily in the courtyard, especially for the benefit of the devotees. So you can make the arrangements.” Śrila Prabhupāda flew back into India from Manila. Kārttika began on October 15.

This was the first and only Kārttika festival he observed in Vṛndāvana with his disciples. Of course, when he lived there in the early sixties he was present during Kārttika. Although it was an internal program to strengthen his disciples, and he was teaching them how to observe Kārttika, this year he did not sing the *“Dāmodarāṣṭaka”* and offer ghee lamps every day. But the festival included his classes on *The Nectar of Devotion, harināma* processions to ISKCON’s new property in Ramaṇa-reti, a celebration of Govardhana-pūjā, and of course his blissful association in the Rādhā-Dāmodara temple during the month of Dāmodara. He’d already described the importance of Kārttika in *The Nectar of Devotion:*

One of the most important ceremonial functions is called Ūrja-vrata. Ūrja-vrata is observed in the month of Kārttika; especially in Vṛndāvana, there is a specific program for temple worship of the Lord in His Dāmodara form. “Dāmodara” refers to Kṛṣṇa’s being bound with rope by His mother, Yaśodā. It is said that just as Lord Dāmodara is very dear to His devotees, so the month known as Dāmodara or Kārttika is also dear to them. . . . Even persons lacking seriousness who execute devotional service according to the regulative principles during the month of Kārttika, and within the jurisdiction of Mathurā in India, are very easily awarded the Lord’s personal service.

One devotee asked Śrila Prabhupāda a question about this: “This is the beginning of Ūrja-vrata. Can you describe what that is and how it should be observed?”

Prabhupāda replied, “Ūrja-vrata—you chant Hare Kṛṣṇa twenty-four hours a day for one month. That’s all.”

The devotees laughed. Gurudāsa Dāsa chanted: “Hare Kṛṣṇa, Hare Kṛṣṇa.”

Prabhupāda continued, “Yes. Don’t sleep. Don’t eat. This is Ūrja­-vrata. Can you execute it?”

The devotee replied, “I don’t know.”

This provoked more laughter. Yet according to the *Gautamīya-tantra,* Kārttika is particularly meant for *mantra-siddhi*—perfection in chanting the Hare Kṛṣṇa *mahā-mantra.*

This was not a public program or a festival attended by devotees from temples around the world. Fifty Western devotees came from the centers in Bombay, Calcutta, and Delhi. They were the same devotees who’d traveled with the World *Saṅkīrtana* Party and then lived in Indian temples while Prabhupāda toured the world. Naturally some Indian devotees attended, I being one of the fortunate few.

## The Reception to Honor Prabhupāda

Śrila Prabhupāda, accompanied by his servant, Śrutakīrti, was greeted by Dr. O.B.L. Kapoor, who held a reception in his honor. Prabhupāda and Dr. Kapoor (Ādi-Keśava Dāsa) were godbrothers and had known each other for about forty years. Dr. Kapoor gave the welcome address:

After his long tour of the world, during which he presented the message of Śrī Kṛṣṇa Caitanya Mahāprabhu to different nationalities, Śrila Prabhupāda is returning to his homeland. This is particularly significant because he has come back with a host of his Western disciples. This signifies to me the meeting of East and West. It signifies the victory of spiritualism over materialism. For the first time in history, the message of Vaiṣṇavism, the message of Mahāprabhu and pure devotion, has been carried to the West—and carried so successfully. I am sure that historians will have a lot to say about Prabhupāda and his movement and that they will even be envious of us for living at the time when this movement began.

I have a secret realization that at this moment, while we are welcoming Prabhupāda, Śrī Rūpa Gosvāmī, Śrī Jiva Gosvāmī, and Śrī Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja—whose *samādhis* signify their eternal presence in the sacred precincts of this temple—are joining us in welcoming Prabhupāda and are showering their choicest blessings upon him. I can hear them saying, “Long live Prabhupāda! Long live Prabhupāda!” I am sure that as a result of their presence, Prabhupāda will live for many more years, broadcasting the message of Caitanya Mahāprabhu all over the world and inspiring us.

Prabhupāda responded with his own words of praise and a little history:

Dr. Kapoor is a Vaiṣṇava, so his association is a great blessing for us. But this reception is actually not my reception. It is the reception of my foreign students. Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu wanted that His message be broadcast all over the world, in every village and every town. I came in contact with Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Gosvāmī Mahārāja in 1922, and he immediately asked me: “Why don’t you go to the foreign countries and preach Caitanya Mahāprabhu’s blessings?” I was a little surprised. Perhaps if I had joined in 1922, by the blessings of Guru Mahārāja I could have done more preaching. Anyway, better late than never. After my retirement, at the fag end of my life, I left Vṛndāvana in 1965, at the age of seventy.

For one year I had no place to live. I had taken some of my books, three parts of the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* printed here. With great difficulty I was pushing on. New York is a very expensive city, a great city, and a great forest *\[laughter\].* Then I began chanting in Tompkins Square, and I think that on the first day this boy, Acyutānanda Mahārāja now, and another boy, Brahmānanda Mahārāja, danced. That was my first encouragement. And after chanting in the park, many young men and girls used to come to my apartment and meeting place. In this way I started, first in New York, then in San Francisco, then Montreal and Boston. Now we have branches all over the world, and we are trying to purchase the Berkshire palace in England. That was occupied by the Duke of Windsor. The price is $500,000.

Dr. Kapoor interrupted, seeking clarification-$500,000?

“Yes,” Prabhupāda said, “five *crores* according to the Indian rate. I have no money \[*laughter*\]*,* but one nice boy, whose name is known all over the world —George Harrison —promised to give me a loan. These young Westerners are fully cooperative.”

Prabhupāda concluded by calling for *prasāda* to be distributed. Then he went to his quarters.

## Prabhupāda’s Quarters

Yamunā went to the Rādhā-Dāmodara temple to prepare Prabhupāda’s rooms for his visit.

**Yamunā Dāsī:** Prabhupāda’s rooms were a mess. The paint was chipping and peeling off, and his seat looked a hundred years old. It had actually been eaten away by worms. I repainted the rooms saffron and white and painted the *mahā-mantra* in Sanskrit along the tops of the walls. We got Prabhupāda a new bed. There was hardly any space for furniture. Only a cot and a small table fit in his room. During the day the room served as an office and was used for *darśana;* at night it was his bedroom. A large fan hung above the desk. You had to crouch to walk through the door; inside, there was hardly room to walk.

He also had his kitchen, with a window looking out on Rūpa Gosvāmī’s *samādhi* and *bhajan-kuṭīr.* The kitchen had just the basics: a burner, a tap, and a few pots and pans. But Prabhupāda was completely at home.

At the back of Prabhupāda’s room, a small door opens onto a tiny passageway, and on the right there is a bathroom and a staircase leading to the roof. There are two small rooms on the roof and a large deck. Gurudāsa and Yamunā occupied one upstairs room, and Acyutānanda Swami, Śrutakīrti, Śyāmasundara, Harikeśa, Siddhasvarūpa, Tuṣṭa-kṛṣṇa, and Hayagriva used the other room and slept on the deck.

Gaṅgāmayī Dāsī, one of the first British devotees to go to India, said Śrila Prabhupāda showed her a method to clean the floor of his room. He told her to dip the rag in the bucket and squeeze it lightly to mop the floor, then to again dip the rag in the bucket and this time squeeze it tightly to wipe up water left on the floor. He crouched down to show her how to do it and said, “You do like this.”

The following day he asked her, “Did you do like that?”

Gaṅgāmayī happily replied, “Yes, Śrila Prabhupāda,” having cleaned exactly as he instructed her.

Living unpretentiously, Prabhupāda shared his quarters and bathroom with his disciples. Sometimes he cooked for himself. He’d sit on a small wooden seat, a couple of inches off the ground, with ingredients, spices, and utensils placed around him and prepare a meal without moving from his seat. He lived simply and set an example of humility and renunciation. Prabhupāda returned to Vṛndāvana with wealth at his disposal, but he was detached. Indians who succeeded in the West usually maintained their new, higher standard of living when they returned to India. With his wealth, however, Prabhupāda printed books, established temples, and traveled and preached. To Prabhupāda, the value of Vṛndāvana was a million times greater than that of London or New York. He liked this sacred, austere place.

## The Rādhā-Dāmodara Temple

There is an extraordinary number of large deities of historical importance on the altar of the Rādhā-Dāmodara temple. In the center are Jiva Gosvāmī’s deities: Dāmodara, with Rādhā to His left, and Lalitā to His right. Others are Jayadeva Gosvāmī’s Śrī Śrī Rādhā-Mādhava; Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja’s Śrī Śrī Rādhā–Vṛndāvana-candra; and Bhūgarbha Gosvāmī’s Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa deities. These deities all originally had Their own temples, but Their servants appropriated the temple properties and combined the *pūjās* at the Rādhā-Dāmodara temple.

Pilgrims to the temple can also take *darśana* of Śrīla Sanātana Gosvāmī’s Govardhana *śilā,* which bears Kṛṣṇa’s footprint. In Sanātana Gosvāmī’s old age, Kṛṣṇa appeared and offered him this *śilā.* The Lord asked Sanātana to simply circumambulate the *śilā* because Sanātana had grown too old to complete Govardhana *parikramā,* as was his daily habit. It is well known that by circumambulating this temple four times, one derives the benefit of Govardhana *parikramā.* No wonder pilgrims flock there.

The temple was constructed by Jiva Gosvāmī, and the six Gosvāmīs met there for *iṣṭagoṣṭhīs.* One side of the Rādhā-Dāmodara compound houses more than 108 samādhis*,* including those of Jiva Gosvāmī, Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja, and Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī (his *puśpa-samādhi).* On the other side of the compound stand Rūpa Gosvāmī’s *samādhi* and *bhajan-kuṭīr,* in their own courtyard. It was here that Rūpa Gosvāmī left this world. Jiva Gosvāmī, his nephew, looked after him in his last days.

The Rādhā-Dāmodara temple was home to Śrila Prabhupāda in the early 1960’s. In 1954, Prabhupāda left his home and family and settled in Jhansi. When things became unfavorable for his mission there, he moved to Mathurā. Prabhupāda eventually shifted to Vṛndāvana, and a little later he rented two rooms at the Rādhā-Dāmodara temple. From July 1962 until August 1965 Prabhupāda stayed there, envisioning the day when Rūpa Gosvāmī’s teachings would be spread worldwide. During these three important years, he completed his three-volume set of the First Canto of the *Bhāgavatam,* which he took to America. At the Rādhā-Dāmodara temple, Prabhupāda laid the foundation of his mission to execute the will and instructions of his spiritual predecessors. He once wrote: “Vṛndāvana is a charmingly beautiful place, and situated there in the grove known as Sevā-kuñja is the sacred temple of Rādhā-Dāmodara. I take the lotus feet of These deities as my only shelter, and I petition Them to be kind upon me and guide me to life’s ultimate goal.”

## Arrivals from Bombay

Traveling by train, about fifteen of us from Bombay arrived at Mathurā station early one morning. We rode to Vṛndāvana on three horse-drawn tongas, costing ten rupees each. In caravan style we passed Kṛṣṇa Janmasthān, the birthplace of Lord Kṛṣṇa in Mathurā, and the Birla Mandir, with its clusters of domes. The tonga drivers amused us along the way as they cleared the busy road by shouting, “Rādhe! Rādhe!” — a transcendental replacement for high-pitched horns. Occasionally a peacock flew low across our path.

As we entered Vṛndāvana, its skyline dotted with temple spires, we heard devotional songs blaring from numerous speakers. Swarms of *sādhus* in saffron dress walked to *prasāda* booths along the narrow roads. Finally, after almost an hour, our tongas stopped in front of the arch leading to the Rādhā-Dāmodara temple. We quickly unloaded and hastened along the small walkway to the temple.

What excited me as much as being in Vṛndāvana for the first time was that this was my first opportunity to be in Śrila Prabhupāda’s close association. During the two Hare Kṛṣṇa festivals in Bombay where I’d seen Prabhupāda, I’d been amid thousands of people. At the *paṇḍāls* I’d heard Prabhupāda’s *vāṇī,* or discourses, and this brought me to his *vapuḥ,* or personal association, for more instruction. I’d come to formalize our eternal relationship as guru and disciple. During the whole journey from Bombay I clutched a letter from Girirāja Prabhu, my temple president, recommending me for *hari-nāma* initiation. Because I was a new, unknown devotee, this letter was my ticket to meet Śrila Prabhupāda, and I couldn’t wait to hear what he would say.

A few devotees greeted us at the door and ushered us into the temple courtyard, where we offered obeisances to Śrī Śrī Rādhā-Dāmodara. I’d never seen so many exquisitely beautiful deities. Then we were brought to Śrila Prabhupāda’s quarters. We entered and saw Prabhupāda sitting on a modest *āsana,* a cushion propped up behind him.

We offered our respectful obeisances and took seats on the cool floor. Prabhupāda looked fresh, bright, and happy to receive us. It seemed as though he was waiting for his spiritual children to arrive. He made us feel comfortable by asking about our journey.

I sat up front, on Prabhupāda’s right side. He could see me clearly. After his initial inquiries about our journey and welfare, Prabhupāda asked about the progress of the Bombay temple. I thought that in Vṛndāvana Prabhupāda would talk about Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa, but he was more interested in talking about the Bombay project, which we were trying to forget. This helped me realize that although Bombay is a materialistic place, for Prabhupāda the Rādhā-Rāsabihārī temple was an extension of Vṛndāvana.

As Prabhupāda spoke, he would slowly turn his head from side to side, making eye contact with each of us. I noticed that when his glances fell on me, they’d stop for a moment. I wondered why I was getting this special attention. I was overwhelmed in Prabhupāda’s presence, so I was sure I wasn’t dozing off. Was my tilaka straight? Some problem with my *dhotī* or *kurtā*? I couldn’t understand.

Finally, Śrila Prabhupāda pointed his finger at my leg and in a strong tone said, “Stop it!”

This is the first direct instruction I received from Prabhupāda: *Stop it.* I was sitting cross-legged and had been shaking my thigh. In my younger days I had the habit of shaking one of my thighs whenever I listened intently. After Prabhupāda told me to stop it, I stopped it forever. It was appropriate that my first instruction was *Stop it.* Spiritual life is full of dos and don’ts, and my spiritual life started with “a don’t”. Later in the month of Kārttika he handed me beads and said: “Do this. Take this Hare Kṛṣṇa *mahā-mantra.* Chant and be happy.”

Before our *darśana* ended, Śrila Prabhupāda, knowing that the Kārttika festival would end with a large Govardhana-pūjā feast, announced that someone had to collect *bhoga*. He deputed Draviḍa Dāsa, a Western devotee serving in Bombay, to go immediately to Agra, seventy kilometers southeast of Vṛndāvana; I went along as an interpreter and helper. But that morning, before leaving Prabhupāda’s room, I handed him my recommendation for initiation, which he placed on his desk.

## The Mahārāja of Bharatpura’s Palace

During our *darśana,* Prabhupāda told us we would be staying in the palace of the Mahārāja of Bharatpura on the bank of the Yamunā at Keśī-ghāṭa, just a ten-minute walk from Rādhā-Dāmodara. About forty devotees stayed at the palace. I remember Kauśalyā Dāsī, Vaikuṇṭhanātha and his wife, Śāradīyā, and the single men–Pṛthu-putra, Dvija Hari, Premānanda, Yaśodānandana, Kulaśekhara, Paramahaṁsa, Guru-kṛpā, and Parivrājakācārya. There were a few new arrivals from abroad: Kṛṣṇadāsa, Śivānanda, and Parikṣit.

The palace was a beautiful eighteenth-century home that could be entered only through one wide gate. Within its grounds were rooms on the first and second floors that opened onto verandas facing an open-air courtyard, at the center of which stood a mango tree. From the rooms facing east we could look out over Keśī-ghāta and the Yamunā, and from the roof there was a panoramic view not only of the Yamunā, but also of Bilvavana, one of the five forests of Vṛndāvana on the eastern side of the Yamunā.

Inside the palace was a small temple that we used for *maṅgala-ārati.* The palace deity was cared for by a *pūjārī* who doubled as the palace watchman. Everyone considered it quite a treat to be at a Mogul palace overlooking the very spot where Lord Kṛṣṇa relaxed after killing the Keśī demon. Prabhupāda said that any demons of doubt would be killed by our living at Keśī-ghāta.

When the electricity went off, we had to grope our way around at night or use candles and flashlights. In the early-morning darkness some of us bathed at the well in the courtyard, and others, who didn’t mind swimming with big turtles, went to the Yamunā behind the palace. Nārāyaṇi Dāsī said, “We used to jump in the Yamunā before *maṅgala-ārati* even though we couldn’t see where we were jumping. But it was deep enough.”

There was a family mood among us. We had to do everything ourselves. We had no hired cooks. Mālatī, Nārāyaṇi, and others cooked at the palace, and Śrutakīrti, Yamunā, and Pisimā shared the cooking for Prabhupāda and his staff. We took care of one another and would go to the classes and *harināmas* together, then squeeze into Prabhupāda’s room for *darśanas.* During one class Prabhupāda noticed Vaikuṇṭhanātha looking pale. He’d caught jaundice. So right there during class Prabhupāda began describing how to cure jaundice by eating sugarcane. It was a family thing to do. We were an intimate group because we all loved Śrila Prabhupāda. He was in the center; we were a team helping one another serve him.

## Bringing Bhoga from Agra

Draviḍa Dāsa and I stayed in Agra about a week, approaching merchants for grain, ghee, *gur,* sugar, and dhal. This was the first time a Western *sādhu* had appealed to them, and they responded generously.

Agra is famous for the Taj Mahal, built between 1631 and 1653 by Shah Jahan as a tomb for his wife. Twenty thousand people worked on it, including experts from Italy and France. Semiprecious stones from all over the world are embedded in its marble walls. It ranks as one of the Seven Wonders of the World. I’d heard a lot about the “glories” of the Taj, and I’d developed a desire to see it.

In *Kṛṣṇa* book, Śrīla Prabhupāda says that the Taj Mahal is especially pleasing on the full-moon night of the *śarat* season, which is the eve of the month of Kārttika: “During the night of the full moon of the *śarat* season, many foreigners go to see the beautiful reflections of the moon on the tomb.” Our visit to Agra coincided with the full-moon night, and that day I read this statement to Draviḍa to convince him that Prabhupāda approved of foreigners visiting the monument. He reluctantly agreed to go, which provided me the excuse I needed to see the Taj.

When we arrived in the evening, we saw large crowds drinking in the beauty caused by the full moon’s reflection on the dome. I also tried to appreciate the vision, but failed miserably. Unlike everyone else, I kept wondering what was so wonderful about this world-wonder. No matter how I tried, I couldn’t increase my appreciation. I was happy to realize that I didn’t have the eyes to see it.

At the end of our stay in Agra we hired a Tempo, a medium-size truck, to transport all the *bhoga.* We happily rode back to Vṛndāvana, knowing that Prabhupāda would be pleased with our efforts.

## Vṛndāvana Initiations

In Vṛndāvana, Śrila Prabhupāda commemorated the Śarat Pūrṇimā by visiting Sevā-kuñja, where Kṛṣṇa danced with the *gopis.* That same day Śrila Prabhupāda awarded *sannyāsa* to Guru-kṛpā, Parivrājakācārya, and nineteen-year-old Paramahaṁsa. That initiation took place when I was in Agra. Another initiation was held not long after I got back. Early in the morning an arena for a fire *yajña* was set up in the courtyard next to Rūpa Gosvāmī’s *samādhi,* and I was initiated, along with Manmohan Dāsa from the congregation in Bombay, Śacimātā Dāsī from Calcutta, and a few others whose names I’ve forgotten. Śrila Prabhupāda presided over the ceremony, chanting all the mantras, as we offered grains into the fire.

Draviḍa Dāsa was taking *sannyāsa,* and he was asked to come forward first. Prabhupāda asked him to repeat the *sannyāsa-mantra* after him, then named him Pañca-Draviḍa Swami, and handed him a *daṇḍa.*

I sat in front of Prabhupāda, watching him chant on my beads. I have seen footage of this, but it lasts only a few seconds. I don’t recall someone filming. I was surprised to see I wore just a *kurtā,* not a *hari-nāma cādar.* During the ceremony I was thinking, “I am here in the courtyard of Rādhā-Dāmodara temple in the month of Kārttika, during the auspicious early morning hours, taking my beads from the hands of my beloved Śrila Prabhupāda.” I was getting a gift from another world, the gift of the holy name.

Prabhupāda asked me if I knew the regulative principles; I recited them for him. He handed me my beads and said, “Chant sixteen rounds daily. Your name is Lokanāth.” The devotees responded, *“Jaya*!*”*

Prabhupāda usually used the first letter of a person’s name as the first letter of one’s initiated name, but with me he kept the last part of my name intact (“nāth”) and changed the first part. I had been Raghunātha, but from then on I was Lokanāth, by his order.

Śacimātā was the next to be initiated. She is now the wife of Saṅkarṣaṇa Dāsa, a disciple and relative of Śrila Prabhupāda, who runs a hospital for minor operations in Svarup Ganj, in Navadvīpa.

## “The Nectar of Devotion” Classes

A few devotees would walk from the palace to the Rādhā-Dāmodara temple in the dark for *maṅgala-ārati* and stay there to chant *japa,* but most remained at the palace and later walked to the temple for the *Bhāgavatam* classes. After class we’d either return to the palace for *prasāda* or wait until Prabhupāda finished *prasāda* and then go into his room for *darśana.*

On some mornings Prabhupāda would walk back and forth on the roof chanting *japa,* and devotees would watch him from the *samādhi* area below. Sometimes we were allowed to sit with Prabhupāda in his room as he chanted. One morning Prabhupāda noticed Devaṛṣi, with his extra-large beads hung around his neck *sādhu-*style. He was sliding them around his neck as he fingered them. Prabhupāda became angry.

“Where is your beadbag? Why are you chanting without one?” he shouted.

Everyone stopped chanting to hear Devaṛṣi’s answer. He stammered an explanation, but Śrila Prabhupāda was not satisfied. Pointing toward the door, Prabhupāda demanded that Devaṛṣi leave and return with a beadbag. Prabhupāda upheld the standard of chanting with beads in a bag (or covered). The *Hari-bhakti-vilāsa* says that one’s beads must be *gupta,* hidden or covered; otherwise, the effects or results of the chanting are stolen by demons, ghosts, and enemies. There is also the practical aspect of keeping the beads clean.

Because Prabhupāda was speaking on *The Nectar of Devotion* for two hours every evening at five, in the afternoon the devotees would again walk to the temple. Sometimes they would have a rip roaring *kīrtana* outside his room, in the main courtyard, before class.

Some preparation in the side courtyard, prior to Prabhupāda’s visit, had been required for holding classes.

**Yamunā Dāsī:** The side courtyard was quite different from what it is now. There was a lot of rubble near the *samādhi.* The courtyard floor was only hard-packed earth and dust.

The whole temple looked dilapidated, as if it had been through a war. The front entrance was half broken. The walls surrounding the temple were crumbling, and broken bricks lay everywhere. Bushes were growing all over the place. We knew we had to get the courtyard ready for his lectures, so we did the work in the mornings, when it wasn’t too hot.

Śrutakīrti said, “Prabhupāda eagerly spoke about the *Nectar of Devotion* lectures weeks before going to Vṛndāvana. On the flight to India he said he wanted me to read, and that he would stop me to speak, but instead he got Panditji—Pradyumna—to read.”

Before beginning class, Prabhupāda chanted “*Jaya* Rādhā-Mādhava,” his head tilted in concentration. Facing away from the temple and wearing a sweater to protect himself from the chilly weather, he sat on a simple cushion behind a low desk on a raised platform covered by a cloth. Someone would garland him. The devotees sat in front of him, the ladies on his left—the side of Rūpa Gosvāmī’s *bhajan-kuṭīr,* and the men on his right, the *samādhi* side. With everyone responding to his lead, the courtyard echoed with praise of Kṛṣṇa. Sometimes as Prabhupāda sang, the devotees danced.

Prabhupāda then handed Pradyumna a copy of *The Nectar of Devotion* and told him to read. Looking scholarly, Pradyumna sat up, wearing his glasses halfway down his nose. Next to him sat Śrutakīrti, who recorded all the wonderful lectures. From these classes came the series of tapes on *The Nectar of Devotion.* What Rūpa Gosvāmī wrote, Śrila Prabhupāda shared with the devotees.

On October 16, sometime after Pradyumna started reading that evening, Prabhupāda interrupted, saying to the devotees, “Arrange a light here,” pointing to the bare socket hanging above his head. It was getting dark.

As the class continued, it was “business as usual” for the Vrajvāsīs circumambulating the temple. Some would stop to offer lamps at Rūpa Gosvāmī’s *samādhi.* Others would stop to stare at the Westerners. Birds chirped in the bushes behind Prabhupāda, and monkeys sometimes came and disturbed us with their monkey business. A few Vṛndāvana residents attended Prabhupāda’s classes.

The electricity went off.

“I think that you are straining to see,” Prabhupāda said to Pradyumna, who was reading in the twilight.

“Someone is bringing a candle, Prabhupāda,” Gurudāsa Dāsa said.

“Acyutānanda, you can start *kīrtana* until the light comes,” Prabhupāda instructed.

At the end of the class, an Indian gentleman questioned Śrila Prabhupāda about his married disciples.

**Śrila Prabhupāda:** Sometimes my godbrothers criticize that I am a *sannyāsī,* taking part in marriages, but still I have to execute my duty. There are many couples here. I got them married. Here are a couple: Gurudāsa Dāsa and his wife, Yamunā. And where is Mālatī? There is Mālatī and her husband, Śyāmasundara. And another couple, Jānaki and Mukunda, are not here. I sent all of them as the first missionaries to London, and for one year they struggled very hard. Then they called me to say that they’d started a temple. My Guru Mahārāja wanted to start a temple in London. He sent two *sannyāsīs,* but it was not possible. But these *gṛhasthas* started one. So we want to see that the mission is fulfilled. It doesn’t matter whether one is a *gṛhastha* or a *sannyāsī.* These couples have helped me. I know I’ve benefited by getting them married. They have done so much. In Europe and America boys and girls live like friends. I told them, “You cannot live as friends. You must get married.” Where is Śāradīyā? When she was young, fifteen years old, she frankly told me, “I want to marry Vaikuṇṭhanātha.” \[Laughter.\] So I told Vaikuṇṭhanātha that he is reserved for her: “As soon as she’s over sixteen, you have to marry her.”

The guest was awed by Prabhupāda’s way of arranging such things. “Oh, wonderful!” he responded. Prabhupāda enjoyed glorifying his disciples’ accomplishments. He was especially pleased when they preached vigorously and succeeded all over the world. Here he was, sitting in Vṛndāvana, and his mission was simultaneously continuing to spread.

Some evenings he shared with us and the guests the news of the successful preaching that his disciples were doing. He’d received a newspaper clipping about the preaching in Durban, South Africa, and held it up for us to see. It had a photo, and he read the headline: “Krishna Message for South Africa.” He also mentioned a letter he’d received from two *sannyāsīs* traveling in the United States.

Addressing his disciples, Prabhupāda said, “You are also members of our Society. Try to preach this message everywhere, and you will get strength, the more you preach.”

Śrila Prabhupāda had once lived at the Rādhā-Dāmodara temple and then gone out to preach; now he was encouraging us to do the same.

Devotees took turns fanning Śrila Prabhupāda with a *cāmara,* to whisk away the flies. I took many opportunities to fan him. What a wonderful experience it was being right next to Śrīla Rūpa Gosvāmī’s *samādhi,* performing devotional service to Śrila Prabhupāda! It made me feel proud and perfectly fixed in my constitutional position as a menial servant of the spiritual master. The service had a practical purpose, and it was quite a meditation. Sometimes the pain in my arms increased after fanning for a long time. The devotees were listening to class, so no one thought of relieving me.

**Śrutakīrti Dāsa:** I felt proud of performing such a service in front of my godbrothers. I twirled the fan just like a *pūjārī* performing *ārati.* I wasn’t rendering any service. I was just making a show. At one point Prabhupāda started to wave away the flies himself. Finally, he just gave me a stern look and said, “Get someone up here with some intelligence.” I finally realized what it means to be the size of one ten thousandth the part of the tip of a hair.

After the evening lectures, Prabhupāda would let the devotees sit in his quarters with him. Sometimes Prabhupāda would sit quietly and no one would speak, but we relished even silence in his presence. Near the end of the *darśanas,* Yamunā would serve cups of honey-flavored hot milk and bowls of spiced puffed rice. When the devotees finished their *prasāda,* they offered Prabhupāda obeisances. His reply would often be: *“Jaya*! All glories to the devotees!”

## Prize Pick Secures Temple Property

Since December 1971 Śrila Prabhupāda had insistently directed his Indian disciple, Kṣīrodakśāyī Dāsa to acquire a 4,800-square-yard plot of land, in Ramaṇa-reti from the prospective donors, Mr. and Mrs. Saraf. Other religious organizations were busy trying to persuade the Sarafs to donate the land to them, despite the Sarafs already having promised it to ISKCON. So during the week before Gaura Pūrṇimā in 1972, Kṣīrodakśāyī had met them to settle the matter.

After three days of indecisive negotiations, Mr. Saraf said, “Śrīmatī Rādhāraṇī is the queen and controller of Vraja. If She agrees, then we can give the land to you. We shall prepare two papers saying ‘yes’ and ‘no’ and place them at the lotus feet of our Śrī Śrī Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa deities. Then you can pick one paper. If you choose the paper that says ‘yes,’ we will give the land to you.”

Kṣīrodakśāyī said, “No. I cannot gamble like this.”

Mrs. Saraf said, “Think about it tonight and let us know tomorrow.”

In this difficult situation, Kṣīrodakśāyī stayed awake all night thinking.

The next morning after *maṇgala-ārati,* Mr. Saraf asked, “Do you agree to our proposal?”

Kṣīrodakśāyī hesitated to answer and began chanting the Hare Kṛṣṇa mantra and meditating on Śrila Prabhupāda. After ten minutes he agreed to the proposal; he chose the paper that said “yes.”

Mr. Saraf then declared, “We will donate this land to Bhaktivedanta Swami for a temple.”

Soon thereafter Prabhupāda came to Vṛndāvana; on March 12 he signed the documents. Then he immediately prepared to lay a cornerstone.

**Kṣīrodakśāyī Viṣṇu Mahārāja:** That night, until eleven, we discussed laying the cornerstone. Śrila Prabhupāda apparently did not sleep. Around one-thirty he woke me up and called me to his room. The electricity was off, and Prabhupāda was using his flashlight. He asked if I had made all the arrangements for the ceremony according to his instructions.

On the new property, at three the next afternoon, a local *brāhmaṇa* under the guidance of Prabhupāda started the ceremony by chanting Vedic mantras, and devotees chanted Hare Kṛṣṇa. A sixteen-foot-deep hole had been dug. Kṣīrodakśāyī went down the hole on a ladder and put the auspicious ingredients in place. In the end everyone threw in the dust of Vṛndāvana to fill the sanctified pit.

The area was undeveloped and considered unsafe. Local people and even disciples used to tell Prabhupāda that although he was planning to spend so much money, no one would go there, because it was so isolated. Undeterred, Prabhupāda would say that it was a good location. He had a vision. When land is donated, he said, it is auspicious. He asked his disciple, Surabhi Dāsa, a Dutch architect, to design the temple.

## Harināma to Ramaṇa-reti
