Before Śrila Prabhupāda had arrived in October, the devotees already living in Vṛndāvana—they were living at the construction site—were sometimes performing *harināma.* When they’d first gone chanting, a very mean bull was living under a tree on the *harināma* path that they took. He wouldn’t let anyone walk on his side of the road. The *harināma* procession infuriated the bull. He’d stomp the ground and charge. Devotees had to run for their lives. One uninitiated Indian devotee was almost gored. For seven mornings in a row, the devotees confronted the bull on *harināma,* and the bull would stomp, charge, and scatter them. Then on the seventh day the bull disappeared and never returned. The local residents said it was a miracle.

During Kārttika, now that the number of devotees in Vṛndāvana had increased, Prabhupāda requested us to perform *saṅkīrtana* every day from Rādhā-Dāmodara to Ramaṇa-reti. “Ramaṇa-reti” means the dust sanctified by the touch of Radhika-Ramaṇa. I remember the biting cold feeling of that dust on my bare feet. I wasn’t used to it. To a tenderfoot like me, the spiritual sands of Ramaṇa-reti were difficult to appreciate.

Through the chilly morning air Acyutānanda Swami led the singing: *“Jiv jāgo, jiv jāgo* ...” We started from the temple, walked through the town, reached the Madana-Mohana temple, and then followed the *parikramā* path backwards. We continued past Kāliya-ghāṭa and what is now the Bhaktivedānta Swami *gośālā,* and then zigzagged through small lanes, passing many large cement-block *āśramas* along the way, before we reached the ISKCON land.

When we reached the property we all offered obeisances in the sacred dust and chanted for some time before continuing the *harināma* back. This became our daily routine. On some mornings Śrila Prabhupāda accompanied us, taking his morning walk and providing inspiration at the same time. When Prabhupāda joined, it increased our enthusiasm and the number of devotees who attended.

Upon reaching Ramaṇa-reti, Prabhupāda would check the progress and instruct the devotee-builders. He would walk around the property, pointing his cane as he shared his vision with us: “This is going to be here; that is going to be there.”

Śyāmasundara once told Prabhupāda that the devotees working at Ramaṇa-reti were isolated and needed more of his association. Prabhupāda replied, “They should come every morning to my lectures. That much they must do; otherwise, working, working, working—how are they different from the *karmīs?”*

## Bhakti-rasa Is the Key to Happiness

Pradyumna was reading to the devotees in the courtyard: “Every service has some attractive feature that drives the servitor on and on. Everyone is perpetually engaged in some sort of service, and the impetus is the pleasure he derives from it. Driven by affection for his wife and children, a family man works day and night. A philanthropist works in the same way for love of the greater family, and a nationalist for the cause of his country and countrymen. That force which drives everyone is called *rasa,* a kind of mellow, or relationship, whose taste is very sweet.”

Prabhupāda stopped Pradyumna to comment: “A householder works day and night, but there is some pleasure in serving the wife and children. This is a crude example. Similarly, *bhakti* means service with some taste. The *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* says, *svādu svādu pade pade:* the more you render devotional service, the more you relish taste. It is practical. Just like Rūpa Gosvāmī, a minister in the government—materially, he was getting honor and money, but he resigned his post and joined Caitanya Mahāprabhu. So unless he tasted something better, how could he give up his government service? Go on.”

**Pradyumna:** *“Bhakti-rasa is* a mellow different from the ordinary *rasa* enjoyed by mundane workers.”

**Śrila Prabhupāda:** Yes. In the material world sometimes we become fed up with our activities. We stop doing them and become a renouncer: *The grapes are sour.* You know the story? A jackal entered a vineyard and jumped to get the grapes, but they were too high. When he failed, he said, “Oh, these grapes are sour anyway” *\[laughter\].* People are like that. They work hard, and when they cannot relish any permanent happiness, they give up worldly activities as *false—jagan mithyā.*

But they do not relish anything. They do not relish *brahma-sukha,* spiritual happiness. Therefore they resume material activities. They renounce, and then they again take up political or philanthropic activities. They see that people are suffering for want of education and food. If Māyāvādī *sannyāsīs* think that this world is false, then why are they agitated by the sufferings of the world? “It is false.” But they do this—because they have no spiritual engagement.

The Vaiṣṇava, the devotee, cannot accept that this world is false. No. How it can be false? Suppose you enter a beautiful garden—so many nice trees and flowers. The proprietor of the garden shows you. And if you say, “Oh, it is all *mithyā,* false,” then how depressed he will become: *I brought this friend to show him the beauty of this garden, and he says it is false.*

Similarly, we don’t want to depress Kṛṣṇa *\[laughter\].* That is not our business. Kṛṣṇa has created this nice world. The sun is rising just on time. It is setting in exact time. The moon is rising. The seasons are changing. And we are getting nice food, nice fruit, and nice flowers. So we are not depressing agents. We won’t discourage Kṛṣṇa. Why? Kṛṣṇa has created this nice world. How can I say that it is false? It is the by-product of Kṛṣṇa’s energy, and Kṛṣṇa’s energy cannot be false. It is eternal. Kṛṣṇa is eternal. Kṛṣṇa is truth. So the energy of Kṛṣṇa is also truth. . . .

And everyone is tasting; otherwise, he cannot work. Therefore Kṛṣṇa says, “Whatever activity you do, do it for Me.” Don’t take the result yourself. Give it to Kṛṣṇa. Then you’ll taste the transcendental mellow, *bhakti-rasa.* And then you’ll be happy.

In material dealings, our *rasas* are temporary. They’ll be finished as soon as this body is finished. I have some affectionate dealings with my son, but as soon as the son dies, or I die, the *rasa* is finished. But if you deal in the same way with Kṛṣṇa, who is the reservoir of all *rasas,* the *rasa* will continue. If you love Kṛṣṇa as a friend in this life, if you develop your Kṛṣṇa consciousness as a friend of Kṛṣṇa, then when you go to Kṛṣṇa, giving up this body, then you go there as Kṛṣṇa’s friend.

Exhibiting a variety of moods, Prabhupāda was sometimes grave—with eyes closed, he’d gesture with his index finger to emphasize his points—and other times he relaxed, smiling and laughing, exercising his sense of humor.

## Monkey Business

**Śrutakīrti** ***uvāca:*** Local residents offered fruits and flowers by placing them at Prabhupāda’s lotus feet. One evening, in the middle of a meeting, a monkey darted into the room and lunged for some bananas. Viśākhā quickly threw her quilt over the monkey and tugged at the bananas as he ran out past her. She managed to retrieve most of the bananas, but the monkey was quickly gone with a prize banana in his hand.

“Just see how intelligent this monkey is in the matter of eating,” Śrīla Prabhupāda said. “This shows that all living entities are intelligent in their own way. How long do you think it would have taken you to do that—to run in and out of here and get the banana? The monkey is intelligent enough to do it in a few seconds. Practically no one even saw him. He just took the bananas and ran. This is what it’s like in the material world. Everyone is expert in his own sphere. So we have to become expert devotees, not expert like the monkeys.”

Another time, Prabhupāda went into his room to dress after taking his bath, and I heard him shouting. I was still outside on the patio cleaning up. I raced into the room. I had no idea what was wrong, and although I ran in, out of habit I offered my obeisances. Prabhupāda was behind his desk. As I quickly sat up and looked toward him, he hurled his ball of *tilaka* over my head. It missed my head by inches. I was shocked and frightened.

“What’s the matter?” I blurted out.

“That monkey stole my shoe,” he said, pointing in my direction.

I turned around and saw it run out of the room with Prabhupāda’s sandal. Monkeys were always looking for a beadbag, a pair of glasses, or some other valuable item to steal and barter for eatables. The monkey vaulted to the roof of Śrila Prabhupāda’s sitting room and waited.

“Get some sweets and come outside,” Śrila Prabhupāda said.

I grabbed a few sweets from a glass jar above the bookcase and followed Śrila Prabhupāda, who took along his cane. The monkey was sitting on the edge of the roof with the sandal in his mouth, waiting for the negotiations to begin. The short roof was almost within reach from where we stood. Śrila Prabhupāda began jumping up and down, swinging his cane over his head to intimidate the monkey. The monkey seemed to enjoy the attention. He appeared unthreatened and unconcerned that Prabhupāda might get the slipper. The furry fiend began waving the shoe, taunting us. Next he was making faces at us. He was a crafty thief, who enjoyed his mischievous negotiations.

I cautiously held a sweet into the air, hoping the monkey would give me the slipper in exchange. True to his nature, he tried to cheat me. He attempted to snatch the sweet without relinquishing the slipper. Again I tried. I held up the sweet and motioned for the shoe. The monkey started to deliver the slipper with one hand and reached for the sweet with the other. Convinced I was successful, I let down my guard, prematurely proud of my anticipated success. I failed, and lost the sweet. To my chagrin, I lost three sweets without even getting close to retrieving the slipper. At this point the monkey began chewing on the heel of the slipper with gusto. He managed to tear some cloth from the heel and put teeth marks into it. Śrīla Prabhupāda was not amused.

“Forget it,” he said. “He has ruined the shoe.”

He went back into his room and prepared for lunch. I then looked up and saw the monkey drop the sandal at his feet and run away. I guess he somehow understood it was of no use to him if it was of no use to us.

Anyway, I figured I may as well get the slipper, so I called for Girish, Hayagriva’s son, and asked him to go up on the roof and get Prabhupāda’s slipper. Girish was about ten and eager to help. I watched from below. Just as he bent over to pick it up, a gang of monkeys appeared out of nowhere and scared the heck out of him. One monkey started swinging at the boy. Girish screamed my name. I looked around and saw a bamboo stick, which I threw to him. As soon as he grabbed the stick and started swinging it at the monkeys, they retreated. Girish grabbed the slipper and raced down the stairs, the monkeys hot on his heels. Girish gallantly delivered the slipper. I brought it back to Śrila Prabhupāda because he had worn these slippers for some time and seemed to be fond of them. He had others to choose from, but he favored these. I showed him the torn slipper.

“It doesn’t look so bad,” Prabhupāda said. “Get some glue and see if you can repair it.”

I took it to my room and mended it to the best of my ability. I hurried back to Prabhupāda’s room with the saved slipper, offered my obeisances; and showed him my handiwork. With a smile on his face and a nod, he said, “That’s all right. I can still use them.”

## Mixed Local Reactions

The local people had mixed reactions to our *harināmas* and to our presence in general. In lectures Prabhupāda would tell the Vrajvāsīs that they should accept us because we were trying to spread the glories of Kṛṣṇa and His *dhāma* worldwide. There was some appreciation from a lot of nice, pious people, but there was a lot of skepticism among those who were unconvinced that we were genuine. Many caste *goswamis* and *brāhmaṇas* adhered to the view that you had to be born Indian to be a *brāhmaṇa.* These people had reservations about embracing us with open arms.

Not only that, many more people were suspicious of Prabhupāda’s great success. This was the first time Europeans and Americans had come to Vṛndāvana in large numbers. Indians speculated that a great power must be behind it, and they thought that power was the CIA, the Central Intelligence Agency of the American government. Formerly, the British had sent traders and missionaries and got a hold of the government of India. People were suspicious for that reason.

During our *harināmas* in Loi Bazaar the local people threw carrots, tomatoes, and small rocks at us—luckily, no eggs, which people in Paris or London would have thrown? Maybe the children did it only to tease us, but we felt the adults’ resistance. They were registering a protest. Westerners made up more than ninety percent of Prabhupāda’s group, and the local people didn’t want them invading. Common taunts were: *You don’t belong here; go back! You’ve just adopted the guise of devotees*— *you’re undercover agents or hippies! You have no right to practice this religion!*

Unfortunately, several devotees were inconsiderate in their dealings, and this did not help us become accepted into the Vṛndāvana fold. A few devotees were fighting with ricksha drivers all the time. Incidents of devotees beating and punching people were becoming common. Hayagriva mentioned such an incident in his detailed memoir of the Kārttika festival, *Vrindavan Days:*

We had just returned by boat to the palace, and Śyāmasundara informed us that one eccentric London devotee, Kulaśekhara, got into a row in the marketplace. A boy was beating a cow with a stick, and Kulaśekhara descended on him with all the justified anger of the great Vedic monarch, Mahārāja Parikṣit. Kulaśekhara grabbed the boy’s stick and slapped him in the face.

The people who saw this were furious, and word spread that a Hare Kṛṣṇa devotee attacked an innocent child. The news reached Prabhupāda.

More bad news was brought to him by Viśwambhara Goswami, as described in Mahānidhi Swami’s short book about Śrila Prabhupāda at the Rādhā-Dāmodara temple.

“Prabhupāda, are you putting on a circus in Vṛndāvana?” Viśwambhara Goswami asked.

“Circus? I am not putting on any circus,” Prabhupāda said, not understanding the cryptic question.

“Yes, you are. You caught many lions and have thrown them in the Vṛndāvana market. Many lions are dancing there.”

“Lions dancing?” asked Prabhupāda. “Goswamiji, you are an advocate, a big scholar, and a leading person of Vṛndāvana, and you are my friend. Why are you saying this?”

“With my own eyes I have seen your disciples riding on a ricksha and snatching food from the shopkeepers and eating. The shopkeepers asked me to talk with you.”

“Who has done this?” Prabhupāda demanded of his disciples. He became very angry, like a lion-tamer. When the guilty parties were brought to his room, he told them it is shameful to snatch something. He said you can ask a shopkeeper to give you a sample if you have no money; a merchant in Vṛndāvana may give you anything you want.

Śrīla Prabhupāda was protective of his disciples despite the trouble they were causing him. He asked them not to go to Rādhā-kuṇḍa. He cautioned them about their choice of association and didn’t want them to mix freely. Prabhupāda explained that there were many *sahajiyās* who could capture them; if they associated too freely with others, they would become sidetracked or confused. He was teaching them to chant and actively serve, but others emphasized chanting within the mind and meditation.

## Services on the Roof

**Śrutakīrti Dāsa:** I massaged Śrīla Prabhupāda on the roof of his quarters at eleven in the morning. After his massage, he bathed in the same spot, using a brass bucket filled with water warmed by the sun. That usually worked, except when the monkeys decided to have a drink from it and then knocked the bucket over. His massage would go on for an hour, and most of the cooking went on during that time. He would take *prasāda* at one.

For those two hours of the day Śrutakīrti would be extremely busy, and the rest of the time he would just be waiting for Prabhupāda to ring his bell to summon him.

“In Vṛndāvana,” he said, “I chanted sixty-four rounds daily for the whole month. It was really my good fortune it happened that way.”

Gauracand Goswami, the caretaker of the Rādhā-Dāmodara temple, had a habit of playing his radio loud all day and into the night. The noise carried into Prabhupāda’s quarters and disturbed his meetings and translation work, so Prabhupāda decided to move into the upstairs room then occupied by Gurudāsa Dāsa and Yamunā.

Once they had vacated the room, there was an opportunity to render personal service to Prabhupāda by cleaning the room before he moved in. Gopal Krishna Sharma and I volunteered for the service. Gopal Krishna had been following Prabhupāda since seeing him at a Delhi *paṇḍāl* in 1971. We got brooms and buckets of water and enthusiastically swept and thoroughly washed the room on the roof for Śrila Prabhupāda’s pleasure.

Shortly after cleaning Śrila Prabhupāda’s room, Gopal Krishna received first initiation in that room. He worked in Delhi, so he was in Vṛndāvana for just a few days and was given a special appointment. I took him to see Śrila Prabhupāda at five-thirty in the morning. Prabhupāda chanted on his initiation beads in our presence, changed Gopal Krishna’s name to Kṛṣṇa Gopāla Dāsa, and then asked Gurudāsa Dāsa to explain the ten offenses. Kṛṣṇa Gopāla showered flowers on Prabhupāda’s feet and body and jubilantly returned downstairs.

A week or so after the ceremony Kṛṣṇa Gopāla, his wife, and their six-year-old son and four-year-old daughter visited Vṛndāvana. They went upstairs to see Prabhupāda and to pay their respects.

“Oh, see the small children!” Prabhupāda said. “They look so nice with *tilaka* and their hands in their beadbags. May I have your son?”

Kṛṣṇa Gopāla replied without hesitation, “Yes, Guru Mahārāja, you may take him.”

Prabhupāda then asked Kṛṣṇa Gopāla to ask his wife’s permission. Such a decision could not be made without consulting her.

She paused, unable to speak; then in a faltering voice she said, “Well, now he is small, but later on we can see.”

Tears formed in her eyes as she thought of separation from her only son. Seeing the mother’s sadness, Prabhupāda quickly backed down and told her to raise him in Kṛṣṇa consciousness at home. Prabhupāda often asked family men to give him a son so that he could train boys to become preachers. Prabhupāda had a habit of exposing people’s family attachments. It is natural that a mother may exhibit more attachment to her children than a father. After I joined the movement, I received a message saying, “Your news of joining the temple has shocked your mother. She desperately wants you to come home. Start immediately.”

In November 1977, Kṛṣṇa Gopāla’s wife—although not donating her son (now eleven)—at least got him initiated by Prabhupāda, and herself, too, before Prabhupāda left this world. She became Pṛthā Dāsī, and he Rūpānuga Dāsa.

## “Bengali Sweets—Essential to Life.”

During Kārttika I had another special opportunity to render service to Śrila Prabhupāda. Prabhupāda asked me to purchase *jalebīs* for him. In those days ISKCON devotees were just learning Indian cooking, and cooking *jalebīs* is the job of a postgraduate cook. So I went to Loi Bazaar and looked for a shop making *jalebīs.*

I watched the *jalebiwālā* pour the batter into a large wok and pull out the cooked, dripping, spiral-shaped *jalebīs.* I chose the freshest and hottest *jalebīs* and rushed back with the package for Śrila Prabhupāda.

Śrutakīrti recalled a related episode. Once, Śrila Prabhupāda rode past devotees lined up at a sweet shop in Loi Bazaar. He stopped the car and opened the window. The devotees cringed, but Śrila Prabhupāda’s reaction surprised them. He leaned out and said, “Ah, Bengali sweets— essential to life!”

## Govardhana-pūjā Incident

On Govardhana-pūjā day, just before the *pūjā* time, a group of angry ricksha *wālās,* students, and local people blocked our wide palace entrance. They were protesting Kulaśekhara’s slapping a local boy. The devotees managed to close the two solid doors when the incident began. The protestors tried to push the doors open, made threats, and threw rocks at us through the fence. Both sides yelled, and our men were scared, running here and there, making strategies.

**Mālatī Dāsī:** To see so much anger was surprising. I’d read terrifying accounts of massacres by the Indians and British. I had a white body and a baby. I ran upstairs and put large *tilaka* marks on my body and my daughter’s body. Then I went back down, thinking we might die.

Acyutānanda Swami and the ladies who were cooking had the idea to open the doors and give rice, *capātīs,* and *sabji* to the angry people. We brought the pots into the courtyard and stood behind them. When the doors were opened, the angry crowd poured in. From their bags they took their plates and bowls and charged the pots, frightening the devotees, who for an instant mistook the eating utensils for weapons. The devotees began serving *prasāda* as if their lives depended on it, and the *prasāda* pacified everyone. Later, Prabhupāda called for the police inspector, who was friendly and promised to protect us.

On the auspicious day of Govardhana-pūjā (and *go-pūjā),* we celebrated Kṛṣṇa’s pastime of humbling lndra’s pride. Cows with painted horns, wearing garlands, received *pūjā.* Temples in Vṛndāvana were decorated with long strips of mango leaves. Ornamental flower garlands hung in the temple courtyards, and *prasāda* was distributed profusely.

Prabhupāda had us observe the Annakūṭa ceremony, the festival of grains. He wanted a mountain of *prasāda* distributed to the residents of Vṛndāvana, with the aim of improving our public image. From the foodstuffs we collected in Agra, we made a big Govardhana hill of rice and *halavā,* decorated with *purīs, sabjīs, pakorās, samosās, kachoris,* and many milk sweets. We had a *kīrtana* and circumambulated the hill. Hundreds and hundreds of Vṛndāvana’s residents came and enjoyed the feast. Śrila Prabhupāda was very pleased.

## How Wonderful Vṛndāvana Is

During Kārttika Śrila Prabhupāda discussed sublime topics daily. His long classes on *The Nectar of* Devotion—the main feature of the festival—benefited us as he hoped, by deepening our appreciation of devotional service and our understanding of the six *Gosvāmīs*. Actually, Prabhupāda benefited anyone who will read or hear what he said there. I’d have liked to quote excerpt after excerpt from his classes, but that would have made this chapter too long.

Prabhupāda had come to Vṛndāvana not only to lecture, but also to look after the temple construction. When Gurudāsa Dāsa and Yamunā agreed to remain in Vṛndāvana to help Prabhupāda build the temple, Prabhupāda welcomed them with gratitude. ISKCON needed a nice place in Vṛndāvana for the devotees and guests who would come in the future. ISKCON was a worldwide movement, and Prabhupāda did not forget the rest of the world while staying in Vṛndāvana. He wrote to the devotees in New York from Vṛndāvana on October 20, 1972:

I have appreciated very much the enclosed booklet, *Krishna, the Reservoir of Pleasure.* Although none of you have yet been here in Vrindavan, still, Kṛṣṇa has given you the spiritual vision, and you are seeing Vrindavan actually—the paintings are so nice. Just see how the cows are looking at Kṛṣṇa. Now you distribute these books by the millions all over the world. Then everyone will see how wonderful Vṛndāvana is. That is the best preaching work.

Kārttika ended on November 16. The devotees had a send-off for Prabhupāda, who left for New Delhi on the fifteenth. After a brief stop at the ISKCON temple, he took a flight to Hyderabad for a *paṇḍāl* program.

## Chapter 3. The 1973 Māyāpur Festival

Encouraged by the success of the first Gaura Pūrṇimā festival, Śrīla Prabhupāda invited all his disciples to attend the second one. For many who planned to go, it was their first pilgrimage to India. They started collecting funds for a ticket months ahead. Jayādvaita Swami remembers making an arrangement to keep a portion of his *saṅkīrtana* collection:

The Brooklyn temple authorities didn’t mind and told me that if I didn’t have enough money by the end of the year, they would supply the rest. I kept the money in an envelope, taped to the underside of a drawer in my filing cabinet. I went every year after that.

For two weeks before the festival, Prabhupāda stayed in Calcutta. There, on March 6, he wrote in a letter: “I shall go to Māyāpur for the festival celebration of Lord Caitanya’s Appearance day. We are expecting many thousands to visit, and our nice building is almost complete. My plans are to go to Māyāpur as soon as my quarters are finished.” He would stay in Māyāpur for three days, arriving the morning of March 16 and staying through to the eighteenth, Gaura Pūrṇimā.

A grand welcome was planned for Prabhupāda. About two hundred devotees, mostly Americans and Europeans, would meet him at the Yoga-pīṭha and offer him a new car. He would ride in it the remaining distance—about a kilometer—to the ISKCON property.

The devotees walked in a *kīrtana* procession to the Yoga-pīṭha and waited in great anticipation. When Prabhupāda arrived by car, the devotees’ excitement exploded. They were clapping, singing, jumping, and shouting *“Jaya* Śrīla Prabhupāda!” The atmosphere became surcharged with vibrant *kīrtana* and deafening *mṛdaṅgas* and *kartālas.* But when Prabhupāda alighted from the car, a momentary silence prevailed as everyone fell flat on the ground and murmured the *Praṇāma-mantras* to their spiritual master.

After being presented with garlands, Prabhupāda entered the temple at the Yoga-pīṭha for *darśana,* followed by a rush of disciples eagerly surrounding him with roaring *kīrtana.* This was the first time Śrīla Prabhupāda had seen so many of his Western devotees in Māyāpur. As he led them into the temple, it seemed as though he were making an offering of his disciples to the lotus feet of Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu.

After taking *darśana* of the deities, Prabhupāda was led toward his new Ambassador, decorated with nets made of marigolds and mango leaves. He accepted the offering by stepping in. Then the *kīrtana* party crowded round his car and slowly proceeded along Bhaktisiddhānta Road to the ISKCON property. The transcendental procession—led in melodious *kīrtana* by Acyutānanda Swami—enthusiastically made its way along the narrow road, singing and dancing all the way.

Jananivāsa was at the temple because he’d stayed behind to get things ready. As he looked out toward the road, he saw the parade coming and thought: *It really looks out of this world. Generally this place is completely deserted, but now people are everywhere, dancing and singing.*

Śrīla Prabhupāda entered the Lotus Building, greeted Śrī Śrī Rādhā-Mādhava and welcomed his disciples. Then he retired to his quarters on the second floor. Later, word got around that Prabhupāda’s godbrothers were criticizing him for not stopping at his Guru Mahārāja’s *samādhi,* at Caitanya Maṭha, about half a kilometer before the Yoga-pīṭha. When informed of this, Prabhupāda countered their objection by saying that he never thinks of his Guru Mahārāja as only in *samādhi;* he always feels that his Guru Mahārāja is next to him.

## Blueprint Unfolds

After purchasing land in Māyāpur, in 1971, Prabhupāda began unfolding his transcendental blueprint. His first goal was to build a guesthouse for the many hundreds of disciples who would visit Māyāpur. But he required more land. Thus throughout 1972, Prabhupāda had instructed Tamal Krishna Mahārāja, Jayapatākā Mahārāja, and Bhavānanda Mahārāja to buy more.

Land was acquired, and the construction of the guesthouse was begun. The architectural design, by Surabhi Dāsa, included a temporary temple on the ground floor. Prabhupāda pushed the three leaders—all Americans—to complete the guesthouse, and they hired one hundred men. In August 1972, Prabhupāda wrote to Tamal Krishna Goswami, “I am very, very much pleased to hear about the progress in Māyāpur, and this working with one hundred men both day and night is the American style.”

**Tamal Krishna Goswami:** The idyllic life of the sage surrounded by a few disciples was not Prabhupāda’s idea of the Māyāpur project. “Begin construction!” he ordered. But what did we know of construction? Then he described how to dig the foundation, how to make a plinth, and erect what turned out to be the most beautiful guesthouse in Nadia District.

I remember our pride in sending photographs to London, showing Prabhupāda that we had purchased steel, sand, and stone chips. Prabhupāda wrote in response, “You have sent pictures of building materials, but I want pictures of a building. What is the value of showing me these materials? Where is the building?” We leaders then felt like the foolish disciples we actually were. Prabhupāda’s words pushed us on, and we began the construction in earnest.

The Māyāpur property was no longer a simple, serene setting consisting of a *bhajan-kuṭīr* standing peacefully amid green fields. Now it was a construction site amid the same fields. Between the *kuṭīr* and the construction site was a field used for growing rice and wheat in different seasons. (The fountain and spacious park there now were built after 1977.) The basic structure of the guesthouse stood four stories tall. It was covered by a network of bamboo scaffolding held together with rope. Building materials lay all over the ground in an orderly mess—piles of bricks, mounds of sand and rock, and stacks of marble slabs. Workers sat near the rows of marble, chipping away with their tools to square the slabs.

At the time of the festival, the guesthouse was less than half complete. The first two floors were built, but there were no railings yet on the edges of the verandas, and not all the rooms had doors and windows. Prabhupāda wanted the work to continue during the festival. Devotees suggested that the noise would disturb him, but Śrīla Prabhupāda didn’t want the work stopped by any means. He said that the devotees shouldn’t think it was a mundane sound.

The ground floor (where the Caitanya-līlā dioramas are today) was the temple room for the presiding deities, Srī Srī Rādhā-Mādhava, and a deity of Mahāprabhu, who all stood on a silver, lotus-flower-shaped *siṁhāsana.* Their altar faced the Gaṅgā. The devotees were pleased to finally offer the deities a suitable room. But many people and some members of the Gauḍīya Maṭha objected to having the deities on the ground floor, because it meant that people would be living over Them.

**Tatpar Swami:** They asked us: “Why are you staying upstairs and the deities downstairs? You are making an offense.” We told them that this was a temporary arrangement and that above the altar on each floor we had built a demarcation so that we didn’t walk or sleep over the altar. Still they criticized. Prabhupāda instructed us to counter this criticism by saying, “You have not yet seen the temple. This is not the final temple.

On the first floor, adjoining the staircase, was a room where five or six resident *brahmacārīs* stayed. That was all that was complete on the first floor. Visiting devotees could sleep on the twelve-foot-wide verandas or in unfinished rooms and wherever else space was available throughout the building. Śrīla Prabhupāda himself had planned the wide verandas, which in India is a sign of opulence. Prabhupāda said they could be used for sleeping and *japa* walks, and that they would allow breezes to flow through the rooms because they were on both sides of the building.

On the second floor, two rooms for Prabhupāda were ready by the time he arrived. One was a sitting room, where he received guests during the day and translated books at night. The adjoining room was his bedroom. There he would also honor *prasāda,* so a small marble table was set against the wall beside his bed.

One room next to Śrīla Prabhupāda’s was made ready for Jayapatākā Swami and Bhavānanda Goswami, the directors of the Māyāpur project. The third floor was just a maze of wooden beams and steel rods, but the devotees were willing to stay even there. They preferred this to experimenting with staying in a hotel in Navadvīpa.

Prabhupāda asked Śatadhanya Dāsa—because he’d preached in Japan—which country he liked better, India or Japan.

“India, Śrīla Prabhupāda,” Śatadhanya replied. “I like it, and would like to stay here my whole life.”

“Yes, India is the perfect place for spiritual advancement, “Prabhupāda replied, smiling. “Life is simple, and one can live very easily while making spiritual progress.”

“But, Prabhupāda, there is one problem, and that is the extreme heat in the summer.”

“Yes, that is the only inebriety,” Prabhupāda agreed.

A *gośālā* was under development on our small property. The cowshed was situated between the *bhajan-kuṭīr* and the construction site, and guests and devotees would visit it. Local people would gather to watch a Westerner milk a cow with both hands, sitting and holding a bucket between the legs, after tying the cow’s legs so that she wouldn’t kick.

The temporary kitchen was in a primitive condition. The local devotees were cooking outside on kerosene burners. In pairs, they would carry sacks of rice into a storeroom or wash the dishes, with one devotee working the pump and the other washing. Expecting a large number of guests, the Māyāpur devotees had set up a festival kitchen: Enormous pots were placed in slight depressions in the ground, over holes for fires.

Devotees would eat close by, while the cooking was going on. Śrīla Prabhupāda once looked down from the balcony of the first floor and saw a combination of hot *puris* and date *gur* being served. He said, “Oh, very good combination!”

## The Art of Doll-making

Śrīla Prabhupāda never missed an opportunity to use the talents of his disciples in the service of the Lord. At the Henry Street temple in Brooklyn, New York, Prabhupāda saw a large statue of Lord Caitanya that Bharadrāj had made for a parade. He told Bharadrāj, “You should go to Bengal and learn how to make dolls. There the artists are expert.”

A day or two later, Bharadrāj was in Prabhupāda’s room when Prabhupāda started explaining that he wanted devotees to go to Māyāpur and learn how to make dolls for dioramas and displays. A young devotee volunteered, but Prabhupāda ignored him and turned to Bharadrāj: “No, you will go.” Russian-born Bharadrāj wanted to preach in Russia, but Prabhupāda wanted him to go to Māyāpur, so that’s where he went, along with Rukmiṇī, his wife.

Bharadrāj and Rukmiṇī arrived in India for the Gaura Pūrṇimā festival, and Bharadrāj went to see Prabhupāda, who started telling him about the ideas he had for doll displays. To have such displays was his Guru Mahārāja’s desire and his own desire, Prabhupāda said, so Bharadrāj had to be serious and understand its importance. Prabhupāda explained that dioramas leave deep impressions in the mind, and if a spiritual instruction is imparted in this way, it can be quite effective.

Prabhupāda asked Bharadrāj to find an artist to teach him, and to begin work as soon as he learned the art. He told Bharadrāj to learn by using the “make and break” technique: make a model, break it, and make it again. Prabhupāda concluded, “You have a lot of work. You had better get started.”

Bharadrāj left Prabhupāda’s room, but just outside he met Devānanda Mahārāja, his old friend from Montreal. They began talking. A few minutes later Satsvarūpa Mahārāja came out of Prabhupāda’s room, straight over to Bharadrāj, and said, “I have a message from Prabhupāda: *You have too much service to do. You don’t have* time *for casual conversations.”* Bharadrāj excused himself and hurried off to look for a doll-maker.

Looking back on those years, he said, “It was really an intense, forward-moving momentum that I felt. I suppose anyone experienced it who had that kind of direct instruction from Prabhupāda, or a direct service for him.”

Because Prabhupāda had moved into the Lotus Building, Bharadrāj and Rukmiṇī began to use his *bhajan-kuṭīr* as a doll studio. They learned how to make dolls by working with very difficult Bengali instructors. Often the instructors would not show up. It seemed they were uninterested in teaching foreigners.

Before Bharadrāj and Rukmiṇī had left for Māyāpur, they’d been painting pictures for the temple and for Prabhupāda’s books. Once, as they’d showed Prabhupāda their separate paintings, he commented that the husband’s work was better than the wife’s, which encouraged a transcendental competition. Many months later, when Prabhupāda was shown the dolls they were learning to make, he teased them again, but this time by saying that Rukmiṇī’s work excelled Bharadrāj’s.

Their effort to make dolls eventually proved successful. Bharadrāj and Rukmiṇī taught other artists, and within a few years they opened a computerized exhibition at the Los Angeles temple called the First American Theistic Exhibition (FATE), complete with music, narrations and lighting effects.

## Morning Walks a Highlight

In Māyāpur Śrīla Prabhupāda kept to his schedule of going on morning walks, across the fields and up and down Bhaktisiddhānta Road. In the holy *dhāma* during the quiet, cool mornings, his walks were especially peaceful. Farmers were already out plowing their fields with bulls, completing some work before the sun rose higher and made the mornings hot.

Prabhupāda’s walks were a highlight of the festival, but it was impractical for all the devotees to accompany him. It was announced after *maṅgala-ārati* that only the senior men and sannyāsīs could accompany Prabhupāda.

**Saṅkarṣaṇa Dāsa:** I was allowed to go because I was a family member. I was born into Prabhupāda’s family from his previous *āśrama* and was later initiated into his spiritual family. I was doubly blessed.

**Rukmiṇī** ***uvāca*****:** Dāmodara Mahārāja, Prabhupāda’s godbrother, owned a little property on the north side of the ISKCON land. He was unfriendly and often seemed to be reporting to others what was going on in the neighborhood. During one morning walk Prabhupāda and his disciples stopped in front of his property. Dāmodara Mahārāja came running, as if to ask, “What do you want?” and Prabhupāda answered his unasked question: “I am just looking at the *tulasī.”*

While Prabhupāda was out walking the next morning, I was cleaning his room with other ladies. On the floor next to his desk there was a cardboard box containing *rasagullās* and *gulābjāmuns,* but the box was soggy because the juice from the sweets had soaked through the cardboard. So I took the box down to the kitchen and told the cooks it was from Prabhupāda’s room, and to save the sweets for him.

Later I got a message that Prabhupāda wanted to see me. I went to his room, where he was meeting devotees and visitors. When I walked in, I saw his eyes sparkling, and I could tell he was in a happy mood. He was always fatherly to me because I was only sixteen when I joined.

“Come over here and sit down,” he said. “Where are my sweets?” he asked, pretending to be angry. I explained that I had taken the leaky box to the kitchen.

“Oh, you have not eaten any?”

“Oh, no, Śrīla Prabhupāda.”

“Go to the kitchen and bring them back. I want to see.”

So I brought the box and showed it to him. He counted the sweets as though he knew how many were there before I took them away, and he was laughing.

“OK, they are all there. You may go.”

## “Śrī Guru-Vandanā” Introduced

Near the end of 1972 the devotees in Calcutta started chanting *“Śrī Guru-vandanā” (Śrī-guru-caraṇa-padma* ...) at *guru-pūjā.* Most likely Acyutānanda Swami had learned this song during his stays at Gauḍīya Maṭha temples before ISKCON had established temples in India. Śrīla Prabhupāda approved the *Guru-vandanā* prayer as the standard song for *guru-pūjā,* and it was then introduced at the Māyāpur festival, where *guru -pūjā* was offered in Prabhupāda’s presence. For most devotees, this was their first time singing the prayer, and they struggled to follow both the melody and the words. Before this, the *guru-pūjā kīrtana* was simpler. After the *Govindam* prayers at the deity greeting, everyone used to chant a regular Hare Kṛṣṇa *kīrtana* while offering flowers.

## Darśanas with Prabhupāda and Śrīdhar Mahārāja

Prabhupāda gave classes in the morning and then returned to his rooms. He would take breakfast and then meet with leaders and do other duties. He held *darśanas* at five in the afternoon in his personal quarters. Most devotees saw him in the temple during the morning program and then again at the *darśanas.* They had no opportunity to go on his morning walks, and the classes were held in a formal setting. So the *darśanas* were their opportunity for close, informal association, as intimate as they could hope for. There was no control on who could attend. Although not many could fit in the *darśana* room, getting there early or squeezing in usually worked. The devotees were one big family, and there are never too many people around when there is love and affection.

Being in the close association of Prabhupāda for the first time in their lives made some devotees anxious, but for many, the *darśanas* were the perfection of their Māyāpur visit. One evening, devotees were discussing their regional problems and seeking approval for possible solutions from Śrīla Prabhupāda. As Prabhupāda dealt with the difficulties, Jananivāsa harbored a desire to hear from Prabhupāda how everyone in Vṛndāvana loves Kṛṣṇa.

**Jananivāsa Dāsa:** In the course of discussing the problems, Prabhupāda started describing how everyone loves Kṛṣṇa. It was as if Prabhupāda had read my mind. Immediately Kṛṣṇa became the center of attention, and everyone became focused-drawn into oneness. When Prabhupāda talked about Kṛṣṇa, at least temporarily devotees forgot their problems. He surcharged the atmosphere with unifying Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Initially the devotees were in anxiety, but in the presence of Kṛṣṇa in the form of a narration of His pastimes their passions were stilled.

Just before *sandhyā-ārati* Prabhupāda brought the *darśana* to a close, saying, “OK, it is *ārati* time. All of you should go.” The devotees left, but some leaders stayed back.

**Jananivāsa Dāsa:** I was back in Prabhupāda’s room after *sandhyā-ārati,* and Prabhupāda asked Bhavānanda Mahārāja: “Did you go to *sandhyā.-ārati?”*

“No,” he replied.

“Tamal Krishna Mahārāja?” asked Prabhupāda.

“Ah, personally, Śrīla Prabhupāda, today I did not go.”

Prabhupāda then asked the rest of the leaders in the room, and he reminded them: “You are leaders. You should be in the *kīrtana.* The devotees have all come for the festival. The *kīrtana* should go on very strong.”

Late in the afternoon on March 17, Śrī Śrīmad Śrīdhar Mahārāja visited Śrīla Prabhupāda. In his room, surrounded by disciples, Prabhupāda shared his *āsana* with him. They discussed various topics from their early days. Śrīla Prabhupāda recalled the first time he met his Guru Mahārāja in 1922, and the famous first instruction: “You are all educated young men, why don’t you preach Lord Caitanya Mahāprabhu’s message throughout the whole world?”

“I was always thinking,” Prabhupāda said, “from the very beginning, that I had met a real *sādhu. Lava mātra sadhu-saṅge sarva-siddhi haya:* by a moment’s association with a *sādhu,* one can achieve perfection. He impressed me so much.”

Prabhupāda then told of his first meeting with Tīrtha Mahārāja, which was the beginning of his close affiliation with the Gauḍīya Maṭha. Tīrtha Mahārāja visited Prabhupāda’s shop, the Prayāg Pharmacy, during the 1928 Kumbha-melā. He was soliciting help to establish a temple in Allahabad.

Shortly thereafter Śrīdhar Mahārāja became a close friend of Prabhupāda’s. He joined the Gauḍīya Maṭha in 1927 and was a *brahmacārī* at that time. He used to visit Prabhupāda’s home in Allahabad with *sannyāsīs* and hold programs. Years later, Prabhupāda had the opportunity to associate with him closely.

Prabhupāda said to his disciples, “Śrīdhar Mahārāja lived in my house for a few years, so naturally we had very intimate talks, and he was my good adviser. I took his advice, his instructions, very seriously, because from the very beginning I knew he was a pure Vaiṣṇava and devotee.”

Prabhupāda and Śrīdhar Mahārāja reviewed the history they both knew so well, how gradually a process began in which Kṛṣṇa took away Prabhupāda’s wealth and increased his spiritual association. Prabhupāda’s business had expanded when he became an agent for Bose’s laboratory and started a laboratory in Lucknow, but then it gradually declined as his spiritual activities increased. He’d eventually handed over his business to a creditor.

Prabhupāda proudly said, “Somehow or other, this intention for preaching the cult of Caitanya Mahāprabhu increased and the other side decreased. It decreased, decreased, decreased—almost it became nil—and then I left home in 1950.”

Śrīdhar Mahārāja reminded Prabhupāda that during those business years they’d canvassed together in Bombay, and that reminded Prabhupāda of Goswami Mahārāja, the third member of their team, who was so impressed with Prabhupāda that he suggested to Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvati that Prabhupāda—Abhay Bābū—take charge of the Bombay Gauḍīya Maṭha.

Prabhupāda then repeated his spiritual master’s response to the idea: “It is better he live separately from you people, and he will do the necessity in due course of time.” Prabhupāda recalled his thoughts at that time:

I could not understand what Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta meant by that. His blessings were always upon me. He was so kind. Whatever position I have got, it is by Guru’s mercy and the Vaiṣṇavas’ blessings; otherwise, I am insignificant. So I wish that Śrīdhar Mahārāja may bestow his blessings as he was doing always, and my Guru Mahārāja may help me so that I can give some service.... I thought: *Guru Mahārāja wanted, and these Gauḍīya Maṭha people did not do anything,* so *let me try in this old age.* The inspiration came, and I went. By his grace it has become a little successful, that’s all. I have no credit.

Soon it was time for *sandhyā-ārati,* and Śrīla Prabhupāda concluded the conversation.

## Parikramā to Yoga-pīṭha

We gathered into one group at the front gate for *parikramās.* At the head of the procession a devotee carried a large poster of Nitāi and Gaurāṅga dancing, Their arms raised high. A devotee next to him carried a garlanded painting of Śrīla Prabhupāda. Devotees held flags in the air and had large smiles of anticipation.

As we walked up Bhaktisiddhānta Road, gradually the tall palms and towering white spire of the Yoga-pīṭha came into view. Inside the temple, in the room on the right, we saw Lord Jagannātha and the Pañca-tattva. Caitanya Mahāprabhu sits on a throne, with Nityānanda on His right and Gadādhara Paṇḍita on His left. Advaita Ācārya is in front with folded hands, and Śrīvāsa holds an umbrella over Their heads. In the room on the left were Gaurāṅga and Rādhā-Mādhava. In the center room were Lord Caitanya and His wives, Śrīmatī Viṣṇupriyā Devī and Śrīmatī Lakṣmīpriyā, along with an eight-inch-high deity of Adhokṣaja Viṣṇu that Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī found during the excavation for the temple, in 1934.

A few steps to the right of the main temple is a small house where baby Nimāi rests in the lap of his mother, Śacīdevī, next to Jagannātha Misra, His father. The house stands beside a neem tree said to be a cutting of the original tree under which the Lord appeared. We circumambulated the tree and house, singing “*Jaya* Śacīnandana!”

Behind the house is a small temple for Kṣetrapāla Śiva, who protects the *dhāma.* Following tradition, we asked for his protection while on *parikramā* and murmured *vaiṣṇavānāṁ yathā śambhuḥ:* “My obeisances unto you, Lord Śiva, the supreme among Vaiṣṇavas.”

In a temple at the back are old deities of Gaura-Gadādhara and Lakṣmī-Nṛsiṁha. On the south side of the compound is Gaura-kuṇḍa, a pond named after Mahāprabhu. As we approached the entrance again, to the left we saw the memorial to Śrīla Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura.

A few hundred meters further along the road, our procession stopped at Śrīvāsa Aṅgana, where the Lord performed nocturnal *kīrtanas* behind closed doors. At this spot the congregational chanting began. Śrīvāsa’s house was the center of Lord Caitanya’s *saṅkīrtana* activities until He took sannyāsa and left Māyāpur.

This place is also known as Khol-bhāṅga-dāṅga, the place where Chand Kazi’s men broke the *mṛdaṅgas* of Lord Caitanya’s *saṅkīrtana* party. On the altar we saw a broken *mṛdaṅga*—not an original, but a replica to remind us of Chand Kazi’s harassment. We danced and chanted before the Pañca-tattva, who are also depicted performing *kīrtana,* and we absorbed ourselves in the holy name, remembering the blissful *kīrtanas* performed here.

A popular sport after the *parikramās* was swimming in the Gaṅgā. The river flowed about a half a kilometer away from our property. Before taking our bath we would scoop up three palms full of water and sprinkle drops into our mouths and onto our heads, then offer obeisances to mother Gaṅgā before wading or diving in. We took shelter from the heat of Bengal in her cool and refreshing waters. The Gaṅgā became an arena for a variety of activities. Some devotees played and wrestled or took mud baths, and others competed to be the first to swim to the other shore. I would take part in this swimming competition, but never won. Some devotees sat and watched the fun, while others sat in groups discussing *Caitanya-caritāmṛta*. Beautiful sunsets over the Gaṅgā contributed to the serenity in Māyāpur. Both the rising and setting sun looked huge and majestic.

## Health Problems

The main trouble for the Western devotees was keeping good health. A Western body is acclimated to a different range of germs and diseases than those found in India. Although a few simple precautions could have made all the difference, almost inevitably many devotees experienced health problems. The strain of living and traveling in India, the lack of hygiene, improper cooking by inexperienced cooks using low quality ingredients, overeating, heat exhaustion, sleeping without mosquito nets, culture shock, and homesickness all contributed to a disturbed mind and body.

Sunburn was especially common on *parikramās.* Devotees would be walking in the hot sun and seem to be glowing. It wasn’t a divine aura. It was sunburn. Often they’d get thirsty during the walks, and not having purified water, they’d drink whatever was available. Many contracted dysentery by doing this. On top of all that, devotees tended to not want to be caught up in “the bodily concept of life,” especially in the *dhāma,* and sometimes they neglected their health to their own detriment.

One night around midnight Prabhupāda went with a *sannyāsī* from room to room, checking on the sleeping devotees. On the way they met Kṛṣṇa-kiśora, from the Hyderabad temple.

**Kṛṣṇa-kiśora Dāsa:** Prabhupāda said, “Take rest, take rest.” He saw the devotees sleeping like children. Normally we are adults, but at night we sleep like children, and Prabhupāda was taking pleasure in seeing the devotees sound asleep.

A mother and father take pleasure, after a day’s labor, in putting their children to bed, and when the children are fast asleep, the parents like to watch them, just as they like to watch them play. Similarly, Prabhupāda was pleased to see his spiritual children asleep.

## Gaura Pūrṇimā Day

On March 18, the Caitanya Maṭha’s *parikramā* party wound its way onto our property for *darśana* of Rādhā-Mādhava. The procession included at least two hundred bright faces: devotee playing gongs and whompers, a few jumping *sannyāsīs,* and ladies carrying *tulasī* plants or their bags on their heads. A bicycle cart transported their trumpet-shaped loudspeaker. Some were unsure of what to make of the Western Vaiṣṇavas, but their doubts were allayed when we offered them a warm reception and some garlands. Jananivāsa distributed *caraṇāmṛta* to everyone.

During the day Prabhupāda gave initiation in the temple room. Pradyumna conducted the fire sacrifice. Under Prabhupāda’s direction, Pradyumna had learned both Sanskrit and how to conduct sacrifices. On this occasion Prabhupāda criticized him for not wearing an *upavastra* (top cloth). Pradyumna sat bare-chested, and Prabhupāda said, “He knows everything—still he does not do.” Prabhupāda was in a joyous mood. As one initiate received his beads and paid obeisances, Prabhupāda reached over and pulled his *śikhā.* Everyone laughed.

At dusk the full moon rose in the east as the sun sank in the west, over the Ganges. During evening *ārati,* the Bengali and Indian devotees displayed their expertise on *mṛdaṇgas,* and the Gaura Pūrṇimā festival reached its climax in an enthusiastic, exhausting *kīrtana,* full of jumping and spinning and running. Afterward, the devotees broke their all-day fast.

## Chapter 4. The Māyāpur Festival blossoms

By 1974 the whole aspect of the Māyāpur festival had changed. Seeing the results of the previous years and the increased facility in Māyāpur, Śrīla Prabhupāda again issued invitations all over the world, but he said that this year in India the devotees would spend half their time in Māyāpur and half in Vṛndāvana. Not needing to be asked twice, devotees enthusiastically poured in from around the world, eager for Prabhupāda’s association in the holy *dhāmas.*

This was the inauguration of the Māyāpur-Vṛndāvana festival. In 1972 information had been sent to the temples letting devotees know about an annual festival, but devotees outside India were unsure about it, or unprepared to attend until the next year. In 1973 the Māyāpur festival began to gather momentum; in 1974 it blossomed.

More than four hundred disciples inundated ISKCON Māyāpur— the largest gathering of Prabhupāda’s followers ever seen in India. Even the international devotees’ arrival was a big event: For the first time, a chartered planeload of devotees landed in India. The devotees had chartered an Air India jet originating in New York, which stopped in London, where the English devotees joined the flight. In Paris, Dhanañjaya boarded the plane. From Paris the flight went as far as New Delhi’s Palam Airport. One reason it did not continue to Calcutta was that there the airstrip was too short to accommodate a big international carrier. Thus, in Delhi, three hundred devotees were put up at the prestigious Ashoka Hotel, and the next morning they flew in a domestic Indian Airlines plane to Calcutta.

**Dhanañjaya Dāsa:** My wife, Bāla Gopāla, and I had been in Rome since September, starting a temple, and Prabhupāda was happy with the progress we were making. In a hand-written letter Prabhupāda said to me, “I hope that you are also coming to Māyāpur.” Actually, Prabhupāda had asked his servant, Śrutakīrti, who is of Italian descent, to start the temple in Rome, but Śrutakīrti refused to give up his service to Prabhupāda. So I volunteered to go, and an Italian devotee came along to translate.

**Bhīma Dāsa:** I came from the temple in St. Louis, Missouri—the Midwest. The devotees weren’t going to eat the airline food, so the temple cooks in New York prepared packages of *prasāda* for us. All the packages were put in boxes, and Rāmeśvara had to arrange to get those boxes on the plane. The airline staff stopped him at first. But after five minutes of heavy discussion—Rāmeśvara kept pointing his finger at them—the staff let him bring the boxes on board.

**Prahlādānand Swami:** I went to Māyāpur for the first time in 1974. I was the temple president in Austin, Texas. When I arrived at the Calcutta airport, I was amazed to see the contrast between airports in America, where everyone seems busy, and the Calcutta airport, where everyone was busy doing nothing.

**Ramāi Swami:** Prabhupāda said that all the devotees should regularly visit the *dhāmas* to recharge their spiritual batteries. He said it’s difficult to preach in the West because of the adverse conditions and that we naturally get run down. By attending the Māyāpur-Vṛndāvana festival, we got to meet devotees from countries that we’d never get to visit, and we heard about the glories of preaching around the world.

In Bombay, Girirāja Prabhu was preparing a list of devotees who could go to the festival, and I wanted to make sure that I didn’t miss this one. With many devotees coming from the West for the first time, like Viṣṇujana Swami, I knew that the *kīrtanas* would be even better than the year before. Viṣṇujana Mahārāja had just passed through Bombay, en route to the Māyāpur festival, and I’d heard a sampling of his wild, rocking chanting. He was eventually going to join the devotees holding festivals and traveling by boat on the Ganges in West Bengal.

I approached Girirāja, hoping to persuade him to add me to the list, but he seemed unwilling to consent. I persisted in trying to convince him, and finally he conceded, on one condition: I had to enroll a patron member before I could go. I was perplexed by his offer because until then I had been an assistant in the Membership department and mostly carried around the briefcase of a team leader. I was just starting the service, and I’d never made a member myself. His offer in effect promoted me to the position of a team leader, because team leaders were the ones making the members and getting the credit. I was determined to go, so I took up the challenge. I prayed to Mahāprabhu and Rādhā-Rāsabihārī for Their blessings to succeed, and my prayers were heard: After several days of serious endeavor I succeeded in enrolling my first patron member. A few days later, our Bombay group left for Māyāpur by train, eagerly anticipating seeing Hari, Guru, and the Vaiṣṇavas.

## Prabhupāda’s Arrival

When Śrīla Prabhupāda traveled to Māyāpur, he usually stopped at a mango grove before the town Ranaghata to honor breakfast *prasāda* in peace. A few young curious onlookers would assemble to watch the uncommon visitors. Far from any crowd or city, he sat amid the old tall mango trees, which stood widely spaced apart, and the devotees with him took their *prasāda* sitting around him. Then, after washing his hands, Prabhupāda would get back in his car and resume the journey, his thoughts turning to the disciples awaiting him.

Prabhupāda arrived in Māyāpur a week before Gaura Pūrṇimā, which was on March 8 this year. Devotees from all parts of the world met him outside the main gate, overwhelmed with enthusiasm. They danced wildly alongside, behind, and in front of his car. The devotees did not restrain their love for Prabhupāda. It was truly an affectionate welcome in the grandest style. And as they were happy to see Prabhupāda, he was just as pleased to see them.

The decorated roadway inside our compound welcomed Prabhupāda with colorful flowers and mango leaves. There was a new sign on the Lotus Building this year, the name Māyāpur Candrodayā Mandir, meaning “temple of the rising moon.” Like a rising golden moon, Lord Caitanya had appeared here and was now bestowing benedictions worldwide. As Prabhupāda entered the recently completed building, cooling rays emanated from the temple room’s sparkling marble floor, freshly painted walls, and clean crystal chandeliers. The devotees had done a really special job of cleaning.

After offering obeisances before the magnificent, small deities of Rādhā-Mādhava, Prabhupāda turned and walked across the temple hall and sat on his *vyāsāsana.* He beheld the international gathering of disciples, and his happiness visibly increased. This was the stateliest and largest reception for him in Māyāpur ever. He welcomed his disciples by acknowledging that Śrīla Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura’s prophecy had come true: The fortunate Westerners were raising *kīrtana* in the streets and joining the Bengali devotees in chanting *“Jaya* Śacīnandana!”

After concluding his welcome address, Prabhupāda went up to his quarters and briefly relaxed. Then he was eager to receive leading disciples to hear their reports. This year, the members of the Governing Body Commission (GBC) would hold a meeting in Māyāpur for the first time.

## Prabhupāda and the Dhāma Alleviate All Discomfort

A great deal of progress had been made on the Lotus Building during 1973. The Māyāpur devotees were gaining experience in management, construction, and Indian business ethics. Although much work was still to be done, the building was no longer just a concrete skeleton in the fields. Building ornamentation had been added, land cleared, and gardens planted.

Tents were supplied to the resident devotees and Indian devotees for the duration of the festival, freeing the building to serve its purpose of sheltering VIP’s and hundreds of foreign devotees. Somehow or other, all the devotees fit in. Everyone had a straw mat and a mosquito net, and each room resembled two rows of cocoons joined to a string in the middle of the room. Some rooms were complete, but quite a few were not. The unfinished areas lacked proper windows and flooring.

Although the Māyāpur devotees were gaining experience in holding the Gaura Pūrṇimā festival and had improved the accommodations since the 1973 festival, many visiting devotees were making their first trip to India and found the arrangements inadequate. Still, the festival soon got into full swing. Any shortcomings were generally made up for by Prabhupāda’s presence and the mystique of Māyāpur. Prabhupāda was the main source of the devotees’ real pleasure, and their almost inexpressible bliss and happiness at being with Prabhupāda in the holy *dhāma* diminished their physical and mental discomforts. Nearly all the devotees lived and preached where the modes of ignorance and passion ruled. To them, this was a pilgrimage for purification, rejuvenation, and enlivenment. Because they had Śrīla Prabhupāda’s association, they all felt they achieved this and much more.

**Pārvatī Dāsi:** I was from Baltimore, but had been on the road for four years. I was used to lots of hardship. I’d hitchhiked to Māyāpur from Nepal, with Mahādeva and Caraṇāravindam. The women and a few children were staying in a room filled with rubble. The worst part was that everyone had a mosquito net except me. I just went outside and slept in a display booth under a picture of Ananta Śeṣa. At three in the morning I would be woken up by someone’s loud japa, but I didn’t mind, because the festival was a new and wonderful experience and the atmosphere vibrant. I came into ISKCON completely fresh and green, obsessed with the idea of getting connected to Śrīla Prabhupāda. I excitedly wrote a letter of application for initiation. To my surprise, I was recommended.

**Dhanañjaya Dāsa:** I could not sleep during my first night in Māyāpur; the atmosphere was too amazing and electrifying, and I kept hearing the crickets. I got up in the middle of the night and was chanting my rounds on a veranda when Prabhupāda came down the stairs. I paid my obeisances. Prabhupāda was checking all the rooms to see if the devotees were comfortable. When Prabhupāda saw devotees without blankets or warm clothes, he would tell the leaders and ask them to supply these things the next day. Prabhupāda asked me if I liked Māyāpur, and I said, “Oh, Prabhupāda, it’s a wonderful atmosphere.”

Prabhupāda also asked me to help him. He said that a boy from Washington, D.C. wanted second initiation, and he asked if I knew the *gāyatrī mantra.* Some devotees had stopped chanting their *gāyatrī;* others weren’t chanting it properly. Prabhupāda had given me the *gāyatrī mantra* in December 1969, in London, and I said I knew it. Then Prabhupāda said I could teach this devotee how to chant it. He told me the devotee’s name and said, “Find him and chant the mantra into his right ear.”

Jananivāsa’s twin brother, who’d received initiation and the name Paṅkajāṅghri in England some months before, came to the 1974 festival.

**Paṅkajāṅghri Dāsa:** There were hundreds of devotees, dozens of *daṇḍas.* It was a little overwhelming for me. Everything was wonderful, but I was so inexperienced that I couldn’t comprehend what the whole program was. I didn’t even know where the *prasāda* was served. I was fortunate because Jananivāsa was feeding me. In one class Śrīla Prabhupāda apologized for the discomfort. It was very touching.

## Kīrtanas Conjure Up the Spiritual Sky

*Kīrtanas* in Māyāpur were the greatest, with the biggest names in *kīrtana* assembled from around the world. Viṣṇujana Swami, Acyutānanda Swami, Madhudviṣa, Agnideva, Dīnanātha, and many others took turns leading *maṅgala-ārati, guru-pūjā,* and *sandhyā-ārati.* Our regular *kīrtanas* were normally ecstatic, but in Māyāpur, with Śrīla Prabhupāda and all the devotees present, *kīrtanas* had the most euphoric atmosphere imaginable, just like the taste of sweet rice enhanced by camphor.

**Ajāmila Dāsa:** The first *maṅgala-ārati* was absolutely thunderous. It was as if the spiritual sky opened and allowed us a breathtaking glance. It was unforgettable. Tamal Krishna Goswami led that *kīrtana,* and Śrīla Prabhupāda told him just afterwards that if we all kept chanting like that, Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura would come and take all of us back to Godhead.

Prabhupāda said that every morning we should sing Bhaktivinoda Thakura’s song *Aruṇodaya-kīrtana*: “When the rising sun appeared in the East, the jewel of the twice-born, Lord Gaurasundara, awakened, and, taking His devotees with Him, He went to all the countryside towns and villages and played the *mṛdaṇga*. The cymbals chimed in time. Lord Gaurāṇga’s shimmering golden features danced, and His footbells jingled.” So after *tulasī-ārati*, everyone would sit down and chant this song describing how the Lord and His associates gathered early in the morning to distribute the holy names to the sleeping conditioned souls.

## Morning Activities and Morning Rāgas

After arriving in Māyāpur I made friends with Jananivāsa. Throughout 1973 in Bombay I had served as the head *pūjārī* for Śrī Śrī Rādhā-Rāsabihārī, so I was more than eager to do deity worship in Śrīdhāma Māyāpur. Jananivāsa let me bathe and dress the golden form of Śrīmatī Rādhāraṇī. Naturally this was a spiritually rewarding service. As I gently took care of the deity, I would hear the devotees chant their *japa* in the temple room, and I would think of Prabhupāda taking his morning walk on Bhaktisiddhānta Road.

One morning Prabhupāda walked toward the Yoga-pīṭha. As he came to the first right bend in the road, he stopped under a big, old, shady tree. The devotees gathered around him. Prabhupāda said, “This tree was here at the time of Caitanya Mahāprabhu. He would come to this tree to hold *kīrtana* and talk. Many, many hundreds of people would come and listen to Lord Caitanya.”

Prabhupāda humbly added, “But me—I am just an old Bengali. If I stood under this tree, how many people would come? That is why we are building the Māyāpur project. Nowadays people are attracted to big buildings. Tourists go to New York to see skyscrapers. They are not particularly interested in hearing, but they want to see.”

Unfortunately, years later, a flood washed that tree away.

Many outsiders used to walk on our Māyāpur property because there were no walls, boundary markers, or natural property dividers. A new sugarcane operation run by some Bengali devotees created a lot of traffic. Numerous sugarcane farmers brought their produce to the devotees, who crushed it and cooked the juice down to *gur.* Puṣkara Dāsa, a BBT artist from America, kept complaining to Bhavānanda Mahārāja about all the passersby. One morning Puṣkara suggested erecting a fence and a gate to control the pedestrians.

**Puṣkara Dāsa:** “No,” Bhāvananda said, “we don’t need fences. These people are all residents of the holy *dhāma,* even the dogs. Why should we stop them?” Just then Prabhupāda passed by on his morning walk and said to Bhavānanda Mahārāja, “Why all are these people walking over our property, and why are all these dogs on our land?” Bhavānanda glanced over his shoulder at me and smiled, acknowledging that I was right.

The Māyāpur devotees hired a group of *śahnāī* musicians to play every morning. The *śahnāī* is a Vedic instrument, similar to a clarinet, but opening more like a trumpet, and it makes a soothing sound. A morning *raga*—a suitable morning melody—regularly filled the air with mystical vibrations. Śrīla Prabhupāda approved of the *śahnāī rāgas,* which were a new experience for the foreign devotees. Some devotees complained that they had difficulty concentrating on their chanting, but the *śahnāī* band played on.

## Flower Petals at Guru-pūjā

Wherever Prabhupāda walked in the morning, he always managed to return in time for the greeting of the deities. Without looking at his watch, and no matter what direction he walked in, he managed to be on time. After the deity greeting, Prabhupāda took his seat on his new marble *vyāsāsana* flanked by parapets—low railings on the floor. Behind his seat hung large paintings of the *guru-paramparā.* In the course of the year since the last festival, the temple room had been extended lengthwise, though I can’t remember why. After some discussion, Śrīla Prabhupāda’s *vyāsāsana* had been shifted to the far end of the temple room. When a devotee asked Prabhupāda whether it wasn’t now too far away from the deities, Prabhupāda replied, “There is no question of distance between me and Kṛṣṇa.” So the *vyāsāsana* stayed at the back of the temple room.

Prabhupāda picked up his *karatālas* and began playing them as the lead singer began the *Guru-vandanā* prayers. The atmosphere turned vibrant, with hundreds of devotees singing the glories of their eternal spiritual master. Each devotee stepped forward and offered a handful of flower petals at Prabhupāda’s feet and paid obeisances. Prabhupāda sat still, only shaking his head slightly to the beat as he sang along. On some days he had his eyes closed most of the time, in a meditative mood, but would occasionally glance at the devotees happily dancing. When Prabhupāda appreciated the dancing, a smile would erupt on his face. We could read the feelings on his face. Devotees watched Prabhupāda, so there was reciprocation and a transcendental competition.

By the time all the devotees finished offering flowers, a big pile of flower petals covered Prabhupāda’s lotus feet. There were even flowers in his lap. The *kīrtana* began to pick up speed as the final verse was sung, and Prabhupāda would stop playing *karatālas.* With his right hand, he took big handfuls of flowers and threw them with all his strength, showering them over the dancing devotees. The devotees would go mad in ecstasy as Prabhupāda did this several times, until almost all the flower petals were gone. Devotees would jump and leap, trying to catch the flowers before they landed on the ground. Prabhupāda would throw flowers in different directions, making sure the ladies got their quota and that the devotees standing at the back got some. This was done at every *guru- pūjā,* and Prabhupāda would always choose the same time to start throwing the flowers—just when the tempo increased. The devotees looked forward to that moment.

## Lectures on Caitanya-caritāmṛta

Before Śrīla Prabhupāda left India to travel to the West he had written several essays based on the *Caitanya-caritāmṛta.* Then, in 1968, in America, he had completed a study of the *Caitanya-caritāmṛta* called *Teachings of Lord Caitanya.* Now, in 1974, Prabhupāda was in the middle of translating the *Caitanya-caritāmṛta,* so he lectured on it, and these classes were a highlight of the festival. It was a treat hearing the *Caitanya-caritāmṛta* “hot off the press” from Prabhupāda’s own mouth in the holy *dhāma.*

Often Prabhupāda asked Nitāi-chānd Dāsa, a devotee born in Māyāpur, to sing *“Jaya* Rādhā-Mādhava” before class. Being Bengali, he chanted or sounded almost like Prabhupāda. Pradyumna sat inside the parapet and read the verses, translations, and purports, and Prabhupāda would stop him at different points to comment. Ten to twenty devotees armed with tape recorders gathered around the two poles that supported the speakers, eager to record for their friends the transcendental message of Śrīla Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmi spoken by Prabhupāda. Two devotees would stand on either side of Prabhupāda and fan him with *cāmaras* and peacock fans. This would go on in shifts. Prabhupāda said it was all right to stand close to him and touch him with the fans to keep the flies away. The overall mood was enhanced by the morning light filtering through the openings in the *jālis,* the carved-cement walls.

Annadā Dāsi, a devotee from Delhi, remembered that when Prabhupāda chanted *“Jaya* Rādhā-Mādhava,” he used his whole energy, everything he could give, and she’d see the strength of his mind, body, and soul. “It was intense,” she said. “I would look at him and feel the power of his devotion. Actually, nothing is more powerful than that. I will never forget it.”

On March 1, in his first class, Prabhupāda spoke on *Ādi-līlā* [7.1](https://vedarama.com/en/books/cc-adi/7.1) , wherein Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja writes: “Let me write about Lord Caitanya’s magnanimous contribution of devotional service in love of God.” Śrīla Prabhupāda continued this theme by describing his own literary work and the role of his movement.

Māyāpur is the place of Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu’s appearance. In this place, during these holy days, this is the appropriate literature. Last year, one gentleman suggested: “You kindly translate Śrī *Caitanya-caritāmṛta.”* So I took the initiative and began. Now it is almost complete. I am now translating *Madhya-līlā,* twentieth chapter. My Guru Mahārāja used to read *Caitanya-caritāmṛta* whenever he had time, and he predicted that the whole world would read it—and for this they would learn Bengali. So, following in his footsteps, I have kept the Bengali text and have given the meaning of each word of the Bengali poem.

The great author, Kavirāja Gosvāmi, depicted the activities and teachings of Caitanya Mahāprabhu, who traveled on this street 488 years ago. Now that memory is going to be revived by this Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement all over the world, because Caitanya Mahāprabhu wanted this. Śrī Kavirāja Gosvāmi described the magnanimity of Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu, and Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu’s birthsite is here, and the annual birth anniversary will be on March eighth. So I am very glad. I welcome you all. You have taken so much labor to come here. Take advantage of the teachings of Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu described by the fittest author, Kavirāja Gosvāmi, which we have tried to translate as far as possible.

At the end of each class, a few devotees were always ready to immediately shout, *“Jaya* Śrīla Prabhupāda!” and everyone would join in the chorus. Then a sweet, mellow *kīrtana* would start, and Prabhupāda would clap or play *karatālas.* Sometimes he gave out sweets.

## “You Can’t See Prabhupāda.”

Devotees came to the Māyāpur festival because they’d heard that Śrīla Prabhupāda was accessible and they knew his association was invaluable. Devotees who’d attended the festivals in previous years reported on his walks and *darśanas* after returning to their countries. This year, however, with increased attendance at the festival, that accessibility became minimized. Big devotees stood in place around Prabhupāda, and he became protected—out of love or over-concern for his security. Even senior disciples who led large centers were denied abundant access, but this was no consolation to the eager devotees dying for Prabhupāda’s association.

**Ramāi Swami:** It wasn’t easy to enter Prabhupāda’s presence. My feeling was that the older disciples monopolized his time. I had a really high level of awe and reverence toward Prabhupāda as a guru and a writer before I saw him in his room for the first time, and at *darśanas* I got to see his personal side.

**Bhīma Dāsa:** When I got to Māyāpur, the first thing I wanted to do was see Prabhupāda. I asked several devotees where his room was, and I walked up to the second floor. When I reached his screen door, I saw many *sannyāsīs* inside. I offered my obeisances and was about to enter when someone shouted: “Hey! He’s going into Prabhupāda’s room. Quick, grab him!” I looked around and saw two devotees flying down the veranda toward me.

“You can’t see Prabhupāda,” they warned me.

“Oh, you can’t?” I said innocently.

“No. He is too busy meeting the leaders right now. You can’t disturb him. But you can do some service.”

Then I and another devotee from St. Louis got to mop the floor in front of the bathroom next to Prabhupāda’s room. While we were on our hands and knees mopping, Śrīla Prabhupāda came out of his room in his *gamchā,* and when he passed us, he said *“Jaya*!*”* and went in the bathroom. We offered obeisances. Then I realized that we become qualified to see him by doing service.

At five in the evening Prabhupāda would allow devotees into his quarters for *darśana.* Prabhupāda would speak on all varieties of topics, with the one aim of increasing his disciples’ Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Some devotees came with gifts for his pleasure. The Australian and New Zealand devotees brought ghee; this was a good excuse to meet Prabhupāda—to hand him a can of top-quality ghee. During the festival we would see the cans stacked, largest to smallest, in a corner of his room.

Prabhupāda had his servant offer some of the ghee to the Māyāpur deities, then distributed the rest to other Indian temples. After the festival our group took at least a hundred liters of it back to Bombay to offer to Śrī Śrī Rādhā-Rāsabihārī. We not only offered it to the deities, but also transferred it into smaller containers and distributed it to members and patrons as a *prasāda* gift from ISKCON. The Indian members would be amazed at the bright yellow color of Australian ghee.

## Iskcon Food Relief

**Jayapatākā Swami** ***uvāca:*** During the festival, devotees ate a feast, and after *prasāda* Prabhupāda was taking a nap. Dogs were howling outside, disturbing his sleep. He went to a window and saw dogs and children fighting over the *prasāda* remnants that had been left on the ground.

Prabhupāda called for his leaders. Normally, if Prabhupāda wanted to say something, he would say, “Bring my secretary,” but in Māyāpur occasionally he would call everyone if it was an important matter.

The leaders were just recovering from the feast. They came quickly, wondering what the matter was. They saw Prabhupāda looking out the window with watery eyes. Everyone also looked out and saw the big pile of leaf plates and about fifteen dogs and a dozen boys and girls eating the leftovers and licking the leaves. The children were ordinary villagers, basically clean, but poor.

Prabhupāda was silent, and everyone was astonished. Prabhupāda then said, “How hungry they must be! See how they are picking *prasāda* from the pile! We must arrange for their *prasāda!* If you want to make this a *mandir,* then you must see that no one goes hungry within a ten-mile radius. That will be your success. Kṛṣṇa is everyone’s father.
