How can the children go hungry in the presence of the father? Immediately arrange to distribute *prasāda.”*

When Prabhupāda wanted something done immediately, it meant the next day was too late. But it was already late in the afternoon, and there was no way we could get it together before the end of that day. The next day we cooked a big quantity of *kicharī* and served it out under a big tent. This is how we started ISKCON Food Relief in Māyāpur. Prabhupāda said that if we could have a *prasāda* distribution center for every ten square miles of land throughout India, then we could transform all of India. People would become so grateful that they would all become followers of Lord Caitanya’s movement. Simply by *prasāda* distribution, all the effects of Kali-yuga would be stopped.

## The Pañca-tattva Mantra

In class on March 3, Śrīla Prabhupāda described why devotees offer obeisances to Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu by chanting the Pañca-tattva mantra: Kṛṣṇa first spoke about Himself in the *Bhagavad-gītā,* but foolish persons misunderstood Him to be ordinary. To help people really understand Him, Kṛṣṇa manifested again in five features. Therefore we worship the Pañca-tattva by chanting *śrī-kṛṣṇa-caitanya prabhu-nityānanda śrī-advaita gadādhara śrīvāsādi-gaura- bhakta-vṛndā.* Next we chant the Hare Kṛṣṇa *mahā-mantra.*

“Then it will be successful,” Prabhupāda said. “Without going through the Pañca-tattva, without Their mercy, nobody can understand Kṛṣṇa. He will remain a myth.”

## Devotees Cautioned about Parikramā Visits

Devotees went on daily *parikramās* to the historically prominent places and heard the pastimes of Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu. Every day Prabhupāda would get a report of what happened.

S**unitā Dāsi (wife of Toṣaṇa-kṛṣṇa Dāsa):** We were a big ecstatic group, Prabhupāda’s white elephants. We went on the *parikramās* with half a dozen *mṛdaṅgas* and dozens of *karatālas.* Yaduvara and Viśākhā would film and photograph the party. Prabhupāda was so proud.

One day the *parikramā* party was taken, on invitation, to Śrīdhar Swami’s *maṭha* in Navadvīpa. Acyutānanda Swami arranged it, because he’d stayed there in the early days. Jayapatākā Mahārāja arranged the boats to transport the devotees across the Ganges. There was a *kīrtana,* some talks, and a feast. The devotees thought it was a spiritually historic meeting, but they’d forgotten one important detail: to get Śrīla Prabhupāda’s permission.

When we returned, Prabhupāda, as usual, was waiting for our report. When we gave the report, he called for a general assembly in the temple room. He angrily expressed his displeasure that the devotees went there. The devotees were a little bewildered, but Prabhupāda explained that people would think that all the Western devotees had been made by the preaching of his godbrothers, who had never left India. In 1968 his godbrother Bon Mahārāja had canvassed his Western disciples for reinitiation, so Prabhupāda was wary.

“You cannot do this. You don’t understand how tricky these Gauḍīya Maṭha people are,” Prabhupāda warned. He asked what Śrīdhar Mahārāja had said. He wanted to hear the tape, and he found out that our devotees did most of the talking.

**Jayapatākā Swami:** There were several speakers from different Gauḍīya Maṭhas. One speaker addressed the devotees by saying, “You people have the backgrounds of meat-eaters.” He mentioned all the worst opinions of foreigners, then continued: “And by the mercy of Caitanya Mahāprabhu you could get the blessings.” We sort of humbly listened.

Śrīla Prabhupāda said, “This is very offensive. This is insulting. This is not the way they should speak. All of you have become spiritually scarred by listening to such talks. Just like when you get cut and it heals—still there is a scar. It will never leave. You should be very careful. Don’t listen to all these things people say. They are actually trying to diminish your faith in me.”

*Parikramās* continued. Although buses were transporting the devotees, they became exhausted because of the heat and the bumpy, dusty roads, and by traveling to many places.

**Jayādvaita Swami:** The organizers used to arrange these super-ambitious *parikramās* that would knock everyone out. There were boats and buses, and there was walking. We had really impressive parties going out on Bhaktisiddhānta Road—huge, long *kīrtana* parties, and devotees from many countries. By the end of the day, if there was anything left of you, you deserved a medal. It never occurred to the organizers that maybe this was pushing the troops. Jayapatākā Mahārāja is built like a piece of heavy machinery, so what he can handle is not what everyone else can handle. That’s how those programs went on. It was tough, but very impressive.

## The Cure for Sickness: Work

Bottled water did not even exist in those days, and the cooking was substandard. Many devotees had stomach problems. They had to sleep during the day to recover. When Prabhupāda heard about this, he investigated.

Puṣkara Dāsa: I was sitting there when Prabhupāda came by. He was walking past the sick bodies—fifty or sixty guys stretched out—and none of them stirred when he entered. It looked like a hospital in a battle zone. I wasn’t sick. I was just reading. Prabhupāda didn’t say a word. He was walking with a cane, accompanied by two devotees.

After this inspection Prabhupāda called for some leaders. He held them responsible for the devotees’ sickness. The managers said the cooking oil was of a low quality.

“Even if men are sick, why are they sleeping?” Prabhupāda asked. “Sleeping is the worst thing they can do. They should take some rest, but then you should engage them in practical work. A man who works, can digest stones.”

No one knew much about cooking. Prahlādānand was asked to roast peanuts on Ekādaśī and was told how to do it Indian style—by putting sand in a wok and putting the peanuts in the sand. He asked where to get sand and was told to take some dirt from the ground. Then he asked how to get the dirt off and was advised to wash the peanuts after they were roasted.

**Prahlādānand Swami:** I got some dirt instead of sand and put it in a huge wok to roast the peanuts. Later, I added water and washed the peanuts for five or six hours, but still couldn’t get off all the dirt. Finally, it was time for the offering. The peanuts weren’t clean, but they were offered anyway. When I asked devotees how they liked the peanuts, they said, “They tasted all right, but they had a sandy crunch to them.”

**Jayādvaita Swami:** I do recall being sick half the time. None of the Westerners really knew how to live in India, except for the ones who had been living there. So you are living in this little room, and you’re eating whatever the authorities are providing, and you don’t know how to live at all. I remember lying on my back in this little room hearing Prabhupāda’s class over the loudspeaker and being half-asleep. That’s the kind of thing that was going on. We were not up to full strength back then. Quite a number of us became too sick to travel to Vṛndāvana for the second half of the festival.

## “My God, She Looks Like Prabhupāda!”

Gāyatrī Dāsī was Pisimā’s servant, and carried her pint-sized bottle of Ganges water. Pisimā doused anyone and everyone with handfuls of this water, especially everyone’s beadbag. She thought she herself contaminated places, and thus she’d sprinkle Ganges water on the floor where she sat and even on a door frame if she brushed against it, to counteract any impurity.

In the afternoons Gāyatrī assisted Pisimā in climbing the steps to Prabhupāda’s room. Pisimā was old and heavy-set, so she couldn’t climb stairs by herself. But she always managed to attend Prabhupāda’s *darśanas,* even though she knew little English.

**Puṣkara Dāsa:** My first encounter with Pisimā took place when I was unrolling a seven-foot-long painting of Prabhupāda that the devotees had brought from Los Angeles. An old lady popped her face into mine and pointed at the painting, saying, “Prabhupāda!” I looked at her and thought, “My God, she looks like Prabhupāda!” I pointed to Prabhupāda in the painting. She smiled broadly. Then I found out she was Prabhupāda’s sister.

While on *parikramā,* Pārvatī got thirsty, and Pisimā handed Pārvatī her own *loṭā* to drink from.

**Pārvatī Dāsi:** I poured the water into my mouth. Everyone around me thought this was a breach of etiquette, but I didn’t know who she was, and I didn’t know the culture. Pisimā was motherly and charming.

Once, Pisimā asked Sunitā to help her climb the stairs to Prabhupāda’s quarters. Sunitā thought it was Kṛṣṇa’s mercy that Pisimā had asked her for help, because now she had a chance to see Prabhupāda.

**Sunitā Dāsi:** They both spoke in Bengali. Prabhupāda went out to the veranda with Pisimā. He surveyed the thousands of pilgrims and lifted his head in an expression of satisfaction and pride. I never saw Prabhupāda look so proud. He said to Pisimā, “Just see! See the thousands of people.” Pisimā said, “Yes, this is all your doing.” Prabhupāda responded, “What is my doing? What can I do? It is all to my Guru Mahārāja’s credit.” It was so sweet to see. He was proud and happy that the movement was growing, but not claiming the glory himself.

## Photo Display Is an Important Festival Feature

For the many thousands of guests on Gaura Pūrṇimā, Prabhupāda eagerly wanted a public exhibition of ISKCON’s international activities. He said a booth should be set up for every temple. He’d written to Bharadrāj on January 25, 1974:

Regarding a photographic exhibit in Māyāpur, this is an important part of our Caitanya Festival. Bali Mardana has agreed that the photographers at ISKCON Press can blow up photographs of our preaching work and men can take them who are going to Māyāpur. As for mounting them, however, that you can do there, and arrange them into an exhibit.

Prabhupāda toured the exhibition, spending a few minutes at several booths, appreciating the efforts his disciples were making for him. National flags were displayed at the booths to impress upon visitors that devotees from many different countries were united under the banner of Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu.

## Gaura Pūrṇimā—Fasting and Initiations

On March 8, the devotees were filled with anticipation. They’d be fasting, attending morning class and *kīrtanas,* and bathing in the Gaṅgā. There’d be initiations, many visiting pilgrims, an *abhiṣeka* at moonrise, and an evening feast.

Śrī Śrī Rādhā-Mādhava wore splendid new outfits, and the temple room contained varieties of colorful decorations, painstakingly put up the previous night. Garlands of flowers and mango leaves were hung from the ceiling and wrapped around pillars. Two tall banana trees stood at the temple entrance, forming an arch, and the *śahnāī* band played softly in the background. All these arrangements evoked auspiciousness in everyone’s mind.

Late in the morning, about thirty devotees received initiation.

**Pārvatī Dāsi:** When it came time to accept my beads, I tried looking into Śrīla Prabhupāda’s eyes, but he slid his eyes back so that I couldn’t see them. This helped me realize that I was materially contaminated and trying to understand something almost totally beyond my comprehension. Śrīla Prabhupāda asked me what the regulative principles were. I couldn’t remember the rule against gambling, and Prabhupāda filled in the blank for me. Then I got my beads and felt complete, because that was the moment I’d been waiting for.

Prabhupāda told the devotees that during the day the men should shave their heads and they should all bathe in the Gaṅgā. The men were on the banks of the Gaṅgā trying to shave up when Prabhupāda arrived with a big group of *sannyāsīs.* Everyone watched him walk to the edge of the Gaṅgā, crouch down, and sprinkle three drops of water on his head saying, “This is the same as taking a bath.” Then he walked back to the temple.

In the afternoon those who became *brāhmaṇas* got to visit Prabhupāda to receive the *gāyatri-mantra*.

**Ādityā Dāsi:** I was the only person in the room with Prabhupāda, so I thought it was appropriate to sit at a ladylike distance from him. But by sitting where I did, I had a problem following him when he was saying the mantras. I was holding a copy of the mantras in front of me, and he said, “Don’t look at the paper. Just listen. Just hear me.” I kept looking at the paper. To get my full attention, he insisted in a louder voice: “Don’t look at the sheet. Just hear me as I say it.”

Fasting all day used to be hard for many of us in those days. For me, it was not only a new experience but a difficult one, because I was thin, with no reserves of energy, and I operated on a daily intake. Especially the period from noon until moonrise used to be quite a task. I’d count the hours left, then the minutes left. Coming from a Hindu family, I had experienced fasting with no grains—surviving on fruits and roots. But a total fast was a new experience. Moonrise signaled Mahāprabhu’s appearance and the end of the fasting period.

At the end of the festival Girirāja Prabhu brought to Prabhupāda’s attention the news that the municipality of Bombay had denied ISKCON permission to build a temple, on the grounds that the chanting in the temple would be a nuisance. It was decided to hold massive public chanting programs in Bombay to win public support. So a contingent of devotees immediately went to Bombay instead of going to Vṛndāvana for the second half of the festival. This group included all the Australian devotees and some Americans and Europeans. They chanted early in the morning through the local neighborhoods around Juhu Beach, led by Acyutānanda Swami, Dinanātha, and others. Later in the morning, longer chanting processions were led by Viṣṇujana Swami—sometimes in downtown Bombay and sometimes in the nearby areas Santa Cruz and Ville Parle. Then the devotees would return for lunch.

**Kurma Dāsa:** Śrīla Prabhupāda also came to Bombay after the Vṛndāvana festival, and we got to go on some of his morning walks. After his evening classes, we’d chant and dance in front of him. Then he would distribute the whole plate of deity *mahā-prasāda* with his own hand. After three weeks in Bombay, we headed back to Australia.

## Chapter 5. The Vṛndāvana Festival begins

Śrīla Prabhupāda planned to stay in Vṛndāvana for a week. To be with him, more than half the devotees who went to Māyāpur caught a plane or train to Delhi, then rode chartered buses to Vṛndāvana. Of the rest, over a fourth were too sick to travel, and some went to Bombay to chant as a public demonstration.

Excitement built as the devotees on the chartered buses traveled south on the Delhi-Agra highway and finally turned left onto Chattikara Road, the last leg. It was a narrow road, just wide enough for one vehicle. The buses often pulled over to the side to accommodate oncoming vehicles, or vice versa.

The vegetation and landscape of Vṛndāvana differed from Māyāpur’s. Vṛndāvana also had more dust and larger cows and people. Western devotees were having a lot of difficulty coming to terms with India in general and the *dhāmas* in particular. They lacked prior exposure to Indian culture, and Vṛndāvana was nothing like what they’d read about in *Kṛṣṇa* book: fruits hanging from desire trees, *cintāmaṇi* soil, crystal-clear lakes with swans and blooming lotuses, and the nectarean waters of the Yamunā.

**Jayādvaita Swami:** Westerners see Vṛndāvana and have to come to terms with the difference between Vṛndāvana in the books and the Vṛndāvana that meets the mundane eye. That always needs adjusting. When you read *Kṛṣṇa* book, you have such a high impression of Vṛndāvana that you don’t want to pass urine there. So there was a sense that this was a mystical place and that it was all just under the surface. Prabhupāda told us we have to purify our vision, so we definitely had the feeling that it was our shortcoming that we couldn’t see Vṛndāvana in its true terms. We had the philosophical background, but we didn’t have any cultural training or refinement.

## Settling in

The Vṛndāvana temple wasn’t even built. The construction was going slow. The columns were just bare stacks of bricks, and there were no walls or roof. Work had started on the altars, but just rough work, enough to tell that it was going to be a temple. The construction site was considered off-limits to the devotees because Śrīla Prabhupāda wanted the work to go on without disturbance. So Gurudāsa Dāsa and Yamunā Dāsī, the festival organizers, arranged for the devotees to stay at Fogal Ashram, half a kilometer down the road toward Vṛndāvana town.

The Fogal Ashram had more than enough rooms to accommodate everyone, and it also had a kitchen, so devotees cooked and ate there. They did almost everything at Fogal Ashram. That’s where the morning program was held, and every day Śrīla Prabhupāda would walk from the Krishna-Balaram temple to give a class there.

The setting was simple, with a single lightbulb hanging above Prabhupāda. A *dari* was spread out for the devotees to sit on, and they crowded around his makeshift *vyāsāsana* to catch his words. An electric fan was keeping the flies away from Prabhupāda. One day he apologized: “We are sorry you have come all this way, and we haven’t got proper facility for you.” Sometimes he also came in the evening and gave class in an outdoor pavilion.

Since Dhanañjaya mainly saw Prabhupāda at Fogal Ashram, he at first had the impression that Prabhupāda was staying there. But in among all the rubble at the Krishna-Balaram temple, part of Prabhupāda’s quarters were usable. Despite the noisy work that was going on in the other rooms in his house, Prabhupāda was happy to call this his Vṛndāvana home. He took walks on Chattikara Road and took massages in a roped-off section of the construction site. He’d first used his front room in February, a month before the festival.

**Nava-yauvana Dāsa:** I visited Prabhupāda that February from Tehran, en route to the festival. Prabhupāda had come to Vṛndāvana to check on the progress of the construction and to instruct Gurudāsa Dāsa. It was a miracle the temple was being built under devotee supervision. At that time, Ānanda Prabhu, one of Prabhupāda’s godbrothers, who was seventy, regularly cooked and served *prasāda* to about twenty-five devotees.

On the anniversary of Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta’s appearance, Yamunā was cooking a feast. The devotees were fasting and had the morning off, so we got to sit with Prabhupāda in his room till noon. George Harrison and Ravi Shankar arrived unannounced. George had learned that Prabhupāda was in Vṛndāvana and wanted to see him. Prabhupāda asked Gurudāsa Dāsa to take George to the kitchen and get some *samosās* from Yamunā. Prabhupāda put Ravi at ease by chatting about his flight and travels. When George returned, Prabhupāda said to him, “Now you are famous. But where are you going in your next life? So take Kṛṣṇa consciousness seriously.” After listening to Prabhupāda for a short time, he bowed down before he and Ravi left.

There was a big carpet in Prabhupāda’s room, but Prabhupāda didn’t like it because it got dirty. When Caraṇāravindam Dāsa made the helpful comment that the dirt was nothing but the dust of Vṛndāvana from the lotus feet of devotees, Śrīla Prabhupāda sarcastically replied to him, “You are a devotee. I am not a devotee.” He insisted that the carpet be removed.

Naturally, the Vṛndāvana devotees—occupied with construction—were far less experienced festival managers than the devotees in Māyāpur. To make matters worse, Māyāpur had used up most of the money from the festival fees, leaving Vṛndāvana with a small festival budget. And that wasn’t all that was going on:

**Gopal Kṛṣṇa Goswami:** In those days, devotees paid one combined Māyāpur-Vṛndāvana festival fee, but the division of the funds between the Māyāpur and Vṛndāvana temples was often not proportionate. A lot more funds were spent for the Māyāpur festival. Mahendra, the manager of the North American BBT, was a festival coordinator, and he collected fees from all the American devotees and helped pay the festival expenses. Māyāpur would always go over its budget, and the balance of the collection would go to Vṛndāvana, which had to be happy with that. Still, I can confess that some years the Vṛndāvana temple made a profit from the festival fees and lived for the next two or three months off that profit.

## Varnāśrama-dharma Discussed

Śrīla Prabhupāda’s morning walks were as invigorating and enlightening as his lectures. His *sannyāsīs* made a joint effort to inquire from Prabhupāda about establishing *varṇāśrama-dharma.*

Śrīla Prabhupāda started his walk on March 12 by saying, “Everywhere, wherever we have got a center, a *varṇāśrama* college should be established. ... The most intelligent men should preach, read books and instruct, take care of the deity worship and the temple, and another class should be strong managers, seeing that things are going on nicely—that everyone is engaged, not just eating and sleeping.”

Hṛdayānanda Goswami asked, “Should devotees be formally designated with a particular occupation?”

Prabhupāda said, “No. Devotees are devotees. But for management of material things, we have to divide. Just like in the body, there are divisions. Kṛṣṇa was acting as a *kṣatriya.* In His boyhood He was acting like a *vaiśya.* But Kṛṣṇa is neither *kṣatriya* nor *vaiśya.* So devotees are like that. They may act in any position, but they are above all the material conceptions of life. Their position as Vaiṣṇavas is the same, but for the sake of management, one is cleaning, one is seeing to the construction, one is preaching—like that.”

**Ātreya Ṛṣi Dāsa:** “So in our movement the leaders must decide how every devotee and every resource is engaged.”

**Prabhupāda: “**That is leadership—which man is fit for which work. But the first business is to engage everyone. Nobody should be without engagement. An idle brain is the devil’s workshop, and the devil is *kāminī-kāñcana:* women and money. So if you remain idle, then you shall think of the devil.... If our men are not prepared to do the plowing work—Tamal Krishna Mahārāja—then what is the use of purchasing land?”

**Tamal Krishna Goswami:** “They are not prepared.”

**Prabhupāda:** “Then you have to engage laborers and spend two hundred rupees per head at least, including salary and food, and the production is nil. In this way, there must be ten thousand, twenty thousand expenditure. Am I right or not, that you bring money some way, from anywhere, and—*Let us spend lavishly*! What kind of management is that? We should consider that the money, after all, is earned with hard labor. So somebody will bring money earned with hard labor, and somebody else will spend it like an irresponsible prince? This should be stopped. That is management. . . .

“People are against religion because they think that it means to make a class of men simply idle—opiate of the people. But, actually, if we show that we are producing, we are managing, we are educating, then we can counteract the communist tendency. They are feeling like that. But if you show that you are actually doing something ideal, then they will appreciate. Show ideal life, not idle life.

“As you are making counter propaganda against *māyā, māyā* will make propaganda against you, very strong. So unless you become a very sincere devotee—*daivī hy eṣā guṇa-mayi mama māyā duratyayā*—then it will not be possible to conquer over *māyā.* You’ll succumb. In India the *brāhmaṇa* class said, ‘I am born in a *brāhmaṇa* family; why shall I work?’ Therefore the whole society has gone to hell.”

“In your country still, people accept any kind of work. It doesn’t matter. And here in India if someone happens to be a *brāhmaṇa,* he’ll not take work like plowing. He’ll not agree. The land is lying fallow. There is no production. A *kṣatriya* will go to the office and fight with the pen as a *kṣatriya.* Instead of studying the *Vedas,* a *brāhmaṇa* will study the rules and regulations given by the office, but he’ll not come to plow—therefore this scarcity of food. He’ll go to a mill to work as an ordinary laborer. So much land we see lying unused. And they are crying for grain.”

## Not Impressed

An American devotee named Puṇḍarīka Dāsa had taken a year to memorize every verse in the *Bhagavad-gītā.* He introduced himself to Dhanañjaya and said, “As far as I know, I am the only disciple of Prabhupāda who’s memorized every single verse, and I want to meet Prabhupāda.” Dhanañjaya took him in to see Prabhupāda, who looked at Dhanañjaya and said, “Make sure he does something practical while he is here.”

## Sincerity Is the Real Qualification

Śrīla Prabhupāda continued giving class from the *Caitanya-caritāmṛta, Ādi-līlā,* picking up where he’d left off in Māyāpur: “Caitanya Mahāprabhu was a great scholar, no doubt, but He presented Himself as a great fool, abiding by the decision of his spiritual master. The real necessity is that one should be humble and meek and follow the instruction of the spiritual master. This is the real qualification.

“You simply have to become very sincere and serious,” Prabhupāda reminded his disciples. “Rūpa Gosvāmī said that the only price is *laulyam:* eagerness. You must be very, very eager to see the lotus feet of Kṛṣṇa in this life—by service! Eagerness for service can be acquired by anyone, simply by sincerity.”

“But this eagerness to achieve Kṛṣṇa’s mercy, to approach the lotus feet of Kṛṣṇa in this life, this kind of determination to do anything, sacrifice anything—this is not very easily obtained. Kṛṣṇa is not so cheap. You may show yourself as a great *sannyāsī* or Vaiṣṇava or whatever it may be, but as long as you engage in sinful activities, there is no question of Kṛṣṇa consciousness.”

“ ... When a disciple becomes perfect in spiritual advancement, the spiritual master feels very, very happy: ‘I am a nonsense, but this boy has followed my instruction and achieved the success. That is my success.’ So Caitanya Mahāprabhu says that when He chanted and danced and cried in ecstasy, His spiritual master thanked Him and told Him: ‘You go on. You dance. You sing and chant in the society of devotees.’”

“So our Kṛṣṇa consciousness society is a society of devotees. Never try to go away. Discrepancies may be there. You should adjust. This chanting and dancing within the society of devotees has got great advantage, great value. A devotee doesn’t want to go back to Godhead. That is not his desire. ‘When Kṛṣṇa desires, Kṛṣṇa will allow me.’ Otherwise, ‘Let me go on in this way, in the society of devotees. Chanting and dancing is my business.’ This is required. Not anything else.”

Classes like this really pleased the disciples of Prabhupāda. He created an amazing spiritual mood among them.

**Annadā Dāsī:** “You didn’t notice any ego when you were around Prabhupāda. Your false ego just disappeared—like you were relieved of a burden. Everyone was intensely focused on Prabhupāda and pleased in his presence. You didn’t think in terms of me or him or her. You were happy just to be there.”

## Parikramās to Mathura, Gokula, and Dauji

After morning *prasāda,* the devotees would pile into buses for *parikramā.* Our leader, Gurudāsa Dāsa, was full of energy, and he planned extensive programs. Bhīma Dāsa said, “I probably went to more places that week in Vṛndāvana than I can add up in all the time that I have spent there since. We went *everywhere.”*

We visited Janma-bhūmi, in Mathura. We visited a small room underneath the mosque that is the prison cell where Kṛṣṇa appeared. We took *darśana* of Keśava-deva, a deity installed by Vajranābha, Kṛṣṇa’s great-grandson. Lord Caitanya also saw this deity.

A first-timer has a different attitude than a second- or third-timer. The first-timers are slower. They want to see details, and smell, touch, and stay in a place longer than is conducive to the running of a large *parikramā.*

**Jayādvaita Swami:** Vṛndāvana *parikramās* were always rush, rush, rush. Three hundred people trying to see through a doorway—that sort of thing. You don’t get any impression of anything except a lot of shoulder blades. The leader is saying, “This is the place.” And the stragglers come late and ask, “What’s that?” Then the leader says that it’s time to go.

Crossing the Yamunā, we traveled eight kilometers to Gokula, the town where Kṛṣṇa and Balarāma enacted childhood pastimes to attract all living entities. Here we really saw the *Kṛṣṇa* book come alive. There was nowhere to turn without seeing a site connected to Kṛṣṇa’s adventures. Pūtanā, Tṛṇāvarta, and Śakaṭāsura were all killed by Kṛṣṇa in this area.

From Gokula, singing *“Jaya* Rādhe, *Jaya* Kṛṣṇa, *Jaya* Vṛndāvana,” we walked for a kilometer to Brahmāṇḍa-ghāṭa. This is where mother Yaśodā made Kṛṣṇa open His mouth so that she could see whether or not He had eaten dirt. When she looked into His mouth, she saw the *brahmāṇḍa,* the entire cosmic creation. People there sell tiny “sweets” made of this dirt, and we ate some, as a re-enactment of this pastime. We also climbed down wide steps to the sacred water and put the customary three drops on our heads. We rested there, taking in the undisturbed serenity.

The *parikramā buses* then took us another ten kilometers to Dauji, a town built around a colorful Balarāma temple. The word *“dau”* is a Vrajavāsī word meaning “elder brother,” and *“ji”* is a term of endearment. The deities are a strikingly beautiful six-foot-tall Balarāma and His wife Revatī.

**Jayādvaita Swami:** When we came back from *parikramās,* Prabhupāda wanted to know everything we’d done. He asked many questions: “Who told you you could go there? Why did you go there? Who did you associate with?” He was concerned, because devotees would come back with their black Rādhā-kuṇḍa *tilaka* and black neckbeads from Rādhā-kuṇḍa. Prabhupāda wasn’t impressed that the devotees tried artificially to become Vrajavāsīs. We went on *parikramās* in groups, and Prabhupāda was very concerned that individual devotees could be picked off.

## Kṛṣṇa’s Invitation Recalled

Śrīla Prabhupāda had noticed a Sītā-Rāma *kīrtana* going on constantly across the way from Fogal Ashram, and he reminded us that in Vṛndāvana we should try to understand Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa. He put Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa-līlā in proper perspective during the class on March 15. Pradyumna read the *Caitanya-caritāmṛta* verse: “Lord Śrī Kṛṣṇa, the Supreme Personality of Godhead, is the supreme enjoyer in the *rāsa* dance. He is the leader of the damsels of Vraja, and all others are simply His associates.”

Prabhupāda said, “Kṛṣṇa’s business is *rāsādi-vilāsa:* dancing with the *gopīs.* He has no other business. People try to imitate Kṛṣṇa by dancing in ballrooms and nightclubs. Since we are part and parcel of Kṛṣṇa, we have the same spirit of dancing. But because we are in the material world, the material covering has perverted the dancing propensity. Our dancing and Kṛṣṇa’s dancing are not the same. This perverted reflection inebriates and frustrates us. But in the dancing of Kṛṣṇa there is no inebriation, no frustration, because it is not false. It is *real*—*cid-vilāsa*: He is the enjoyer of the spiritual potency.”

Śrīla Prabhupāda was enlightening us about our whole purpose in visiting Vṛndāvana.

“Those who come to Vṛndāvana should try to understand Kṛṣṇa. Kṛṣṇa exhibits His *līlā* here personally so that you may hear about Him and become attracted. ‘Oh, how can I go and play with Kṛṣṇa? How can I go and dance with Kṛṣṇa? How can I develop paternal affection for Kṛṣṇa? How can I become a tree or a blade of grass in Vṛndāvana so that Kṛṣṇa may trample over me?’ This is called *kṛṣṇa-prema.* Kṛṣṇa demonstrates His activities to give facility to the conditioned soul. This is Kṛṣṇa’s business. ‘Why don’t you come back to me and dance with Me? Why you are rotting in this rotting dance? You want association. You want sporting. Why don’t you come to Me and take part in My sporting with the cowherd boys?’ This is the invitation of Kṛṣṇa. So anyone with intelligence will try to enter into the transcendental pastimes of the Lord.”

## Chapter 6. The 1975 Māyāpur Festival

Śrīla Prabhupāda toured the world between late January and mid­March: Hong Kong, Tokyo, Honolulu, Mexico City, Caracas, American cities, then London, Tehran, and Bombay. The fourth annual Māyāpur festival started March 23 and officially lasted five days, but Prabhupāda and the devotees stayed in Māyāpur past the first week of April. This year’s pilgrimage promised to be special because of the Krishna-Balaram Mandir opening on April 20. Śrīla Prabhupāda invited all his disciples, and sent letters to friends and acquaintances in India, requesting them to join him for these two celebrations.

Śrīla Prabhupāda stayed for more than two weeks in both Māyāpur and Vṛndāvana, and he attended a one-week program in Hyderabad sandwiched in between. This gave the devotees more than five weeks of his transcendental association. By 1975 it had become standard practice for temples to send devotees to the festivals. Devotees came to be purified and to preach in India as Prabhupāda’s white elephants. The festivals increased their faith, and with greater faith and renewed enthusiasm they could return to their countries to preach and perform devotional service. So the GBC men led to the festival the devotees from North and South America, Europe, Asia, Australia, and Africa. *Kīrtanas* surged to new heights in Māyāpur with Śrīla Prabhupāda’s introduction of a tradition continued to this day: circumambulating the altar and ringing the bells.

## Getting There Was Half The Fun

The majority of devotees at the festival were Americans, and most were book distributors who’d worked hard all year and wanted to claim their transcendental reward. Rāmeśvara Dāsa, a North American BBT trustee, had talked some devotees into staying back on the plea of maintaining the book distribution, but when Śrīla Prabhupāda heard about it, he reprimanded him, saying that coming to the *dhāmas* is as essential as eating.

From New York, the devotees chartered an Air India 747 named *Emperor Ashok.* The journey was organized by Kīrtirāja Dāsa and Mahendra Dāsa in such a way that devotees joined the flight on stopovers in London and Frankfurt. The stewardesses wore saris (nothing special for them) and greeted the devotees respectfully. Sweet Indian flute music played over the plane’s speakers as all the passengers took seats. The devotees brought huge amounts of *prasāda* for the journey.

In 1975, the German devotees needed special encouragement because the government had confiscated their funds and they faced much public hostility, so Prabhupāda requested the North American BBT to sponsor twenty-five airplane tickets for them. Nikhilānanda Dāsa, a seventeen-year-old *brahmacārī* translating Prabhupāda’s books into German, was one recipient of the BBT’s generosity. For some reason his ticket stated that he should depart from London’s Heathrow Airport, so he went to London by train and ferry.

**Nikhilānanda Dāsa:** Heathrow’s transit hall was a transcendental sight because the American devotees began distributing the latest BTG while waiting for the plane to be serviced. There were happy, smiling devotees everywhere.

About fifty devotees boarded the plane at Heathrow, but some arrived almost too late for the flight. The airline manager was quite upset, but the late arrivals pacified him with *samosās.*

It was like boarding a celestial airplane taking us to Vaikuṇṭha, because we knew we were going to the holy *dhāmas.* The airplane’s walls even had pictures of Kṛṣṇa playing His flute.

The next stop was Frankfurt. About eighty percent of the passengers so far were devotees, and everywhere there was chanting, *Kṛṣṇa-kathā,* or honoring of *prasāda,* as if the festival had already started. In Frankfurt, more *prasāda* was brought onto the plane, and everybody feasted during the flight. Although sitting through the eight-hour journey was difficult, devotees had the perfect remedy for boredom: *saṅkīrtana* stories and current topics about the growth of the movement. They obtained permission to give a half-hour class over the public address system. The other passengers had no choice but to listen, and during the flight they were also blessed with almost three hundred devotees chanting *japa.* At *maṇgala-ārati* time, Haṁsadūta led a boisterous *kīrtana.* The devotees jubilantly sang, which startled the stewardesses. The plane vibrated with the holy names.

**Kavicandra Swami:** Haṁsadūta started to dance, and others got out of their seats and began to jump up and down and left and right, doing a wild dance in the aisles. The jet was visibly affected. The captain turned on signs requesting passengers to fasten their seat belts. He announced he was losing control of the plane. The *kīrtana* continued unabated, but after he made repeated requests the devotees surrendered and continued singing in their seats.

After landing in Bombay, the devotees took a domestic flight to Calcutta. Chartered buses were waiting to take them to Māyāpur. Vaidyanātha Dāsa said that as soon as he stepped off the plane, his cold fingertips, still frigid from the German winter, immediately warmed up. Gopi-parānadhana Dāsa was twenty, a new Sanskrit proofreader at ISKCON Press in Brooklyn, taking his first plane ride ever and even making his first trip out of America. What an auspicious first flight!

**Gopi-parānadhana Dāsa:** I will never forget the sight of little children coming up to us at the buses, calling out “Hare Kṛṣṇa” and begging for food and money. They received our leftover *prasāda.* Some children didn’t even have legs. They looked almost like a different species.

**Smita Kṛṣṇa Swami:** A few devotees got right back on another plane and headed home. They flipped out at the airport. Those who stayed endured the culture shock because of their attachment to Prabhupāda.

**Saudāmanī Dāsi (the wife of Ravīndra-svarūpa Dāsa):** At the front of my bus, a devotee read aloud sections of the *Caitanya-caritāmṛta.* As we got out into the country, the scenery became beautiful. I was struck by the difference between colors in West Bengal and America. There is so much red in the earth. And the sky is a lofty blue—the kind you get by painting with watercolors—whereas in the West it’s a heavier blue, as if it were painted with oils.

As the buses drove through a village near Māyāpur, Saudāmanī saw a boy sitting close to the road in the dust, playing a tiny *mṛdaṅga.* For her, this was a firsthand experience of how spiritual training is imbibed in childhood.

**Saudāmanī Dāsi:** When we took the turn off the highway where you actually take the road to the *dhāma,* without any kind of intellectual effort it just came to me—an overwhelming feeling of coming home. It was like coming home from school when your mom has baked cookies and encountering those familiar smells and feelings. I’d never— in this lifetime—smelled Māyāpur’s aromas or seen the place, but still I had a feeling of coming home. I felt happy to be there.

**Mahāmāyā Dāsi (from her book,** ***Śrīla Prabhupāda Is Coming*****!):** Although usually I was not openly emotional, as we got close to Māyāpur I choked up. I was on the verge of tears while watching the beautiful countryside from the bus window, thinking, “Soon I’ll be at Lord Caitanya’s birthplace, which probably looks much the same as it did when He appeared there and started the Hare Kṛṣṇa movement.” I tried to hide my emotions. But I became overwhelmed when someone shouted, “Look! Māyāpur!” and I finally saw the saffron-colored Lotus Building across the rice fields. ISKCON Māyāpur! It was like love at first sight, and I wanted to stay forever. A few weeks wasn’t going to be enough.

By Kṛṣṇa’s mercy, I was again allowed to attend the festival with the Bombay devotees. This year we were in for a big surprise on the train journey. As we rode from Calcutta to Krishnanagar, eagerly anticipating our arrival, our fun was temporarily put on hold. Suddenly a deafening screech of brakes filled the air and we were thrown against the train walls. I lost all sense of direction, not knowing up from down. The lights went out, and people were screaming and calling for their loved ones. The train came to rest at a forty-five-degree angle. It’d been derailed.

Luggage and bodies lay all over the place. People began climbing out of the train and struggling to find their luggage as the sun set. Everyone managed to get out of the train, and fortunately our group suffered only a few cuts and bruises. Even more fortunately, a local train came by and stopped to pick up the stranded passengers. Traveling by the Indian railways is always a crowded experience. Imagine what it was like when one trainload of people got into an already full train—just people on top of people. Sometimes one must execute austerity to get the mercy of the dhāma—no pain, no gain. We reached Māyāpur safely at about 10 p.m.

## Prabhupāda’s Arrival Signals the Beginning

Śrīla Prabhupāda left the Calcutta temple early in the morning on March 23, accompanied by his servant and other disciples. Five cars followed his. Four cars carried *sannyāsīs,* and the fifth carried Pisimā and other ladies. The caravan was a colorful sight. Strands of marigolds in crisscross patterns decorated all the cars. Prabhupāda stopped at the mango grove for Ekādaśī breakfast *prasāda.* Then he quickly resumed his journey. The Gaura Pūrṇimā festival would officially begin when he arrived at the front gate.

**Jayapatākā Swami:** Before Prabhupāda’s expected arrival time, a few devotees went to the roof of the Lotus Building with binoculars and tried to spot Prabhupāda’s maroon ambassador coming down the road. We didn’t have walkie-talkies, so when they saw his car, they lit firecrackers. This made a big, exciting sound that we could hear at the front gate—the signal that Prabhupāda was arriving.

**Saudāmanī dāsī:** We were all going to meet Prabhupāda at the gate, but I wasn’t waiting there because I don’t handle the sun well. I went to stand in the shade of the temple. You weren’t allowed in the temple because it was immaculately clean. The devotees had stood big banana trees on both sides of the altar. Then I heard shouting from the roof: *Prabhupāda is coming!* You could see these cars up the road. I watched the grand welcome from a distance. Then the cars drove right up to the temple. There were so many devotees and a really rough *kīrtana.*

Perhaps the best part of the Māyāpur festival was greeting and receiving Śrīla Prabhupāda. His car slowly approached the temple, with the *kīrtana* procession in pursuit. The car doors opened, Prabhupāda gracefully emerged, and the sound of the *Govindam* prayers began booming from the temple speakers.

The devotees welcomed Prabhupāda with flower garlands. Then he walked to the altar. He offered obeisances to Lord Caitanya and Śrī Śrī Rādhā-Mādhava, then stood with folded hands. Prabhupāda took his place on the *vyāsāsana* and said that it is not a small thing to come from a distance of ten thousand miles. He thanked the devotees for coming both to the Māyāpur festival and to the grand opening of the Vṛndāvana temple. Afterward, in his room, he spoke with leaders.

“It is all the mercy of Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura,” he said. “He wanted Europeans and Americans to come here. Before your coming, he predicted that somebody will bring them. Maybe that *somebody* I am \[*chuckling*\]*.* So—there is no scarcity of space for all the devotees?”

Prabhupāda was told that everyone had a room, and Jayapatākā Swami added, “Without the new dwellings along the front wall, there would have been no hope.”

Śrīla Prabhupāda replied, “Therefore I said you must complete them and that whatever amount is required, I shall pay \[*laughter*\]. I could analyze the need. So, anyway, Kṛṣṇa has given us a nice place. Stay here.”

Prabhupāda asked whether local devotees were growing fruits and grains. He talked about ripe and unripe bananas, and Jayapatākā Swami knew the local words for them because he was learning Bengali.

## The Dhāma’s Development Surges Ahead

The Māyāpur property—surrounded by rice fields, small banana groves, and lower gardens—had been further developed since the 1974 festival. The Lotus Building was now finished and painted saffron. Bas-relief lotus flowers—painted white and red—adorned each of the many arches along the verandas on the three upper floors. A pond had been dug opposite the *bhajan-kuṭīr,* and the temple now kept several more cows. Bamboo fencing lined the main path through the fields, from the front gate to the Lotus Building.

On either side of the front gate, all along the front of the property, was a new boundary wall parallel to the road. It served more than the purpose of providing security. Inside the wall, small rooms had been built along its entire length. They were used for housing, storage, and as workrooms for handlooms.

Inside the wall, too, at the far right end, was a new *prasāda* pavilion with a high roof that could hold about a thousand people. ISKCON Food Relief was the name for our program of serving nutritious *kicharī* to local people on weekends. Prabhupāda said that his godbrothers had been in Māyāpur for years and still their relationship with the local people was poor, but that good relationships could easily be made by distributing nice *prasāda.* While touring the pavilion Prabhupāda said that artists should paint two gigantic pictures on its high walls: Lord Kṛṣṇa taking *prasāda* and Lord Caitanya distributing it.

Attached to the pavilion was a big kitchen, where cooks also prepared the daily meals for residents and guests. Bhavānanda Mahārāja was the commander for *prasāda* distribution during the festivals and shouted commands like a captain: *Come here! Bring that bucket! Serve this line!* Everyone was impressed with his ability to organize the serving.

## Close Company, but No Complaints

This was the best-attended Māyāpur festival yet, and accommodations were the best ever, but there were insufficient facilities for so many devotees. Kavicandra Mahārāja remembers that he was part of a group of twelve devotees who were given a small room along the front wall. They couldn’t all fit in the room, even standing up. In general, many devotees slept outside, but no one complained. Vaidyanātha Dāsa said, “Living conditions were austere, but not much more austere than the *brahmacārī āśramas* in our German temples. Dust would blow into the rooms under the doors every day, but there were nice water pumps for taking bath and washing the dust off our bodies.”

Gopi-parānadhana slept on the veranda outside his room. He received a nasty electrical shock while trying to tie his mosquito net to an outlet. During his first week in Māyāpur, he asked Rūpānuga Dāsa, his GBC, if he could go home. “No, no, no. You can’t. Prabhupāda is here, and you have to attend the opening of the temple in Vṛndāvana,” Rūpānuga said.

The ladies’ quarters were above the big kitchen. Since one room was quite hot, its occupants slept on the roof. One day these ladies realized that their room was especially warm because it was directly over the huge cooking stoves, which radiated heat day and night.

A shop was set up so that visitors did not have to go to Navadvīpa to obtain their basic necessities, but it was in its first year of operation and understocked.

**Saudāmanī Dāsi:** It was a big thing if you could get a bucket because you needed a bucket for washing and bathing. The shop didn’t have enough. It was also a big thing to loan your bucket, because you didn’t always get it back. Devotees would leave their watch with you if they wanted to borrow your bucket. There were a lot of bucket thefts.

No one knew Bengali. If we went across the river by boat and up the hill on the other side, we had to pay a fare to get into the town, and since we didn’t speak the language we didn’t know what it was all about, so we got confused. The merchants and local people perceived us as rich Westerners. Devotees got discouraged going there. So it really became a matter of learning to depend on Kṛṣṇa and using whatever was available in our compound.

## Jump High, Catch His Eye

After daybreak, waiting for Śrīla Prabhupāda to come down the steps of the Lotus Building, the *gurukula* boys, led by Hare Kṛṣṇa Dāsa (the son of the musician Murāri Hari Dāsa), would chant by the stairs. Those were some of the sweetest *kīrtanas* ever, and Śrīla Prabhupāda was very pleased with them. Prabhupāda requested that the *gurukula* boys learn and sing the Bengali verses from his *Gītār-gān,* his poeticized version of the *Bhagavad-gītā.*

All the devotees would stand waiting to see Śrīla Prabhupāda as he walked down. When he arrived, they offered obeisances. If he was going on a walk, they followed Prabhupāda briefly. Everyone was just thinking of Prabhupāda and waiting for his next move, hoping to catch a glance or a smile from him.

**Jayapatākā Swami:** Sometimes Prabhupāda had me lead the way through the fields on the small dirt paths. Prabhupāda exaggerated and called them highways—”Jayapatākā Swami knows which highway to take.” So I had to find the paths between the fields.

**Subhāga Swami:** During one walk Prabhupāda noticed a Western couple sitting in a public place and asked them not to sit together because the *brahmacārīs* would probably get disturbed and the guests would misunderstand and criticize. He said at home they can sit together, but it is improper Vaiśṇava etiquette to do so in public, especially in the *dhāma.*

Walking with Prabhupāda, the devotees often presented the viewpoints of materialists, and he would contend with their arguments.

**Prabhupāda:** This grass is coming out of the earth. Wherefrom it is coming? Who has put the chemicals? And the eggs of the birds, they are produced in the womb of the birds, and from there the life is coming.

**Pañcadraviḍa Swami**: Well, that’s just a complex chemical Reaction. We can’t explain it yet.

**Prabhupāda**: “Can’t explain” means you are fool “We shall see in the future.” Why “future”? What about now?

**Pañcadraviḍa Swami**: Well, a hundred years ago we couldn’t make a movie camera, either.

**Prabhupāda:** That’s all right. But you cannot make life. A hundred years ago people could not produce electricity. That is craftsmanship. A hundred years ago, you could not produce a motorcar. But that does not mean that now you have become God, you rascal.

**Pañcadraviḍa Swami:** Well, isn’t God just a craftsman also?

**Prabhupāda:** Yes, He is everything. He’s everything—but not a fool like you.

Prabhupāda would arrive back at the temple to a crowd of devotees singing *kīrtana.* When he reached the temple, the *gurukulīs* threw flowers at his feet.

**Vaiśeṣika Dāsa:** We would watch Prabhupāda come across the field after his walks, not long after the sun had come up, and we had *kīrtana* waiting for him. He would stop and smile at our *kīrtana* group. He’d enlisted us in his service, and we’d helped him do a lot—like distributing many books and building the new temple in Vṛndāvana. All of us gave up our material activities, like the *gopīs* did when they heard Kṛṣṇa’s flute. Then Kṛṣṇa, the supreme mystic, engaged us in fulfilling Prabhupāda’s desires.

Śrīla Prabhupāda entered the temple to greet the deities, and the devotees followed him inside. Everyone paid obeisances. Because he was always on time, Prabhupāda didn’t have to wait long before a *pūjārī* appeared and blew a conch, and drew the curtain. The *kīrtana* hall resounded with *“govindam ādi puruṣam”* as Prabhupāda again paid obeisances and everyone else also fell flat on the marble floor. After standing up, he would lovingly gaze at the deities. Sometimes he’d sing the *Govindam* prayers and sometimes not. At the end of the prayers Prabhupāda again offered obeisances.

Next Jananivāsa approached Prabhupāda: *“Caraṇāmṛta,* Prabhupāda?” Prabhupāda might nod his head, and Jananivāsa would give him some. Jananivāsa would then pour some water over his hand, and a *sannyāsī* would give him a cloth to dry his hand.

After kindly receiving *guru-pūjā,* Śrīla Prabhupāda would chant*”Jaya* Rādhā-Mādhava” with so much feeling that all the devotees would feel the spiritual potency. When Prabhupāda chanted, there was no comparison. It wasn’t a question of a powerful voice, but heartfelt intent. Everyone would be affected. It was just like being hit by a wave of devotion.

**Mahābuddhi Dāsa:** When Prabhupāda chanted, he put that chanting into our hearts, and that was one way he gave all of us mercy in Māyāpur. We felt his desire to be merciful to us—an extra spark in his smiles and glances and kind words—and we took that special feeling with us and lived off his loving dealings the rest of the year.

Again this year Śrīla Prabhupāda gave classes on the *Caitanya-caritāmṛta,* starting with the very first text. He delivered lectures seriously and sometimes in a thundering voice: “Śrīla Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmi is a very, very advanced devotee and scholar. He is not an ordinary human being. He was empowered by Madana-Mohana.” Devotees remember him stressing in his lectures the importance of coming to the holy *dhāmas.* We could see how pleased Śrīla Prabhupāda felt in the association of his enthusiastic disciples from all over the world.

After class Prabhupāda stepped off his *vyāsāsana* to take another *darśana.* An enthusiastic *kīrtana* accompanied him to the altar. He circumambulated Śrī Śrī Rādhā-Mādhava, and this year he stopped on each side of the altar to ring a new large bell. The two bells produced slightly different rings. When he returned to the deities, he stood before Them in a joyful mood, then would spin around and continue circumambulating. Every time he went around, Prabhupāda would ring the bell on the side of the altar in rhythm with the *kīrtana,* pause in front of the deities, spin, and keep walking. The devotees were surprised at first, but when Prabhupāda started ringing each bell for a good minute or two, the devotees became thrilled at Prabhupāda’s participation in the *kīrtana.*

Smiling and letting go of the bell rope, he would continue walking, and many devotees competed to catch the rope. He had a special way of letting the cord slide out of his hand, and the devotees would jump over one another to give it a yank or two.

When Prabhupāda approached a rope, the devotees competed to be the one to hand it to him. The largest group of devotees was right behind Prabhupāda, so he turned and faced them, ringing the bell­—*dong, dong, dong*—in rhythm with the *kīrtana.* The louder Prabhupāda rang the bell, the higher the devotees jumped. The *kīrtana* reached newer and newer crescendos, with devotees leaping wildly into the air, *mṛdaṅga* men jumping, and devotees clashing whompers and *karatālas.* As Prabhupāda held the rope with one hand, he waved his free hand upward, signaling the devotees to dance and jump higher. These *kīrtanas* were one of a kind. Photographers raced around trying to capture these rare moments and the beautiful smiling faces of Prabhupāda and the devotees.

All the known *kīrtana* leaders of the day gave their best to keep our feet from touching the ground. We always performed ecstatic *kīrtanas* in our temples, on *nagar kīrtanas,* and during Ratha-yātrā, but these Māyāpur festival *kīrtanas* were different. Śrīla Prabhupāda was not only present, but also actively inspiring and encouraging us to dance, urging us to forget everything and chant Kṛṣṇa’s holy names. Feeling the reciprocation from our beloved spiritual master, we used all our youthful energy to dance and jump high, to catch his eye and please him. Prabhupāda was full of pleasure, and seeing Prabhupāda’s pleasure increased ours, thereby increasing Prabhupāda’s pleasure again. Neither’s pleasure could be defeated by the others. In this transcendental competition there were no losers.

**Jayādvaita Swami:** Each time he rang the bell, devotees would go berserk. It was like a spontaneous, ecstatic insanity that everybody went into. There would be shouting and more thunderous *kīrtana.* We had never seen anything like that. We’d never even done *parikramā* around the deities before, because that facility wasn’t there in our temples.

**Nirañjana Swami:** I’ll never forget those *kīrtanas* with Prabhupāda ringing the bells. He made us behave like madmen. Those *kīrtanas* resembled those pastimes of Lord Caitanya in which great devotees were unconcerned what other people thought of them, because they were totally absorbed in chanting and dancing.

Finally, Prabhupāda offered his obeisances and went upstairs to his quarters. The *kīrtana* continued, the atmosphere now spiritually surcharged. During the day, devotees talked about the *kīrtana,* awaiting the next morning to relive the experience. No one wanted to miss it.

## Prabhupāda Fully Absorbed

One day Prabhupāda became silent before ending his morning lecture—something he had done a few times before in other places. Mahāmāyā Dāsi said, “The devotees felt privileged to see his highly advanced state of spiritual absorption.”

“Kṛṣṇa is explaining Himself in the *Bhagavad-gītā*,” said Śrīla Prabhupāda. “Kṛṣṇa is sending His representative, the *ācārya*, to teach you. And Kṛṣṇa is within you, trying to teach you if you are actually serious. Then where is the difficulty? Inside, outside, always—books, knowledge—He is prepared. So where is the difficulty to make yourself perfect in Kṛṣṇa consciousness? There is no difficulty at all. Provided you are serious, you can become fully Kṛṣṇa conscious in this very life. You are all young men. You are not an old man like me. I have no opportunity.” With these words he fell silent for some time.

**Sunitā Dāsi:** It was like time stood still. You could hear a few birds chirping outside. Hundreds of disciples sat silently waiting. It was so poignant. Haṁsadūta eventually started a *kīrtana,* and Prabhupāda slowly rocked his head to the beat, picked up his *karatālas,* and played them for a little while.

Naturally, no one wanted to miss Śrīla Prabhupāda’s classes, but the resident devotees needed help preparing breakfast. So—following a rotating schedule—some visiting devotees had to miss class.

## Parikramās to Caitanya Maṭha and Godrumadvīpa

Every day leading up to Gaura Pūrṇimā, devotees were going on *parikramā* after breakfast and returning for lunch *prasāda* in the afternoon. Carrying colorful flags on bamboo poles, our group exited through the main gate, senior devotees in front, followed by the *kīrtana* crew with many *karatālas* and *mṛdaṅgas,* and the rest behind. Everyone sang Hare Kṛṣṇa, Hare Kṛṣṇa, KṛṣṇaKṛṣṇa, Hare Hare, Hare Rāma, Hare Rāma, RāmaRāma, Hare Hare—our international language.

One day the *parikramā* procession went to Caitanya Maṭha, the headquarters of Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvati Ṭhākura’s Gauḍīya Maṭha. His *samādhi* is there, near the temple of Śrī Śrī Gāndharvikā-Giridhāri (his deities), and so is the *samādhi* of Gaura Kiśora Dāsa Bābājī, his Guru Mahārāja.

Another day we crossed the Jalaṅgi in boats, entering Godrumadvīpa, and visited the house and *samādhi* of Śrīla Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura. We climbed some stairs, went inside the house, and stood on the veranda facing ISKCON’s Māyāpur Candrodayā Mandir. This is where he stood and envisioned an astounding temple predicted by Lord Nityānanda.

From there, by walking a short distance, we came to Surabhi Kuñja, where a heavenly cow lived under a banyan tree, giving the area its name *(go* means “cow” and *druma* means “tree”). This cow accompanied Indra to Navadvīpa to worship Lord Gauṝāṅga. She’d earlier accompanied him to Govardhana Hill to pacify Govinda. It was here that Nityānanda Prabhu started *nāma-haṭṭa* preaching, and that Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura later on revived it.

One day Nikhilānanda was returning from a *parikramā,* walking with another German devotee and singing *“hari haraye namaḥ Kṛṣṇa.”* A skinny farmer, attracted by their chanting, walked out of his field and joined them, dancing and laughing. By the time he joined them, they were singing the words *“janame janame hoy ei abhilāṣ”* and he repeated the words with great pleasure. Nikhilānanda said, “I was surprised that this song was known even by a simple man, who may have been illiterate. Maybe the farmer didn’t know the song and he was just repeating our Bengali, but he was chanting with feeling.”

Kavicandra Swami recalled meeting up with an elderly Bengali Vaiṣṇava who tried to teach the white-skinned *sādhus* the right pronunciation of *“haribol.”* He kept shouting, “Not ‘hawrribol’”—which sounds like “horrible” —“but *‘haribol,’*” and he made them repeat the correct pronunciation after him. The only saving grace is that the Lord takes the essence of a devotee’s attitude and is unconcerned with the externals.

The health of the devotees was slightly better than at previous festivals. Cautioned by those who had previously attended, the Western devotees now took preventative measures against sunburn, and got vaccinations for cholera, malaria, and jaundice. A common problem was that devotees drank large amounts of muddy, unfiltered Ganges water and then contracted dysentery. Anyway, the prospects of seeing Śrīla Prabhupāda and attending the opening of the Krishna-Balaram Mandir outweighed the risks. Mahāmāyā Dāsi said that the girls from Germany and Australia out did the other sick girls when it came to doing service. They overcame sickness through hard work, just as Prabhupāda had recommended.

## Bathing In The Ganges

When pilgrims visit a *dhāma,* they shave their heads upon arrival and then take a dip in a holy river.

**Dānavīra Swami:** The first thing we did was get our heads shaved by the barber. There were no barbershops like that in the West—we’d always shaved each other. In Māyāpur we just sat down under a straw roof, paid a few rupees, and the barber shaved us with only water and his straight, edge razor.

The *Agni Purāṇa* says that one is purified of sins simply by seeing, touching, or drinking Ganges water and by putting clay from the Ganges bank on one’s head or body; if someone even chants “Gaṅgā, Gaṅgā,” eight hundred miles away from the Ganges, he goes to Vaikuṇṭha.

Lord Caitanya went to the Gaṅgā as a youngster to disturb the *brāhmaṇas’ pūjā* and to tease the young girls. He later taught his students there, and He swam every day after *kīrtana* and enjoyed water sports with His devotees. In the same manner we relaxed at the Gaṅgā. One of our favorite sports was to choose a partner and ride on his shoulders while fighting with other pairs of devotees.

**Dānavīra Swami:** I chose Brahmānanda as my carrier. No one could lift him. I told him I would be good on top, and we were quite successful.

**Ramāi Swami:** The *sannyāsīs* would climb on each other’s backs and fight with others in the Gaṅgā. There was a lot of camaraderie. We were all disciples of Prabhupāda and all around the same age.

It took fifteen minutes to walk to the Gaṅgā in those days. By the time we reached her, we were sweating and looking forward to a cooling swim. We could swim across the river and back. In the middle we would dive down a few meters where the water was cooler. We’d also sit on the banks, talk, and listen to discourses by Satsvarūpa Dāsa Goswami, Hṛdayānanda Goswami, and others.

**Saudāmanī Dāsi:** In the afternoon almost all of the devotees would go to the Ganges. There was one main path, and at the end of it the boys would go to the right and the girls to the left. The local Bengali ladies or some other residents showed us how to properly wash our clothes by the river and lay them out to dry. It is an offense to use soap in a sacred river or to wash clothes right in the river. You have to draw some water from the river and wash your clothes in a bucket or on a rock on the bank. It was so peaceful. We would all swim across and sit on the other side, then swim back. The bank was muddy, and we could put mud on our skin to draw out toxins. We also learned to put on our saris Bengali style. After spending the late afternoon at the Gaṅgā, we ladies would walk back to the temple for evening *ārati* in one long line. We were told that one evening Prabhupāda had been on the roof watching us return, and that he complimented the girls, saying, “They looked very beautiful as they came back from the Gaṅgā—all clean and fresh, like a group of gopīs returning home.”

**Jananivāsa Dāsa:** Prabhupāda took an unexpected and unusual bath with the sannyāsīs. They were walking down the road, and Prabhupāda decided to go to the Gaṅgā. Once there he told them, “You can take a bath,” but they didn’t have gamchā. “It is all right,” Prabhupāda said, “no one is here. You can bathe in your kaupins.” At that time it was an isolated area, so they started bathing, and only Prabhupāda was wearing a gamchā. There is a picture of them standing around in kaupins. They started sporting and splashing, and Prabhupāda laughed and said, “Caitanya Mahāprabhu did like this, so you can too.” Jayapatākā Swami told us that Prabhupāda then covered his eyes, nose, mouth, and ears with all his fingers, in a kind of bathing *mudrā*, and dipped himself in the water three times. Prabhupāda said, “I would increase my longevity if I could bathe in the Ganges every day.”

## Full House at Darśanas

In the late afternoon, Prabhupāda’s small room was open for *darśana.* However, because hundreds of devotees wanted to go in, there wasn’t enough room.

**Saudāmanī Dāsi:** *Darśanas* weren’t open to everyone. You had to be somebody to get in. There was always a devotee guarding the room, and if you knew that person or someone inside, then you might get invited in. Most of the ladies I knew never even considered asking if they could attend.

Śrīla Prabhupāda reciprocated with the devotees who attended in a way that instructed and encouraged them.

**Bir Kṛṣṇa Goswami:** A book distributor from Caracas came, and I wanted to introduce him to Prabhupāda. During the *darśana,* Prabhupāda was politely acknowledging the reports devotees were making. A few devotees showed him pictures of a new temple, and he nonchalantly looked at them. When I had the opportunity, I said, “Śrīla Prabhupāda, this devotee distributed ten thousand copies of *Bhagavad-gītā.”* Prabhupāda’s eyes opened wide. “Really?” he said, looking at the devotee. “He has the mercy of Kṛṣṇa.” The other devotees were a little taken aback that Prabhupāda was more animated by this report. But, after all, it was ten thousand books. If the number of new temples had been ten, maybe Prabhupāda would have become more animated by that report. Anyway, Prabhupāda then looked around and said to us, “You all have the mercy of Kṛṣṇa. But just as in one sense the *gopīs* are considered higher, so the book distributors are also special.”

If one devotee left the *darśana,* the guard would let in another, always keeping the room at full capacity. Prabhupāda wanted every devotee and guest to receive a *prasāda* sweet before leaving.

**Subhāga Swami:** I had the service of handing out sweets. I sat next to the door, with two jars of either coconut laḍḍus or sesame-and-gur sweets. The sweets were tasty and popular. Some devotees whispered to me as they left, “Please give me a big one,” and sometimes they complained if I divided them and gave them only a piece instead of a whole one. I would reply, “Prabhu, be considerate. There has to be enough for everyone.”

Besides his *darśanas,* Śrīla Prabhupāda held other meetings during the Māyāpur festival. He held a conversation with disciples just to discuss how to preach to the theologians. Prabhupāda suggested to Prajāpati Dāsa, a former theology student, the idea of holding a meeting with all the theologians in America to discuss the nature of God, the next time he visited America. Ravindra-svarūpa Dāsa, a doctoral candidate in religion, later read to Prabhupāda a list of twelve names of God from the Old Testament. Śrīla Prabhupāda replied, “When you have names, that means God is a person. You may have twelve names or twelve thousand names, but when He has a name, He’s a person. Now, our point is: ‘Who is that person?’ We ascertain who is really God.”

Prabhupāda held another meeting with a German girl who intended to write a favorable article about the devotees in Germany. She was a journalist, well informed about Kṛṣṇa consciousness, and she attended the whole festival. She was particularly impressed by a dance performance she had seen the previous night, for she had not realized that one can do just about anything for Kṛṣṇa.

## Dances and Plays

Devotees entertained other devotees with dances and dramas performed on a temporary stage. Śrīla Prabhupāda had written a letter on February 17 to Gurudāsa Dāsa and Prajāpati Dāsa (whose wife was a dancer) expressing a desire to offer performances at the festival:

I request you to prepare different dances (a new one for each day of the Māyāpur festival) from the *Caitanya- caritāmṛta.* I also request you to prepare different dances from the *Kṛṣṇa* book for the Vṛndāvana festival. When you come to India, I shall give you some personal direction concerning these dances. There are many nice stories that can be performed.

The Vaikuṇṭha Players from New York performed *Mahārāja Parīkṣit Punishes Kali* and other scenes from the *Bhāgavatam.* Lokmaṅgala Dāsa and Nandakiśora Dāsa were the main players, and others also did the acting.

Lohitākṣa Dāsa and his wife, Rasajñā Dāsi, headed a dance-and-drama troupe from Los Angeles. The expertise of their realistic performances enthralled everyone.

Gopi-parānadhana had a role in *Parīkṣit Meets Śukadeva Gosvāmi.* Nandakiśora played Śukadeva, and Draviḍa Dāsa played Parikṣit. The group spent all day rehearsing on the bank of the Gaṅgā. Gopi-parānadhana had only one line to speak. That night, Gopi-parānadhana enacted his part as a sage who, prior to the arrival of Śukadeva Gosvāmi, suggests the proper course of action for Mahārāja Parikṣit.

**Gopi-parānadhana Dāsa:** My line was: “My dear Parikṣit Mahārāja, the best thing for you to do is to practice the eightfold meditation system: *aṣṭāṅga-yoga.”* I practiced that line for hours and hours. The play was nicely and seriously performed. Devotees weren’t goofing off. The deities were part of the audience, Prabhupāda was present, and the assembled devotees were there, so we took it seriously.

I was unable to see Prabhupāda’s face or anyone else’s because I wasn’t wearing my glasses. With as much gravity as I could summon, shaking my finger at the king, I spoke my line. I said it seriously, but Prabhupāda thought it was funny. He burst out laughing. Then the devotees around him and the whole audience laughed. That was the beginning and end of my dramatic career.

Svarūpa Dāmodara Dāsa brought dancers from Manipur to perform the *rāsa-līlā* dance as an evening’s entertainment. A chair was set up for Prabhupāda in the middle of the temple room so that he could have the best view. The dancers wore elaborate Manipuri costumes and put on a fine performance, but about halfway through the program, Prabhupāda left.

The Manipuri troupe had danced with their backs to the deities. An organizer of the show later told Prabhupāda that the deities can see everything anyway, and thus it seemed all right for the dancers to be so positioned. Prabhupāda replied that since the deities can see everything, then the show could have been on the roof.

Some devotees said that Prabhupāda said it wasn’t good for *sannyāsīs* to watch ladies dancing, but Prabhupāda did sit through the performances of the Vaikuṇṭha Players, a team that included his female disciples. After a play on April 6, using Mahāprabhu’s example, Prabhupāda instructed the devotees on the standard for dramas:

Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu also introduced these dramatic plays when He was a *gṛhastha* in Navadvīpa, and all the devotees used to see them, but all the parts were played by males. There was no such thing as a female taking a part. This was introduced by the dramatist Girish Chandra Ghosh, imitating the Western way of dramas. In our childhood, we never saw any play performed by a woman. So if you organize *this* kind of play, at least it will be appreciated by the saintly order. The *sannyāsīs can* also see it. But dramas played by women the *sannyāsīs* cannot see. That is prohibited. Caitanya Mahāprabhu showed by His example. There was a musical play by women in the Jagannātha temple. Caitanya Mahāprabhu became ecstatic: “Such nice music is coming from Jagannātha’s temple. Let Me go see.” Then His servant prohibited Him: “Sir, these songs are from a woman.” Mahāprabhu said, “Oh? It is from a woman? Govinda, you have saved My life” \[*laughter*\]. Of course, ordinary visitors— they can see, but *sannyāsīs* and *brahmacārīs* are strictly prohibited from hearing or seeing a woman singing or dancing. In your country it is very difficult. But if the parts are played by males, everyone can see, in front of the deity. There is no harm.

## The GBC Meetings

In a document signed on July 28, 1970, Śrīla Prabhupāda appointed twelve senior devotees to the Governing Body Commission. Later Prabhupāda instructed them that an annual meeting should be held in Māyāpur. The first such meeting was held in 1974, but no records exist of this meeting. At the 1975 Māyāpur festival, however, record-keeping of the annual meetings began.

The GBC members—now fourteen—met in the conference room on the first floor of the Lotus Building. Prabhupāda inspired and guided the meetings by approving or modifying their decisions. He’d instructed the GBC body to review the previous year’s work and to plan the preaching for the coming year—then to stick to the plan. The GBC’s would discuss resolutions during the day and present them to Prabhupāda before lunch or at the end of the day. The passed resolutions were posted daily.

There are recordings of the GBC members reading resolutions to Prabhupāda and hearing what he has to say about them. On one tape, the GBC’s discuss their responsibilities to organize the opening of temples and the training of temple presidents, and to uphold spiritual standards pertaining to worship, financial management, book distribution, and recruitment.

A devotee read, “. . . To ensure that the proper criteria for recommending candidates for initiation be followed.”

Prabhupāda reminded them of the current waiting periods of six months to a year for first initiation and one year more for second initiation. “That’s all,” he said. “If one does not come to the standard, then he’s unfit.”

The reading continued: “The role for the GBC man is to act as the designated representative of Śrīla Prabhupāda to settle all philosophical and procedural disputes not settled at the temple level.”

“Then the GBC should be consulted,” Prabhupāda confirmed, “and if the GBC body cannot answer, then I’ll answer.”

Regarding financial reports, Prabhupāda affirmed the GBC’s view that all centers must submit regular reports on what they have collected and spent.

Besides these zonal responsibilities, said the GBC proposals, a GBC man should have a functional responsibility.

Prabhupāda clarified it: “The main functional responsibility is to go and see that the regular temple work is going on and that the president is doing nicely—to check in this way. You can sit down in the class and see how things are going on. Don’t bring in politics. Then it will be ruined.”

Bringing up the next topic—the serious financial difficulties of the *gurukula*—the GBC men told Prabhupāda they would discuss a proposal that had been made.

“Everyone’s son is there,” Prabhupāda said, “so from every center some contribution should be given to the *gurukula.* But at the same time it should be examined how the *gurukula* is spending money.”

Finally, Prabhupāda was read a pledge that was being discussed: “I state that I have been elected or nominated a member of the Governing Body Commission under the recommendation of my Guru Mahārāja, His Divine Grace A. C. Bhaktivedānta Swami Prabhupāda, founder-*ācārya* of the International Society for Krishna consciousness. I state that I have imposed all my faith, integrity, and honesty in my Guru Mahārāja, with the result that he is the sole responsible person and supreme authority of my present position and status that I have gained and am holding in the International Society for Krishna consciousness. I hereby swear in the name of Kṛṣṇa that I will bear true faith and allegiance to the constitution, by-laws, rules, regulations, and directions, including the four regulative principles. I shall faithfully discharge the duty upon which I am about to enter or have already accepted to that effect. I further pledge that His Divine Grace A. C. Bhaktivedānta Swami is the only source of authority, represented by his instructions and books, and I shall follow him.”

Prabhupāda was satisfied by the pledge and gave a further recommendation: “I think that an oath of allegiance should also be signed by the presidents.”

The meeting was adjourned for lunch.

## Lunch Exemplifies Punctuality

Śrīla Prabhupāda strictly followed his daily schedule no matter where he was, and he made no exceptions at the festivals. In Māyāpur, Prabhupāda would take lunch *prasāda* exactly at one o’clock. If devotees or guests were in the room when the *prasāda* came, Prabhupāda would still stop. He’d just bring the conversation to a close, get up, and walk into his adjoining room to eat. He was punctual.

**Paṇkajāṇghri Dāsa:** Pālikā Mātājī once brought Prabhupāda’s plate a little late. Prabhupāda sent her away, saying, “Take it away. If you can’t be on time, I don’t want it. I was ready to eat at one.” Pālikā left the room crying. And just to teach us a lesson Prabhupāda didn’t eat lunch that day. Prabhupāda later said: “If you’re regular, the body starts to produce acids when it is time to eat, and your mouth starts to water when you see the *prasāda.”*

When Prabhupāda’s plate came out of his room after breakfast, lunch, or at any time, whoever was carrying it would be surrounded by dozens of devotees, all eager to get the mercy. They waited on the veranda and raced like a swarm of bees to a beehive. In the scramble, the plate or a bowl sometimes fell on the floor.

After rising from his afternoon nap at three-thirty or four, Prabhupāda would drink the water of a *dob,* a tender, green coconut. Prabhupāda sometimes used a gold straw. (The straw and a gold-plated glass were a gift, and this set is now kept in his house in Vṛndāvana.) He was sometimes offered watermelon, other fruits, and juice. But even if Prabhupāda agreed to juice, he always took a *dob.*

## Pisimā

Sometimes Pisimā cooked Prabhupāda’s lunch. This year she stayed on the spacious first-floor veranda of the Lotus Building, because all the rooms were filled with devotees and life members. She was content to be there, near the ladies’ bathroom, because it was hard for her to walk. Gāyatrī Dāsi was again her assistant, and as usual carried around Pisimā’s bottle of Gaṅgā *jal.*

Saudāmanī Dāsi said, “Pisimā carried everything in cloth—different things in different small handkerchiefs, all wrapped together in one large cloth. It was really a practical way to carry things.”

**Mahāmāyā Dāsī:** Devotees were thrilled to meet Pisimā.
