Late one afternoon a group of us ladies joined her on the veranda for *bhajans.* Pisimā had a sweet, childlike voice and sang her favorite *bhajan, “Mānasa, Deha, Geha*.” Becoming absorbed in singing, we missed the evening *ārati.* After another day or two of the same schedule, Śrīla Prabhupāda, who’d heard about our extended *bhajans,* asked us to stop in time for the evening *ārati.*

## Pilgrims Flock to Gaura Pūrṇimā

There were five new outfits made for the deities, one for each of the days leading up to and including Gaura Pūrṇimā. The seamstresses had to stay up several nights to finish the sewing, but they weren’t the only ones working overtime. The visiting men followed a rotating schedule to maintain a twenty-four-hour *kīrtana* in the temple. For some, that meant sleepless nights.

On Gaura Pūrṇimā, during his morning walk, Prabhupāda saw Girirāja Dāsa and me and asked us who was taking care of Bombay. Many senior devotees from Bombay had come to Māyāpur. Realizing Śrīla Prabhupāda’s concern, Girirāja later decided that we should return as soon as possible. He and I were the presidents of the Bombay temple, so we left for Bombay the day after Gaura Pūrṇimā. When we arrived, we had to arrange to bring our donors and members to the opening of the Krishna-Balaram Mandir. We had to book their train tickets and do a lot of communications.

But on Gaura Pūrṇimā we all shaved our heads, bathed in the Gaṅgā, fasted, and occupied ourselves until evening. The devotees’ *prasāda* in the evening was very attractive. It unified us. Hundreds of devotees were eating at the same time.

**Ramāi Swami:** We were astounded at how many people came on Gaura Pūrṇimā. Bengalis completely filled the main walkway all day. Visitors streamed in endlessly, starting early in the morning. Many thousands of pilgrims poured into Māyāpur. We’d never seen so many people. It was a shock. For the most part they were also shocked to see us.

If you went outside our gate, the local people would throw dye on you because it was the Holi festival for them. We didn’t have strict control over the people who came into the temple grounds, so there were also people throwing dye inside. Originally, the festival of throwing dyes was connected to the pastimes of Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa, but nowadays this has been forgotten in the name of revelry. It’s now a national festival: Holi Pūrṇimā. Holika is a goddess.

**Mahāmāyā Dāsi:** A busload of devotees went to Calcutta to take part in the Holi parade because Prabhupāda wanted ISKCON represented at the parade. We chanted Hare Kṛṣṇa and danced before huge crowds. We didn’t have a float or banners and flags, but we situated ourselves directly behind another group’s *mūrtis* of Lord Caitanya and Lord Nityānanda. Guess who missed the feast that day? Yes, the Holi parade volunteers! We returned late at night and had to be content with a few *purīs.* No one complained though, because we fulfilled Prabhupāda’s desire to represent ISKCON at the parade.

A main feature of our Gaura Pūrṇimā festivities was the public exhibition of ISKCON’s worldwide activities. Most of the temples set up large photos of their deities, buildings, devotees, and preaching programs. Bharadrāj and Rukmiṇī had their dioramas on display, too. These stands exposed both visitors and devotees to the international dimensions of our movement. Once in a while, a devotee became inspired to serve in another country.

**Ṛddha Dāsa:** I joined ISKCON in Vancouver in 1972, and for three years I distributed many books in North American airports. I came to the festival thinking I could rejuvenate my spiritual life by visiting the holy places and associating with my guru, but I felt the need for a change. Having been born in a Gujarati community in South Africa, I visited the South African stand at the exhibition. The promotions indicated it was a fertile preaching field, though apartheid made it a politically sensitive area. The devotees in charge of South Africa were carefully seeking recruits who could enter the country unhindered and distribute a lot of books. Thus I got the opportunity to go to South Africa, and six months later Śrīla Prabhupāda made his only tour there. I described all this in my book, *Destination South Africa.*

## Godbrothers Decline to Cooperate

After Gaura Pūrṇimā, Śrīla Prabhupāda invited his godbrothers in Māyāpur to the Candrodayā Mandir. They all gathered behind Rādhā-Mādhava’s altar in the temple room, because some of them were too old to climb the steps to Prabhupāda’s quarters. Prabhupāda had the devotees put out backrests and mattresses covered with clean white sheets. His godbrothers arrived in twos and threes, and devotees greeted them and offered them these places to sit. Many devotees crowded around them.

**Jayapatākā Swami:** Śrīla Prabhupāda began discussing how they could cooperate: “By Kṛṣṇa’s grace, I have some money, books, and buses, but I have no preachers. It is my request that I give you buses and books and manpower, and you be the group leaders and take them out and preach, and I will maintain your temples.” Prabhupāda really wanted the Gauḍīya Maṭha to unite, and he gave his godbrothers good reasons to come together, but none of them accepted. He said, “Why are we all separate? We can form a governing body and work as our guru wanted. Śrīla Prabhupāda said we should all jointly preach the message of Rūpa Gosvāmi and Raghunātha Gosvāmi. Now I have enough followers and enough money. I can finish off the temples you have been trying to build for twenty years. I will help you finish them. I will give you a couple of foreigners for your temple so that you will have more preaching potency.

Prabhupāda explained all these things from a position of strength. If he had spoken this way before he started ISKCON, then his godbrothers would have wondered what he had to offer them. But now he was offering everything. Their combination would please Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvati Ṭhākura, and surely something wonderful would come of it.

One of them said, “No, we can’t.” No one agreed. One of them confessed that in his *maṭha* he was a big fish; if he joined us then he would become a little fish in the big ISKCON pond. At least he was honest. Some said they would think about it, but no one came forward and agreed.

As his godbrothers were leaving, Prabhupāda asked that they be given copies of the volume of the *Caitanya-caritāmṛta* containing this passage:

. . . Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura, at the time of his departure, requested all his disciples to form a governing body and conduct missionary activities cooperatively. He did not instruct a particular man to become the next *ācārya*. But just after his passing away, his leading secretaries made plans, without authority, to occupy the post of *ācārya*, and they split into two factions over who the next *ācārya* would be. Consequently, both factions were *asāra*, or useless, because they had no authority, having disobeyed the order of the spiritual master. Despite the spiritual master’s order to form a governing body and execute the missionary activities of the Gauḍīya Maṭha, the two unauthorized factions began litigation that is still going on after forty years with no decision.

Therefore, we do not belong to any faction. But because the two parties, busy dividing the material assets of the Gauḍīya Maṭha institution, stopped the preaching work, we took up the mission of Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura and Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura to preach the cult of Caitanya Mahāprabhu all over the world, under the protection of all the predecessor *ācāryas,* and we find that our humble attempt has been successful.

## The Hyderabad Paṇḍāl Programs

On the morning of April 10, Śrīla Prabhupāda left Māyāpur for Calcutta. He was flying to Hyderabad. About 150 devotees had left by train a couple of days earlier, to be in Hyderabad for his arrival. Another group of devotees—about two hundred—went directly to Vṛndāvana to help prepare for the temple opening.

Prabhupāda wanted the visiting Western devotees to preach in cities other than Māyāpur and Vṛndāvana. He wanted to demonstrate the impressive results of his preaching in the West and remind the Indian people of the importance of Kṛṣṇa consciousness. He also wanted his Western disciples to imbibe Vedic culture during their visits to India.

The devotees reserved two *bogis* (train cars) just for their group. Lots of senior devotees and the Vaikuṇṭha Players were on board. For most devotees, it was a pleasant journey, and they enjoyed seeing India through the windows of a train. Bhāgavata Dāsa arranged *prasāda* and kept the devotees happy with a big supply of *samosās* at every meal. Dīna-bandhu and Nitāi, traveling with their wives, kept the ladies entertained with many stories about Kṛṣṇa and Prabhupāda.

When the train pulled into Rājamandri, Andhra Pradesh, a Gauḍīya Maṭha *sannyāsī* and his assistants boarded the train to feed the devotees. That *sannyāsī* was Śrī Śrīmad Bhakti Vaibhava Puri Mahārāja, Prabhupāda’s godbrother, and Rājamandri was one of his preaching bases. Somehow it had been arranged that he bring several *tokaris* (baskets) of lemon rice and other opulent *prasāda.* He gave everybody a leaf plate and served all the devotees with his own hands—a genuine example of Indian culture and hospitality.

**Bhīma Dāsa:** He was in a cheerful mood, serving us and enjoying our company. When the train started pulling out of the station, Mahārāja couldn’t get off in time. He said, “Never mind,” and continued serving until the train arrived at the next station. Everybody was happy in his association.

**Kavicandra Swami:** The German devotees were especially suffering from the heat and quickly used up all the water in the train’s water tanks to take cool baths. Then at every stop they would quickly bathe under the taps on the platform.

Devotees had packed lots of small books on the train for the journey. Gopī-parānadhana and a few others Western devotees began distributing books on the train and whenever the train stopped. They spoke in English, but the local people were buying.

Hyderabad is the capital of Andhra Pradesh, and Andhra Pradesh stretches along the eastern sea—the Bay of Bengal—surrounded by Orissa in the north, Maharashtra in the west, and Tamil Nadu in the south. When the train pulled into Hyderabad, the tired devotees made their way to the temple. Soon after reaching the temple, they received news that Prabhupāda was arriving, so they rushed to the airport to greet him with flower garlands and *kīrtana.* Prabhupāda clapped and chanted with his disciples in the terminal building. The airport staff and all the other travelers had never seen such an enthusiastic welcome offered by an international group.

G. Pulla Reddy, an active supporter and a life member, hosted Śrīla Prabhupāda and his senior disciples in his house. Mr. Reddy’s sweet shop stood just across the street from our rented temple building, advertising sweets that contained pure cow’s milk and ghee. He’d donated land on which a large ISKCON temple was being built, right behind the rented temple building.

Devotees went on huge *nagar kīrtanas* daily, led by Viṣṇujana Swami. Prabhupāda’s dancing white elephants were attracting large crowds wherever they went. All the while, pamphlets were distributed advertising the evening *paṇḍāls.*

The programs took place in a large grassy park. Thousands of people attended each night, and Acyutānanda Swami led the audiences in *kīrtana.* To the devotees’ surprise, the people joined in the chanting, clapping and waving their hands, and some danced. This was unlike receptions in the West. The devotees sat on stage with Śrīla Prabhupāda when he spoke. He lectured in English and spoke forcefully, challenging people with the idea that although they were learned and cultured, that wasn’t enough—they also needed Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

After Prabhupāda’s talk at one program, a man in the audience asked Prabhupāda, “What about all the starving people in India?”

With confidence Prabhupāda responded, “Bring all the starving people to our temples. We will feed them all with *kṛṣṇa-prasāda.”*

To a related question about overpopulation, Śrīla Prabhupāda answered, “Because no one is going back to Godhead, but instead taking birth again and again, there is no upward mobility. The system is simply getting jammed up.” Everyone applauded.

At the end of the programs, Acyutānanda Swami, in his humorous style, auctioned off items used in the temple or by Śrīla Prabhupāda.

Mr. Reddy invited Śrīla Prabhupāda’s disciples to a daytime program and feast at his house. The devotees walked and chanted in procession to his house, situated in a scenic area overlooking the city. Śrīla Prabhupāda was treated graciously by Mr. Reddy’s relatives and guests. Everyone sat under a decorative *paṇḍāl* to hear Śrīla Prabhupāda speak before taking the feast. Śrīla Prabhupāda took *prasāda* inside the house with the older devotees, and the rest of his disciples sat in lines outside. Mr. and Mrs. Reddy closely supervised the menu and the temporary outdoor kitchen. This was another experience of Indian hospitality that the Western devotees had that year.

**Gopī-parānadhana Dāsa:** We were all sitting in the garden, and Mrs. Reddy and her relatives reverently and gently served the most wonderful *prasāda* we’d ever tasted, including hot *puris* right out of the wok. There were spongy *rasagullās, gulābjamuns,* various types of *sandeśa* with different toppings, and many other sweets. We’d never been served like this in the West.

When Mr. Reddy asked, “Is everyone satisfied?” someone shouted, “We want more sweets.” So Mr. Reddy sent a servant to his sweet shop, assuring the devotees that this was no trouble. Soon the servant returned with two big pots strapped to the back of his bike. The pots contained orange flavored *rasagullās* and *gulābjāmuns.* The devotees ate every single sweet. The men also had a juice-drinking contest and drank all the syrup in which the sweets had been soaking. They became so intoxicated that some were laughing uncontrollably and rolling on the lawn. The devotees could not walk back to their residence after eating so much and had to be driven back in hired buses.

One morning the devotees joined Śrīla Prabhupāda at an undeveloped farm property less than an hour’s drive from Hyderabad. This land belonged to ISKCON. When the devotees’ bus arrived, Prabhupāda was already sitting in the shade of a tree, chanting his *japa.* A dozen reporters from the city came to the farm to interview him.

One intelligent lady reporter asked a thoughtful question: “In spite of being God, why did Lord Rāma have to suffer during His exile?”

Prabhupāda quickly answered, “This shows that the association of women is always the cause of suffering, even if you are God.” Everyone burst into laughter.

Later that day, Śrīla Prabhupāda gave a short talk. Then everyone sat in rows to take *prasāda.* Unfortunately, the food was prepared by unprofessional, hired cooks from the city. The cooking was quite bad, and Śrīla Prabhupāda later asked why the devotees didn’t ask him to cook rather than hiring laborers.

After *prasāda* about a dozen American *mātājis* went swimming in a nearby lake. Upon returning, they found that the buses had somehow left without them. However, the kitchen crew was still there, packing paraphernalia into a truck, and these men offered to take the ladies back to the city on the back of their truck. The ladies accepted the offer, and just then Śrīla Prabhupāda came out of his room to go back to the city in his car. Seeing his spiritual daughters, he asked how they would get back to the city, and also how they liked India. They were delighted to have this unexpected fatherly-like conversation with Prabhupāda.

After spending a week in Hyderabad, the devotees caught a train to Mathurā for the Vṛndāvana festival. Śrīla Prabhupāda flew to Delhi, then went to Vṛndāvana by car. Throughout the many hours on the train, the devotees’ anticipation of attending the opening of the Krishna-Balaram Mandir increased. The opening was a milestone for ISKCON in India, and a huge step forward. Since the acquisition of the land in 1972, three long years of struggle, labor, and sacrifice had ensued. Finally, the momentous occasion had arrived. Devotees were flying to Delhi from all parts of the world just for the opening. This was ISKCON’s first major temple opening in India, and it happened in Vṛndāvana, Śrīla Prabhupāda’s “home.”

## Chapter 7. Opening the Krishna-Balaram Temple

The land donated to Śrīla Prabhupāda was on the outskirts of the holy city of Vṛndāvana, in a sparsely populated area. Shops, *āśramas,* and buildings were few and far between. Devotees could hear the chirping of many birds and chipmunks (“squirrels” in India) and could even feed peacocks by hand. Monkeys darted here and there in the trees to see what the devotees were giving the peacocks and to try to get some for themselves. Oxcarts regularly plied the road, and the bells on the oxen’s necks jingled sweetly. It was simple and pastoral. It was here, in the forest and on the soft sands near the Yamunā, that Lord Kṛṣṇa and Balarāma tended cows with Their friends. This was one reason why Prabhupāda had decided to name the temple after Kṛṣṇa and Balarāma. Besides, the names of the existing temples all started with “Rādhā.” Ours would be unique. In the *Caitanya-caritāmṛta,* Prabhupāda mentioned still another reason for the temple: “We are establishing a Krishna-Balarām temple to broadcast to the world that worship of Gaura-Nitāi is the same as worship of Krishna-Balarāma.”

The temple took a long time to construct. For one thing, the building was ornate. Then there were cement shortages and bureaucratic hassles. The opening had been postponed a few times, but now the date was set.

In other parts of the world where Kṛṣṇa consciousness was taking off, the centers were selling many books and sending proceeds to India for the temple construction. Tejiyas Dāsa raised money in Delhi, and the Bombay devotees found donors for the guesthouse rooms. In Japan, Guru-kṛpā Swami and his men raised most of the funds for the temple. One important figure from England, Guṇārṇava Dāsa, worked closely with Surabhi and later was the temple president.

**Guṇārṇava Dāsa:** Much of the responsibility fell to Guru­kṛpā Swami and Yaśodānandana Swami. They went to Japan with a hard-working team of *brahmacārīs*. I think that they spent practically every day for two years collecting funds. When they occasionally took a break and came to Vṛndāvana, we had a lot of rapport with them because they were collecting and we were overseeing the construction. We’d have heartfelt *kīrtanas*.

We were staying in huts made of bricks and cow dung, with straw roofs, and sleeping on cots made of straw. Prabhupāda would live the same way before his house was built. He’d bathe, squatting on a plank of wood, right next to us. He sometimes took us out in the evenings to functions held by his godbrothers. There was so much to experience, not just the building of the temple, but the culture—the wonderful way Prabhupāda lived and dealt with people in Vṛndāvana. Bhagatji, his friend, was a great help and inspiration. He came to see us every day. With a big smile he would say, “You can do it! You can do it! I’ll help you.” Whenever we had disputes with the workers or traders, Bhagatji sorted them out.

The new deities were made in Jaipur. Gurudāsa Dāsa and Yamunā rejected the first set that had been made because they were not a good likeness—they looked too fierce. When Bharadrāj and Rukmiṇī arrived in Vṛndāvana to set up a doll-making studio, Gurudāsa Dāsa sent Bharadrāj to Jaipur to supervise the completion of the second set.

Surabhi Dāsa and others responsible for the construction made sure that everything was of the best quality. The temple was designed to be practical yet beautiful. It had a high ceiling, an open-air central courtyard, and a black-and-white-checkered marble floor. Behind the temple stood a new forty-room guesthouse with modern plumbing in every room, a first for Vṛndāvana. Śrīla Prabhupāda’s house nestled between the two buildings. By the time the construction ended, 350 workers were operating round the clock. A lot of decorative carving was being done and had to be finished. There were lights strung everywhere, and the whole site was lit up throughout the night.

Yaśomatīnandana Dāsa came from Bombay before the opening and stayed in Vṛndāvana for two months. He was, among other things, in charge of printing the invitations in Mathurā and inviting the dignitaries. The most respected and popular local Māyāvādī, Akhaṇḍānanda Sarasvati, accepted an invitation, but another leading Māyāvādī, Karapātrī Mahārāja, refused. He strongly objected to the idea of making Western people *brāhmaṇas.*

So did the *sannyāsī* who was the guru of the Dhanuka family, ISKCON’s next-door neighbor. He did not like Śrīla Prabhupāda making Westerners *brāhmaṇas,* but he did allow Yaśomatīnandana to book his Dandi Ashram, close to the guesthouse, as the location to cook and serve *prasāda* to all the devotees and guests, three times a day. *Brāhmaṇas* were hired to cook, and a group of visiting devotees helped prepare, cook, and serve. Śrīla Prabhupāda would ask Yaśomatīnandana about the costs and the menus—*puris* and *sabji* in the evenings, and so on.

Two hundred devotees, instead of going to Hyderabad, came directly from the Māyāpur festival to help with the preparations. A week before the opening, there was still debris lying all over the place, and the devotees rushed to clean it up. The courtyard and marble floors were thoroughly washed by throwing buckets of water and then quickly sweeping and mopping away the water.

## Preparations Please Prabhupāda

Daiviśakti Dāsi had been cooking for Prabhupāda in Māyāpur. While he was at the Hyderabad program, she and her husband were flown to Vṛndāvana to get Prabhupāda’s house ready. His house was serving as the temporary temple, complete with *maṇgala-ārati* and *prasāda.* It also contained a temporary sewing room for the deities’ dresses. On the morning of her first day, Daiviśakti thought she had a few days to prepare everything. But right after *maṇgala-ārati,* a telegram came saying that Prabhupāda would arrive at seven that evening. This left half a day to get the place ready.

**Daiviśakti Dāsi:** A room at the back of Prabhupāda’s house was being used to hold the new deities. The devotees moved the deities to the altars and then frantically cleaned—starting from the furthest back corner to the front door. Just as we finished, Prabhupāda arrived in his car from Delhi.

Śrīla Prabhupāda was pleased to see the four-story guesthouse and restaurant. It had been completely built in the seven or eight months since his last visit. During that time, the three tall domes over the temple had also been constructed. As he finished inspecting the complex, Prabhupāda sighed with relief, and expressed satisfaction as he looked up at the domes.

“The domes have come out very nice. What do you think?” he asked his disciples.

Everyone agreed they were magnificent—that Surabhi had done an excellent job.

Śrīla Prabhupāda said, “Yes, everyone says how nice Surabhi is doing.”

He smiled and turned to Surabhi, who had gone with very little sleep for weeks, and said, “But I can’t say that. Only me—l am criticizing you, because that is my job.”

**Gurudāsa Dāsa:** A few days before the opening I was running around making sure everything was in order. Prabhupāda called me up to his *vyāsāsana* one morning, and I approached him and said, “Yes, Śrīla Prabhupāda?” He just reached out, ruffled my *śikhā,* and patted my head, and that was my reward.

After returning to Vṛndāvana from Jaipur, Bharadrāj painted the deities and the *mūrtis* of Prabhupāda and Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvati. He was painting in a little room in Prabhupāda’s house.

**Bharadrāj Dāsa:** For weeks I had been meditating and concentrating on Prabhupāda’s face while helping with the *mūrtis.* I had to look at old photographs, because Prabhupāda didn’t pose for the painting—most of the time he wasn’t there. One morning I walked into his room. He was sitting there, and I looked at his face. It appeared so beautiful that I felt wonderful emotions. It was like I’d never seen it before. This was the effect of so much concentration.

**Dhanañjaya Dāsa:** Prabhupāda wanted each of the *mūrtis* and deities to have a plate, which meant ten plates. He told us that the purest material to use for plates was lotus leaves, but lotus leaves were not readily available. Then there was gold, then silver, then bell metal. I said we could afford bell metal. So I bought a set of bell metal plates and had all the deities’ names engraved on them. Prabhupāda also went over the deity menu with me. For the main daily offering, he wanted us to offer twenty-five preparations.

## Devotees Arrive En Masse

The international group of devotees traveling to Mathurā by train from Hyderabad arrived a few days before the opening. In total, about six hundred devotees and members of ISKCON worldwide arrived and took part in the festivities.

The Bombay temple booked a whole train car to accommodate its devotees, members, and friends. We all had a good time on the way, with *kīrtana* and *prasāda.* We brought *puris* and *pakorās* from the temple, and purchased fruit from vendors along the way. When the train stopped at major junctions, the devotees quickly assembled on the platform and had a *kīrtana* and distributed books.

I invited my parents to come to Vṛndāvana for a pilgrimage, to have *darśana* of the deities, and especially to see Śrīla Prabhupāda. Being simple villagers, my parents had never set foot outside Maharashtra, our home state. They had a few pleasant surprises. They were respectfully treated by all the devotees, many of whom came forward to touch my parents’ feet. They reciprocated with smiling glances, unable to speak even a word of English or Hindi. The devotees were kind to them throughout the journey. I was thanking the Lord for bestowing upon them the good fortune of the devotees’ association on the train ride to Vṛndāvana. Anything is possible by Śrīla Prabhupāda’s mercy.

When we arrived at the Mathurā train junction, we were greeted by devotees who escorted us to hired buses. All the Bombay devotees and guests felt elated to be in Vṛndāvana. They held enthusiastic *kīrtanas* in the buses on the way to their accommodations in a *dharmaśāla* in the township of Vṛndāvana.

Many ISKCON devotees stayed at Fogal Ashram. Although Fogal Ashram is a big building, it was crowded with four hundred devotees staying there. Some senior devotees, life members, and VIP’s occupied the new guesthouse. Other members and guests had to take rooms at *dharmaśālas* in town. Śrīla Prabhupāda had told the festival directors to make sure the devotees and guests had comfortable facilities. He was acting as the main host of the celebration, expressing his natural concern. His genuine attention motivated the devotees to do their best.

**Saudāmanī Dāsi:** The *sannyāsīs* stayed in the guesthouse, along with many guests. The ladies stayed in the interior rooms of the guesthouse, but there weren’t enough rooms for all of us. Fortunately, there was a twenty-four-hour *kīrtana,* and someone would ask you, “Please let me know when you go to the twenty-four-hour *kīrtana* so I can sleep in your spot.” One batch of girls slept while another batch sang. Tap water was being rationed to the ladies so that there would also be enough for the guests.

## The Installation Gets Under Way

The whole temple complex, including the boundary, was opulently decorated with flags, festoons, flower garlands, and mango leaves. Small banana trees were tied to the temple pillars. The whole place glittered brightly with colored electric lights.

The *prāṇa-pratiṣṭhā* ceremony—the deity installation—started on April 18. Local priests had constructed, on the side of the courtyard opposite the *tamāla* tree, a small bamboo hut decorated with flags and garlands. The deities were placed inside. The *brāhmaṇas* positioned many waterpots of various sizes, with yellow and red threads wound round them, on platforms of varying heights. They summoned Śrīla Prabhupāda to be present as they called the different demigods to reside in the ceremonial pots. The pots were covered with *cādars* and garlands and topped off with coconuts resting on mango leaves around their necks.

Prabhupāda sat gravely in the hut during these rites. He normally stayed busy in his quarters most of the day. When he came to this ceremony, it was another chance for us to see him, and we all crowded into the courtyard to watch his every move. The *brāhmaṇas* decorated his forehead with turmeric, *kuṁkum,* and sandalwood paste. He patiently sat while they chanted mantras and asked him to hold this, touch that, throw this, sip this, and chant that. He dutifully sat with senior devotees through some of the rituals, but he appeared unenthusiastic about them.

Rajaśekhara Dāsa, a devotee from England, was one of the first full­time *pūjārīs* at the Krishna-Balaram temple, and he observed:

These ceremonies involved a lot of expense, but Prabhupāda said that if we didn’t do them, then the people of Vṛndāvana would criticize us. They would feel that our temple had not been opened in a systematic, bona fide way. Perhaps ten to fifteen *pāṇḍās* were doing the whole show. We knew what Prabhupāda’s idea was, so we went along with it.

## Touring Vṛndāvana’s Temples

Before being installed, deities traditionally take a tour of the town. So, on April 19, a huge group of devotees accompanied the small deities on a *harināma* procession to the major temples of Vṛndāvana. Two elephants—dressed in decorative headgear and bright red cloth—led the parade. The deities were placed on an altar that stood on a bullock cart covered with an elaborately decorated red-and-silver canopy. Two *pūjārīs* sat on either side of the deities, one holding them as the cart moved along the bumpy roads, and the other fanning. Speakers attached to the cart amplified the *kīrtana* so that everyone could hear the chanting: Hare Kṛṣṇa, Hare Kṛṣṇa, Kṛṣṇa Kṛṣṇa, Hare Hare, Hare Rāma, Hare Rāma, Rāma Rāma, Hare Hare. We were broadcasting the glories of the deities in the form of Their holy names. Devotees took turns pushing the deities’ cart. About forty devotees carried a colorful assortment of flags. A marching band brought up the rear.

All of a sudden Śrīla Prabhupāda showed up at the procession and made his way to the deities’ cart. The devotees surrounded him, now dancing and singing all the more ecstatically. He chanted and danced with them, increasing everyone’s enthusiasm and joy. This parade solidified their faith in Prabhupāda’s authentic gift of Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Śrutakīrti waved a *cāmara* around Śrīla Prabhupāda, and another devotee held an ornate red-and-silver umbrella above him. Dr. Kapoor and Mr. Kartikeya Mahadevia, a prominent member from Bombay, walked with Prabhupāda, who stayed for two hours and found himself drowning in garlands as the procession wound its way through Vṛndāvana. As dusk fell, the torchbearers lit kerosene lamps, and the scene took on a mystical appearance. Five hundred devotees were dancing and singing *“Jaya* Kṛṣṇa­Balarāma!”

## Dignitaries Arrive

Finally, the big day arrived. Prabhupāda wanted to make an impact. At his invitation, Dr. Channa Reddy, the governor of Uttar Pradesh, had agreed to be the chief guest. He arrived with aides and many security guards. The Inspector-General of Police from Lucknow, and dignitaries from Delhi, also accepted Prabhupāda’s invitations. Prabhupāda wanted the distinguished guests to be well looked after. He told the new temple president, Dhanañjaya, not to leave Dr. Reddy’s side.

Many prominent life members and distinguished guests attended the opening. Prabhupāda’s godbrothers came, as did Śrīman Viśwambhara Goswami from the Rādhā-Ramaṇa temple, Dr. Kapoor, Śrī Śrīmad Nṛsiṁha Vallabha Goswami, Śrī Śrīmad Puruṣottama Goswami, Dr. Sharma from Agra (who later became principal of the *gurukula),* Bhagatji from around the corner, and even the *dhobis,* the laundrymen. All the people who helped in one way or another were there. The temple was packed with visiting devotees, VIP’s, and Vṛndāvana residents. Akhaṇḍānanda Saraswati, the most prominent leader of the monistic school, sat with Prabhupāda. Prabhupāda tactfully wanted the whole Vṛndāvana community to recognize his temple, so he invited important persons, regardless of their philosophical point of view.

## The Abhiṣeka

From the hut in the courtyard, the deities were moved closer to the altar, into the *darśana-maṇḍapa* of the temple. By various rituals and mantras, the different parts and senses of the deities’ transcendental bodies were enlivened. At first They were covered from head to toe in white cloth, but by the end of the ceremony only Their waists (or waists and chests in the case of the *gopis)* were covered with white cloth. They also wore white blindfolds. When the blindfolds were taken off and the beeswax over Their eyes removed with a gold nugget, they were considered fully installed.

The deities immediately stole everyone’s heart. Some devotees thought these were the most beautiful Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa deities they’d ever seen. Others marveled at Kṛṣṇa-Balarāma’s strong arms and innocent poses. Still others were attracted to merciful Gaura-Nitāi.

The Vrajavāsi priests offered garlands of rupee notes to the deities and then removed the garlands. Jovially chanting mantras in loud monotones, they offered other preliminary items and then performed the *abhiṣeka.* Smiling and happily bathing the small deities with hundreds of liters of milk, yogurt, Yamunā water, and other auspicious liquids, they sprinkled and splashed any liquid remaining in the bathing conches onto the large deities. The temple became animated. The devotees’ *kīrtana* bubbled over and flooded out the front gates. The whole of Vraja came running.

**Rādhā-kuṇḍa Dāsi (Yaśomatīnandana’s Wife):** We were outside distributing books during most of the program. We could hear all the conches, bells, gongs, and instruments. The sound grew more and more lively. Finally, we couldn’t resist. We put the books away and ran into the temple. Inside was a sea of devotees, and there was total devotion everywhere—north, south, east, and west. In this *kīrtana* no one was being left out—everyone was flying, jumping, and singing wholeheartedly, like no other singing of this world.

Throughout the three-day ceremony, Prabhupāda stressed that actually it is *kīrtana* that calls the Lord into His deity form. The rituals become complete only when there is chanting of Hare Kṛṣṇa. We did the rituals because the *smārta-brāhmaṇas* and caste goswamis would not have accepted deities installed just by the chanting of Hare Kṛṣṇa. But they themselves had now authorized the temple and installed the deities. With the installation over, the devotees welcomed the *arca-vigrahas* by singing and dancing for Their pleasure. By *kīrtana,* the devotees were feeling Their presence.

**Dhanañjaya Dāsa:** Prabhupāda had given me envelopes containing the *dakṣiṇā* to hand to all the *brāhmaṇas* who did the *prāṇa-pratiṣṭhā.* They all got different amounts, from 1,111 rupees to 11 rupees, according to the status of their families.

These Vṛndāvana families pass down their knowledge and tradition. Bhakti-vidyā-pūrṇa Swami said that the grandson of one of the head priests who did the three-day installation of Kṛṣṇa-Balarāma did the ten-day installation ceremony more than twenty years later at ISKCON’s Glory of India project, in New Delhi.

## “They are Vaiṣṇavas, not the Cia.”

After the deities were carried to the altar to be dressed and offered *bhoga,* the *kīrtana* slowly came to a close, and everyone settled down in the courtyard to hear talks by the guests and senior devotees. Chairs had been set up for Prabhupāda, Governor Reddy, and other important guests. Acyutānanda Swami, an American *sannyāsī,* spoke in Hindi to make a good impression on the guests. Prabhupāda also gave Yaśomatīnandana Dāsa, a Gujarati, the opportunity to speak in front of the VIP’s, to provide a balance. Some of Prabhupāda’s godbrothers also spoke in Hindi. Śrī Śrīmad Nṛsiṁha Vallabha Goswami mentioned many references to the effect that Western devotees should be accepted as genuine *brāhmaṇas* and Vaiṣṇavas. He spoke words of appreciation, and congratulated Prabhupāda and the devotees:

It is Bhaktivedānta Swami’s boundlessly compassionate heart that took him to the West and brought you all here today. Here in Vṛndāvana we have no foreigners. Today for the first time we are seeing so many from all nations. We are indebted to His Grace for showing the world the true glories of India. Seeing this beautiful Krishna-Balaram Mandir and all these happy devotees has made this the happiest day of my life.

Mr. Channa Reddy, in Hindi, thanked and encouraged Śrīla Prabhupāda and the devotees for spreading Lord Caitanya’s message and building the magnificent temple. Finally, Śrīla Prabhupāda took his seat on the *vyāsāsana* to address the gathering. He recited an invocation and then spoke in English:

Balarāma became Nitāi, and Kṛṣṇa became Caitanya Mahāprabhu. These two brothers, Kṛṣṇa-Balarāma, Rāma­Lakṣmaṇa, came again as Caitanya Mahāprabhu and Nityānanda Prabhu. So what was Their business? *Pāpi tāpi jata chilo hari-nāme uddhārilo:* introducing this chanting of the Hare Kṛṣṇa *mahā-mantra* to improve society. Jagāi and Mādhāi were addicted to sinful activities, but by hearing the chanting, they became purified. Now it is actually being enacted worldwide. We send our men from village to village in Europe and America. We are taking advantage of modern facilities. We’re using nice buses and cars to distribute books and chant Hare Kṛṣṇa. Throughout the whole world this should be introduced.

And especially in India, because in India the people are generally Kṛṣṇa conscious. By artificial means they are trying to forget. This is the position. This should be stopped. They have a natural tendency to become Kṛṣṇa conscious. Therefore in these days still so many hundreds and thousands of people come to Vṛndāvana to relish the transcendental mellow of Kṛṣṇa. And now the foreigners are also attracted to Kṛṣṇa consciousness, and I am trying to place them in our temple. This movement is undoubtedly increasing in the foreign countries, and why not in our country? They have taken it very seriously. They are not the CIA, not American nor European. They are Vaiśṇavas. They are servants of Kṛṣṇa. Don’t misunderstand them. And try to cooperate and help this movement for the benefit of the whole human society.

Prabhupāda expressed his pleasure and gratitude and thanked everyone for coming. He left his seat and made his way to the altar, while the devotees started a big *kīrtana.*

## Prabhupāda Offers the First Ārati

Finally, it almost was time for Kṛṣṇa-Balarāma’s first *ārati* ceremony, the first opportunity to see Them in Their new home in new outfits. Everyone eagerly anticipated beholding Their beautiful forms and receiving Their merciful glances. In just a few more minutes Śrīla Prabhupāda’s long cherished desire to open a grand temple in Vṛndāvana would be fulfilled.

The heavy altar doors swung open, and Prabhupāda blew the conch. Then the curtains flew back. The breathtaking beauty of the deities stunned us. They wore multi-colored outfits designed by the French devotee Oṁkāra Dāsa and Kiśorī Dāsi, Surabhi’s wife. Each piece of the outfit was a different color, and the colors combined exquisitely. The *pūjārīs* had done a spectacular job dressing the deities.

**Dīna-bandhu Dāsa:** The crowd went wild trying to see all three altars. I was in front of Kṛṣṇa-Balarāma’s altar and was literally lifted off my feet and taken fifteen feet to one side and then fifteen feet back. I thought I was going to get crushed to death. I broke through the crowd like a football player and made it to the safety of the courtyard.

**Ākūti Dāsi (Dina-bandhu’s wife):** I stood with my back to a pillar, thinking I was safe, but suddenly I was drowning in an ocean of people. I saw some ladies drop to the ground and crawl to safety, because they knew they were going to get crushed by all the bodies. I crossed my hands in front of my chest to protect my lungs, so that I could breathe. Someone then tapped my shoulder and asked, “Can you roll *puris* in the kitchen?” I said “Sure,” thinking this was Kṛṣṇa’s plan to save me. I somehow made my way out of there.

Śrīla Prabhupāda chose to offer the first *ārati* to Kṛṣṇa-Balarāma. He wore his perfectly pressed, shiny-silk *dhoti* and *kurtā,* and several rose garlands that almost reached his knees. He offered the articles with precise, concentrated movements. The devotees could sense Prabhupāda’s deep appreciation of Kṛṣṇa and Balarāma. Was Śrīla Prabhupāda thanking Kṛṣṇa and Balarāma for appearing, and welcoming Them to Their new home to enjoy Their pastime of attracting the hearts of Their devotees?

All the while, a joyous, roaring *kīrtana* was resounding in the temple room, a completely Kṛṣṇa conscious *kīrtana,* the kind you dream of. We were singing purely for the pleasure of the Lord and Śrīla Prabhupāda. We were singing for the sheer pleasure of chanting. Magnificent beats rumbled and snapped out of the *mṛdaṅgas,* and the devotees all sang like Gandharvas.

Two devotees stood on either side of the central altar, throwing bright red rose petals toward the feet of Kṛṣṇa and Balarāma, gradually covering the floor in front of Prabhupāda. As he finished offering each article in slow circles to the deities, Śrīla Prabhupāda would turn around and offer it to the devotees. He had a beautiful, satisfied look on his face. As the *ārati* progressed, his smile grew bigger and bigger, as if he had conquered the world. The opening of the temple in Vṛndāvana certainly was a milestone in Prabhupāda’s preaching. Almost ten years had passed since he’d gone to New York to preach.

I was reminded of how, as a little boy, Prabhupāda used to watch the *ārati* of Rādhā-Govinda in his hometown of Calcutta. Now it was our turn to watch him offer *ārati* in Vṛndāvana. Prabhupāda’s offering the first *ārati* was the highlight of the festival. It has been etched in the minds of all devotees who saw it and even those who have only seen pictures of it. The lights glittered as they reflected off the backdrop. After Prabhupāda blew the conch to end the *ārati,* he paid his obeisances in the bed of rose petals at the feet of Kṛṣṇa and Balarāma.

We sang at the tops of our voices: *“Jaya* Gaura-Nitāi, *Jaya* Kṛṣṇa­Balarāma, *Jaya* Rādhā-Śyāmasundara,” and ended with a long chorus of *“Jaya* Prabhupāda.” We knew Śrīla Prabhupāda was very, very pleased, and that was the perfection of our happiness.

## The Play and the Feast

After the *ārati* and *kīrtana,* the Vaikuṇṭha Players staged a drama called *The Killing of Jarāsandha.* Arjuna, Bhīma, and Kṛṣṇa, disguised as *brāhmaṇas,* went to fight with Jarāsandha, and Bhīma tore him apart. Rajaśekhara said that Śrīla Prabhupāda sat with Dr. Reddy and more than once leaned over and commented during the play. They knew each another, and Prabhupāda seemed to like him. The guests and devotees enjoyed the play.

Then came a fabulous feast. People were sitting and eating everywhere—from the temple all the way out to the road. Others went to the Dandi Ashram, in the back, to eat. Prabhupāda recited the *prasāda* prayer, and the people honored *prasāda* to their heart’s content. The governor and the VIP’s took *prasāda* in Prabhupāda’s house. Prabhupāda specified that South Indian dishes be prepared for the governor, because he was from Tamil Nadu. Prabhupāda supervised the serving by telling the devotees the right order in which to serve the items.

During the three-day opening, Daiviśakti was in the kitchen cooking for Prabhupāda and his guests. Prabhupāda had requested that everyone who visits his room get nice *prasāda.*

**Daiviśakti Dāsi:** I spent most of the time cooking *halavā, pakorā* after *pakorā,* and sweets, even during the installation. I also missed the grand procession because Prabhupāda didn’t like the curtains in his room and I stayed back to put up new ones. During Prabhupāda’s walks we would clean his rooms. So from early in the morning until eleven at night I was in Prabhupāda’s house or in the kitchen, and it was ecstatic.

## Unsynchronized Gaura-ārati

This temple was one of the first in ISKCON to have three altars, so the devotees who did *ārati* had had no opportunities to practice offering the articles simultaneously, three at a time.

**Rajaśekhara Dāsa:** During the festival practically everyone wanted to have a turn at offering *ārati,* but the devotees didn’t understand that for every *pūjārī* to do everything simultaneously, you had to watch the devotee in front of you. While one *pūjārī* was still offering a ghee lamp, the other two were already offering water. Or one would be offering the peacock-feather fan, while the others were still waving *cāmaras.* For a few days it was like that, because everybody wanted to go on the altar and offer an *ārati* to the deities. After three or four days we got it together so that everything became nicely synchronized.

However, the first Gaura-ārati was unsynchronized. The first *pūjāri* came out to blow his conch, but seeing no other *pūjārīs,* went back behind the curtain. Meanwhile, the two other *pūjārīs* walked out to blow their conches, but not seeing the first *pūjāri,* they also returned to the altar room, just as the first *pūjārī* made his way again to the front. It took a minute or two for all of them to be in the right place at the same time. On top of that, they were all tense, because it was such a big occasion. Hundreds of people were watching them.

**Vaiśeṣika Dāsa:** I offered the first Gaura-ārati to Rādhā­Śyāmasundara, and Tripurāri Swami offered to Kṛṣṇa­-Balarāma, while Sura offered to Gaura-Nitāi. I was worried because we were told that all the Vṛndāvana goswamis would be there. Besides that, we had to offer paraphernalia like lamps with tall ghee-wicks, which we weren’t familiar with. We were all book distributors from America and weren’t specialists at *pūjā*. Prabhupāda attended and stood with love and devotion before Rādhā-Śyāmasundara. When I tossed the water out over the heads of the devotees, I hit Tamal Krishna Mahārāja in the face, and he had to wipe his glasses dry. Later, Prabhupāda asked who the other two *pūjārīs* were with Tripurāri Swami, and he was told our names and that we were book distributors, not experienced *pūjārīs*. He said that was OK.

After the evening *ārati,* Har Govinda, the leader of a famous *rasa-līlā* troupe—said to be one of the best in Vṛndāvana—entertained the guests with Kṛṣṇa-līlā and Caitanya-līlā. Later, Śrīla Prabhupāda told Har Govinda in front of Dhanañjaya that the drama of Caitanya-līlā was so nice that he wanted to take it all over the world.

## “Sirs, This is the Problem …”

There was a big rush in the morning from Fogal Ashram, as devotees made their way to the new temple for the first *maṇgala-ārati.* The previous day had been a long day, and we had not taken rest till late, but everyone woke up early in anticipation of seeing the deities again. Now we had our own deities and our own temple with a sound system and a proper *kīrtana* hall.

**Kavicandra Swami:** I remember the morning walk the day after the installation. Prabhupāda was delighted that the Krishna-Balaram temple was open. He said, “Kṛṣṇa is flattering His brother: ‘My dear Balarāma, the trees are great sages, and they are all offering their obeisances to you. The birds are chanting your glories.”’ Śrīla Prabhupāda stopped and said, “Now Balarāma is here. Everything is all right. He can give you spiritual strength if you ever feel weak.” He talked about how Kṛṣṇa and Balarāma are nondifferent from Gaura-Nitāi, and said that if we ever have difficulty, we can approach Kṛṣṇa and Balarāma or Gaura-Nitāi and say, ‘Sirs, this is the problem,’ and they will do the needful.”

In this festival of firsts, as Prabhupāda returned from his walk we beheld another first. Śrīla Prabhupāda led us into the temple for the deity greeting. He paid full obeisances at Gaura-Nitāi’s altar. Then he stood and proceeded to Kṛṣṇa- Balarāma’s altar and again paid his full obeisances. He repeated the procedure at the altar of Śrī Śrī Rādhā­Śyāmasundara. Most temples had one altar, so this was a new experience for most of the devotees.

After the greeting, Prabhupāda took his seat on the new *vyāsāsana* to accept *guru-pūjā.* All these years, in many places, he’d had makeshift arrangements for an *āsana,* and now we had the satisfaction of properly honoring him. He ascended the steps to his elegant raised seat. It was literally a *siṁhāsana*—a seat of lions. A pair of ferocious-looking lions flanked the seat, and it seemed as though Prabhupāda were riding between their backs. The lions had been newly painted, by Muralīdhara Dāsa, a BBT artist from America, and they looked lifelike with their half-open jaws.

Another special feature of the festival was gathering together one morning with Prabhupāda in front of the temple for a group photograph. It took at least an hour to set up, because the BBT photographers wanted everything perfect. They made everyone move around until all three hundred devotees fit in the picture. Not every devotee was there. I wasn’t, but I don’t remember why. About half the men were sitting. The ladies had to climb to the roof. Finally, Prabhupāda arrived with the GBC men, who made their way through the patiently waiting group and secured positions around Prabhupāda. The helpful photographers asked the men who were standing and the ladies to raise their arms and say, “Haribol!” Prabhupāda stood with his cane planted on the marble walkway.

At the first fire sacrifice in the new temple, Prabhupāda gave first and second initiations. Acyutānanda Swami was the priest. Only half an hour before the ceremony, Gopi-parānadhana was asked to cut firewood for the sacrificial fire, but he was given only a kitchen knife and wet wood to work with, and he missed most of the preliminaries of the ceremony.

There were many second initiates, so Prabhupāda asked that they come to receive their *brāhmaṇa* thread from him in batches. Each day, for a few days, a group of devotees would wait in line outside Prabhupāda’s room. In those days, the front area where the books are now sold was not closed off. It was a small patio with a two-foot-high wall. Buddhimanta Dāsa sat there, guarding Prabhupāda’s house. The second initiates lined up around the patio, waiting to see Prabhupāda one at a time.

**Bhīma Dāsa:** Prabhupāda read through the *gāyatri-mantra,* and I repeated it. Then he showed me how to count the mantras, and he put the thread over me. He patiently went through this procedure with each one of us. What patience! There were lots of devotees. I had now received my thread, and I was there with Prabhupāda, relishing the pleasure of being with him for the first time.

He asked, “When did you receive first initiation?” I told him I got it the year before in Māyāpur.

“Oh, that is all right,” Prabhupāda replied. “Are you chanting sixteen rounds?”

“Yes, Prabhupāda, every day,” I said.

I sat there quietly, unsure of what else to say. It didn’t enter my mind what to do next. Prabhupāda broke the silence by asking me to tell the rest of the devotees that they should come tomorrow. Maybe that was a hint for me to leave, but I didn’t catch it, because I didn’t want to go anywhere. So he finally said, “All right, go!” Then I quickly offered obeisances and started to leave. Prabhupāda reminded me: “Don’t forget to tell the rest to come tomorrow.” When I went outside, the other devotees were already gone. Prabhupāda’s servants must have decided he’d already had enough for the day, and asked them to leave.

## Parikramās and Paṇḍāls Conclude the Festival

The festival took place over two weeks. The first week was given to the opening, the second week to *parikramās* and *paṇḍāl* programs. Bus tours went to the major holy places: Govardhana and Rādhā-kuṇḍa, Varṣāṇā and Nandagrāma, and more. To see the holy places of Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes is normally a profound experience, but things got off to a bad start.

**Padyāvali Dāsi:** The managers put the ladies and the children on one bus. They had another bus for the men. We started off down the road and soon realized that our driver was a total fool. He was driving the bus wildly, and we all freaked out. We tried to stop him, but couldn’t; he finally drove into a ditch. That stopped him. We were furious. We got off the bus and went to the other bus to complain to the men.

There were *paṇdāl* programs in Mathurā and Agra. Śrīla Prabhupāda wanted the Australian devotees to do a play, so they did a drama about Jagāi and Mādhāi at the Agra *paṇdāl.* The Indians loved it, and Prabhupāda congratulated the players for cheering everyone up. There was also a press conference in Agra and riotous *prasāda* distribution.

## The Life of the First Pūjaris

Śrīla Prabhupāda had had the devotees conduct a twenty-four-hour *kīrtana* during the installation. Devotees took turns, in three-hour slots. Then Prabhupāda asked every temple to send a man for a permanent twenty-four-hour *kīrtana.*

**Dhanañjaya Dāsa:** Prabhupāda wanted one devotee from each of the 120 temples for twenty-four-hour *kīrtana.* He said, “Every temple president should send one good man for three months at a time.” Thus each temple would send four men a year. They played in two-hour shifts—about eight men per shift. This twenty-four-hour *kīrtana,* organized by Madhudviṣa Swami, continued for a year, until Rāma-navamī in 1976. But after Madhudviṣa Swami was unable to continue organizing it, it died out.

The first few months at the Krishna-Balaram temple were extraordinary for the *pūjārīs.* Along with Rajaśekhara, the other full-time *pūjārīs* were Narottama Dāsa, Drūpada Dāsa, and Śivanātha Dāsa. Śrīla Prabhupāda gave them special instructions before he left Vṛndāvana on May 1.

**Rajaśekhara Dāsa:** Prabhupāda said the *pūjārīs* shouldn’t come out of the *pūjārī* room, but should “stay inside there and remain pure.” We had our own room, a shower upstairs, and everything. He also said they should eat only *mahā prasāda.* We started doing these things, but then we had to stop because there was too much opposition from the other devotees. They complained: “Who do you think you are? You never associate with us, and you’re eating all the *mahā prasāda.”* We were just following instructions, of course. At least for some time, many of us were doing six *āratis* a day. We practically lived with the deities, taking Śrīla Prabhupāda’s instruction to heart.

Drūpada said that Śrīla Prabhupāda referred to the deities during the installation as Śrī Śrī Rādhā Govinda. Prabhupāda was later told that the devotees liked calling them Rādhā Śyāmasundara, and he said it was all right to call them that. Dhanañjaya recalls that the deities were unnamed and that he thought a nice name would be Rādhā­Śyāmasundara, because Kṛṣṇa was blackish and beautiful. Prabhupāda told him, “Yes, you can call Them Rādhā-Śyāmasundara.”

## Chapter 8. The 1976 Māyāpur Festival

During the 1976 Māyāpur festival Śrīla Prabhupāda guided and inspired the members of his society to work together. He united them by resolving a dispute between the Rādhā-Dāmodara traveling parties and the temple presidents in North America. He also directed the development of the Māyāpur project by starting and overseeing the construction of the Long Building.

**Pañcaratna Dāsa:** Śrīla Prabhupāda set the standard for his leaders to follow by his regular attendance at the Māyāpur festivals. He went to great lengths to enliven and inspire the devotees. In Māyāpur he made himself available to the devotees—and not just for a few days. He would be there the whole time, going on walks, giving classes and *darśanas,* and overseeing the GBC meetings.

## Prabhupāda Arrives

Late in the morning on January 15, after taking *prasāda* on the way in the mango grove, Śrīla Prabhupāda arrived at Śrī Māyāpur Candrodayā Mandir accompanied by his new servant, Hari Śauri Dāsa. All the Māyāpur devotees—fifty adults and thirty young students—greeted him with a *kīrtana* on Bhaktisiddhānta Road and offered their obeisances. The outside of the front wall was now being sculpted into many panels, on which were being painted the seventeen different front covers of the *Caitanya-caritāmṛta,* titles and all. Śrīla Prabhupāda had told Ānakadundubhi and Pāṇḍu to use their artistic talents to paint pictures in such public places.

After Prabhupāda stepped out of his car, he was presented with a pair of scissors on a silk cushion, and he cut a twenty-foot-long garland that hung across a new metal entrance gate. Prabhupāda smiled. Each door of the gate had the name “ISKCON” welded onto it. Above the gate was a new ornate gatehouse, with three saffron arches and five domes. There were small upper rooms on either side, where guards could stay or musicians could play *śāhnāis.* Everyone cheered as the gate swung open. Prabhupāda entered, followed by the devotees singing vibrantly.

A devotee held an orange silk umbrella above Prabhupāda, shading him from the strong sunlight as he moved along the path toward the Lotus Building. The setting had not changed much for four years. Lush green fields still surrounded the property; and everywhere Prabhupāda looked he saw crops or flower beds. But a smooth, wide path leading to the temple had replaced the skinny earthen ridges that had once wound through the fields.

When he neared the temple entrance, two ladies on the first floor—Svātī and Kāmadhuk—showered him with rose petals, surprising him and his party. It was a heavenly experience. For a moment it seemed as if the demigods were giving their blessings. Entering the temple room, Prabhupāda greeted the deities. A *śālagrāma-śilā* was now present on the altar, Māyāpur being one of the few temples in ISKCON where Prabhupāda had authorized this form of worship.

In his arrival address, Prabhupāda said:

This temple is meant for a transcendental United Nations. What the United Nations has failed to do will be achieved here by the process recommended by Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu: preaching in every town and village. You have come from all parts of the world and are living together in this temple. I am especially glad to see the children from all other countries and Indians and Bengalis, all together, forgetting their bodily consciousness. That is the greatest achievement in this movement—that everyone forgets the bodily concept of life and is simply ecstatic in chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa. So continue it very jubilantly. Caitanya Mahāprabhu, the master of Māyāpur, will be very much pleased upon you, and ultimately you will go back home, back to Godhead.

In the course of the next few days, Prabhupāda inspected the grounds, visited the *gośālā,* and boarded the boat that the devotees traveled in from village to village. Hari Śauri kept a diary that abounds in descriptions of Prabhupāda’s activities during the six weeks before the festival and thereafter.

In January and February, Māyāpur experiences occasional storms that rise suddenly, but quickly vanish. During one such storm, the lights went out, and huge gusts of wind arose. As lightning streaked across the sky, Hari Śauri rushed about Prabhupāda’s room to bolt the doors and windows, while Prabhupāda sat calmly at his desk. He commented that rainfall at this time of year is timely and auspicious, for it provides nourishing water for the winter crops.

## The Longest Building in Bengal

Close to a thousand devotees were coming to the 1976 festival, many of whom were newcomers. Prabhupāda had seen the inconveniences that the devotees experienced in previous years and wished to offer them more accommodations. To them, Māyāpur was the home of which they had heard so much—the place Prabhupāda had prepared for them. He wanted them to be comfortable.

Initially the managers had wanted to build grass huts for the festival, but Prabhupāda discouraged them. He told them not to spend unnecessarily—making cottages and then taking them down. It was better to spend for rooms. When Jayapatākā Swami said there was not enough time to construct a new building for this year’s festival, Prabhupāda had encouraged him not to hesitate.

“Do as much as possible,” Prabhupāda told him. “Engage a hundred workers and it will be done. I will give you the money, if money is the only question. You can begin immediately.”

The devotees asked Prabhupāda where to situate the new building. He said to build it as a sort of boundary wall along the northern border of the land, parallel to the Lotus Building.

Then they asked him how big it should be—as big as the Lotus Building?

Prabhupāda replied, “No, it can be bigger than that.”

“Twice the size?”

“No, make it larger,” came his quick and short response.

“Well, three times the size, Prabhupāda?”

Then Prabhupāda asked, “How far does our land extend?”

The devotees indicated the border.

Prabhupāda said, “Yes, you build it starting from the road, right to the end of our land.”

The distance was almost a thousand feet. The new two-story building would be the longest building in West Bengal. Although its name was to become the Chakra Building, from then on it has also been known as the Long Building, for it was as long as a train.

At a *darśana* Prabhupāda compared the Māyāpur project to Matsya-avatāra. Lord Matsya was at first tiny enough to be kept in a waterpot. Eventually He grew so large that the ocean could not hold Him. Now Prabhupāda was seeing his vision expand into dimensions he’d not anticipated.

**Jayādvaita Swami:** It was quite impressive to go out to Māyāpur. In those days the state of construction in Māyāpur was massive. It meant that something was happening.

Prabhupāda would observe the construction site from the balcony of the Lotus Building. The managers employed a hundred and fifty workers, and on March 1, Prabhupāda ordered them to work double shifts. As a result, by festival time the ground floor was almost complete. He told Subhāga and other devotees: “These bricklayers are going so slow. Why don’t you go there and put bricks also and help speed up the work?” So some devotees began helping the workers. Prabhupāda wanted the building finished in six months.

Prabhupāda had considered leaving vacant space around the plinths to allow the Gaṅgā’s floodwaters to flow under the new building, but thinking this a waste of space, he told the workers to enclose the area and make ground-floor rooms. “For a few more rupees,” Prabhupāda said at a *darśana,* “you get thousands of square feet of useable space.”

He seemed to know what was right for every situation and problem. The devotees sought his advice on landscaping, the storage and distribution of water, and even buying bricks. He was thorough, and his expertise extensive. Walking past the walls of the new building·, Prabhupāda asked if they were standing properly—at right angles to the ground. If they were leaning, they would eventually fall over. Jayapatākā Swami replied, “I have seen while the masons were laying bricks—they were testing all the time. In the future, I’ll personally test it.”

## Everyone Arrives

At the front gate there would be maybe one ricksha waiting. There was no permanent bazaar, just temporary shops set up during the festival. By mid-February, the number of devotees started increasing. They began arriving from all parts of the world. Śrīla Prabhupāda invited Hṛdayānanda Goswami, who was preaching in South America, to come to Māyāpur to be his secretary in February, and he did so. In early March, Puṣṭa-Kṛṣṇa Swami arrived from Germany, driving a new maroon Mercedes limousine meant for Prabhupāda. Prabhupāda also asked him to be his secretary.

**Bhakti-vighna-vināśak Nṛsiṁha Swami:** I arrived with Gargamuni Swami and his men on February 29. Kīrtirāja, who’d been preaching in Poland, was with us. We’d driven through Krishnanagar in six blue-and-white Mercedes vans, all mounted with loudspeakers blasting the *mahā-mantra.* When we got to Māyāpur, Gargamuni Swami went to Prabhupāda’s room and fell at his feet, and Prabhupāda rubbed his head. Śrīla Prabhupāda was quite pleased with him for bringing the vehicles to India. Gargamuni Swami had gone to Europe, collected money, bought the vans, and driven them all the way to Māyāpur.

Pañcaratna Dāsa, from New York, unexpectedly found a new home in Māyāpur, and simultaneously took up a large part of the responsibility for organizing this year’s festival.

**Pañcaratna Dāsa:** Guru-kṛpā Swami’s men were there trying to help get the festival organized, but the Māyāpur temple actually had only ten or twelve devotees available to help, so I was asked to help. By the time the festival started, I was in charge of half of it. Prabhupāda wanted to bring some devotees from the West to manage projects and temples in India, so after I started doing the festival management, the managers in Māyāpur asked me to stay there. Madhudviṣa Swami even took me to see Prabhupāda to discuss this.

To convince Prabhupāda that I should stay in Māyāpur, he said, “Śrīla Prabhupāda, we know that Māyāpur is your most important project.”

“No,” Prabhupāda strongly replied, “New York is my most important project.”

Prabhupāda was thinking that maybe I should go back to New York, because that was where I’d been serving for years, but I also wanted to stay in Māyāpur.

Eventually Prabhupāda said to me, “Well, if you also want, then you please stay.”

Temple leaders arrived from Great Britain, others from Europe and Australia, and there was a strong presence of American *sannyāsīs* and temple presidents. By March 8, 150 Western devotees were there. On March 9, sixty-five devotees from the United States arrived, and on March 10, a plane landed in Calcutta, carrying 350 North American devotees, including more than seventy from the Rādhā-Dāmodara Traveling *Saṅkīrtana* Party (RDTSKP). Eight buses carried them to Māyāpur.

**Bhakti Rāghava Swami:** In that airplane were Tamal Krishna Goswami, Viṣṇujana Swami, and Śrī Śrī Rādhā-Dāmodara. Traveling with *sannyāsīs* was special for me because in our little Canadian temple in Ottawa we rarely saw such personalities. For *maṅgala-ārati* we devotees circumambulated Rādhā-Dāmodara in the plane aisles, so that we could all have Their *darśana.* Going to India was like a dream come true for me. Little did I know it would be the first of seventeen consecutive Gaura Pūrṇimā festivals I’d have the good fortune to attend.

**Prahlādānand Swami:** I came with the Rādhā-Dāmodara party. Tamal Krishna Mahārāja and Dhṛṣṭadyumna took all our money to pay for their onward tickets, so I was left with ten rupees for the whole festival, and that made it a little austere.

**Guruprasāda Swami:** I found India very nice at that time. Practically no one had TV’s. There wasn’t much advertising—fewer billboards—more like Vedic times. I was happy to see that. I didn’t really experience much culture shock. I had more of a culture shock when I returned to the West and saw how horrible everything was.

**Ramāi Swami:** We used to come into Calcutta—the Australian devotees—through Madras or Bombay. Like the North American and European devotees, we also had *kīrtanas* on the plane. We once had to stay overnight in Bombay, so we camped out at Santa Cruz Airport and had bucket showers in the toilets. We had *maṅgala-ārati* and a class—the whole morning program.

**Jananānda Dāsa:** I was with the devotees from London. We reverently took dust from the feet of passengers and customs officers, from the airport floor—everything. It was like: “Wow! Residents of Bhāratavarṣa!” In the bus we had nonstop *kīrtanas.* It was exciting approaching Māyāpur. We were sticking our heads out the windows, searching for our temple. Finally we saw the saffron building standing in a field, just like in the photographs we’d seen. “Haribol! We made it!” Those were exciting moments of our lives.

I took a long train journey to Calcutta with a large group of Bombay devotees. After arriving in Calcutta at Howrah Station, we had to catch a ride to a different train station to catch a train to Navadvīpa. And from the Navadvīpa station we rode rickshas to Gaṅgā-ghāṭa. Then we crossed the Gaṅgā by boat and caught rickshas to Candrodayā Mandir. It was much more austere than taking a flight from New York to Calcutta. For the Western devotees, buses would be waiting outside the airport to bring them to the gates of the Candrodayā Mandir. It took us longer to travel to Māyāpur than it did devotees coming from the West. For Indian devotees, it was not an easy journey.

Since devotees from the third world, including India, could not afford to pay a high festival fee, we got the worst accommodations. That meant whatever was left. I didn’t mind, because we used our rooms only at night. Most of the time we were in the temple room or in the *prasāda* hall or out on *parikramās* or outside speaking to guests. Once you’re fast asleep, it doesn’t matter what category of accommodation you are in. Besides, some austerities are welcome and beneficial. We had not come to Māyāpur to enjoy comforts.

The first two floors of the Lotus Building were occupied by Guru­kṛpā Swami’s Nāma-haṭṭa party and the RDTSKP. Being elite book distributors, these devotees were offered some of the comfortable rooms. The GBC men, *sannyāsīs,* and life members filled the rest of the building.

Śrīla Prabhupāda let the Library Party use the room on the roof that he had been using from ten till four for his massage, bath, lunch, and nap. The group—Satsvarūpa Mahārāja, Suhotra, Ghanaśyāma, Cāru, Mahābuddhi, Kālakaṇṭha, Śeṣa, Mahā-dyuti, and Śrī Galima—kept their belongings in the room, but slept on the roof. Satsvarūpa Mahārāja informed Śrīla Prabhupāda that two of the Library Party men didn’t come, just so they could man a BBT booth at the Association for Asian Studies convention in Toronto.

The Long Building was still a construction site, the floor, bare concrete slabs. On top of the slabs (the roof of the ground floor), the management erected tents of split bamboo and tarpaulin to give some sort of shelter. A reverential mood was needed to tolerate the austerities. Baths were taken at hand pumps. Most toilets were just holes in the ground.

**Janānanda Dāsa:** We were put into rooms along the front wall—eight or ten to a room. I slept on the veranda my first night and was almost eaten alive by mosquitoes. The next morning the devotees said they’d never seen anyone with so many bites. My whole body was swollen. But I didn’t care. I also didn’t mind running to the toilet every hour with diarrhea, because the whole idea of the festival was to have Śrīla Prabhupāda’s association. Anyway, the next night I used a mosquito net. We all tied up our nets in this small room, leaving little space to walk about. The ceiling fan was running full blast to keep the heat down, and, boy, was it noisy.

Pisimā had shown up in Māyāpur a few weeks after Prabhupāda’s arrival, and this time Mahāmāyā, who was living in Māyāpur, became her servant. Years later, Mahāmāyā told many endearing anecdotes about Pisimā in her memoir about Prabhupāda. She said that of all of Prabhupāda’s family members, only Pisimā appreciated his spiritual standing in the same way that his disciples did. Hari Śauri said in his diary that when thirteen members of Prabhupāda’s family visited him around Gaura Pūrṇimā, he was warm and cordial and made sure they were accommodated, but he remained impartial and didn’t give them special attention, being above bodily identification.

The festival officially began on March 11. Just before class started, Prabhupāda extended his personal welcome. His message of heartfelt appreciation that so many devotees had come for the festival was greeted with cheers. He was again apologetic. He knew the conditions. There was a water shortage and the drinking water was impure. Most devotees were not used to such austerities and suffered severely. Prabhupāda advised them to tolerate all the inconveniences and be joyful because “this is Caitanya Mahāprabhu’s place.”

## Sannyāsīs and Temple Presidents Conflict

The day after Prabhupāda arrived in Māyāpur, he dictated a letter requesting the GBC members to arrive in Māyāpur early: “It has come to my attention that the GBC members have been unable to fully participate in the Māyāpur festivities because of the annual meeting. Therefore, I am asking all GBC members to come to Māyāpur early and hold the meeting in my presence beginning the morning of March 6.”

Satsvarūpa Dāsa Goswami describes in *Śrīla Prabhupāda-līlāmṛta* that as the festival drew near, some of the *sannyāsīs* had arrived in Māyāpur and begun discussing with Śrīla Prabhupāda the controversial issue of the role of *gṛhastha* men in ISKCON and the influence of women and children on the *sannyāsīs* and *brahmacārīs.* The *sannyāsīs* Gargamuni and Guru-kṛpā were suggesting that ISKCON should be structured to separate the renounced orders and the preachers from the householders. One reason was so that the householders would not be a financial burden. Hṛdayānanda Goswami thought it was all right for the *gṛhasthas* to live in the temples.

Viṣṇujana Swami maintained a strict principle of not preaching to women. He advised Tamal Krishna Goswami, “Whenever you make a woman a devotee, you lose one man,” referring to the fact that the women would eventually get married , and the men would be diverted from preaching. Several years of this kind of preaching had created a problem for the women.

**Viśākhā Dāsi:** Women were no longer viewed as partners in a spiritual renaissance. Rather, they were seen as personifications of the illusory energy, who threatened to cause men to deviate from their noble spiritual quest. Women were tolerated more than welcomed. They had to sit through many discouraging and disparaging lectures in which the intelligence, motives, and capabilities of women were scorned or criticized.

The Rādhā-Dāmodara party stood accused of luring unmarried men from the temples by offering them the freedom of traveling with *sannyāsīs,* after warning them that they would become materially entangled by working under the *gṛhasthas* who were temple presidents.

Śrīla Prabhupāda expected the GBC to discuss and settle these matters. He advised the *sannyāsīs* and GBC men not to discriminate on the basis of the external body. All devotees, he said, were part of Kṛṣṇa’s family; the dress didn’t matter—white or saffron or sari.

This was the first year that the GBC elected a chairman and secretary. Tamal Krishna Goswami became the first GBC chairman, and Satsvarūpa Mahārāja, the first secretary. When the GBC resolutions were read to Prabhupāda, he expressed disapproval of several of them, such as the one that said that single women with children but no husband could not live in the temples. The GBC struck the resolutions that Prabhupāda disliked.

But when the temple presidents heard the remaining GBC resolutions, many objected. They knew that Prabhupāda had approved them, but they thought that some *sannyāsīs* might interpret them in a way which would cause a split in ISKCON. Prabhupāda took the side of the dissenting temple presidents and advised that a small committee of *gṛhasthas* should define how they would live, and then the GBC resolutions could be revised. To him, real unity meant advancing in Kṛṣṇa consciousness, and factionalism would hamper that.

Prabhupāda repeatedly quoted the verse *kibā vipra, kibā nyāsī, sūdra kene naya, yei kṛṣṇa-tattva-vettā, sei ‘guru’ haya,* thus dispelling the contention that *āśrama* determines the quality of a devotee. Whoever knows the science of Kṛṣṇa, whoever is fully engaged in the service of Kṛṣṇa, Prabhupāda stressed, that person is a pure devotee. A *sannyāsī* could preach, but he could also manage: A *gṛhastha* could manage, but also preach. Although Prabhupāda had already brought up these points, now he was settling the issue completely. This discrimination, he said, must stop; the GBC should remove all resolutions discriminating against *gṛhasthas.*

**Viśākhā Dāsi:** Although Śrīla Prabhupāda’s words were unequivocal, and from that day on the Rādhā-Dāmodara party never again had the same mood or influence, thereafter the attitudes toward women in the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement never had the original, familylike warmth that Śrīla Prabhupāda had emanated in the early days.

Śrīla Prabhupāda was pleased that the GBC acted in its role of guiding and managing ISKCON. With his help, at least now the stage was set for an uplifting festival.

## Morning Walks: Wit and Wisdom

Quite often Prabhupāda walked upstairs on the roof of the Lotus Building and took in the surrounding panoramic view. The roof was forty-five feet wide and 150 feet long. It had been decorated with *tulasī* plants, potted plants, and shrubs, making it a pleasant place for Prabhupāda to walk and chant in the early hours. He would circumambulate the two centrally located rooms, closely followed by an entourage of *sannyāsīs* and GBC men.

Other times, such as when the arrival of many senior men overcrowded the roof, he would come down from his room and meet a group of devotees waiting for him at the bottom of the steps. The gurukula children greeted Prabhupāda with beautiful *kīrtana* melodies, which Prabhupāda liked to hear. They would chant and dance in front of him. Women were not allowed on the walks, but Viśākhā Dāsi was the exception, because she was the official photographer. She joked that her camera was her passport to the walks. Some ladies begrudged the exclusivity.

**Gaṅgāmayī Dāsi:** We would videotape Prabhupāda starting and returning from his walks. We didn’t go with him after it became exclusive. We’d sometimes hear something about his walks, but his gestures, body language, and mannerisms—we missed all that. That’s a shame, because Prabhupāda had such a wonderful personality. Unfortunately, we didn’t get to hear those conversations for years—not until the recordings came out or were published.

Janānanda, however, didn’t begrudge not being able to go on the walks:

The whole point of the festival was to hear Śrīla Prabhupāda’s lectures. I really felt that the walks were reserved for the *sannyāsīs* and others who asked questions about the global activities in ISKCON, which I didn’t think was any of my business. Seeing Prabhupāda coming back from the morning walks was a sweet experience. I remember him walking up that approach road. We’d be down at the foot of the Lotus Building, dancing and chanting and watching Prabhupāda come closer step by step. It filled us with joy.

**Satsvarūpa Dāsa Goswami:** The devotees at the Māyāpur festival would hang on Prabhupāda’s every word. They discussed whatever he said and how to carry it out. There were inevitable clashes of ego among leaders who sought to be prominent, but Prabhupāda said that was material.
