With an astounding ability to manage us all, Prabhupāda extracted service from each person and kept us together as a more or less harmonious camp. Everything was for Kṛṣṇa and for spreading Kṛṣṇa consciousness to the world. For vision, we looked to Śrīla Prabhupāda. We all acknowledged that we could not see, but we had full faith that he could see. He was compassionate toward the suffering of the world, and he could defeat the nonsense. No one could defeat him. We walked beside him on the morning walks like children yet like soldiers, ready to fight the enemy on his word.

**Sunitā Dāsi:** Every time I saw Prabhupāda walk by in the morning with a lopsided garland, I’d get angry. “Why don’t they offer the garland properly?” I wondered. But Prabhupāda didn’t care. I was the one who was asked to make those garlands for Prabhupāda every time I went to Māyāpur. It was some kind of good karma I had—wherever I went, I was asked to make garlands for him. I tried to make nice garlands from all the beautiful flowers in Māyāpur, with pretty centerpieces, because, Prabhupāda liked that. He once said that flowers are the smiles of the Lord. But whoever garlanded him would often put the garlands on lopsided, and my work on the centerpieces wasn’t shown to good advantage.

**Anuttama Dāsa:** I was a new *bhakta* from America, and I was asked to turn off the lights throughout the building as soon as the sun came up. I was told that it was Prabhupāda’s pet peeve that we wasted energy. So my service was to go to the roof, and as soon as it was light enough to see my hand, I’d run down from floor to floor and turn off all the lights in the halls and other public areas.

On March 11, I joined Prabhupāda on his morning walk. He was on the roof, surrounded by a large group of senior men. This was my first year as a *sannyāsī,* so I had the privilege of free access to Prabhupāda. I offered my obeisances and was about to join the group when Prabhupāda kindly greeted me: “Hare Kṛṣṇa.”

In the same breath, he asked, “So why you are becoming a *paramhaṁsa?* This is *paramhaṁsa* dress.” He pointed at my clothing.

The whole retinue had stopped, and I was facing them all, with Prabhupāda in the center. I was in the limelight. I immediately realized that the cloth I was wearing was not bright saffron. It looked dull and faded.

“I have to dye it today,” I said, embarrassed.

Prabhupāda rebuked me: “Don’t become *paramhaṁsa* immediately. *Paramhaṁsa* is the last stage of *sannyāsa.* The first stage is *kuṭicaka,* then *bahūdaka,* followed by *Parivrājakācārya,* and then the final stage, *paramhaṁsa.* Therefore *paramhaṁsa* stage is the topmost.”

“Do the *paramhaṁsas* wear white?” I humbly asked.

Prabhupāda resumed walking and explained that the *Parmahaṁsa* is not under any rules and regulations. He is not actually in the material world. He is on the stage of *mahā-bhāva.* In the *kuṭīcaka* stage, one lives outside the village and accepts foodstuffs from his family. When one is a little experienced, he lives by *mādhukarī.*

*“Mādhukara* means the bee,” Prabhupāda explained. “He collects a little honey here, a little honey there. He does not collect a lump sum of food from any one place. To any *gṛhastha* he can say, ‘Please give me a little piece of bread.’ That is not difficult for them—‘All right, take.’ A traveling *sannyāsī* may go to many *gṛhasthas,* begging a little piece from everyone. So he is called *bahūdaka.* Then, when he’s further experienced, preaching place to place, country to country, that is *Parivrājakācārya.* And when he has sufficiently preached, he can sit down anywhere. That is *Parmahaṁsa.”*

The topic ended there, on that philosophical note. Prabhupāda didn’t pursue it, and I was relieved to be off the hook. For some time there was no talk. A chilly breeze blew across the roof, and from the main gate the melodious *śahnāī* music became conspicuous.

Then again Prabhupāda spoke: “In every big temple there is a *śahnāī.*All throughout the year, morning and night. And not only temples, but also rich men’s houses. At night they go to bed hearing the *śahnāī* and early in the morning they wake up by hearing it.”

“People keep cocks and hens for waking up,” I said.

“Yes,” agreed Prabhupāda. “What is that called? Alarm. *‘Kaa-kaa-kaa:* Get up please. *‘Kaa-kaa-kaa,* please get up.’ Nature’s alarm.”

Amused by Prabhupāda’s excellent mimicry, the devotees laughed.

Guru-kṛpā Swami complained, “But sometimes they crow at twelve o’clock at night.”

Prabhupāda gave a befitting reply: “Just to cheat you, because you sleep too much.”

The devotees burst into laughter again.

“Because you do not rise early in the morning, they are sometimes engaged in cheating you.”

From a philosophical deliberation upon *sannyāsa* to the practical matters of *śahnāī* music and waking up early, Prabhupāda was enriching us with his wealth of wisdom and sharp wit.

**Subhāga Swami:** On a walk Prabhupāda stopped and pointed at the ground, saying, “What’s this?”

The devotees didn’t say anything, and Prabhupāda called me and repeated: “What is this?”

I looked closely and saw Prabhupāda pointing to a small mound of rice.

I said “This is *lakṣmī,* Prabhupāda.”

In India, *lakṣmī* refers to any type of opulence, and grains are one type, because we live by them.

Prabhupāda said, “Didn’t your parents teach you anything?” So I bent over and picked up the rice and put it in my pocket.

“And what will you do with it?” he asked.

I said, “I will give it to the kitchen.”

Prabhupāda said, “You were born and raised in India. Don’t forget what your parents taught you.”

Our parents and Śrīla Prabhupāda taught us that we should respect every morsel and grain of food. One could have easily overlooked or ignored a little pile of grain, but in the future you may be in a situation where you need just that amount of rice to save your life. As much as you waste, that much you will be short of in times of need.

## Highlights of the Temple Program

**Kāliyaphaṇi Dāsa:** For several days in a row, I closely observed Prabhupāda receiving *guru-pūjā.* I strategically situated myself in a special place. I crawled through the legs of the devotees and crouched down in front of the *vyāsāsana,* next to the parapet, resting my chin on it. There I watched Prabhupāda receive *guru-pūjā.* I was impressed by how transcendental he was to the worship. He wasn’t thinking “All this worship is for me.” I was reduced to tears every time.

Prabhupāda lectured this year on Prahlāda Mahārāja pacifying Lord Nṛsiṁhadeva with prayers. That section of the *Bhāgavatam* had not yet been published, which only increased the devotees’ eagerness to hear it.

“Prahlāda prayed: My Lord, O unlimited reservoir of transcendental qualities, you have killed my father, Hiraṇyakaśipu, and saved me from his sword. He had said very angrily, ‘If there is any supreme controller other than me, let Him save you. I shall now sever your head from your body.’ Therefore I think that both in saving me and in killing him, you have acted just to prove true the words of your devotee. There is no other cause.”

Śrīla Prabhupāda elaborated on the reasons for Lord Nṛsiṁhadeva’s appearance:

One reason is *mat-prāṇa-rakṣaṇam*—saving the life of Prahlāda. Another is *sva-bhrtya-ṛṣi-vākyam ṛtaṁ vidhātum. Sva-bhrtya:* Brahmā is the servant of Kṛṣṇa. Brahmā did something very obnoxious by offering the entire benediction to Hiraṇyakaśipu. Hiraṇyakaśipu was a very cunning materialist. He asked to become immortal, but when he learned that immortality was not possible, he thought, “I am an intelligent diplomat. I can screw out the fulfillment of my desire indirectly.” All creatures have this cheating propensity, even the cats and dogs. You know the story? A dog with flesh in his mouth saw his reflection in the water and wanted to take the flesh from the mouth of the other dog, his reflection, and he lost both. This cheating—misuse of intelligence—is going on, but that is never successful. It will never be successful because Kṛṣṇa is always more than you are. Kṛṣṇa can cheat you more than what you know. Therefore Hiraṇyakaśipu, who considered himself intelligent and wanted to cheat Kṛṣṇa and His servant, Lord Brahmā—actually he was cheated. The demon was killed, and the benediction given by Brahmā was kept intact, and Prahlāda Mahārāja was saved. That is the action of the Supreme Personality—in one stroke He is doing so many things.

Nobody can do anything unless it is sanctioned by God. A devotee always says, “If Kṛṣṇa desires, it will be done.” That is the fact. So when Hiraṇyakaśipu was very proud, unnecessarily challenging Prahlāda, Prahlāda Mahārāja knew: “Unless Kṛṣṇa desires, you cannot kill me.” If one is proud and challenges the authority of the Supreme, he will not succeed. In Kali-yuga this demonic spirit is very, very prominent. Everyone is thinking, “I am God.” So it is Caitanya Mahāprabhu’s mercy to cure all these madmen. They speak all nonsense. We should be careful not to accept their proposal. We shall stick to Kṛṣṇa consciousness. We shall accept what Kṛṣṇa says and reject everything else. Then we will be saved.

Prior to the festival proper, after one *Bhāgavatam* class, Prabhupāda had turned to Nitāi-chānd Dāsa and told him to give a synopsis of the class in Bengali for the benefit of the local guests. Prabhupāda seemed pleased with his effort, and this became a regular feature of some classes.

**Gaṅgāmayī Dāsi:** At the end of class, when Śrīla Prabhupāda would get off the *vyāsāsana,* there would be a lot of competition as to who would give Prabhupāda his cane. Whoever got the cane would give it to Śrīla Prabhupāda. Many devotees wanted to serve Śrīla Prabhupāda at once: “Let me be the one to give you the cane today.” There was intensity and love behind it.

**Pārvatī Dāsi:** After class, the devotees accompanied Prabhupāda with *kīrtana* out to the stairway. He passed very close to Pisimā. Pisimā was so humble that she came to hear Śrīla Prabhupāda’s classes, though she didn’t understand English. Once, she reached down and picked up a rose that was crushed under Prabhupāda’s foot. She immediately put it in her mouth and ate it. I remember thinking that Prabhupāda was pretty special, and she was special, too.

The devotees who were there to witness Prabhupāda’s circumambulation and ringing of the altar bells often recall this as their happiest memory of the festival.

**Śeṣa Dāsa:** Prabhupāda used to do things that would get us excited and inspired. Everyone was going crazy while he was ringing the bell. It was a very nice part of the whole festival. I didn’t follow behind him, but went back and forth in front of the altar. If you went behind the altar, then you got behind a big crowd and couldn’t see Prabhupāda. He really had us under his control. He would ring those bells, and everyone would start jumping—just as though he had us on a string. The louder he rang them, the higher we jumped.

**Pañcaratna Dāsa:** On the *parikramā* of the deities we could really understand that Prabhupāda was our leader. He was showing us the path, and we were following in his footsteps, making sure not to fall too far behind or stand too far in front—and close enough so that we didn’t lose track and follow just anyone. Prabhupāda used to ring the bell and turn around with a big smile on his face. I remember that being one of the nicest things.

The Māyāpur festival *kīrtanas* were the best of all. When some of the top *kīrtana* leaders in the world got together for a festival in one big hall in Māyāpur, all sorts of beautiful singing went on. Some of them would play whompers and gongs, others would play *mṛdaṅga* as they led. Dīnanātha Dāsa, a black American devotee, had a tremendously powerful and melodious voice, and Śrīla Prabhupāda liked to hear him sing. He used to chant all night on Ekādaśīs. He very successfully led a few evening *āratis* and got many devotees to sing harmoniously. Prominent *kīrtana* leaders like Viṣṇujana Swami and Jaya Śacīnandana Dāsa, a scholarly *brahmacārī* from New York, showed all their skill in *kīrtana,* and usually there would be a wild blast-off during the *āratis.*

**Anuttama Dāsa:** I was somewhat put off by the roughness of some of the *brahmacārīs* during *kīrtana.* I think that it was primarily Guru-kṛpā Swami’s men. They’d bump into some of the devotees and knock them down. I was only twenty-one, six feet tall, and quite healthy, so I wasn’t threatened by their aggressiveness. But I remember feeling uncomfortable about how this must have intimidated some less sturdy devotees or the ladies, for whom there was little space left to dance.

There were always devotees jumping wildly and running back and forth. *Kīrtanas* would be such a wild and ecstatic thing. The Rādhā-Dāmodara men danced and jumped high all the time. You’d come out sweating. The intensity was the closest we got to appreciate the ecstatic feelings of Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu. It was soothing. You wanted it to continue. When it stopped, although you were tired, you’d be looking forward to the next session. The *kīrtanas* would go on for as long as an hour over the scheduled time, and finally the organizers had to put a time limit on them so that the rest of the programs could go on.

## All Sorts of Festivities

One evening just before the festival began, some local people performed *lāṭhi khel,* the martial art of stick fighting, for Prabhupāda’s pleasure. On the lawn adjacent to the guesthouse, dozens of villagers paired up and staged mock fights. Others formed a circle, dancing and twirling their bamboo *lāṭhis.* Still others blew huge flames high into the air. Prabhupāda enjoyed the display from the balcony of the Lotus Building.

Prabhupāda was strongly in favor of the annual *parikramās,* and emphasized that all the devotees attending the festival should try to go. After morning *prasāda,* large groups would make pilgrimages by foot, bus, or boat. During this particular festival we were also transported in trucks with metal floors and no roofs. It was extremely hot that month, but we were packed into trucks that normally carry sand. This was a special austerity.

Prabhupāda also emphasized that everyone should take part in a twenty-four-hour *kīrtana.* Each temple would have assigned shifts, once a day or once every two days. If a *yātrā* was small, then two *yātrās* would be on the same shift.

**Jananānda Dāsa:** The devotees took part in the twenty-four-hour *kīrtana* with such sincerity. There were a lot of devotees keeping it going. Not just one or two devotees hanging in there, but twenty or thirty. It was a serious feature of the festival.

**Ghanaśyāma Dāsa (later, Bhakti Tirtha Swami):** I spent most of my time at the festival serving *prasāda.* I’d rush through whatever else I had to do so that I could serve *prasāda.* And I was thinking that if I can also serve those serving *prasāda,* then I’ll be even more of a servant. Then, when I leave, I’ll have really dropped a lot of attachments and gained some good impressions.

In the *prasāda* hall, in the afternoons, announcements were made about additional classes being held. Pañcaratna would shout out what was going to happen for the rest of the day. Many classes were given on the banks of the Gaṅgā and some in the temple room. Everyone relished them. Unfortunately, one speaker was problematic.

**Bhakti Caitanya Swami:** Siddhasvarūpa gave a really mental class on how we should not judge others; otherwise, we are not being humble. At the end he did a strange thing. He stood up and started a *kīrtana,* but it wasn’t an ordinary *kīrtana.* It was a “haribol” *kīrtana.* He simply screamed “Haribol, haribol, haribol, haribol,” and played the *kartālas.* It was weird. He was apart from mainstream ISKCON and had some followers. His group was known as the Haribols. They couldn’t work under ISKCON authorities. Still, Prabhupāda encouraged him to continue his efforts to preach Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

**Anuttama Dāsa:** I was a new book distributor from Denver, and I didn’t know many of the devotees from the Rādhā-Dāmodara party, but I’d always heard how staunch and fired-up they were. That year it was hot, and one day in the middle of the afternoon I was walking on the veranda of the Lotus Building, where the Rādhā-Dāmodara men were staying. I was trying to read *Bhagavad-gītā* and memorize verses to stay awake and beat the heat. All the Rādhā-Dāmodara men, despite advice to the contrary, were sprawled out all over the floor, fast asleep. While I was walking and reading I had to step over their snoring bodies. I was surprised to see such staunch book distributors succumbing to the heat. I remember thinking, “These guys aren’t so tough.”

The photo exhibit this year was the best ever. Prabhupāda had informed the BBT that he wanted one booth at the festival showing photographs of all our temple buildings around the world. The BBT staff from Los Angeles arrived with 250 enlarged photos of ISKCON temples, deities, *Bhāgavatam* paintings, and book reviews. The enlargements—mounted and covered with protective film—soon went on exhibit. Prabhupāda was very satisfied, especially with the book reviews. Some of the reviews were read aloud as he and the devotees toured the exhibit. In India, the printing of his books in local languages was getting underway, and Prabhupāda looked forward to wide sales and to standing orders from Indian libraries, just like in the West.

Prabhupāda visited the exhibit twice, but he complained about the shed in which the exhibit was held. It was made from old tarpaulin and crudely woven fencing, and access to the exhibit was up a slippery, muddy slope, because it was near the pond.

Prabhupāda inspected the photo exhibits every year and would often be amazed at the magnitude of his devotees’ efforts to distribute books, open temples, distribute *prasāda,* and propagate the holy name. It greatly encouraged the devotees to see Prabhupāda enlivened. They in turn became enlivened many times over. Preaching for Prabhupāda’s pleasure produced a special taste.

Besides all this, the bullock cart from the Hyderabad farm arrived, under the leadership of Gopeśvara Dāsa, after completing a 1,500 kilometer journey. And two big German buses arrived, under the supervision of Haṁsadūta, who was going to use them for traveling and preaching in India.

## Darśanas—Intimate Occasions

*Darśanas* were more or less informal. Devotees sat on sheet-covered mattresses and raised questions. Prabhupāda would sit behind his desk and speak *Kṛṣṇa-kathā.* Sometimes he would receive news of the preaching around the world or lovingly examine photographs of new deities. Śrīla Prabhupāda’s room filled up fast in the evenings. Pisimā would stack the cotton-covered bolsters into a pile, creating a little extra room.

**Mahābuddhi Dāsa:** I felt very fortunate to serve Prabhupāda during the *darśanas.* We had a chance to see him in the company of his godbrothers. Sometimes Prabhupāda had me read to them the reviews of his books written by professors from different parts of the world. Prabhupāda would explain to them in Bengali that these professors were highly respected.

Bhūrijana Dāsa remembers Prabhupāda’s words during *darśanas* as being like “ . . . sweet ripe fruits falling from a tree.”

**Annadā Dāsi:** Abhirāma was sitting right by Prabhupāda’s table, and Prabhupāda had his hand on a bump on Abhirāma’s forehead. Prabhupāda was asking about it in a fatherly way.

Later, devotees who were displeased with Siddha-svarūpa started expressing their negative feelings about him. Prabhupāda said, “In my opinion, since he has come so far to see me, there must be some love. Kṛṣṇa’s love is unconditional, just like a father’s love. No matter what the son does, the father loves the son.” Prabhupāda was showing that his love was also unconditional.

**Śeṣa Dāsa:** It was the perfect time to go and get intimate association with Prabhupāda, but I was afraid to go. Another devotee had been telling me about these wonderful *darśanas,* but still I didn’t go. I felt I would be intruding upon Prabhupāda. This thought used to bother me all the time. But now I really regret missing those opportunities.

**Paṅkajāṅghri Dāsa:** My fondest memories are of Śrīla Prabhupāda giving *darśana.* We would always look forward to that the whole day. At five, we’d finish our service, take bath, and go up and see Prabhupāda. I couldn’t say anything because I was only a mouse, but at those times I felt completely satisfied to watch Śrīla Prabhupāda with my godbrothers.

The Australian devotees had a short but sweet *darśana.* They presented Prabhupāda with $1,350 in *guru-dakṣiṇā* and sixty-nine kilos of their famous ghee. That evening, a transcendental transaction took place: Prabhupāda sold the ghee to the Māyāpur temple for twenty-five rupees a kilo.

**Bāsu Ghosh Dāsa:** Prabhupāda once preached to someone who didn’t want to hear what Prabhupāda had to say. I interrupted Prabhupāda and chastised the man. I said, “If you want to hear Prabhupāda, you must be submissive.” Prabhupāda did not say anything to me, so he must have approved of my saying it.

**Kāliyaphaṇi Dāsa:** Śrīla Prabhupāda was giving *darśana,* and the room was packed. I came to the door to see if I could squeeze in. He was not actually speaking at the moment when I arrived. There was a silence when I came in the door. I was just a new English devotee, and he looked over to see who was at the door. I was hesitating, so he nodded, indicating that I should come in. I was very moved to be noticed by Prabhupāda.

**Bir Kṛṣṇa Goswami:** I was in Navadvīpa buying brass paraphernalia from a person whom Prabhupāda had called a cheater. This particular man had some good brass, but I was with a devotee from Latin America, who didn’t use much discrimination. He said, “My guru said you are a rascal and cheater.” Later, in the middle of a *darśana,* this brass *wālā* came in, really angry, and said to Prabhupāda, “Your disciple said that you said I was a cheater.” Prabhupāda didn’t deny it. He said, “Well, that is all right. Just take some *prasāda.”*

**Hari Śauri Dāsa:** More and more visitors were coming for *darśanas.* Most were simple villagers, who sat in his room and just stared. So Prabhupāda told his servants to stand at the door and give out sweets. The guests should not be asked to come in, but neither should they be refused entry. Those who simply wished to look could come to the door, offer their *praṇāmas,* and receive *prasāda;* they would go away happy. Those who had something specific to discuss or who wanted to enter were asked to fill in a form stating their name and business.

Prabhupāda wanted everyone who visited him to get *prasāda,* even to the point of sending his secretary to catch someone who’d left without taking *prasāda.*

**Hṛdayānanda Mahārāja:** Prabhupāda would look at me and say, “Make sure that person gets *prasāda.”* I would take some out of the jar, quickly pay my obeisances, and run out the door. I loved it, as I was young and full of energy, and this gave me a chance to run for Prabhupāda. I remember running down the hall, chasing people and calling out: “Here! Prabhupāda wants you to have this.”

This was my first extended stay in India. One day, when I went out on the balcony to see Śrīla Prabhupāda, I expressed my enthusiasm for his preaching projects in India. I said, “Your American disciples are beginning to appreciate India. When we first arrived, we could not understand why you were so busy here, but now we see that India is the most important place on the planet.” Prabhupāda raised his eyebrows and said decisively, “It is the most important place in the universe.”

Naturally, Prabhupāda himself took care of special guests he’d invited. In March, Mr. M. N. Chaudhari, a favorable West Bengal official in charge of planning and development, visited with his wife and children. His cooperation would be important in acquiring land in Māyāpur, so Prabhupāda received the Chaudharis graciously, and he had nice *prasāda* served to them on the balcony facing the Jalaṅgi. He watched them take *prasāda,* sitting next to them on a rocking chair, making sure everything was served nicely. He spoke in Bengali, joking and conversing.

**Subhāga Swami:** There were many guests from Calcutta. When ministers and officials came, Prabhupāda would preach to them, and he instructed us how to treat them nicely. Prabhupāda would show his godbrothers his books, show them the favorable, scholarly reviews, and give them reports of his global preaching activities. Some of them were appreciative, and Prabhupāda treated them respectfully.

Prabhupāda continued to invite the Gauḍīya Maṭha devotees to join us in preaching in India. Since we had the finances to manage their temples and they could preach in the local languages, it would be a successful combination. He observed that none of them were doing much preaching. Rather, they were only seeking funds to support their temples. Still, he got no immediate response from them.

Occasionally Prabhupāda spoke to his Bengali- or Hindi-speaking guests in their mother tongues. Although the Western devotees would have preferred to understand what was being said, just being with Prabhupāda was transcendentally valuable. However, for the benefit of the Western devotees, Prabhupāda mostly spoke in English.

During the evening *darśanas* the devotees would hear the conch being blown for *ārati,* and Prabhupāda would expect them to go to the *kīrtanas.* Shortly afterward, at six-thirty, Jananivāsa would walk around the Lotus Building with a clay pot full of burning frankincense. The idea was to fill the rooms with smoke, then open the windows and turn on the fan. As a result, all the mosquitoes would fly out.

Jananivāsa would enter Prabhupāda’s room and ask whether he wanted the frankincense that evening. Prabhupāda would simply nod his head to answer. Sometimes the smoke in his room would become so thick that it would be difficult to see anything, but Prabhupāda wanted the windows and doors to remain closed for some time. He would be content in the room full of thick smoke.

Although frankincense smoke does not really make you cough, the psychological effect of sitting in a room full of smoke may cause some to start coughing. But not Prabhupāda. Hari Śauri would be coughing and his eyes would water, but Prabhupāda liked the smoke.

**Śeṣa Dāsa:** I was one of the devotees who guarded Prabhupāda’s room at night. I had a shift for a few hours. Our duty was to make sure no one disturbed Prabhupāda. Maybe that’s how I got into this mood—“Don’t disturb Prabhupāda.” I would sit in a room before the entrance to Prabhupāda’s room and check people out: “Who are you? What are you doing here?” Prabhupāda was well protected in that regard. I don’t know if it was his mood, but it was definitely the mood of those around him. One time I was moving some of his belongings to his room. No one was in the room, so I walked out onto the back balcony and saw Prabhupāda taking his massage there. Hari Śauri motioned to me to go away. That mood was there.

**Padyāvali Dāsi:** During the festivals Śrīla Prabhupāda was the focus—his association, his *darśanas,* and his classes. The atmosphere was surcharged with his presence. But senior godbrothers used to aggressively keep everyone away from him. Of course, they had to protect Śrīla Prabhupāda. But I always had the feeling that I couldn’t go to Śrīla Prabhupāda that I couldn’t get near him. Although I had come ten thousand miles and paid three thousand dollars, that didn’t mean anything to some people. The senior devotees saw him constantly, but they didn’t accommodate those from around the world who did not have that much association. It was just expected that you wouldn’t even ask. And if you did ask, you were made to feel that you were inconsiderate.

Prabhupāda was the main attraction of the festival. Everyone wanted to see him, hear him, and be with him. He was a natural attraction because he continuously thought of how to teach his disciples Kṛṣṇa consciousness from all angles.

## Vaiṣṇava Humility

Prabhupāda was giving a class about how the demigods, having failed to pacify Lord Nṛsiṁhadeva with their prayers, pushed Prahlāda forward and said, “You pacify the Lord. He appeared for you.”

**Śrīla Prabhupāda:** Prahlāda Mahārāja said, “I am *ugra-yoni*: born in a horrible family. So how can I offer my prayers to the Supreme Personality of Godhead?”

He was born in an *ugra* family, but he was a devotee. He was qualified, because a devotee does not belong to any of the material qualities. Anyone engaged in devotional service transcends the material qualities. Prahlāda Mahārāja, though born of a demonic father, was completely pure, completely on the transcendental stage. Therefore he could offer prayers to the Lord. Others cannot. So a Vaiśṇava is very humble.

Although the demigods and everyone admitted he was on the stage of *prema,* he was a Vaiṣṇava and he was thinking, “I am the son of Hiraṇyakaśipu. How can I offer prayers?” This is Vaiṣṇava humility. That is the teaching of Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu. One has to become humbler than the grass and more tolerant than the tree. Then he can become a perfect Vaiṣṇava, because as soon as you become a Vaiṣṇava, your father will be your enemy, just like Prahlāda Mahārāja. What was his fault? Five years old—simply he became a Vaiṣṇava, Kṛṣṇaconscious, and his father became his enemy. This is the position. So a Vaiṣṇava has to tolerate in order to make progress in spiritual life. He has so many enemies, even at home. So don’t be hesitant and don’t be agitated by such circumstances. Prahlāda Mahārāja had to suffer so many things. Still he remained Kṛṣṇaconscious, and ultimately he saw Lord Nṛsiṁhadeva and had the privilege of offering prayers.

**Sunitā Dāsi:** I always remember how sweetly Prabhupāda spoke about Prahlāda Mahārāja during that festival. In every lecture that I hear about Prahlāda Mahārāja, I remember how beautifully Śrīla Prabhupāda spoke about him.

## Gaura Pūrṇimā

Hṛdayānanda Goswami didn’t feel well, so instead of going on the morning walk, he stayed in his room to recover. A Venezuelan devotee came to see him, to ask if he’d recommend his wife for second initiation that day. Prabhupāda had told the leaders that devotees should be carefully recommended, so Hṛdayānanda Mahārāja replied, “Bring your wife, and I’ll interview her to make sure she’s qualified.”

As Prabhupāda was leaving for his morning walk, he noticed Hṛdayānanda Mahārāja’s absence. Someone said that he was ill. Then the lady who was to be interviewed walked up the stairs, just as Prabhupāda was going down. She looked up, and there she was, right in front of Prabhupāda.

Śrīla Prabhupāda asked, “Where are you going?”

The surprised lady replied, “Oh, I am going to see Hṛdayānanda Mahārāja.”

**Hṛdayānanda Goswami** ***uvāca*****:** This didn’t look good for me, and Prabhupāda manifested his transcendental anger and sent someone to get me. The devotee came running and said, “Prabhupāda wants to see you. You’re in big trouble.”

I came running and was only halfway down to offer my obeisances when Prabhupāda started chastising me. I felt ecstatic being chastised by Prabhupāda, but I covered my face while offering obeisances because I didn’t want Śrīla Prabhupāda or the devotees to see the big grin on my face.

As I stood up, he said, “What is this nonsense?” I can hardly remember what else he said. I joined the walk. That kind of got me over my illness.

Later, I concluded that I should take a vow to never again speak to a woman. I went to discuss this idea with Śrīla Prabhupāda. Prabhupāda was in a jovial mood and dismissed the incident after hearing the explanation.

“Well, Prabhupāda, I was thinking of taking a vow to never again speak to a woman.”

Prabhupāda just laughed and said that there is no such injunction.

At the morning class, Puṣṭa-Kṛṣṇa Swami read: “My Lord, You have appeared in different incarnations—as human beings, as animals, as a great saintly person, as demigods, as a fish, and a tortoise. In this way you maintain the whole creation and kill the demoniac principles in every age. My Lord, You protect the principles of religion. In the Age of Kali You do not assert yourself as the Supreme Personality of Godhead. Therefore You are known as Tri-yuga, or the Lord who appears in three *yugas.”*

**Śrīla Prabhupāda:** Today is the appearance day of Caitanya Mahāprabhu, so we must discuss this very thoroughly. Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu appeared in order to give mercy to the fallen souls, who are so foolish that they cannot take to Kṛṣṇa consciousness. He is personally teaching how to take to it, and that is this *kīrtana.* In Prayāga He was chanting, and Rūpa Gosvāmi offered his obeisances: namo *mahā-vadānyāya Kṛṣṇa-prema-pradāyate.* He is the *channa-avatāra-*covered. He is Kṛṣṇa, and He has come to give you *Kṛṣṇa-prema,* but He’s acting like a Kṛṣṇa devotee. This is covered. He is not commanding, “Now you do this!” Because people misunderstood: “Who is this person commanding?” Even some rascal, so-called scholar said, “It’s too much to demand.” They have remarked like that—yes, sophisticated persons are thinking like that. But our process is to submit. Today is the appearance day of Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu, and this is the birthplace, this Māyāpur, and you are all present here. It is good fortune. Always remember Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu and chant *Śrī*-*Kṛṣṇa-caitanya prabhu-nityānanda* ś*rī-advaita gadādhara śrīvāsādi-gaura-bhakta-vṛnda.* This will make your life perfect.

Before noon an initiation ceremony was held in the colorfully decorated temple room. Twenty-five devotees received first initiation; fifteen, second; and seven were awarded *sannyāsa:* Jagat-guru, Haṁsadūta, Rāmeśvara, Ādi-Keśava, Dhṛṣṭadyumna, Śatadhanya, and Pramāṇa. Again Śrīla Prabhupāda quoted the verse that had become a theme for the festival: *kibā vipra, kibā nyāsī, sūdra kene naya:* whether one is a *gṛhastha* or a *sannyāsī,* it doesn’t matter-he can become a guru by understanding Kṛṣṇa. And Prabhupāda stressed that because people are suffering all over the world for want of Krishna consciousness, the *sannyāsīs* have to do so much preaching work.

This movement is so important that everyone from every part of the world should take part in it, and then there will be peace in the world. This is Caitanya Mahāprabhu’s mission. In this land, in this very spot we are sitting now, He appeared for this mission. And it is being done. People from all parts of the world are responding. Anyone who knows the science of Kṛṣṇa, he can spread this Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement. And there is great necessity, great necessity. The preaching work is meant for the *sannyāsīs.* So we have got some *sannyāsīs* who are doing very nicely, so today we shall make a number of *sannyāsīs* more to spread Kṛṣṇa consciousness all over the world. And those who are going to take *sannyāsa,* they should remember how much responsibility they have got. So live like a very strict *sannyāsī.* Caitanya Mahāprabhu Himself took *sannyāsa* at the age of twenty-four. So it is not that in old age one has to take *sannyāsa.* No. Rather, the young men—just like Caitanya Mahāprabhu did personally.

Prabhupāda was giving a lot of instructions and association to the new *sannyāsīs,* encouraging them to preach as much as possible. Before the festival, he had already sent two new *sannyāsīs*—initiated in February—away to preach.

**Guruprasāda Swami:** I could type, so someone gave me the *sannyāsa-mantra* to type. It was for the initiates to memorize. I was thinking at that time that it would be nice to take *sannyāsa.* That’s what got me thinking about taking *sannyāsa.* I didn’t know what the mantra meant, but it sounded nice.

**Sunitā Dāsi:** Prabhupāda was initiating an Indian man. (Unfortunately, he later gave up Kṛṣṇa consciousness.) He came forward to get his beads, and Prabhupāda asked, “What are the regulative principles?” He gave the customary reply. Then Prabhupāda gave him his name. As the new initiate reached out to take his beads, Prabhupāda asked, “And what about chewing paan?” Everybody burst out laughing, and Prabhupāda also started laughing. It was funny because that man had a paan shop right outside the Māyāpur temple.

Everyone fasted until after moonrise, which was at twenty after six, and most of the devotees walked down to the Ganges to bathe. Jayapatākā Swami told them that, according to *śāstra,* whoever bathed in the Ganges at Māyāpur during moonrise on Gaura Pūrṇimā would be liberated.

People came to the temple from all over Bengal that day. The attendance was more than 100,000 pilgrims, and the devotees distributed *halavā* to any guest who wanted it. In Calcutta, people had seen the billboards advertising the Gaura Pūrṇimā festival, and by word of mouth tens of thousands of villagers had heard about Śrī Māyāpur Candrodayā Mandir. In only four years it had become the center of attraction in West Bengal, spreading the glories of the birthplace of the *yuga-avatāra.*

## A Family Photo

On the morning of March 18, right after class, all the devotees gathered on the lawn beside the Lotus Building for a group photo. The devotees stood in a semicircle, with Śrīla Prabhupāda—surrounded by *sannyāsīs*—in the center. All the *sannyāsīs* held their *daṇḍas,* and Prabhupāda held his cane in the air, his right hand in his beadbag. He wore a garland of marigolds that almost reached his knees. I was there, a few *sannyāsīs,* to the right of Prabhupāda. Hari Śauri stood to the right of Prabhupāda, and a devotee fanned Prabhupāda from behind with a peacock fan. We all felt like a big family, with Śrīla Prabhupāda as our spiritual father, and we were very happy to be there.

Unfortunately, there were so many there that morning who are no longer with us today. It is such a sad thing. I wish that we could again—by our efforts to unite Śrīla Prabhupāda’s family—come together once more, keeping Śrīla Prabhupāda in the center, just as he was in the center of that photograph and the center of our lives on that bright spring morning in Māyāpur.

## Prabhupāda Visits His Childhood Neighborhood

**Puṣṭa-Kṛṣṇa Swami:** When Prabhupāda was finally finished conducting the Māyāpur festival, I asked him if he was tired. Prabhupāda said, “What? Tired? So many people are coming and hearing about Kṛṣṇa. When you’re preaching, you feel refreshed.”

On March 22, Prabhupāda left Māyāpur. A loud, rousing *kīrtana* awaited him as he stepped into his new Mercedes. After a stopover in Calcutta, he was going to attend programs in Delhi and another city on the way to Vṛndāvana, accompanied by a few hundred devotees.

In Calcutta, Prabhupāda visited his childhood neighborhood one evening. He stopped at the old Rādhā-Govinda temple to have *darśana* of his beloved deities, and he showed the devotees where he’d held Ratha-yātrā festivals as a child.

**Śrīla Prabhupāda:** Rādhā-Govinda are very old deities of the Mullik family—I think not less than two hundred years old. This whole quarter belonged to the Mullik family. Our house was just down the street, behind the building called Govinda Bhavan, and we had the opportunity of seeing Rādhā-Govinda from our very childhood. They are the inspiration of my devotional life. I asked my father then, “Give me Rādhā-Govinda deities; I shall worship.” So he gave me small deities. Those deities still exist. I have given Them to my sister. ·

Then I introduced Ratha-yātrā. My Ratha-yātrā was performed very gloriously. My father used to spend money. In those days ten rupees, twenty rupees was sufficient. I hired a *kīrtana* party and went with a small group of friends. According to our children’s imagination, it was very gorgeous.

What I am doing now is the same thing: Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa worship and Ratha-yātrā. We are now performing Ratha-yātrā in San Francisco, Philadelphia, London, Melbourne, and Paris. So the same thing—the same Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa temple worship and same Ratha-yātrā—on a bigger scale. But the same thing was begun as play from this quarter, this *thākurabari* \[land or property of the deity\].

Anyway, this Rādhā-Govindaji is my life, the beginning of my spiritual life. And after so many years, still Rādhā-Govindaji have dragged me here. It is Their kindness. And my father was a very pure Vaiṣṇava. So these opportunities we got. Now it is developed in a wider scale. It is all Kṛṣṇa’s arrangement, maybe from my previous life. But you are cooperating, you Americans and Europeans, so we are spreading the mission of Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu. When He wants to spread the loving affairs of Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa, He takes the form of Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu and very kindly gives love of Kṛṣṇa. It is now spreading all over the world. So about this movement, it may be that these Rādhā ­Govinda deities are the inspiration. You are all fortunate that you have come here. So let us offer our obeisances.

Prabhupāda later showed his disciples other places where he spent his childhood. “Our house was here,” he said, pointing from the car. “I was playing here, from that street to this street. That was our house. This was my school. In this ground we used to play soccer.”

“How far would you carry Jagannātha?” one devotee asked.

“From our house to Rādhā-Govinda’s temple, coming and going, with a procession of children and *prasāda* distribution. My father used to encourage. And in those days if my father would spend twenty-five rupees, it was a great festival. Now that is the same as four hundred rupees. For children’s play, four hundred rupees is not a small amount.”

## Chapter 9. The 1976 Vṛndāvana Festival

Śrīla Prabhupāda had remained in Calcutta for two days, while most of the devotees traveled to Delhi and Vṛndāvana by train or plane. Those who’d stayed behind in Māyāpur and were only now making their way toward Vṛndāvana had the rare and unexpected pleasure of flying with Prabhupāda on March 24. At the Calcutta airport they held a small *kīrtana.*

**Sunitā Dāsī:** Alarkā Dāsī had just bought a *mṛdaṅga* and was playing it. We were chanting *“Jaya,* Prabhupāda!” She was scared that Śrīla Prabhupāda would ask her to stop, but Prabhupāda said, “Go on, go on,” encouraging her to play.

As Sunitā, Alarkā, and others followed Prabhupāda through the airport, he came to a small gate, just big enough for one person at a time to pass through. He stood back to let an unknown lady pass through first. Devotees were impressed by Prabhupāda’s gentlemanly dealings. The flight was an enjoyable, once-in-a-lifetime opportunity for some devotees. Prabhupāda sat at the front of the plane, and the devotees sat in the rows behind him. Hari Śauri served breakfast to Prabhupāda, who ate a little and told Hari Śauri to distribute the rest. The lucky recipients of Prabhupāda’s *mahā-prasāda* honored it appreciatively.

Prabhupāda would spend five days in Delhi before continuing—via Modinagar—to Vṛndāvana for the festival. While Prabhupāda was still in Calcutta, I took a train to Delhi. I was expected to assist Tejiyas Prabhu, the president of the Delhi temple, with the *paṇḍāl* program that Prabhupāda would attend.

Gurudāsa Swami, I, and others received Prabhupāda at Palam Airport. Mr. Laxman Agarwal, Prabhupāda’s host, greeted Śrīla Prabhupāda with a garland of marigolds interspersed with rupee notes. Sometimes, to garland someone with rupee notes is considered undignified, but Prabhupāda didn’t seem to mind it and accepted his host’s offering. Some devotees regarded this incident as Lakṣmidevī herself bestowing a benediction upon Prabhupāda. Mr. Agarwal owned a famous lighting company called Sylvania & Laxman Ltd. During this visit he allowed Prabhupāda to use his Mercedes, until Prabhupāda’s Mercedes arrived from Calcutta.

ISKCON New Delhi was located in a single-story flat at 19 Todarmal Lane in Bengali Market. Prabhupāda had kindly stayed there in the past, but this time, at Mr. Agarwal’s insistence, Prabhupāda stayed in the Agarwals’ modern two-story house in the prestigious suburb of Chanakya Puri, Delhi’s neighborhood of international diplomats. Prabhupāda had stayed at his house before, and he’d helped Prabhupāda meet prominent government officials, to deal with the American devotees’ visa problems. Prabhupāda’s large, paneled, ground-floor room was simply furnished, though French windows opened onto the back lawn. The house was full of paintings of Kṛṣṇa decorated with pure gold from the famous city of Tanjore, in Tamil Nadu. Laxman Agarwal had deities of Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa on his altar.

The devotees offered *Guru-pūjā* and *kīrtana* to Śrīla Prabhupāda every morning in Mr. Agarwal’s ground-floor hall. One day, there was also a *pāda-pūjā* ceremony. A melodious *kīrtana* set the right mood as Prabhupāda’s lotus feet were washed with honey, yogurt, milk, ghee, and rose water. This *pañcāmṛta* was then distributed to the eagerly awaiting devotees and guests.

On March 25, in the evening, Prabhupāda was invited to speak to the New Delhi Rotary Club at the Imperial Hotel. Many important members of the city’s social elite gathered to hear his lecture on *Bhagavad-gītā:*

“. . . Kṛṣṇa chastised Arjuna: ‘You are talking like a very scientific, learned scholar, but you are a fool—number—one because you are accepting this material body as the self.’ This is ignorance. Those who understand the self as the combination of earth, water, air, and fire are defined in the *śāstra* as being no more intelligent than the cows and asses.”

## The Delhi Paṇḍāl Festival

The three-day *paṇḍāl* program took place on a weekend at the Rama Lila Grounds, located off Asaf Ali Road, the headquarters of India’s multinational medical companies. The program was scheduled to begin on Friday evening so that office workers and shop owners could conveniently attend on their way home from work. The advertisement in the newspaper read in part: “Be sure to come and experience the third international Hare Kṛṣṇa festival. Participants are coming from Europe, America, Africa, etc.” Another form of publicity for the *paṇḍāl* program was *harināma-saṅkīrtana.* Taking advantage of the presence of many foreign devotees, we chanted through the streets of Delhi during the day and distributed flyers and made announcements. The devotees were so fired up that they missed *prasāda* to go on *harināma* in the city, then to the *paṇḍāl* in the evening.

Our *paṇḍāl* was quite colorful. Many loudspeakers blasted the *mahā-mantra* in all directions. Prabhupāda’s arrival at the *paṇḍāl* created a rush of people. Śeṣa Dāsa said, “People crowded around his car to the point that it was a disturbance. It wasn’t nice.” After this somewhat chaotic reception, Prabhupāda took his seat on the *vyāsāsana,* and I proceeded to introduce him to the audience.

I took this opportunity to glorify Śrīla Prabhupāda to my heart’s content. First, I pointed out his accomplishments on all continents. Then I said that just as a country normally exports its best products, Prabhupāda was exporting the holy name of the Lord and the message of the *Bhagavad-gītā.* Because of the exceptionally high quality of these products, they now were in big demand worldwide, and Prabhupāda’s transcendental import-export business was successfully fulfilling the demand.

“And what do you think Prabhupāda has imported?” I asked the audience. I paused to see if anyone would give an answer, but no one did. Then I motioned to the Western devotees sitting on the dais. “These Western-bodied *brāhmaṇas* are Prabhupāda’s imported products,” I told them. There was a big round of applause in recognition of Prabhupāda’s achievement.

I was pleased to have this opportunity. Often during public lectures, Śrīla Prabhupāda would ask his disciples to address audiences. He always took great pride and pleasure in seeing the impressions his followers made on the audience by their presentations. These opportunities provided encouragement and training. By giving us a chance to speak before large audiences, Prabhupāda was preparing us for the future. That evening a few other *sannyāsīs* also spoke. The first day of the program was a success. Over four thousand people attended. The receptive crowd asked many questions after Prabhupāda’s lecture.

**Bhakti Tīrtha Swami:** During the questions and answers, a man said, “My mother always sings the glories of her Kṛṣṇa. She sings *bhajans* and offers *bhoga* at night. Kṛṣṇa comes and eats it.” The audience started to laugh, but Prabhupāda stopped them and said: “Yes, Kṛṣṇa can be served like that. But once you see Kṛṣṇa, your life will never be the same. No longer will you take *paan* and tea, smoke cigarettes, read newspapers, and watch cinemas. Just like when the sun rises, automatically the darkness disappears. You can interact with Kṛṣṇa, but it is not an ordinary thing.”

Prabhupāda was greatly pleased by the whole arrangement and stayed to watch a play by the devotees. That night, Śrīdāmā Dāsa, (one of the few initiated *gṛhastha* disciples in Delhi), a few other *brahmacārīs,* and I stayed at the Rama Lila Grounds to safeguard the books and equipment.

The next day, March 27, Śrīla Prabhupāda took his morning walk in one of Delhi’s largest parks, Buddha Jayanti Park, which commemorates the appearance of Lord Buddha. Throughout the park are plaques inscribed with Buddha’s teachings. The park is beautifully landscaped. Spacious lawns are divided by manmade waterways and connected by small bridges. Prabhupāda became inspired to create a similar park for Kṛṣṇa. At the end of the walk, Prabhupāda noticed an undeveloped parcel of land across the road, and suggested that this land be transformed into a Kṛṣṇa Jayantī Park.

Mr. Agarwal said, “Kṛṣṇa is a much more important figure in the history of India than Buddha. There should be a park in honor of Kṛṣṇa.” Prabhupāda encouraged him to look into the possibility of acquiring land through his government contacts.

Prabhupāda shared his vision of a Kṛṣṇa Jayantī Park by describing how there could be beautifully carved shrines for the various incarnations of Kṛṣṇa, nice lawns and gardens, streams and waterfalls, and other wonderful arrangements for Lord Kṛṣṇa’s pleasure. The visiting public would be enlightened with spiritual knowledge and pleased by the natural setting.

**Gopāl Kṛṣṇa Goswami:** Prabhupāda said we should arrange for deities of Kṛṣṇa and His incarnations in the Kṛṣṇa Jayantī Park—not like the stone statues in Buddha Jayanti Park, but regular deities that would be looked after. Unfortunately, the government did not give us that land.

Now Gopāl Kṛṣṇa Mahārāja is working on getting the park adjoining the Rādhā-Pārthasārathi Mandir in New Delhi. This could be transformed into a Kṛṣṇa Jayanti Park, according to Śrīla Prabhupāda’s desire. May Śrī Śrī Rādhā-Pārthasārathi bless this endeavor!

After breakfast, Śrīla Prabhupāda held a press conference in the Agarwals’ home. Reporters were invited to put forth questions, but the main purpose of the press conference was to increase publicity for the *Paṇḍāl* program. Śrīla Prabhupāda sat garlanded, gently fingering his beads, as the pressmen set up microphones and recording equipment. The reporters asked intelligent questions, making for an exciting interview.

**Reporter:** Aren’t we impure and God pure?

**Prabhupāda:** No, both are pure.

**Reporter:** Then why do we commit sins? God doesn’t.

**Prabhupāda:** Yes. At the present moment we are in an impure condition. That is material. The human form of life is a chance to be free from the impurity.

**Reporter:** Where does this impurity come from?

**Prabhupāda:** Just like you are healthy—but where does disease come from? You are not diseased now, but sometimes disease comes. This has to be cured; otherwise, we are pure, as pure as God.

**Reporter:** Then what is the way to purity?

**Prabhupāda:** That is *Bhagavad-gītā.*

**Reporter:** You mean the mere recital of *Bhagavad-gītā?*

**Prabhupāda:** Not recital. To understand and to act. The whole thing is described in the *Bhagavad-gītā.*

**Reporter:** Which particular teachings do you think can purify a man?

Prabhupāda explained that one has to revive the consciousness that one is part and parcel of God and understand what it means to serve God. Prabhupāda later explained the four regulative principles. He said that these are the four pillars of sinful life. If we want to know God, then we must be free from sinful life. God is pure, and if we want to approach God, then we must be pure.

One of the journalists commented that Śrīla Prabhupāda had been working for too long in the West and that India was not getting the benefit of his teachings and guidance. He asked if Prabhupāda had any plans to propagate Kṛṣṇa consciousness in India.

**Śrīla Prabhupāda:** India is so misguided that it will take some time. The *Bhagavad-gītā* was already there. But the Indians’ misfortune is that they are so neglectful. They don’t care. They say, “Why not Sai Baba? Why not this baba, that baba?” That is the difficulty. They have become over-intelligent.

We are preaching that Kṛṣṇa is the Supreme Lord. That is in the *śāstra*. But you won’t believe it. You’ll say, “Why is Kṛṣṇa God? Here is another God.” You’ll bring hundreds and thousands of gods. That is the difficulty.

We have ISKCON centers in India, but the people say “Hare Kṛṣṇa? We have heard it. We have seen *Bhagavad-gītā*.” If you become neglectful, that is the greatest offense. So India is an offender. It is India’s philosophy what I am preaching to others. Indians already know it, but they won’t take it. They have become so unfortunate. Suppose I give you one thousand dollars, but you don’t take it. Then what benefit will be there? India’s position is like that.

Prabhupāda told the press that he found all his disciples to be responsive and that he was indebted to them, because without their cooperation, he would not have written so many books. When asked if a disciple ever disagreed with him over spiritual matters, Prabhupāda laughed and replied, “Not unless he is a fool \[laughter\]*,* a damn fool!”

That afternoon at Mr. Agarwal’s home Śrīla Prabhupāda met Mr. Surendra Kumar Saigal, owner of the Tiger Lock Company, who asked Śrīla Prabhupāda to visit Aligarh, his hometown, to do a program there. Śrīla Prabhupāda agreed at once, setting the date at March 30. Prabhupāda’s party was already scheduled to visit Modinagar on the twenty-ninth, and now with Mr. Saigal’s invitation, we’d visit Aligarh on our way to Vṛndāvana.

Disaster struck the *paṇḍāl* on Saturday, March 27. A violent windstorm lifted the entire *paṇḍāl* into the air. We lost electrical power as the lines became disconnected. The seats were scattered, and the loudspeakers flew into the sky. For a few moments everyone was blinded by dust. The public quickly dispersed. Śeṣa Dāsa and Revatīnandana Swami were in the question-and-answer booth, where books and pamphlets began flying around. In the confusion Revatīnandana Swami fell against his *sannyāsa daṇḍa* and cracked it. The damage to the *paṇḍāl* was so great that there was no scope for continuing the program.

When the storm took place, Prabhupāda was on his way to the program in his own car, which had been driven to Delhi by Ṛddha Dāsa. Naturally Prabhupāda was concerned how the storm was affecting the program. Upon his arrival he found everything in disarray. He couldn’t lecture in such chaotic circumstances, so he asked to be driven back to the Agarwals’ home.

Across the street from Mr. Agarwal’s home was a triangular park where Mr. Agarwal had erected a small, colorful *paṇḍāl* for a private program with his friends and neighbors on Sunday morning. Prabhupāda sat on a *vyāsāsana* on a small stage and was again garlanded with rupee notes. With the large number of devotees attending, the program resembled a morning program of *guru-pūjā, kīrtana,* lecture, and *prasāda.* The only difference was the participation of a public audience.

I had the honor of leading the *kīrtana* that morning as we offered *ārati* to Śrīla Prabhupāda. While the *kīrtana* party sang and danced in front of the stage, devotees stepped onto the dais and offered flowers and prostrated *daṇḍavats* to Śrīla Prabhupāda. The guests were impressed to see the Western devotees falling at the feet of a *sādhu* in humble submission. Prabhupāda looked scholarly, with his *Bhāgavatam* open on the bookstand and his glasses on, ready to speak. By Mr. Agarwal’s influence, a crew from Doordarshan-Indian National Television covered the event.

In the afternoon Prabhupāda spoke for the last time at the Rāma Līlā Grounds. We rented another sound system and managed to get the power connected. We were in business again, but on a smaller scale, with just a stage and some of the *paṇḍāl.* Prabhupāda gave an excellent lecture and again stayed to watch a play: *The Entrance of Kali.*

Despite the interference caused by the weather, the festival overall was a big success. To our surprise, people came from colonies on the other side of Delhi. Usually, they would never have traveled so far. Five to ten thousand people attended, and the devotees sold many books.

**Kṛṣṇa Gopāla Dāsa:** Until ten at night, people stayed there. The public showed proper respect to all the devotees and Śrīla Prabhupāda. Tejiyas and I distributed *burfi prasāda,* and the people enjoyed it.

News of the *paṇḍāl* made the headlines. The local newspapers, both Hindi and English, carried reports and large photos of our program and of Śrīla Prabhupāda. The articles were shown to Prabhupāda, and he was pleased. One newspaper containing the interview accurately noted his main theme. The headline read, “Kṛṣṇa Forgotten in the Land of His Birth.”

## “Modi family, Ki Jaya!”

On March 29, several busloads of devotees followed Śrīla Prabhupāda’s car seventy kilometers to Modinagar, a town *(nagar)* founded by and named after the Modis. One of India’s leading industrial enterprises, the Modi Company manufactures tires, cement, cookware, and other things. The pious Modi family had invited Śrīla Prabhupāda and his disciples. In the middle of Modinagar the Modis had built a Lakṣhmī-Nārāyaṇa temple, said to have cost two million dollars.

In front of the temple Mrs. Modi and an enthusiastic reception committee welcomed Prabhupāda and his followers. There were deities on various altars in the temple, and Śrīla Prabhupāda moved from deity to deity, offering his obeisances to them all. The ISKCON devotees were right behind him, following his example. Of course, Śrīla Prabhupāda taught us that while a Vaiśṇava respects the demigods, he formally worships only *viṣṇu-tattva.*

**Śeṣa Dāsa:** It seemed like we were running after Prabhupāda to see what he was doing, so that we could do as he did. We took *darśana* of the demigods, including Śiva, Gaṇeśa, and Durgā. I felt as if Prabhupāda was taking us on a tour to see the different demigods. Our final destination was Lakṣhmī-Nārāyaṇa’s lotus feet. How we reacted depended upon him, so I tried to keep up with him.

Prabhupāda gave a short talk to the guests and devotees in a small hall at the back of the temple. Śrīla Prabhupāda was in a relaxed mood as he sat on a raised *āsana.* In the absence of her departed husband, Mrs. Modi was now the head of the Modi empire, but she sat respectfully on the floor to Prabhupāda’s left, wearing a garland she’d received from him. Everyone in the hall was beaming with delight as they reflected Prabhupāda’s inner joy.

After the arrival address Śrīla Prabhupāda and his disciples were escorted to the Modi mansion. Prabhupāda and his party stayed there. The family made all the arrangements—opulent quarters for Śrīla Prabhupāda and, of course, nice *prasāda* for everyone. The Modis also arranged a public program for Prabhupāda during the evening, held in an open air amphitheater next to the temple. A large local crowd turned up to see the foreign *sādhus.* Never before had even one foreign *sādhu* appeared in their town, and now busloads of them had shown up. ISKCON was the talk of the town.

Onstage was a gigantic, silver *vyāsāsana,* which looked like something from a previous *yuga.* It had small lions on either side and contained ornate, studded scrollwork, with an engraved peacock right above Śrīla Prabhupāda’s head. A large green gemstone adorned the heavy silver canopy over him. It seemed a befitting *vyāsāsana* for Śrīla Prabhupāda, who was *sākṣāad dharitvena,* a representative of Lord Hari directly embodying His qualities. Prabhupāda lectured in Hindi for the sake of his audience, and Prabhupāda’s Western disciples sat listening, despite not understanding a word.

**Tejiyas Dāsa:** Śrīla Prabhupāda publically showed appreciation for the Modi family’s service. After chanting *“Jaya* Rādhā Mādhava,” while saying the *prema-dhvani* prayers, Prabhupāda said, “Modi family, *kī jaya!* Modi family, *kī jaya!* Modi family, *kī jaya!”* He was happy that Mr. Modi had used his wealth to build the beautiful Lakṣhmī-Nārāyaṇa temple. He was also pleased with the way Mrs. Modi gracefully received the Vaiśṇavas from all around the world.

## The Aligarh Program

On March 30, in a caravan of several cars, we left Modinagar for Aligarh. This time I was traveling with Śrīla Prabhupāda’s staff and many other *sannyās*ī*s.*

Śrīla Prabhupāda stayed at the house of Mr. Saigal. Tejiyas had once thought that Mr. Saigal was sort of a Māyāvādī, because at one point he did not believe in the *mūrti-pūjā,* or deity worship. But in due course, by the association of Śrīla Prabhupāda and his books, Mr. Saigal had installed a beautiful deity of Kṛṣṇa in his residence. Upon seeing the deity, Prabhupāda encouraged Mr. and Mrs. Saigal to install a deity of Śrīmatī Rādhāraṇī, too.

“Why keep Kṛṣṇa alone?” he asked.

Prabhupāda held an afternoon *darśana,* and there was an evening *paṇḍāl* program at the Press Colony Grounds, attended by seven thousand people. A book table was set up at the main entrance, manned by Śeṣa. He was preaching to a student from the prestigious local university when Prabhupāda walked by with his entourage on his way to the dais. Śeṣa quickly offered his obeisances.

Prabhupāda spoke in Hindi, and again his disciples sat through his talk, which was a splendid lecture about the value of chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa. When he spoke in English in Bombay, I heard him make the point several times that many children in the audience didn’t know what he was saying, and because they were sitting and listening to the transcendental subject matter, they were receiving benefit.

**Gaṅgāmayī Dāsī:** Prabhupāda insisted that his disciples remain throughout the programs. We used to attend many outside programs in the homes of Vrajvāsīs, and Prabhupāda usually spoke in Hindi. Once, Gurudāsa Dāsa wanted to leave, but Prabhupāda forced him to stay to show respect to the host and to train us in Vaiṣṇava etiquette.

**Nāgarāja Dāsa:** I remember trying to follow Prabhupāda’s class by listening to the *Bhagavad-gītā* verses he quoted. At that *paṇḍāl,* I fanned Śrīla Prabhupāda with a peacock fan, taking turns with another devotee. A lot of the time, fanning is reverential or ceremonial, but at this *paṇḍāl* we had to fan really hard and fast because there were flies all over the place and we were trying to keep them off Śrīla Prabhupāda.

As the lecture wound down, Prabhupāda turned to his secretary and asked, “So how much time?’’ He wanted to know how long he’d been speaking. Although the lecture was only thirty minutes long, Prabhupāda wanted to make sure he hadn’t spoken too long.

## Back in Vṛndāvana

Early in the morning on March 31, we made the hour-long journey from Aligarh, through Raya, to Vṛndāvana, followed by several busloads of devotees. Prabhupāda arrived in time to greet the deities and give the *Bhāgavatam* class. The atmosphere was surcharged with excitement, because many devotees were visiting Vṛndāvana for the first time, and they now had their own temple in the holy *dhāma.*

Many devotees stayed at Fogal Ashram, where Rājaśekhara took charge of serving the devotees *prasāda.* Some local residents of Vṛndāvana still made fun of the devotees.

**Dānavīra Swami:** I was in Vṛndāvana getting my head shaved, and some teenagers came by and started making fun of me. I decided to start chanting Sanskrit *ślokas.* Then they became more respectful. Other people then criticized them for making jokes. It seemed that knowing *ślokas* made us legitimate.

## “Engage in Kṛṣṇa’s Activities.”

Prabhupāda sat tall on his *vyāsāsana,* and I approached him with sandalwood paste on a small tray. Sometimes the sandalwood paste was mixed with saffron, giving it a golden color. I applied the cooling paste to Prabhupāda’s forehead, then stepped back to offer obeisances, thinking I was finished. Prabhupāda called me back, touching his finger to his temples, to indicate that I had forgotten two important places.

I remember Prabhupāda singing *“Jaya* Rādhā-Mādhava” with his eyes closed and his head tilted. Prabhupāda started playing *karatālas first*, then the *mṛdaṅga* players joined in. He played for fifteen or twenty seconds, then began to sing. Sometimes he opened his eyes and briefly looked around. Then he would speed up the pace. By the third time through the song, the *mṛdaṅga* players were exerting themselves to keep up with him. When he stopped singing, a special silence settled over the crowd as he chanted the *prema-dhvani* prayers: *Jaya oṁ viṣṇupāda paramhaṁsa* ... When he chanted”... *ki jaya!”* he put special emphasis on the *“jaya,”* which made us feel that he was joining in with us.

Prabhupāda continued to give classes on the Seventh Canto, picking up where he’d left off in Māyāpur. In the next four or five days, he came to the end of Prahlāda’s prayers. Pacified by these prayers, Lord Nṛsiṁha offered Prahlāda a benediction.

**Śrīla Prabhupāda:** If Kṛṣṇa is pleased upon you, He can give you immense riches, as much as you want. That is wanted, instead of endeavoring yourself to earn money. People are engaged in earning money, working very hard day and night. So if there is such a thing that simply by pleasing Kṛṣṇa you can get as much money as you want, then why shall I earn money? The foolish people do not know it. It is practical. Just like I went to your country with forty rupees, and now I am getting money, as much as I want. This is practical. No businessman can earn with forty rupees—and within ten years—forty *crores.* There is no instance in history. This is because Kṛṣṇa is the proprietor of all riches. That is Bhagavān. The *bhakta,* of course, does not want anything from Kṛṣṇa. But Kṛṣṇa supplies him wealth when he requires it.

Prahlāda had mentioned ten prescribed methods to attain liberation: to remain silent, not to speak to anyone, to stay in a solitary place, to chant mantras silently, and others. Śrīla Prabhupāda explained that these are for persons who cannot control their senses. It is better not to talk, than to talk foolishly. Those who cannot talk about Kṛṣṇa talk all nonsense, so it’s better that they stop talking. But the real fact is that we cannot stop the tongue’s working. Therefore the tongue should be engaged in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. He continued:

In big, big meetings the politicians, the United Nations, are talking—“Now we have to do this. Then the world will change.” No, it will never change by such foolish talking. Better close this United Nations because it has not given any practical effect. But talking about Kṛṣṇa—just see how it is giving a practical effect. Here you are assembled, from Europe, America, Africa, Canada, and practically all over the world. You are the United Nations, simply by talking about Kṛṣṇa.

Śrīla Rūpa Gosvāmī says, “I have got only one tongue and two ears. So how can I serve Kṛṣṇa while chanting? If I had millions of ears and trillions of tongues, then it would be possible.” But so far as we are concerned—the conditioned souls—even sixteen rounds become a very, very difficult job for us, because we are accustomed to talking nonsense. One who cannot chant the Hare Kṛṣṇa mantra should stop his talking. Better remain silent. This is the recommendation by Prahlāda Mahārāja.

There are so many processes to get out of this material world, but Prahlāda Mahārāja and all the devotees, especially Caitanya Mahāprabhu, have recommended chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa. Very easy! And you benefit. You take it, and chant sincerely, without any offense. You don’t have to follow these other processes. It is not possible nowadays to be a very learned scholar in Vedic literature or to remain silent or to take some vow and remain in a solitary place. In Kali-yuga, we are too fallen to execute all these processes.

In Vṛndāvana you’ll find many devotees in very secluded places. But my Guru Mahārāja did not like this process—secluded. Sometimes, if you sit down in a secluded place, imitating Haridāsa Ṭhākura, then you’ll complain, “I am being disturbed in this way.” You must be disturbed, because your mind is uncontrolled. If you sit down to get some extra credit—“I have become so great a devotee”—it is cheating. You cannot imitate Haridāsa Ṭhākura. He met a beautiful, young prostitute in the dead of night, and she offered him her body, and he was a young man. He simply said, “Very nice proposal. You sit down. Let me finish my Hare Kṛṣṇa” *\[laughter\].*

Don’t laugh! It is a very serious thing. So you cannot do that. I cannot do that. It is not possible. So we should not imitate Haridāsa Ṭhākura. You cannot do that, because your mind is not yet trained up. It is for those in the highest perfectional stage—to sit down anywhere and chant Hare Kṛṣṇa and remain happy. That is not possible in the ordinary stages. Don’t try to do that. Always be actively engaged in Kṛṣṇa’s activities.

## Key Man?

A letter came to Prabhupāda from Haṁsadūta Swami in Māyāpur. He was organizing the Nitāi-Gaura Traveling World *Saṅkīrtana* Party in India, and needed a competent devotee who spoke Hindi. His twenty-five men were all new to India. Haṁsadūta Swami and I both liked doing *kīrtana* together, and therefore we had a friendly relationship. In Māyāpur, we’d had a few conversations about my joining his party. I’d been a *sannyāsī* only a few months; this was my first opportunity to travel and preach. But Gopāl Kṛṣṇa Prabhu (later to become Gopāl Kṛṣṇa Gosvāmi), my authority in Bombay, objected that I was an indispensable Bombay devotee.

Discussions between Gopāl Kṛṣṇa and Haṁsadūta Swami had gotten nowhere, so the matter eventually came before Śrīla Prabhupāda. Girirāja and I were also there, and Prabhupāda took the position of the judge as both parties presented their cases. Haṁsadūta said he needed me because I spoke Hindi and led *kīrtanas.* Gopāl Kṛṣṇa said I was a key man in Bombay.

Śrīla Prabhupāda, sympathetic toward my desire to travel and preach, resolved the issue by saying, “What is this ‘key man’? Kṛṣṇa is the key man. If he wants to travel and preach, let him go.”

So the matter was settled, and hard feelings on both sides were minimized. I wish that Śrīla Prabhupāda had stayed on forever to resolve our conflicts.

## Kātyāyanī Darśana and Initiations

During the morning walk on April 4, Prabhupāda noticed a Durgā temple and went a little out of his way to take *darśana* of Kātyāyanī, the goddess Durgā. Prabhupāda offered his *daṇḍavats* and began glorifying her: *sṛṣṭisthiti-pralaya-sādhana-śaktir ekā, chāyeva yasya bhuvanāni bibharti durgā.* This verse describes Durgā as the Lord’s shadow potency. One can worship her, Prabhupāda explained, to gain admission to Kṛṣṇa’s ṣervice.

**Jayādvaita Swami:** Prabhupāda had tears in his eyes as he spoke: “We are glorifying Durgā, but not in the way the materialists do—‘Give me this. Give me that’—but like the *gop*ī*s.* They were praying to Durgā: ‘You are always carrying out the orders of Kṛṣṇa, so you are very dear to Kṛṣṇa. Therefore please give us your blessings so that we can have Kṛṣṇa as our husband.’”

That day there was a large initiation ceremony. Thirty-five devotees received first initiation and almost the same number received second. The devotees sat in the temple courtyard, and Prabhupāda—shaded by an embroidered umbrella—was seated on a red *āsana* from his *prasāda* room, placed on the semi-circular side steps. In the intense April sunshine, the large *yajña* arena looked gorgeous. Ten pots containing coconuts stood around the arena’s border, and its surface was brightened by pastel-colored flowers and dyed rice flours. As some devotees came forward, Prabhupāda noted what country they were from—Iran, Brazil, America.

**Śrīla Prabhupāda:** We manufacture devotees from any part of the world*—kirāta-hūṇāndhra-pulinda-pulkasā.* This is Lord Caitanya’s mission. In every town and village on the surface of the globe, everyone should at least hear the Hare Kṛṣṇa *mahā-mantra.* Everyone should be interested in understanding the Absolute Truth, but this is Kali-yuga. People are slow, or bad, or victimized; practically nobody is interested. Therefore preaching is required to awaken people to spiritual consciousness. Kṛṣṇa personally comes to awaken this consciousness, and Kṛṣṇa’s servants also try to do the same business on behalf of Kṛṣṇa. Therefore Kṛṣṇa recognizes such preachers as very, very dear to Him.

Initiation means the beginning of receiving transcendental knowledge. Now, if you follow the rules and regulations—if you give up sinful activity— immediately Caitanya Mahāprabhu takes you. On one side, you should personally endeavor, and the other side, you should chant. That is the method of cleansing the heart. Everyone in this age is addicted to sinful life, but if one simply promises that henceforward he shall not act sinfully, then he goes back home, back to Godhead.

**Śeṣa Dāsa:** Nāgarajā and I both received second initiation. Later Hari Śauri called the second initiates, one at a time, into Prabhupāda’s room. Prabhupāda was sitting gravely when I came in. I offered my obeisances and sat next to him. He motioned with his hand that I should give him the thread. He was sitting cross-legged, and when I gave him my thread, he draped it over his knees. Then he asked me, “Do you chant sixteen rounds every day?” I answered, “Yes, Śrīla Prabhupāda.” Then he showed me how to count the mantras, and he had me repeat them after him. Prabhupāda picked up the thread and sort of tossed it, and it went right around me. That was it. I offered my obeisances, and he just returned to his grave mood. I appreciated Prabhupāda’s seriousness. Since that exchange, I’ve always liked grave pictures of Prabhupāda, in which he is not smiling, but doing serious business.

**Anuttama Dāsa:** After the initiation, there was a big feast. I felt horrible after the feast as a result of both eating a lot and being exposed to too much hot sunlight throughout the ceremony. The temple commander announced that all the new initiates must stay and clean the temple after *prasāda.* I was happy to stay, even though I felt ill, but I remember there were a lot fewer cleaners than there were initiates.

## Prabhupāda Strikes a Bargain and Opens a Bank

One morning we were walking in a park and came across a vendor sitting beneath a tree with a basketful of drumsticks, long green vegetable sticks that grow on trees. Prabhupāda stopped to see them. Then he began to bargain for them.

I thought, *Why should Prabhupāda be involved in this petty matter?* Being the only Hindi-speaking devotee present, I asked, “Should we buy some for you, Prabhupāda?”

But he didn’t want me to interrupt.

He responded, “No, no, I’ll talk with him. We can buy the whole thing.”

The bearded vendor quoted a high price, and Prabhupāda objected. The vendor must have realized that Prabhupāda was a seasoned buyer, so he drastically reduced his price. I assumed he was also calculating the pious credit that would come to him by selling his goods to a *sādhu.* They ultimately agreed on a price, and the vendor weighed the drumsticks on his iron scale.

“So, give him two and a half rupees,” Prabhupāda said.

When we heard the price, we were amazed. Gurudāsa Swami congratulated Prabhupāda.

“My father used to do that,” Prabhupāda said. “He’d buy the whole basket, which the vendor would sell cheap because otherwise he’d have to sit all day. All that *sabji* was not required in the family, and my mother became angry: “You are bringing so many vegetables; they are getting spoiled.”

Subhāga Swami also remembers Śrīla Prabhupāda purchasing a basket of farm-fresh vegetables in Māyāpur during a morning walk. The vendor followed Prabhupāda, carrying the vegetables back to the temple.

Later that morning, Prabhupāda attended the opening of a branch of the Punjab National Bank in the guesthouse. A bank had long been needed for the sake of convenience in receiving overseas transfers and handling the temple funds. The new bank window faced the reception room of the guesthouse, the bank office being located between the guesthouse and Śrīla Prabhupāda’s quarters. (The bank has since moved, and its former office is now part of Prabhupāda’s house.) Prabhupāda was in a jolly mood, and he and the bank manager exchanged jokes about bankers and banking.

**Hari Śauri Dāsa:** Śrīla Prabhupāda explained how Kuvera, the treasurer of the demigods, had offered Dhruva any benediction he wanted. Dhruva simply asked Kuvera to bless him with devotion to Kṛṣṇa. To the cheers of the devotees who had crowded into the room to listen, Prabhupāda said that in the same way he now had a bank that could give him any amount, but he simply requested the bank manager’s blessings that he remain Kṛṣṇa’s servant.

The bank manager then told a similar story, and it ended by requesting Prabhupāda’s blessings. He also told a joke about a man who was to have a heart transplant. He had the choice of a general’s heart, a businessman’s heart, or a banker’s heart. He chose the banker’s heart because it had never been used.

Prabhupāda reciprocated further in a light and humorous way by telling a story of how a poor man had only a single penny that he kept very nicely. He deposited it in the bank and stood waiting at the bank. After some time the manager came and asked him what he was waiting for.

The poor man told the manager, “It is said that money attracts money, and now that my penny is in your bank, I am waiting for my money to come.”

“That is true,” said the manager, “but now my money has attracted your money.”

Prabhupāda had everyone laughing at his story, and then in the presence of the manager and staff of the bank and a full house of devotees, he inaugurated the bank branch, cutting the ceremonial ribbon.

## Improprieties at Rādhā-kuṇḍa

Eager to visit the pastime places of the Lord and His devotees, we went on *parikramā* to Rādhā-kuṇḍa. Standing on the carved-stone steps leading into Her waters, we took in the serenity of this most sacred place. Our group soon separated, as the women gathered across the pond for their swim. The water in Rādhā-kuṇḍa is nondifferent from Rādhāraṇī.

Śrīla Prabhupāda told us we could bathe there—but we had to do it respectfully. Most of us bathed attentively. However, others seemed not to understand this simple instruction. They jumped into Rādhā-kuṇḍa and began splashing water and fooling around. A mood of enjoyment crept in, and some men began playing water games, sitting on one another’s shoulders and competing to knock over their opponents.
