**Daniṣṭhā Dāsī:** We’d been told this lake was so sacred that we should stand on the bank, offer prayers, and simply take Her water and pour some on our heads out of respect. Some men—we could hear their shouts—had dived in and begun splashing and swimming. To us women, it was unthinkable to sport in Her waters. We had prayed, not even conversing at first among ourselves, and we entered the lake in quiet supplication.

**Kāliyaphaṇi Dāsa:** We offenders were newish devotees. We just paid obeisances, put a few drops on our head like we were told, then dove in, splashing and laughing, leaving our reverence on the bank. We had a really good time, but when Prabhupāda heard about this, he was extremely disturbed. He said that from then on there would be no more bathing in Rādhā-kuṇḍa. He later changed his mind, I believe, but at that time he stopped it and said just to put a few drops on your head.

Prabhupāda became angry and disappointed. Apparently he instructed that there should be no more bathing. By this direction Prabhupāda was saving his disciples from committing further offenses. Rajaśekhara went back to Rādhā-kuṇḍa and bathed again, but this time gently and reverently. This time he did it right.

Śrīla Rūpa Gosvāmi says in *Upadeśāmṛta, tat premedaṁ sakṛd api saraḥ snātur āviṣkaroti:* “If one simply bathes once within the holy waters of Rādhā-kuṇḍa, one’s pure love of Kṛṣṇa is fully aroused.” Mahānidhi Swami’s book on Rādhā-kuṇḍa points out that Śrīla Prabhupāda, in his purport, talks about taking bath seven times, so devotees shouldn’t be confused by his instruction not to bathe there. In my humble opinion, it was a temporary prohibition meant to teach the inexperienced devotees to do it properly.

Mahāmāyā Dāsī considered the other *parikramās* the real highlight of the Vṛndāvana festival. The ladies and men traveled separately, and the ladies visited more holy places than the men. She liked Nitāi Dāsa, their tour guide, but thought that perhaps they visited too many places—for a few days in a row they returned at ten at night. When Śrīla Prabhupāda heard they were returning late, he instructed them to come back at the same time as the men. However, the next day their bus broke down, and they were stuck sitting in a Durgā temple, waiting for the bus to be repaired. Again they got back at ten, but this time it wasn’t their fault.

**Mahāmāyā Dāsī:** One special place was Mor Kurtīr, at Varṣāṇā. We walked there barefoot on a long forest trail, not knowing where we would end up. We were out in the wilderness. At long last we arrived at this small, two-room temple commemorating the place where Kṛṣṇa danced like a peacock and Rādhā watched. An elderly *pūjārī,* who looked like a hermit, welcomed us with *caraṇāmṛta.* He was amazed to encounter sixty Western ladies.

## “Kṛṣṇa-guru-kṛpā.”

Prabhupāda was relaxed at home in Vṛndāvana. Gurudāsa Swami found some secluded gardens for him to walk in. One was a serene and beautiful place, concealed behind a high brick wall. Some mornings Prabhupāda would be driven to a predetermined place and then take his walk. Other times he’d just walk on Chattikara Road, which at that time was bordered by open, pleasant land.

One morning Prabhupāda’s car went well beyond the outskirts of Vṛndāvana. When it stopped, Prabhupāda began meandering across an uneven field. Someone mentioned a Westerner who had been living in Vṛndāvana for nine years, but who would not even offer obeisances to Prabhupāda because of being influenced by Māyāvādī teachings. Just then, by coincidence, the farmer who owned the land came over to see us. He respectfully offered *praṇāmas* and told Śrīla Prabhupāda that he and his family felt greatly honored that Prabhupāda was walking on their land.

“Hare Kṛṣṇa,” Prabhupāda said to the farmer. Then to us he said, “This is the difference. One is living here nine years and he does not offer respect, and the farmer knows how to offer obeisances. *Māyāvādī bhāsya śunile haya sarva-nāśa:* your spiritual progress is finished if you associate with Māyāvādīs.”

Prabhupāda went on to explain the position of Vrajvāsīs: “Generally Vrajvāsīs are naturally Kṛṣṇa conscious; otherwise, how is this simple farmer offering *praṇāmas*? Even without a spiritual master they are already elevated to Kṛṣṇa consciousness, because the spiritual master is within­*caitya-guru*. Simply by living here, if they do not commit any sin, they’ll go back home, back to Godhead.”

I recalled aloud Prabhupāda having once said that Kṛṣṇa acts like their spiritual master.

To this, Prabhupāda simply responded, *“*Kṛṣṇa *guru kṛpā.”*

I understood this to mean that the Vrajvāsīs have the mercy, or *kṛpā,* of Kṛṣṇa, who Himself acts as their guru.

**Jayādvaita Swami:** One time we were walking near Rādhā-Govinda temple—the old Govindaji Mandir. Some devotees told Prabhupāda that they’d heard an explanation that Vṛndāvana acts like a lotus. The different petals open and close, and that is how Kṛṣṇa is transported from one place to another. Although the distances seem so great, He is able to go from one place in the morning to another in the afternoon in this way. Someone was prompting Prabhupāda to comment on this information. Prabhupāda said Kṛṣṇa takes very big steps. Just as we can cross a room in a few steps whereas it takes an ant hundreds, similarly, Kṛṣṇa’s walking is not like ours; His body is spiritual. Prabhupāda didn’t get into the lotus story at all.

**Nāgarāja Dāsa:** While Prabhupāda was on his walk, most devotees would go back to Fogal Ashram. It was *japa* time. I would go back to the temple and greet Śrīla Prabhupāda as he returned from his walk. I was surprised not many devotees were walking with him. I could walk really close to Śrīla Prabhupāda. Normally, you couldn’t get very close to him. In some ways it was disappointing, because it seemed that people were getting so used to seeing Prabhupāda every day that they weren’t endeavoring to go and see him every time he was available. They didn’t take advantage of it. Other people might disagree with me, but that’s the way I felt.

## Evenings in the Courtyard

In the evenings, instead of giving *darśana* in his quarters, which were too small for all the visiting devotees, Prabhupāda would meet with us in the large temple courtyard, under the *tamāla* tree. During the construction of the Krishna-Balaram Mandir, Prabhupāda had stopped the contractor from cutting down the tree. He reasoned that *tamāla* trees were becoming rare and that they are special because they’re associated with the Lord’s pastimes.

Sitting either on a cushion or a rocking chair, Prabhupāda would sometimes speak and sometimes have other devotees speak. The dealings with Śrīla Prabhupāda during these sessions were sweet and friendly. They had a soothing effect on the consciousness. The devotees would all sit around him, and visitors would also join in.

One day I had my turn to speak. If there were more visitors than devotees, we would address our talk to them. Those who knew Hindi would speak in Hindi, and Śrīla Prabhupāda would simply listen.

I once asked the visitors, “Why do you all call this temple *Angrej ka Mandira* \[English temple\]? We have not imported the deities from England. There is no deity of *Angrez* \[an Englishman\] in this temple. Don’t you see Krishna-Balarām on the altar? This is a Krishna-Balarām temple, not an *Angreji* temple.”

## Śrīla Prabhupāda was appreciative.

One beautiful evening a tall, bulky fellow came into the courtyard during a discussion. He had long, flowing black hair, like Parmahaṁsa Yogananda, but it looked greasy. He wore bright reddish robes and had big brown beads around his neck. He simply sat down. Prabhupāda would often invite people to ask questions, and after a while Prabhupāda motioned to him to say something.

The man stood up and spoke: “It is nice that all you Westerners have come here to Vṛndāvana and that you are chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa , Hare Kṛṣṇa, Hare Kṛṣṇa . In this way you are gradually becoming Vṛndāvana, becoming Vṛndāvana, becoming Vṛndāvana, just as we have already become Vṛndāvana.”

Prabhupāda stopped him: “Thank you very much. That’s enough. Now, Tejiyas, you please say something.” Prabhupāda eloquently silenced him, and that was the end of that.

## The First-Anniversary Celebrations

In an attempt to build up public appreciation for ISKCON as the first anniversary of the opening of the Krishna-Balaram Mandir approached, Śrīla Prabhupāda held a three-day program, starting on April 6. In 1975, his efforts to get the local people to accept the new temple and the Western devotees had included inviting local priests to install the deities and local dignitaries to be present. Similarly, this year he invited leaders from Vṛndāvana and Mathurā, even impersonalists, to visit and speak a few words of appreciation. Śrīla Prabhupāda invited anyone who was prominent, popular, or influential, so that the Krishna-Balaram Mandir would be fully integrated into Vṛndāvana. The programs took place from five till seven-thirty, just inside the main entrance of the temple. Many distinguished guests came to offer appreciations of Prabhupāda’s work.

During the second evening program, the leading visitors spoke in Hindi, and Śrīla Prabhupāda reciprocated by having some of his *sannyāsīs* speak in Hindi. Then he spoke in English, urging his visitors to accept his foreign disciples as genuine Vaiṣṇavas.

At the third program, some Gauḍīya Maṭha men arrived with the High Commissioner and District Magistrate from Mathurā. They praised Prabhupāda’s efforts to glorify the holy *dhāma* worldwide.

The first anniversary of the opening of the temple fell on April 9, Rāma-navamī. Everyone fasted till four in the afternoon. To commemorate the opening, ISKCON held an evening *paṇḍāl* program at Kṛṣṇa Janma-bhūmi, the birthplace of Lord Kṛṣṇa, in Mathura. Prabhupāda spoke in Hindi from a stage, under a huge banner that read “Mathurā Jaycees Welcome You.” The people who gathered—about four thousand—were mostly inattentive. They were gossiping, as if it was a vegetable market, and the young people were undisciplined and disrespectful.

What amazed me was that Śrīla Prabhupāda kept speaking peacefully. He realized that any attempt to keep the people quiet was futile. Disregarding all the noise and inattentiveness, Prabhupāda dutifully spoke the message of Lord Kṛṣṇa. This was a good example of his tolerance and transcendence; it made for a successful program.

## Leaving Vṛṇdāvana

**Bhakti Rāghava Swami:** That year Śrīla Prabhupāda made a request to the GBC men to arrange for as many as two hundred devotees to stay in India and help our projects. I told the Māyāpur authorities I was interested and that I’d ask my temple president. But he wasn’t into it since I was the head cook. On the day I was flying out, I was at the Delhi airport, planning to board the plane back to Ottawa. My luggage was all checked in. Suddenly I was escorted out of the airport by some devotees, and I spent the night at the Delhi temple. The next morning I was sent with a devotee from South America to catch a train to Māyāpur. We were given a big box of *halavā* to eat on the trip and nothing else. Since my luggage had gone to Canada, I had no extra cloth and only a toothbrush and soap. In Māyāpur, Pañcaratna gave me my first plastic razor and arranged some cloth for me from the handloom shops.

On April 11, Ekādaśī, at four-thirty in the morning, Śrīla Prabhupāda, accompanied by Puṣṭa-Kṛṣṇa Swami, Caitya-Gurudāsa Dāsa, and Hari Śauri, left Krishna-Balaram Mandir headed for Delhi—and near disaster.

**Hari Śauri Dāsa:** After an hour and a half of driving, Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami was explaining to Caitya-guru the fine points of the dials and switches in the Mercedes. Caitya-guru was being trained to drive and maintain the car. Puṣṭa Kṛṣṇa Swami failed to notice an oxcart overtaking a camel cart. By the time he saw them, it was too late. He was traveling too fast to stop. He braked, but the wheels locked and the car skidded.

The axle of the oxcart gouged the left rear passenger door next to Śrīla Prabhupāda. The impact ripped the wheel off the cart and sent the occupants tumbling down into a heap of straw. He kept driving. No one was hurt. But Prabhupāda was very angry and showed it by keeping silent for the rest of the trip. The car door was so badly damaged that it would not open when he got to Delhi.

Prabhupāda caught a morning flight to Bombay at ten-thirty. In Bombay, the temple construction was advancing rapidly, and Prabhupāda wanted to inspect the progress.

## Chapter 10. The 1977 Gaura Pūrṇimā Festival

The overriding concern of the devotees at the 1977 Māyāpur festival was Śrīla Prabhupāda’s illness. So far, despite his fragile condition, Prabhupāda had not been hampered in any major way from rigorously traveling, translating, and preaching. Prior to his arrival in Māyāpur, he’d spent two weeks in Bhubhaneshwar. There he’d laid the cornerstone for the Bhubhaneshwar temple and begun translating the Tenth Canto of the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam.* And on a short visit to Jagannātha Puri, he’d spoken strongly at a public program.

However, in early March, his health deteriorated so much that he dropped plans to visit Vṛndāvana. Prabhupāda remained in Māyāpur, unable to attend the Vṛndāvana festival. The visiting devotees went to Vṛndāvana without him.

This year’s Māyāpur festival was the biggest and best organized ever. Māyāpur’s team of directors and general managers set up the photo exhibition, sponsored a full program of theater, held a *kīrtana* competition, and attempted to serve a hundred thousand plates of *prasāda* on Gaura Pūrṇimā. Throughout the week before Gaura Pūrṇimā, thousands of Bengali pilgrims streamed into the temple for evening *ārati,* then went to see the ISKCON photo exhibit or the dramas on the main stage.

During 1976, the Māyāpur devotees had passed the tranquil months of the summer, the rainy season, and winter engaged in serving the deities. The Western devotees in Māyāpur were gradually learning the Bengali ways of shopping, cooking, and cleaning. There was no refrigeration, and the electricity frequently went off, which shut down the electrically pumped water supply. But cows supplied fresh milk, and Māyāpur offered the devotees serenity. They were making the temple grounds beautiful and clean, and in spring, the entire ISKCON land seemed filled with blooming flowers when Śrīla Prabhupāda arrived.

He arrived on February 7, two and a half weeks before the festival started. The Māyāpur devotees, including eighty Bengali *gurukula* students, held an enormous *kīrtana* for him at the front gate, joyously dancing in the presence of their spiritual master. From his car, which slowed down for the reception, Prabhupāda nodded to acknowledge the greeting of his followers.

Satsvarūpa Dāsa Goswami, his current secretary, heard Prabhupāda say softly, “Back to home, back to Godhead,” as the car made its way toward the freshly painted Lotus Building with the *kīrtana* party booming around it. For the devotees, the first indication of Prabhupāda’s weak health was that he was driven along the path to the temple and did not walk.

Pisimā arrived in another car. She liked the foreign devotees’ company. Pālikā also came to Māyāpur, to again be Prabhupāda’s cook. In Calcutta she’d received informal cooking lessons from a prominent life member who was an excellent cook. The little Bengali girls resumed making Prabhupāda magnificent morning garlands. With Prabhupāda back in Māyāpur, all the devotees could offer him their best devotional endeavors.

## The Devotees Arrive by Jumbo Jet

At five in the morning on February 24, a 747 with over three hundred devotees aboard landed at Calcutta’s Dum Dum Airport. This was the first jumbo jet landing at Dum Dum and was nationally publicized. In 1976 the government had sanctioned extending the runway to accommodate 747’s. Scores of dignitaries, including the general manager of Air India, witnessed the first landing.

The flight originated in Los Angeles, and it left New York with about two hundred devotees. In London it took on board another hundred. During the whole trip, the devotees were flying high. At meals, everyone was provided packages of *prasāda.* There were joyful exchanges of *prasāda* between the devotees from Los Angeles, New-York, New Vṛndāvana, and London—*samosās* for *rasagullās, burfi* for *sandeśa.* There were bags and bags of sweets.

New Balarāma *mṛdaṇgas* had just been made and were being brought to Māyāpur. They were used for the inflight *kīrtanas.* Keeping up tradition, the devotees danced in the aisles; the pilot had to make several announcements to please stop rocking the plane. Later, Hṛdayānanda Goswami and Pañcadraviḍa Swami preached to the pilot for a couple of hours. He thanked the devotees, saying, “It is on account of your Hare Kṛṣṇa society that this 747 will land in Calcutta for the first time.” At the airport, ten buses waited to take the devotees to Māyāpur.

**Mahāmuni Dāsa:** Every year, for six years, I’d collected money to go to the festival, but I’d always had to stay back to take care of the temple. When I joined the Rādhā-Dāmodara party, I stipulated that I’d join only if I could go to India that coming year. A promise was given, but later I was told I couldn’t go. I insisted, so I finally got to go. But when we arrived in India, none of us had any money. I was just happy to be there.

**Rādhāpāda Dāsa:** I arrived with my family from Calcutta at nine-thirty at night. The temple was closed, and I didn’t know if there was any place to sleep. But because I was a life member, Bhavānanda Mahārāja arranged rooms in the Lotus Building. Although it was ten-thirty by that time, he also arranged *prasāda* for us. We got hot *purīs* and *sabji.* The *sannyāsīs* even cleared up our used leaf-plates. We didn’t like that, but they said, “No, if we serve you, the Lord will be pleased with us; Śrīla Prabhupāda will be pleased.” The *sannyāsīs* even made up our beds. We—especially our wives—were ashamed that *sannyāsīs* were making up our beds.

In September 1976 Prabhupāda had asked me to lead a bullock cart *saṅkīrtana* party. Prabhupāda personally inaugurated the party in Vṛndāvana, and the first target we set, was to walk 1,500 kilometers to Śrīdhāma Māyāpur in time for the 1977 Gaura Pūrṇimā festival. Our *padayātrā* arrived at Śrī Māyāpur Candrodaya Mandir with a bullock cart pulled by two Harayana bulls. As we walked through the main gate with *kīrtana,* Ātma-tattva Dāsa was standing in the cart, holding a big picture of Prabhupāda garlanded with gardenias. We parked our cart in front of the Lotus Building, and with *kīrtana* in full swing, the party proceeded into the temple. Just then the curtain opened for the noon *ārati.* We fell at the lotus feet of Rādhā-Mādhava, enjoying Their merciful glances, and I thanked them for being with us on our journey from Vṛndāvana to Māyāpur.

After *ārati* we hurried up the stairs, with the idea of going to Prabhupāda’s quarters. Prabhupāda had just come out of the bathroom and was about to enter his room. As he touched the handle of the door, he looked to his right and saw all of us being stopped by Hari Śauri Prabhu.

Coming to our rescue, he said, “Oh, the bullock-cart party? Come, come. Let them come.”

He gave us a big smile as we offered our *daṇḍavats.* Then we stood there with folded hands. Prabhupāda signaled us to come into his quarters. Elated to be in his presence, we followed along behind him.

He sat behind his desk and gestured for us to be seated. He asked Tamal Krishna Mahārāja to offer us garlands. With this unexpected reception—the garlands, his smiling, and his merciful glances—he was practically smothering us with love. It was too much. Words are inadequate to describe our joyful feelings at that time.

The last time we’d met Prabhupāda was in January, at the Mahā-kumbha-melā in Allahabad. At that time, Prabhupāda had wanted a full report from me. Now he was again eager to hear the complete story of our travels. One thing I mentioned is that when we had arrived in Nadia District, we stayed at a Gauḍīya Maṭha overnight.

I said, “They charged us two rupees per head to sleep there.”

Prabhupāda smiled. “That’s nothing. There are places in Navadvīpa where the *pūjārī* uses the s*ālagrāma-śilā* to crack his betel nuts.”

I said, “All in all, Śrīla Prabhupāda, it was an easy journey to the Māyāpur planet.”

Prabhupāda laughed and gave us our next destination, setting another goal: “Now I want you to go to Jagannātha Puri.”

Prabhupāda had a jar of coconut *burfi* on his table and he gave each of us a piece. Ātma-tattva, who had been standing outside because the room was full, quickly came inside when the *burfi* distribution started. He got the last two pieces, and then the jar was empty.

## Long Building Almost Complete

Construction work on the Long Building had continued steadily in 1976 under the supervision of Jayapatākā Mahārāja and Pañcaratna Prabhu. Now it was almost complete. Śrīla Prabhupāda was concerned about ways in which the visiting devotees could fully take part in the festival and benefit by visiting Māyāpur, so he tried to ensure that their accommodations were taken care of.

As he inspected the Long Building, Jayapatākā Swami informed him: “In about five days, the electrical wiring and the flooring will all be finished. The bathrooms on the first floor are already finished.”

However, the local management could not cope with the number of devotees who had made the pilgrimage to Māyāpur. Devotees had to sleep six or eight to a room, and those who couldn’t find space had to sleep on the roof. Bathroom facilities were also inadequate. A number of devotees were compelled to bathe at pumps.

Most devotees had been informed to expect mosquitoes, but they were unprepared for the rice fields of Bengal. The mosquito coils locally available emitted a repellent smoke that did an inadequate job and made everyone cough. Devotees without nets could either squeeze into a room full of coil, smoke or sleep on the roof and tolerate the mosquitoes.

**Mahāmuni Dāsa:** We were running out of water. The toilets were a mess because many devotees were sick, and there were not enough toilets for that many people. It was unhygienic. Once, we couldn’t find a devotee for a while, and we wondered what had happened to him. I found him behind an outhouse, throwing up and suffering from a bad case of dysentery. He’d been there for two days, unable to move because he was so sick. We helped him take a bath and took him to the nursing station, but the dispensary was inadequately equipped and staffed. It couldn’t help much.

The GBC decided that a seven-man committee should be formed to discuss ways to improve the Māyāpur festival the following year. During its three days of meetings, the GBC also resolved that at the next Māyāpur festival, separate but equal facilities would be arranged for the women. It was felt that the ladies weren’t treated equally, and that next year the new building could be divided in half so that they could have the same type of facility as the men.

## Brainwashing Charge

One of Prabhupāda’s *sannyāsī* disciples, Ādi-Keśava Swami, had been formally charged with brainwashing in 1976. Many Americans were disturbed at ISKCON’s success and had tried to fight back through the courts.

Ādi-Keśava Swami met Prabhupāda during the Māyāpur festival to receive his encouragement and instructions on how to handle the case. On February 14, Prabhupāda sat with his disciples and discussed the arguments being made against them. In this way Prabhupāda simultaneously encouraged his disciples in their mission and strengthened their conviction to fight the materialistic philosophies.

“They have no case,” Prabhupāda said. He knew that if ISKCON stuck to the facts, no action could be taken against the devotees.

Endorsements were gathered from many intelligent and respected supporters. A statement from the president of the Indian Brain Research Association at the University of Calcutta said:

The chanting of Hare Kṛṣṇa cannot do any harm to the devotee or to the observer. Rather, the rhythmic dance and music in the Hare Kṛṣṇa *kīrtana* mantra may have a profound, beneficial effect on the distorted human mind. These practices do not have any relation with brainwashing, and the word “brainwash” seems to carry no scientific meaning at all.

“Actually, it is brain washing,” Prabhupāda said, making the devotees smile. *“Ceto-darpaṇa-mārjanam:* chanting cleanses the mind, but it is good for people. They suffer from these dirty things in the brain. So we are washing their brains.”

Prabhupāda then gave his plan for dealing with the courts:

I want that all our books be given to the court. The books are our statement. Let them read. Once there was a big lawyer—Rajbery Ghosh. In one case he brought so many books that the judge remarked, “Well, Dr. Ghosh, you have brought the whole library?” Ghosh replied, “Yes, my lord, just to teach you law.” So any statement we give, it has to be considered. They cannot neglect it. So you simply put these books: “This is our statement. You read them. Then give your judgement.”

Referring to the anti-cult movements that had sprung up in America, Prabhupāda said: As long as we stay within the law, they cannot find any fault with us. Now people are realizing that ISKCON is the most dangerous threat to their whole culture. The poison has already entered *\[laughing\].* Now it is reacting. So they are feeling the pressure. That is our success—when there is opposition. They are not going to oppose a movement like Transcendental Meditation. No, they don’t care. But they are seeing that this is very venomous poison: no illicit sex, no meat-eating. I thought that nobody would take this. But by Kṛṣṇa’s mercy, you have taken it seriously.

Two weeks after the Māyāpur festival ended, *The Times of India* carried front-page news of a favorable decision by the New York court: “The Hare Krishna Movement is a Bona Fide Religion.”

“My mission is now successful,” Prabhupāda said when the news was read to him. “In 1965, I went there. This is now recognized after twelve years.”

## “Don’t Let Anyone Touch His Feet.”

On the opening night of the *paṇḍāl* programs, a West Bengal government minister inaugurated the festivities. He cut a ribbon to open the Long Building, and he and Śrīla Prabhupāda walked together down the long, ground-floor corridor to see the photo display.

The American BBT again provided materials for the photo exhibit, and this year it was properly housed—a big improvement on the temporary shed used the year before. Devotees from each temple set up booths or exhibits to display their preaching efforts. Devotees took pride in displaying their own activities, and they were excited to learn how devotees in other temples were succeeding.

In the *paṇḍāl,* the big attraction was the theater troupe from New York, the Vaikuṇṭha Players: Sudāmā Swami, Lokmaṅgala, and Lohitākṣa and Rasajñā, Nandakiśora and Jāhnavā, Prajāpati and Śatarūpa. They staged *The Rāmāyaṇa, The Birth of Lord Kṛṣṇa,* and *The Age of Kali.*

Śrīla Prabhupāda attended The *Rāmāyaṇa.* Just before he went down for the play, Mahāmuni was outside his room, trying to get some news about his physical condition.

Tamal Krishna Mahārāja, who’d taken over as Prabhupāda’s secretary, told him, “Because Prabhupāda is so ill, when he goes into the *paṇḍāl,* don’t let anyone touch his feet; otherwise, he will have to accept the karma of those people.”

Prabhupāda left his room and walked down the steps to a car that would take him to the stage. Mahāmuni helped clear a path for Prabhupāda. There were many people in the crowd, and just before Prabhupāda reached the car, one man tried to touch Prabhupāda’s feet. Mahāmuni tried to stop him, but Prabhupāda gently said, “No, no, let him do it.”

**Mahāmuni Dāsa:** I was stunned. I thought I’d offended Prabhupāda. I was thinking I was doing a wonderful service for Prabhupāda, but then he stopped me. After Prabhupāda got to the stage, Tamal Krishna Mahārāja got a little heavy with me for letting someone touch Prabhupāda’s feet. He told me again to stop anyone from touching Prabhupāda’s feet. He said that I should be on guard at the end of the program when Prabhupāda would leave.

Prabhupāda sat on a raised *āsana* in the front half of the audience. It was a treat for the devotees to see Prabhupāda. Although the play was the featured program, Prabhupāda was the central attraction.

**Mahāmāyā Dāsi:** We had difficulty in choosing what to watch—the drama or Śrīla Prabhupāda. Many eyes were going back and forth to catch Śrīla Prabhupāda’s expressions in reaction to the dramatic scenes.

Prabhupāda watched the whole play, then applauded appreciatively.

**Mahāman Dāsa:** You could see by Prabhupāda’s expressions that he was really happy to see the drama. What really touched me was that the devotee playing Lord Rāma—an expert actor—offered his full *daṇḍavats* to Śrīla Prabhupāda at the end of the play and then did his *praṇāmas* to the devotees.

Prabhupāda’s car was still parked on one side of the stage, but he exited on the other side, so he just walked back to his room. This made Mahāmuni’s job even more difficult, because now a huge crowd would try to get near Prabhupāda. Mahāmuni was actually pushing people away as Prabhupāda walked behind him.

**Mahāmuni uvāca:** I felt like a bouncer at a bar. As we got close to the building, an older gentleman started to dive at Prabhupāda’s feet, but I had a good angle on him and put my hand on his chest and lifted him back up to a standing position, away from Prabhupāda. Prabhupāda saw this and said, “No, no.” Then he looked at me and recognized me from the earlier incident. In a furious tone of voice he said, “You!” When he said that, I just let go of the man, and he fell down right on Prabhupāda’s feet. Prabhupāda was looking at me with fire coming out of his eyes. I thought, *Now I’ve offended him twice, so I’ll go to hell for sure. My devotional service is finished.* Prabhupāda walked on, but I just stood there. Eventually, I made my way back upstairs, and Tamal Krishna Mahārāja got on my case again. All I said to him was, “I don’t want to hear this. Prabhupāda chastised me twice for following your instructions.” Then I went down to the temple room and prayed to be excused if I had made a big offense.

## The First and Last Kīrtana Competition

Two days before Gaura Pūrṇimā, Jayapatākā Mahārāja staged the *nāma kīrtana* competition, which turned out to be the first and last of its kind.

On the main stage, a day of elimination contests were held, and the next day was the grand finale. Kīrtana groups from all over Bengal came to exhibit their talents—singing, dancing, and playing instruments. Particularly attractive were the special *mṛdaṅgas* beats the native Bengalis knew how to play. Jayādvaita Swami recalls seeing the *kīrtana* leaders from America hanging around near the stage, picking up on the *mṛdaṅgas* beats that the Bengalis were doing, hoping to take their styles back to the West.

Devotees also formed their own *kīrtana* contingents. The different temples took part, including our bullock-cart traveling *saṅkīrtana* party. Some *kīrtana* parties took the competition very seriously and spent many days practicing *mṛdaṅga* beats and choreographed dance steps. Others were spontaneous and did not rehearse.

Guru-kṛpā’s Nāma-haṭṭa party was the most enthusiastic and did the wildest dancing. His men danced with all their strength and passion. Uttamaśloka Dāsa, the temple president from Chicago, had a polished routine, perfected through hours of practice. His group was favored to win the contest. In the bullock-cart *kīrtana* party, I was the *kīrtana* leader, and Abhayāśraya played the *mṛdaṅga*. Ravi, Ātma-tattva, Kṛṣṇa-nāma, and others played *kartālas* and danced.

The *kīrtana* competition went on all day, for two days. Each group performed for about half an hour, trying to put on the best show. The local Bengali singers were smartly dressed, with bright yellow and blue sashes, and they played great *mṛdaṅgas* beats and even cried and howled during their *kīrtanas.* Perhaps they thought they could easily win by displaying such symptoms. So they put on a whole show, embracing each other in feigned separation from the Lord and rolling on the ground. The Bengalis sang in high-pitched voices, and the devotees responded until their throats were hoarse.

All this was being amplified through the Indian sound system. Unfortunately, the speakers were directed toward the Lotus Building, where Prabhupāda was staying, and Prabhupāda became disturbed by the excessive noise. He eventually asked what was happening, and the devotees told him in detail about the competition.

He reacted unfavorably: “This is not *nāma-saṅkīrtana.* It is *nāma-aparādhā.* They are simply singing for money.”

So the competition was stopped, and Jayapatākā Mahārāja decided not to hold any more *kīrtana* competitions. The Los Angeles party of Agnideva, Puṣpadanta, and Rāmadāsa Abhirāma won the competition.

## Vaiṣṇava Humility

Śrīla Prabhupāda gave six *Śrīmad Bhāgavatam* classes during the festival, but when he became particularly ill on March 2, *sannyāsīs* began giving the classes instead. Naturally, this was disappointing for the devotees, who’d come to hear Śrīla Prabhupāda in person. Still, Prabhupāda would come to the classes, sit on the *vyāsāsana,* and listen to his disciples. He obviously didn’t want to disappoint the devotees, who’d traveled so far just to see him.

**Devāmṛta swami:** One thing that stood out for me was that Prabhupāda gave a lecture one evening in Bengali. I just sat there and listened, and even though I didn’t know a word of Bengali, I was fully satisfied.

Pradyumna sometimes chanted *“Jaya* Rādhā-Mādhava” and read the text before Prabhupāda himself lectured. The prayers depicting the meekness of Prahlāda Mahārāja were being studied. Prahlāda was asking, “How is it possible for me, who has been born in a family of *asuras,* to offer suitable prayers to satisfy the Supreme Personality of Godhead? Even until now, all the demigods, headed by Lord Brahmā, and all the saintly persons could not satisfy the Lord by streams of excellent words, although such persons are very qualified, being in the mode of goodness. Then what is to be said of me? I am not at all qualified.”

**Śrīla Prabhupāda:** A Vaiṣṇava is always meek and humble. That is a Vaiṣṇava, even though he is powerful. Prahlāda Mahārāja is so qualified that immediately Lord Nṛsiṁhadeva put His hand on the head of Prahlāda and said: “My dear child, you have suffered so much. Now be pacified.” This is Prahlāda’s position—immediately accepted by the Lord. But he’s thinking, *I am so lowborn-born in a passionate family.* He’s not proud that Nṛsiṁhadeva touched his head: *Ah. Who is like me? I am the greatest personality.* This is not a Vaiṣṇava.

Sanātana Gosvāmi also said he was lowborn. He was born in a respectable family, but because he accepted the service of a Mohammedan king, superficially it appeared that he lost all his brahminical culture. Then he gave that up. He understood: *What I am doing? I am committing suicide.*

Narottama Dāsa Thākura says, “I am taking poison knowingly.” Unknowingly one may take poison, but if knowingly one takes poison, that is very regrettable. We are trying to preach this Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement all over the world, and if people still do not take to it, then they are drinking poison knowingly. That is a fact. It is not that we are imagining something.

Kavirāja Gosvāmi similarly presented himself—lower than the worm in stool. He says, “I am more sinful than Jagāi-Mādhāi. I am so low that if somebody takes my name, whatever little pious action he has, is lost.” This is not artificial. A Vaiṣṇava actually thinks like this. He is never proud, but just the opposite. So we have to learn this and follow the footprint of Prahlāda Mahārāja. Then surely we shall be accepted by Nṛsiṁhadeva, Kṛṣṇa, without any failure.

The next day, Prabhupāda explained how such humility pleases Kṛṣṇa. Prahlāda had continued to pray: “One may possess wealth, an aristocratic family, beauty, austerity, education, sensory expertise, luster, influence, physical strength, diligence, intelligence and mystic yogic power, but I think that even by all these qualifications one cannot satisfy the Supreme Personality of Godhead. However, one can satisfy the Lord simply by devotional service. Gajendra did this, and thus the Lord was satisfied with him.”

**Śrīla Prabhupāda:** Nobody can captivate Kṛṣṇa by material possessions—money, manpower, beauty, education, austerity, and mystic power. Kṛṣṇa says, *bhaktyā mām abhijānāti.* He doesn’t say, “If one is a rich man, he can have My favor.” No. Kṛṣṇa is not a poor man like me. If somebody gives me money, I benefit. He’s self-sufficient. Only *bhakti,* love, is required. *Bhakti* means “to serve Kṛṣṇa without any purpose.” Everywhere this is the statement of the *śāstra* that *bhakti* should be unalloyed.

If we have love for Kṛṣṇa, then we don’t require money or strength or education or austerity. Kṛṣṇa says, *patraṁ puṣpaṁ phalaṁ toyaṁ, yo me bhaktyā prayacchati.* He doesn’t require anything from us. Because we are part and parcel of Kṛṣṇa, He wants to see that everyone obeys and loves Him. That is His aspiration.

People are trying to be very religious and economically opulent. They want to be expert in sense enjoyment and then be liberated. These are the general demands. But the *Bhāgavatam* says, “No, these things are not qualifications.” Kṛṣṇa does not demand, “Unless you are liberated, you cannot serve.” No. You can serve in any condition, because as soon as you begin *bhakti,* you are already liberated. The platform is so great that a *bhakta* without any ulterior motive is already liberated. If Kṛṣṇa takes charge of vanquishing all the reactions of your sinful life, that means you are immediately liberated. And if you keep yourself fixed in devotional service, then you are not bound by reactions. Our life should be dedicated simply for Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Then we are safe; otherwise, we are entangled in so many reactions of our activities and bound up in the repetition of birth and death.

So Prahlāda Mahārāja thought that although he was born in a low family, still if he decides to serve Kṛṣṇa, or Nṛsiṁhadeva, with *bhakti,* following the footprints of the king of the elephants, then he can get the favor of the Supreme Personality. And if Kṛṣṇa is pleased upon you, what remains? You get everything.

You don’t require much money, much education­nothing of the sort. Simply you require your heart: “O Kṛṣṇa, You are my Lord. You are my master eternally. I am your servant eternally. Let me be engaged in Your service.” That is Hare Kṛṣṇa, Hare Kṛṣṇa, Kṛṣṇa Kṛṣṇa, Hare Hare, Hare Rāma, Hare Rāma, Rāma Rāma, Hare Hare. This is the meaning: “O Kṛṣṇa, O energy of Kṛṣṇa, I am Your servant. Somehow or other I have now fallen in this material condition. Kindly pick me up and engage me in service.”

Caitanya Mahāprabhu is teaching us this—to become very humble and meek and always pray to Kṛṣṇa: “Kindly consider me one of the particles of dust at the lotus feet of Your Lordship.” Very simple thing. Think of Kṛṣṇa, become His devotee, offer obeisances and whatever little flower or water you can provide. In this way live very peacefully and be happy.

## Parikramās and Swimming

Acyutānanda Swami was still leading the *parikramās.* The *parikramās* by bus on the dusty roads were a major austerity of the festival because this year, the weather was particularly hot. The festival started in late February.

We took one *parikramā* across the Jalaṅgi to Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura’s House. The devotees were enthusiastically crossing the Jalaṅgi. Through its clear waters, we could see the bottom. The devotees were chanting on the boats, and some were diving off before the boats reached the shore. Mahāmuni, though weak from dysentery, thought a swim in the Jalañgī would be nice on that hot day.

**Mahāmuni Dāsa:** I jumped in and started swimming with other devotees. I swam about twenty feet in front of the boat and soon realized I was too weak and wasn’t going to make it to the shore. I didn’t realize that the water got shallower as you got closer to shore. I just thought I wasn’t going to make it. So I floated on my back until the boat came by, and I had to get back in.

From Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura’s house we danced our way to the Gaṅgā, where we boarded other boats to Navadvīpa and walked to the Dhāmeśvara temple. There we took *darśana* of Dhāmeśvara Mahāprabhu, the deity Viṣṇu-priyā worshiped for eighty years. We were amazed to hear of her austerities. The outstretched hands of the golden deity of Lord Caitanya depict His magnanimous nature—handing out mercy with both hands. The temple authorities say that the wooden shoes on the altar were worn by Lord Caitanya. The *pūjārīs* brought out the shoes and caused a stampede, as devotees rushed to touch the shoes to their heads.

Every afternoon the devotees were walking to the Ganges to swim. The fields that they had to walk through were owned by Muslim farmers, and every afternoon the devotees would trample over their crops on their way to bathe.

**Mahānidhi Swami:** We were wild *saṅkīrtana* men from the West, and we didn’t know the difference between weeds and crops. We couldn’t understand why the farmers were cursing at us in Bengali, and we didn’t care. Then we came to learn that we were crushing their crops every day.

It used to take at least fifteen minutes to walk through the fields and then go down the cliffs. One day Prabhupāda told us that the Gaṅgā would come closer to the ISKCON property and then the devotees would not have to go so far.

Gathered at the Gaṅgā, devotees would hear and chant. There was nice association available, and little circles of devotees were always sitting around the *sannyāsīs.* Tripurāri Swami would often be reading. The scene was reminiscent of the times when Caitanya Mahāprabhu’s associates would gather at the Gaṅgā.

Devotees would jump into the Gaṅgā from her high banks. It was about ten to twelve feet down to the water. Devotees from America, Germany, and England would be jumping, diving, showing off, and challenging one other to jump out the furthest and to perform the best dives.

We would also pick up the plentiful soft mud and cover ourselves with it. When everyone covered himself with the gray, sticky clay, we achieved a real oneness, because you wouldn’t know if someone had an Indian body, an American body, or a European body. You could see only white teeth and eyeballs. We’d wait for the clay to dry and then jump in the Gaṅgā to wash it off.

**Mahāmuni Dāsa:** Whenever I went to the Ganges, Gurukṛpā Swami and Yaśodānandana Swami and their group would throw big handfuls of mud at us. We had to climb down the cliffs to the water, and when we got to the bottom, they’d throw big clumps of mud from above. Guru-kṛpā Swami acted like he was playing king of the hill—a game that boys play. One boy at the top of a hill tries to keep the other boys down. They were being playful, but I didn’t find it fun. I’m a ruffian in many ways, but I didn’t find that pleasurable or transcendental.

## Pisimā’s Motherly Affection

Pisimā was staying in a room at the east end of the ground floor of the Long Building. One afternoon she was sitting in her room taking fruit from the afternoon offering. Mahānidhi was staying in a room next to hers. As he walked past, she called, “Hey, *brahmacari,* come here.” When he went up to her, she put a handful of *prasāda* from her plate into his hands, saying, “You take.” As he started to go, she said, “No, you sit here. I want to see you eat.” So he sat and ate. This happened a couple of times. He felt that this was her way of showing the devotees motherly affection. She also liked to cook for the *sannyāsīs.*

## Prabhupāda a Team Player

One evening there was some discussion with Prabhupāda about the life membership program. Tamal Krishna Goswami, Pañcadraviḍa Swami, Rāmeśvara Swami, Bhāgavata, and I were present. At the time, life members in India were giving more money and receiving fewer books than now. People who wanted the entire set had to purchase a standing order.

Tamal Krishna Mahārāja said, “I don’t see the advantage of this program of providing standing orders to individuals, because with life membership you get more money.”

I pointed out that life members didn’t get all the books, and there were others who were interested only in the books and not in membership.

Prabhupāda suggested the solution of them becoming either a life member or a customer.

Bhāgavata said that the middle class could not afford to pay for either program all at once. Other devotees suggested that there had to be some installment scheme. But I spoke from my experience in Bombay and said that there was a lot of pursuance required to collect installment payments and that even after many phone calls and visits, you still did not get the complete payments.

Pañcadraviḍa Swami said that this didn’t matter because we’d never lose that money, even if people didn’t continue the installments regularly. Sooner or later the money would come in because people were under a contract and they needed to pay to get their membership card or books.

Prabhupāda chuckled and concluded the conversation by saying, “You are only giving five books anyway.”

This is an example of how Prabhupāda was involved in the decision­making processes in India. Prabhupāda acted like one member of the discussion. He was not wearing his big *founder-ācārya* hat and dictating all the rules. He was encouraging us, letting us voice our frank opinions without inhibition, and training us to become his independently thoughtful men.

## Taking Care of Guests

Bāsu Ghosh Dāsa was passing through Visakhapatnam on his way to Māyāpur and offered to bring Śrī Śrīmad B. V. Puri Mahārāja to Māyāpur. He gave Puri Mahārāja a ride to Māyāpur in Haṁsadūta Swami’s Mercedes. Puri Mahārāja was given a nice reception and provided a room in the Lotus Building. In the evening Basu Gosh took Puri Mahārāja to see Prabhupāda and sat with the two godbrothers for an hour.

Bāsu Ghosh Dāsa: They were talking in Bengali, and I was just a passive observer who didn’t understand anything. It was an extremely friendly exchange, and they were heartily joking and laughing. They’d known each other for thirty­five years. It went on and on, and it didn’t bother them that I was there. I was just enjoying their moods.

Bīrsiṁha Dāsa brought one Mr. Singh, a life member from Calcutta. He was a wealthy, established jeweler and friendly to the movement. He hired a bus for his family and relatives traveling to Māyāpur. Mr. Singh got to see Prabhupāda during a *darśana.*

**Bhakti-vighna-vināśak Nṛsiṁha Swami:** Prabhupāda was happy to meet Mr. Singh, and during their talk the man mentioned my name: “Oh, Bīrsiṁha Brahmacari inspired me to come.”

Prabhupāda asked, “Bīrsiṁha? Who is that?”

Prabhupāda didn’t know me. I was at the door, and I just lowered my head, but Prabhupāda saw me and could understand who I was.

Then Prabhupāda called Brahmānanda Swami and told him privately, “You take care of this life member. Talk to him and give him some attention.”

Prabhupāda cared about everybody. I saw how much care he was giving to this family. The guests came and would be impressed. They’d say, “Oh, Prabhupāda is so friendly. He asked about my business and my family.” You couldn’t believe how personal Prabhupāda was with everyone he met.

## Darśana Stories

Prabhupāda’s *darśana* room was small and would often be so full that some devotees would have to peer through the windows. After five or ten minutes Prabhupāda would tell his servants to give everyone some *mahā prasāda,* and all the devotees would take *prasāda* and leave. Then another group of devotees would pile in and the room would fill up again. There were often big queues to get in, and the room would become packed. Maybe fifty or sixty devotees packed into the room, which is hard to imagine when you see how small it is. Mahābuddhi and other godbrothers would take turns standing guard, controlling how many devotees went in to see Prabhupāda and how long they stayed.

At one particular *darśana,* a room full of devotees had nothing to ask Prabhupāda. He said, “So, there are no questions? No one has any question?” Prabhupāda seemed somewhat annoyed. No one spoke, and Prabhupāda became heavy: “Why are there no questions? The duty of the disciple is to question the spiritual master.”

There was a nervous pause, and finally Madhusevita, from Italy, tried to save the situation by asking, “Śrīla Prabhupāda, in the *Śrīmad Bhāgavatam,* in such and such Canto, so and so says such and such a thing. Is that right?”

Prabhupāda looked at Madhusevita in disbelief and replied: “If that is said in the *Bhāgavatam,* then why are you asking me whether it is right? What kind of question is this?”

During another *darśana,* Brahmānanda Swami asked why many young Westerners were coming to Kṛṣṇa consciousness, since they actually had disturbed minds.

Śrīla Prabhupāda replied, “They have seen that simply by bodily comfort, we cannot be happy. Therefore they have come to this stage.”

I said, “So it’s fortunate to take birth in the West now,” which made the devotees laugh.

**Śrīla Prabhupāda:** Not all. Western people are now opposing this movement, so they are not fortunate. Everywhere there are fortunate and unfortunate people. But most in this age are unfortunate.

Most Indians believe in the transmigration of the soul because the culture is based on *pūrva-janma paro-janma:* “If I act sinfully, then I’ll have to suffer in my next life, and because I did not act properly, therefore I am suffering in this life.”

But the so-called educated people are trying to set this aside. People are being trained up not to become sober. Vedic civilization is to teach the youngsters from the very beginning how to become sober under restriction, under regulation. *Brahmacārī guru-gṛhe vasan dāntaḥ. Dānta* means sober.

Śrīla Prabhupāda looked around for his Sanskrit editor. He asked, “And where is our *paṇḍita?”*

“In the next room, Prabhupāda,” I informed him.

“Call him.”

I rushed out of the room and soon returned with Pradyumna. Prabhupāda had called for Pradyumna to ascertain the meaning of the word *dānta.* By doing this, he demonstrated that he considered his disciples to be capable devotees despite their Western upbringing. Indeed, he depended on us and was proud and appreciative of our talents. It was enlivening to hear this exchange and to be in such an informal setting with Prabhupāda. It was particularly pleasing to realize that through Prabhupāda’s mercy we were gradually becoming competent to assist him in his mission.

At another *darśana,* Rādhā-vallabha and Rāmeśvara Swami told Prabhupāda about the book production figures they’d compiled from the Dai Nippon company and the BBT offices. His books were being published in twenty-three languages, they said, and an estimated ninety percent of the published books were already distributed.

“English is first,” began Rādhā-vallabha. Books distributed:

43,450,500. That’s one for every five people in America.”

The devotees laughed and clapped.

“Where is such a publisher?” Prabhupāda said. “No other company or society can claim to have produced such a large amount of quality literature in such a short period.”

“Second is Spanish: 2,947,000. Third is German: 2,125,500. Then Japanese: 2,125,000. Fifth is French: 1,670,000.”

“A big board with these figures should be hung for everyone to see,” Prabhupāda said.

Rāmeśvara had anticipated Prabhupāda’s desire and had prepared a big display for the exhibition.

“This is the blessing of my Guru Mahārāja,” Prabhupāda said. “He wanted it. And because we are trying to do this, he is giving us all blessings. He told me, ‘I wanted to sell the marble in the Calcutta temple and publish books.’ He said, ‘Since this temple has been donated, our men are fighting over the rooms. So I know there will be a blazing fire there. Before that, I want to take out the marble and sell it and produce some books.’ I noted down that he wanted books. So I tried to do that.”

Rādhā-vallabha continued to read the breakdown: “Sixth is Portuguese, 835,000. Seventh, Dutch . . .” The list went on and on: Italian, Hindi, Bengali, Telegu, Swahili, Gujarati, Chinese, Marathi, Korean, Yugoslavian, Oriya, Polish, Hungarian, Czechoslovakian, Tamil, and Russian. The total was 55,314,000.

At still another *darśana,* Prabhupāda saw the fiberglass Balarāma *mṛdaṅga.* Many clay *mṛdaṇgas* were carried out of India with great care and inconvenience, only to break once they got wet or were accidentally dropped even slightly. Iśāna Dāsa, a devotee musician, and others in Los Angeles had been working on a new, unbreakable, fiberglass *mṛdaṅga* for several years. They named it the Balarāma *mṛdaṅga* because of its strength. The first prototype had been brought to Māyāpur in early 1976 to show Prabhupāda. This year, Iśāna presented the latest version of the *mṛdaṅga* to Prabhupāda and was revealing to the other devotees how light it was. They put it on Prabhupāda’s desk and waited for his response.

**Mahānidhi Swami:** They offered it to Prabhupāda, and he hit it once, just perfectly, and said “It is dead,” meaning the sound was flat and not as full and natural as the sound of the clay *mṛdaṅga.* “The clay one is better. Try to improve it some more,” were Prabhupāda’s encouraging words.

Continuing a tradition, the devotees from Australia and New Zealand brought ghee and honey to offer to Prabhupāda. As each devotee approached him, they placed a two-kilo can of ghee on his table, along with tins of full-cream powdered milk and boxes of fresh honeycomb. He was informed that the ghee was made by devotees from milk given by their cows. Prabhupāda’s face lit up. He was pleased to learn that the devotees were meeting some of their basic requirements. They invited Prabhupāda to visit their newest farm, New Govardhana, in the hope that his health would improve with a change of climate. He later requested that the ghee, milk, and honey be distributed to all the Indian centers.

One day a small boy was present during a *darśana* and Prabhupāda told Hari Śauri to give him a coconut *laḍḍu.* Prabhupāda’s jar of coconut-and-gur *laḍḍus* was often in use. Hari Śauri was giving the boy *laḍḍus,* and he was eating them one after another. Then Prabhupāda looked over and said, “How many have you given him? No, stop! Don’t give him so much.” Prabhupāda’s reasoning probably was that a child cannot discriminate and doesn’t know when to stop.

Jananivāsa continued his evening service of filling Prabhupāda’s room with frankincense. It was his ticket into Prabhupāda’s room. He would gently enter the room with a pot of burning charcoal, then put incense on it and make it smoke. Once, Prabhupāda was sitting at his desk chanting on his beads. He had his eyes closed, softly chanting the Hare Kṛṣṇa mantra. Jananivāsa filled the room with smoke and after some time Prabhupāda opened his eyes and said, “It is so nice, so nice. This frankincense creates a very conducive atmosphere for spiritual understanding.” He didn’t open the doors or turn on the fan. He really liked the dense, mystical smoke.

Unfortunately, when Prabhupāda’s illness became serious, his *darśanas* and classes were canceled, much to the anguish of all the visiting devotees. Thereafter, when they did see Prabhupāda, it was in restricted circumstances, and it was a cause of distress to them to see Prabhupāda’s weak condition.

## Concern About Prabhupāda’s Health

The GBC asked Śrīla Prabhupāda whether the devotees in our temples around the world could start twenty-four-hour *kīrtanas* until he recovered his health. Prabhupāda agreed to it, and the GBC passed a resolution on it. He said, “Chanting is the only cure for all diseases. Very good idea.” He’d become very ill a week after the festival began. Often he was carried around in a palanquin. Naturally we were disappointed that he couldn’t take part in the festival, but our disappointment was counteracted by concern. Tamal Krishna Gosvāmi, Hari Śauri, and Nava-yogendra Swami took shifts, so that someone was constantly with Prabhupāda. His illness was on everyone’s mind.

**Tamal Krishna Goswami:** Actually, Prabhupāda had become ill the previous year. By the summer of 1976 in America, he had not recovered. He never got his full health back, and at the Kumbha-melā in January 1977, he got a little worse. He got weaker still in Bhubhaneshwar, and by the time I took over in Māyāpur as his secretary, his health started to seriously deteriorate. A doctor-friend of Prabhupāda’s from Allahabad came and recommended Western medicines. An Ayurvedic doctor came from Calcutta.

**Mahāmāyā Dāsi:** I heard about his Ayurvedic medicine—so bitter, he told one devotee, that if he were younger, three or four men would have had to hold him down to force him to take it. Nothing would make it less bitter, either. But he took it.

**Śeṣa Dāsa:** I could see that Prabhupāda’s face was noticeably thinner. I was shocked to see him so thin. One morning he came down to give class, but just as he started to speak, the electricity went off. So he didn’t speak. He went back to his room. This was a big contrast with the 1976 festival, when he was ringing the bell, making the devotees dance like mad.

**Mahāmuni Dāsa:** I’d go upstairs and talk to the senior devotees to get updates on Prabhupāda’s condition. I always knew what was going on with him, and I was satisfied with that. Although I didn’t get to see so much of Prabhupāda because he was ill, I never felt that I was missing anything, because I knew that we were doing what Prabhupāda wanted. We were going on *parikramās,* attending the *kīrtanas,* and doing the other things he wanted us to do in the *dhāma.*

After Prabhupāda gave us permission to start a twenty-four-hour *kīrtana,* an announcement was made over the public address system that the devotees should immediately leave whatever they were doing and come to the *pāṇḍāl* to do *kīrtana* for Prabhupāda Prabhupāda gave permission for the devotees to chant a special prayer to Lord Nṛsiṁhadeva for his protection. Mahāman Dāsa, an Indian devotee who’d joined the movement in England, was in the middle of washing his clothes. He thought of finishing, but some devotees told him, “Doing your laundry is unimportant. Leave everything and come.” So he stopped and followed them.

Despite being ill, Prabhupāda remained determined and encouraged the devotees constantly. Until he became very ill and unable to walk without assistance, he was planning a tour of Manipur before going to the Vṛndāvana festival.

## A Hundred Thousand Fed on Gaura Pūrṇimā

On March 5, Gaura Pūrṇimā day, a hundred thousand pilgrims were to be fed *kṛṣṇa-prasāda.* Prabhupāda had requested that everyone who visited be asked to take *prasāda.* To feed that many people, it was originally decided to distribute small handfuls of *gur* and peanuts. But Prabhupāda wasn’t satisfied with a token meal, and he planned the menu himself.

“Give them sumptuous food,” he said. “What is this?”—making the devotees laugh as he held out his hand imitating an unsatisfied person receiving a handful of *gur* and peanuts. Under his instruction, the *prasāda* standard became excellent.

He said, “Make one *kicharī,* one potato-eggplant vegetable, tomato chutney, and if possible, sweet rice. Then people will be very much satisfied.”

The serve-out took place on our property throughout the day. Tens of thousands of eager pilgrims—in batch after batch-sat in rows before leaf plates and were served *kicharī* and the other tasty items that Prabhupāda had put on the menu. Dozens of servers satisfied the Bengalis’ eagerness for *prasāda.* This was probably the biggest serve-out in a single day in the early history of Māyāpur.

**Bhakti Rāghava Swami:** Thousands upon thousands of guests streamed through the gates. Crowd control was difficult. There were oceans of people. The whole festival was heavy with sound and quite intense.

Some devotees started distributing books to the public. They took a handful of small books and went off into the crowd. Most of the distributors were foreigners and didn’t speak a word of Bengali, but they still managed to hand out literature. It created a good public impression. One devotee said, “I didn’t think that I could do it in India, but it was fun and easy. For me, it was exciting to be in the *dhāma* distributing books.” After this experience some devotees were attracted to staying in the *dhāma* to distribute books, but their authorities objected: “Prabhu, try to understand that wherever you are distributing books is no different from the *dhāma.”*

A big *yajña* was held on Gaura Pūrṇimā, and Śrīla Prabhupāda accepted over two hundred disciples, awarding second initiation to one hundred. Śrīla Prabhupāda’s illness prevented him from chanting on everyone’s *mala,* so *sannyāsīs* did the *japa* for him.

No sannyāsa *initiations* were given. Śrīla Prabhupāda had complained: The *brahmacārīs* were begging to be given *sannyāsa,* but it had simply become a fashion to be in the renounced order. In response to Prabhupāda, the GBC drew up guidelines for receiving *sannyāsa,* including a one-year waiting period and a further one-year proving period.

In the afternoon, Śrīla Prabhupāda met with Tarun Kanti Ghosh, the home minister for West Bengal, who was quite favorable to ISKCON. Later that day, another *yajña* was performed, and the foundation stone was laid for the Vedic Planetarium. A similar ceremony had been held on Gaura Pūrṇimā in 1972 to inaugurate ISKCON’s world headquarters, but this ceremony was to inaugurate the temple that Lord Nityānanda had predicted—an *adbhuta-mandira,* an astounding temple. Prabhupāda had wanted the installation to happen in his physical presence. He’d made that priority obvious. He’d spoken of the temple many times, and now those words were being translated into action.

Prabhupāda’s illness prevented him from performing the cornerstone-laying ceremony himself, so the Ananta Śeṣa deity was brought to him and he touched it. Śrīla Prabhupāda asked Śrī Śrīmad B. V. Puri Mahārāja to perform the installation. Puri Mahārāja and Yaśodānandana Swami stepped into a small pit inside a roped-off area and placed the small deity and other sacred items in the pit. Bricks and cement were placed around the deity, and then the whole thing was covered with dirt and leveled. A lot of devotees, including the leaders, came forward, helping to fill the hole. Nearly all the devotees present at the festival gathered for the ceremony and held a colossal *kīrtana.*

**Pañcaratna Dāsa:** At that time, a model of the future temple was designed and displayed. Prabhupāda actually wanted to build a city for the divisions of a *varṇāśrama* society. Behind the Lotus Building there was a parking lot, and on Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvati Ṭhākura’s *vyāsa-pūjā,* a little before the festival, we put the temple model on display out there, for everyone to see. Śrīla Prabhupāda went out there to touch the foundation stone, but he didn’t go to the actual site.

Now, twenty-two years later, we need to redo the ceremony. Unfortunately, the Ananta deity was dug up during the night and stolen. We suspected this was the work of some Muslims. A Hindu would think twice before doing such a thing. It must have been someone who had watched the ceremony carefully and seen the glittering jewels on the shining deity, and estimated their value.

**Jananivāsa Dāsa:** When Surabhi was designing the temple, he was thinking, “Maybe this is too far out. It’s bigger than what Prabhupāda wants.” But when he’d take the drawings to Śrīla Prabhupāda, Prabhupāda would say, “It’s nice, but expand here a little.” So the plans would be redrawn bigger. Again he would think, “Maybe I’ve overdone it now,” but Prabhupāda would see it and say, “Yes, that is good, but add something here.” Śrīla Prabhupāda wanted a really big temple and wanted it completed by 1986, the quincentennial of Caitanya Mahāprabhu’s appearance.

**Ramāi Swami:** There was a big push to build the temple. A project manager told us that every book sold overseas was worth a brick here in Māyāpur, so the leaders were inspiring the devotees to sell more books. “One book, one brick,” was a slogan we often heard.

## Prabhupāda Unable to Attend the Vṛndāvana Festival

As in the past, most of the devotees traveled from Māyāpur to attend the Vṛndāvana festival. However, a cloud hung over the Vṛndāvana festival in Prabhupāda’s absence. Devotees found it difficult to relish the Vṛndāvana mood without Śrīla Prabhupāda, the center of their happiness. His absence prepared us for the eventuality that one day he’d no longer be with us and we’d have to learn to depend on his instructions.

Girirāja Prabhu invited Śrīla Prabhupāda to speak at a *paṇḍāl* program in Bombay. Prabhupāda flew there from Calcutta on March 23. When he arrived, his quarters in Juhu were not finished, so he stayed downtown, using Kartikeya Mahadevia’s house, which was only twenty minutes by car from the *paṇḍāl.* Prabhupāda amazed everyone with his powerful preaching on the theme, “Human civilization is a complete failure, and the only hope is Kṛṣṇa consciousness.” He simply wanted to preach nonstop. He made this clear during a conversation in late March with Tamal Krishna Goswami.

**TKG**: Is there any date you want to stay in Bombay until, Prabhupāda?

**Prabhupāda**: I don’t want to stay anywhere. I want work.

**TKG**: So there’s work everywhere.

**Prabhupāda**: Staying—I have stayed in big, big palaces and big, big cities. That is now complete. I have no other desire but to work.

**TKG**: How long do you want to work in Bombay?

**Prabhupāda**: So long there is work. There is no end of it. Our Bombay should be organized. Work is our life. There is no question of, “How long?” As long as possible. Kṛṣṇa is giving us good opportunities. Now we should take it seriously. It is not a joke—Hare Kṛṣṇa movement is a bona fide religion.

While Prabhupāda was relaxing in his quarters, he inquired about the Vṛndāvana temple and festival. Dhanañjaya was in Bombay visiting Prabhupāda and reported, “This year’s Vṛndāvana festival was very successful. Everyone enjoyed it very much—the *kīrtanas* and all the different programs. The decoration was gorgeous. All the domes were lit, and at the front door were two electrically-lit *cakras* and a lotus flower that opened and closed. Thousands of people were coming daily.”

“Yes,” I said, “and during the Vṛndāvana festival we did a big program at the Kṛṣṇa Janmasthān temple in Mathurā*kīrtanas*, discourses, dramas, cinemas, *prasāda* distribution, book distribution. Many, many people came, and the whole temple ground was full.”

“Over ten thousand people came,” Gopal Kṛṣṇa Prabhu confirmed.

Prabhupāda’s emotions were aroused, as though he were transported to Vṛndāvana. In a grave voice he said, “After all, they are Mathura men and they are after Kṛṣṇa. They have natural love for Kṛṣṇa.”

Dhanañjaya continued, “Now signs are up on the old Chattikara Road, announcing the new name: Bhaktivedānta Swami Marg. On the road there are two big marble plaques in very nice stone fittings.”

Later, I began thinking about the new name of the road: Following the Bhaktivedānta Swami Marg, we reach Vṛndāvana. Bhaktivedānta Swami Marg is like the bhakti-marg; the path of *bhakti* takes us to Vṛndāvana. If we’re coming from Delhi and don’t turn onto this marg, we end up in Agra, which contains a full dose of the illusory energy. However, if we turn onto Bhaktivedānta Swami Marg, it leads us to Vṛndāvana and brings us to the lotus feet of Kṛṣṇa-Balarāma and Śrī Śrī Rādhā-Śyāmasundara.

Śrīla Prabhupāda *ki jaya!*

Vṛndāvana dhāma *ki jaya!*

## About the Author

Born in 1949 in Aravade, a village in Maharashtra, India, Lokanāth Swami came in contact with Śrila Prabhupāda at ISKCON’s first Hare Kṛṣṇa festival in Bombay, in 1971 while attending college. As one of Śrila Prabhupāda’s first Indian disciples, he received *harināma* initiation in 1972, and served in various positions at the ISKCON Juhu temple. He was awarded *sannyāsa* initiation in 1975.

In 1976, on Śrila Prabhupāda’s order, he organized ISKCON’s first *padayātrā* which journeyed across India distributing the message of Kṛṣṇa. Later, as Padayātrā Minister, he expanded his service worldwide. He served as the temple president of ISKCON New Delhi from 1978 through the mid-1980’s. Then, as the Minister for Śrila Prabhupāda’s Centennial, Lokanāth Swami coordinated a global, four-year campaign that culminated in ISKCON’s worldwide, grand centennial celebrations of 1996.

Known for his inspiring lectures and melodious *kīrtanas,* Lokanāth Swami has attended hundreds of ISKCON festivals worldwide. Except the very first Māyāpur festival, he participated in all the Māyāpur-Vrindāvana festivals from 1972 till date. At present, he is the zonal secretary for Noida and the state of Maharashtra, where he oversees the development of ISKCON projects. He is the author of *My Prabhupāda, Kumbha: The Festival of Immortality* and *Vraja-maṇḍala Darśana* among other books. He has several new books projected for publishing in the next couple of years.

Lokanāth Swami provided the inspiration to begin ISKCON’s yearly Vraja-maṇḍala *parikramā* in 1987, and currently serves as chairman of the *parikramā* management board. He has personally walked the *parikramā* path for many years, and has given (and continues to give) many of the talks on Vraja-maṇḍala *parikramā*. He is also credited with having developed Navadvīpa-maṇḍala *parikramā,* and has guided groups of pilgrims to holy places like Dvārakā, Jagannātha Purī, Pandharpur, and Tirupati.
